### TECHNICAL BROCHURE

**Installation of underground**

**HV cable systems**

WG B1. 61

```
ISBN : 978 - 2 - 85873 - 594 - 5
```

## Executive summary

Technical Brochure 194 “Construction, Laying and Installation Techniques for Extruded and Self-
contained Fluid Filled Cable Systems” was published by WG 21.17 in 2001. The main purpose was to
describe the Cable System, the Construction and Laying Techniques and the Cable Installation Design
and Laying Techniques for high voltage cables.

The recommendations adopted in TB 194 were prepared considering the conductor cross-sections
available at that moment, mainly up to 1200 mm². The use of cables with extruded insulation and larger
cross sections has become usual in the recent years and it appears that the effects of the thermo-
mechanical forces on extruded insulation and conductor cross-sections considered 20 years ago need
to be better taken into account.

With the purpose of studying in detail the above issue and give recommendations about the approach
to be used when dealing with extruded insulation and large conductor cross-sections, a dedicated
Working Group (B1.34) was established, which produced in 2016 the Technical Brochure TB 669
“Mechanical forces in large cross section cable systems”.

During their 2016 plenary Annual Meeting (August 23rd, 2016), the members of SCB1, upon proposal of
the Strategic Advisory Group, unanimously decided to launch a new Working Group, titled WG B1.
(Installation of HV Cable Systems) to update the TB 194, taking into account the work of several Working
Groups/Task Forces and considering the work done WG B1.34.

On March 2017 the Technical Committee approved the creation of a new Working Group named B1.
titled "Installation of HV Cable Systems".

The terms of reference defined are as follows:

1) To review existing and innovative methods for HV cable installation. The review should include cable
installed in trenches, ducts and tunnels.

2) To compare the relative merits of the installation methods and to give recommendations for their
application.

3) The method of working will be:

1. Remind existing practices for cable installation and identify the factors responsible for the choice of
    a particular practice.
2. Review possible innovations, improvements and alternatives in the light of increasing economic and
    environmental pressure.
3. Give recommendations for the application of new installation technologies to high voltage cable
    systems.

4) To review the calculations and parameters necessary to perform design calculations for cable
installation (including for example, on the one hand, pulling tension during installation, and on the other
hand requirements for installations in tunnels, ducts, manholes and towers).

5) To compare theoretical productions with the results of engineering trials.

6) To recommend simplified methods for the calculation of design parameters for cable laying.

This Technical Brochure is therefore the result of both an updating in the civil construction and cable
installation techniques according to the state of the art, as well as the collection of the design rules for
calculation of thermo-mechanical forces and pulling forces, with some inclusion about system design.

## 1. Chapter one – Introduction

## 1.1 Introduction

Technical Brochure 194 “Construction, Laying and Installation Techniques for Extruded and Self-
contained Fluid Filled Cable Systems” was published by WG 21.17 in 2001. The main purpose was to
describe the Cable System, the Construction Techniques and the Cable Installation Design and Laying
Techniques for high voltage cables.

Nevertheless, the rules adopted in TB 194 were deemed to be not totally appropriate when dealing with
the effects of thermo-mechanical forces on extruded insulation and large conductor cross-sections,
which use is becoming even more usual in recent years.

With the purpose of studying in detail the above issue and give recommendations about the approach
to be used when dealing with extruded insulation and large conductor cross-sections, a dedicated
Working Group (B1.34) was established, which produced in 2016 the Technical Brochure TB 669
“Mechanical forces in large cross section cable systems”.

During their 2016 plenary Annual Meeting (August 23rd, 2016), the members of SCB1, upon proposal of
the Strategic Advisory Group, unanimously decided to launch a new Working Group, titled WG B1.
(Installation of HV Cable Systems) to update the TB 194, taking into account the work of several Working
Groups/Task Forces and considering the work done WG B1.34.

This Technical Brochure is therefore the result of both an updating in the civil construction and cable
installation techniques according to the state of the art, as well as the collection of the design rules for
calculation of thermo-mechanical forces and pulling forces, with some inclusion about the electrical
system design.

The values in this document are for reference only, and specific values will be set after consideration by
each company.

## 1.2 Terms of references

Scope of work is to update the Technical Brochure 194 “Construction, Laying and Installation
Techniques for Extruded and Self-contained Fluid Filled Cable Systems” from 2001. The existing TB
194 has the following Chapters:

1) Introduction

2) Description of the Cable System

3) Construction Techniques

4) Cable Installation Design and Laying Techniques

5) External Aspects

6) Design of a Link

7) Glossary

8) Bibliography

The revisions to TB194 also takes into account the following recent CIGRE SC B1 works:

- TB 640 - Guide for Rating of High Voltage Cables
- TB 669 - Mechanical forces in large cross section cables systems


- The work of WG B1.48 on Trenchless Technology (now TB 770)
- The work of WG B1.41 on Long Term Performance of Soil and Backfill for Cable Systems is also
    close to completion (now TB 714)
- The work of WG B1-34 on Mechanical Forces in Large Cross Section Cable Systems has identified
    some areas where TB 194 should be updated (now TB 669)
- The work of WG B1.38 on After laying tests on AC and DC cable systems with new Technologies
- The work of TF B1-53 Technologies on Installation Related Cable Damages. This last suggests
    including information regarding the following topics:
       o How to co-ordinate cable design, engineering and installation given project interfaces
          between different companies
       o Add best practices and practical examples to the installation guidelines
       o Add examples of cable damage related to installation errors.

The work includes the revision (where applicable) to construction techniques and installation techniques
which mean, as previously defined into TB 194, are as follows:

Construction technique

Technique used to create the cable route, mainly covering the civil works such as trenching.

Installation technique

The installation method used (basically air and buried installations) in relationship with the cable design
and the associated cable design issues.

The scope of work covers extruded and self-contained fluid filled cable systems.

Submarine cables are not included in the scope of this WG.

## 1.3 Document structure.................................................................................................................................

The WG discussed about the definitions coming from TB 194 for “Construction techniques” and
“Installation techniques” and the possibility to leave them separated in the new document or to combine
them into a common part.

With the aim to treat both the construction technique and the installation technique for each specific
topic, it was established that the new document structure combines them into one common part named
“CIVIL CONSTRUCTION AND CABLE INSTALLATION TECHNIQUES”, treating the thermo-
mechanical design and the pulling calculation into a separate part named “System design principles”.

Therefore, this document will be composed of the following main parts:

#### PART 1: INTRODUCTION

#### PART 2: MATERIALS DESCRIPTION

#### PART 3: CIVIL CONSTRUCTION AND CABLE INSTALLATION TECHNIQUES

#### PART 4: SYSTEM DESIGN PRINCIPLES

#### PART 5: APPENDIXES


In particular:

PART 2: MATERIALS DESCRIPTION, covers the description of the main cable and accessory designs.

PART 3: CIVIL CONSTRUCTION AND CABLE INSTALLATION TECHNIQUES, is composed of all the
installation methods used for high voltage cables and includes, with respect to TB 194, some new
technologies and a list of some of the found hazards concerning high voltage cable installation coming
from bad design and/or poor installation technique.

Each section is presented by a “One pager”; a single sheet that illustrates in brief the civil construction
technique phases.

A section relevant to manholes and joint bay design has also been added.

PART 4: SYSTEM DESIGN PRINCIPLES, includes some principles in the design of high voltage
electrical systems, such as ratings and short circuit, metallic sheath bonding, EMF principles and
shielding systems, corrosion, pulling force calculation and thermo-mechanical design, including cleating
systems.

Comparison with TB 194

Comparing this publication with TB 194, it can be observed that, in terms of civil construction, some of
the most popular installations, such as ducting or directly burying, does not differ substantially from the
past, unless for some consideration on the inclusion of additional infrastructures for the inclusion of
monitoring ad diagnostic systems. Some new installation technology has been included and the
description of trenchless technologies expanded. At the same time, trenchless technologies are
progressively replacing some technologies used in the past, such as “Embedding”.

Interfaces section has been expanded to take into account that new cable systems must accomodate
the existing network and current limitations. For example, new cables must be able to adapt to all types
of substations (air or gas insulated) with their ancillary services. If the new cable system must join an
existing line there will be no problem if it is an overhead line, another underground cable or even a
submarine cable. Most of new cables are extruded insulated cables (with or without integrated fiber
optic) but many existing cables are paper type, so they need to be adaptable. Additional
recommendations relevant to pre-commissioning and commissioning test schemes have been
introduced to consider the fact that a cable can be manufactured in one country, installed by a company
of another country and finally be owned of another utility company of other different country.

New pulling calculation considerations have been included, covering long duct length installations, as
well as additional technical information, useful for the correct choice of the pulling method.

Comparison with TB 669

With reference to the thermo-mechanical force aspects, as stated in TB 669, most of the general
equations in TB 194, Chapter 4 were found to be valid and integrated with considerations on the design
of cable systems with 1000 mm^2 and greater conductor cross-sections and extruded insulation with the
objective of increasing reliability and reducing the risk of poor planning and installation.

The 1000 mm^2 cross section is considered the size over which the impact of thermo-mechanical forces
might have a significative impact on the cable system (cable, accessories and structures) and require
therefore special attention in the design because of their magnitude and the effects on cleats and
support structures during service operation.

Variations of friction between core and sheath, as well as different sheathing materials and geometry
affect the mechanical behaviour of cables and, of course, have an impact on the thermo-mechanical
response, thus requiring different design consideration.


This brochure covers the following installation arrangements:

Rigid installation;

Flexible installation;

Duct-manhole systems;

Vertical shafts;

Transition between rigid and flexible installations.

The interaction with accessories and clamping arrangements for “rigid” and “flexible” systems and the
design and application of cable cleats are considered and these are mostly derived by TB 669 with
minor additions.

Where available, analytic calculation methods are given to solve the individual installation arrangements
as above mentioned.

Although some parameters such as the coefficient of thermal expansion, the effective axial stiffness and
the effective bending stiffness are fundamental parameters for a correct evaluation of the thermo-
mechanical forces and their impact on the system design, the exact knowledge of these parameters can
only be evaluated by experimental measurements. This requires a significant amount of data covering
all types of cable structures in use and it is unfortunately not available at the time being.

For the above reason, as WG B1.61, we have decided to not include in this TB all appendixes relevant
to the test setups for performing the above measurements, as well as the mathematical development of
formulas.

These remain available in TB 669.

As stated in there, the reader is advised that the design of cable thermo-mechanical installations is a
complex subject that is particular to each cable application. Formulae and mechanical parameters are
suggested in this technical brochure to help the cable installation designer. The cable installation
designer is responsible for the choice of the formulae and parameters suggested in this technical
brochure, and for the development of different design approaches, formulae and parameters.

## 1.4 Membership

Membership of the Working Group is largely composed (about 50%) of representatives from utilities with
significant experience of cable installation and equally shared for the reminder between cable
manufacturers and consultancy companies.

## 1.5 Survey

A survey was previously done in TB 194. It included a lot of questions and it appears now to be probably
hard to cope with for respondents.

Taking into account such experience and the time available to get the answers, it was planned to make
a survey much lighter and easier to answer to. It was basically set up in the form of a sheet with answers
to choose from and validation features.

The survey has covered the practices from the last 10 years.

The following topics have been included in the questionnaire:

- Installation mode (general)


- Installation mode (specific)
- Legal aspects
- Environmental and technical constraints
- Safety aspects
- Reliability, maintenance and repair and de-commissioning aspects
- Developments
- Monitoring systems

Fifty (50) questionnaires have been received and the results are shown in Appendix C.

## 1.6 Useful links

We would like to mention a series of future technical brochures and papers that could be useful during
the construction design phase.

They are the following:

- WG B1.54 “Behavior of cable systems under large disturbances”
- WG B1.83 “Grounding aspects for HVDC land cable connections”
- JWG B1/C3.85 “Environmental impact of decommissioning of underground and submarine
    cables”
- IEEE P2417/D7.7 “Draft Guide for Early Detection, Mitigation, Preventative Measures, and
    Response to Smoke, Fire, and Explosions in Underground Electrical Structures”


## 2. Materials description

Scope of this chapter is to give a quick overview of the HV cable system components, which choice is
directly related to the type of civil constructuion and installation technique selected, as well as the
environmental characteristics.

Particular relevance is also given to the fact that HV cable systems require a high level of quality
assurance and personnel training.

## 2.1 Cables

### 2.1.1 Cable design types

The following chapters are relevant to a brief description of the cable technologies nowadays used in
high voltage land cable systems. Although some insulation properties and characteristics may change
for AC and DC applications, they can be divided into two main families, namely:

- Cables with extruded insulation; Low Density Polyethylene (LDPE), High Density Polyethylene
    (HDPE), Cross-linked Polyethylene (XLPE), Hard Grade Ethylene Propylene Rubber (HEPR)
- Cables with lapped insulation; Self-contained Fluid Filled (SCFF), mass impregnated with traditional
    Kraft-paper (MI) and Paper-Polypropylene Laminated (PPL), as well as pipe type

Generally, high voltage cables are characterized by the presence of the following main components:

- Conductor
- Semi-conducting conductor screen
- Insulation (also called “dielectric”)
- Semi-conducting insulation screen
- Metallic sheath or screen (or a combination of the two)
- Reinforcement (for lapped insulated cables)
- Protective outer covering

Each of these components is described in the following paragraphs.

### 2.1.2 Cables with extruded insulation

The cable systems used today are a product of the industry evolution generated by ever-increasing
design experience and project requirements. Significant improvements have been made by progressive
developments and many earlier problems have been eliminated.

Examples of product improvements for extruded insulated cables include, for example, improved
procedures to improve raw material purity, true-triple head extrusion to improve bonding of the conductor
and insulation screen with the insulation layer to reduce voids, degassing requirements, the use of
different additives to assist with HVDC polarity reversal, the use of various metallic sheaths, as well as
system and accessories designs.

### 2.1.2.1 Conductors

Conductors are made from copper or aluminium, using wires or segments. Copper conductors are
mainly used when a high current carrying requirement is needed (typically >1000 A). In case of lower
requirements of current rating, aluminium conductors are frequently preferred due to the lower cost
impact.

Another important parameter that has to be taken into account is the resistance with alternating current.
Because of the skin effect, the “a.c. resistance” of a conductor tends to be more significant as the cross
section increases. The skin effect forces the current to flow along the peripheral areas, so the central
section of the conductor becomes less efficient. For this reason a special conductor construction, called
“segmental Milliken”, is used with big cross sections (typically from 1200 mm^2 on). The conductor is
typically divided into segments (typically from 4 to 6) that are slightly insulated from each other, so the
current is “constrained” to flow inside the segments.


Moreover, in order to obtain a further reduction of the skin effect, it’s possible to oxidize or enamel the
wires inside the segments.

Water blocking of the conductor is generally recommended in order to limit the water propagation along
the conductor in case of damage to the cable.

### 2.1.2.2 Insulation and semi-conducting screens

The main materials used for high voltage extruded cables are detailed below:

- Low Density Polyethylene and High Density Polyethylene (LDPE/HDPE) are thermoplastic
    materials that were used for a limited period. Due to their limitations with regard to working and short
    circuit temperatures (respectively 70°C and 130°C for LDPE and 80°C and 160°C for HDPE). They
    have been replaced by other extruded materials. LDPE/HDPE have also been observed presenting
    some difficulties in handling the cables at low temperatures.
- High Modulus or Hard Grade Ethylene Propylene Rubber (HEPR) is a thermosetting compound and
    it is made from a blend of components, which formulation can be modified in order to obtain specific
    characteristics. It offers good performance in terms of “water treeing” resistance, as well as good
    elasticity characteristics. HEPR has high dielectric losses compared to XLPE and this has limited
    its use to the maximum voltage level of 170 kV, even if higher voltages are allowed for this insulating
    compound. HEPR has a rated maximum conductor temperature of 90°C, an emergency rating of
    approximately 130°C and a conductor short-circuit rating of 250°C. The same performances are
    valid also for EPR (Ethylene Propylene Rubber).
- Cross Linked Polyethylene (XLPE) is a thermosetting material. It offers a degree of purity higher
    than that offered by the HEPR/EPR and this makes this insulation a suitable material for applications
    up to 500 kV. Contrarily to EPR insulation, XLPE is very sensitive to moisture, which lead to its
    degradation. For this reason it necessary to prevent water penetration into the insulated core. This
    is achieved by applying a radial water barrier. XLPE cables have rated maximum conductor
    temperature of 90°C and an emergency rating of up to approximately 105°C (depending on
    emergency time). The conductor short-circuit rating is 250°C. Semiconducting screens are used on
    all high voltage cables to ensure a smooth electrical interface between conducting and insulating
    regions. The stranded profile of the conductor would initiate localised field concentration (i.e. high
    stress areas) if interfaced directly with the insulation and a consequent risk of ionisation and
    ultimately electrical breakdown. Hence provision of semiconducting screens removes these high
    stress areas and provides uniform stressing at the interface with the insulation. To ensure a good
    interface, all three layers (i.e. conductor screen, insulation and insulation screen) are extruded in
    one process (triple extrusion).

### Other materials e.g. PP-based HPTE, don’t have the same extended experience yet but they

### appear to behave similarly to the above mentioned insulating materials. The TB refers

### therefore to XLPE as this is the most commonly used extruded insulation type, but the same

### considerations may be applied to all extruded cables

### 2.1.2.3 Metallic screen

While EPR/HEPR cables up to Um = 170 kV may be used without any metallic barrier, it is recommended
that all XLPE cables with rated voltages higher than 60 kV are provided with a radial moisture barrier
comprised of one of the following sheathing methods:

- Extruded lead sheath;
- Extruded corrugated aluminium sheath (CAS)
- Smooth longitudinally welded aluminium sheath (WAS)
- Aluminium or copper laminated and glued foil
- Corrugated copper sheath
- Corrugated stainless steel sheath

The main functions of the metallic sheaths are:

- Protection against the ingress of moisture
- To give mechanical protection, thus preventing damages against external actions


- To withstand the single-phase fault current
- To carry the charging current
- To provide the earth or near-earth potential reference for the cable insulation

Sometimes the above designs may be used in combination with copper or aluminium wires in order to
increase the short circuit carrying capability.

All the above sheathing methods have advantages and disadvantages when compared towards the
following topics:

- Thermal performance
- Electrical performance
- Corrosion resistance
- Water resistance
- Mechanical fatigue
- Weight
- Environmental impact

Consideration of the optimum sheathing system will usually depend on the specific application
(installation technique) and sometimes from specific Customer's requirements.

Guidelines for tests on high voltage cables with extruded insulation and laminated protective coverings
are discussed in the TB 446.

### 2.1.2.4 Outer sheath

Generally PE (polyethylene) is used as the sheathing material for buried cables. This is normally
medium-density polyethylene (MDPE) or alternatively high density polythene (HDPE), both of which
provide good mechanical protection, good corrosion protection and good resistance to abrasion. PVC
(polyvinyl chloride) may also be used for buried cables, especially in areas where hydrocarbons are
present.

Cables laid in air (gallery, shaft, tunnel, etc.) normally have either a flame retardant low smoke PE or a
PVC sheath. Both systems are widely used. Extruded semi conductive outer layers may also be present.

Where cables are exposed to termite and/or rodent attacks, it is necessary to provide additional cable
protections.

Plastic sheath hardness, associated with the HV cable size, is sometimes sufficient to prevent termite
attack but in some cases it is necessary to provide further protections such as additives or nylon tapes
or brass tapes or polypropylene..

Rodents attack is much more dangerous and may cause severe sheath damage. Where cables are
directly buried, an armour layer may be sometimes necessary. For cables laid into ducts, if duct is
undamaged, sealing the entrance of the duct may be enough to prevent rodent from intruding into the
ducts.

### 2.1.3 Self-contained fluid filled cables (SCFF)

The self-contained fluid filled (SCFF) cable system has been in service for almost one century and has
proven to be a very reliable underground cable system for the distribution and transmission of electrical
power.

The cables systems used today are a product of the industry evolution generated by ever-increasing
design experience. Significant improvements have been made by progressive developments and many
earlier problems have been eliminated.

Examples of product improvement include, for example, improved quality of the cellulose paper,
insulation Impregnating fluid development, the use of improved lead alloy sheaths and corrugated
aluminium sheaths, as well as system and accessories designs.


### 2.1.3.1 Conductors

Conductors are made from copper or aluminium.

For single core cable there is generally a central hollow core that allows the dielectric fluid to move freely
into the cable. Segmental "keystone" designs are normally used but constructions with segmental
"Milliken" type conductors with a central spiral support to form the oil duct are also used. Three core
cables usually have a separate helical duct or ducts bound up in the fillers with the cores inside its metal
sheath. However, some three core cables have been constructed without fillers or oil ducts and the
space between insulated cores allows the fluid to flow freely within the metal sheath.

### 2.1.3.2 Paper/Fluid insulation

Fluid filled cables have a multi-layer cellulose paper dielectric lapped onto a screened central copper or
aluminium conductor, with the screened core being enclosed by a pressure-tight metallic sheath. A
central hollow core in the conductor provides a passage for dielectric fluid. The oil pressure necessary
to prevent void formation is 1 to 3 bar, but operation at higher pressures (up to 15 bar) is possible.

In three-core cables the screened cores are laid up within a common metallic sheath, with fluid ducts
normally located in the core interstices.

Advances in lapping technology have involved the use of dense papers of high impermeability, which
raise the dielectric impulse withstand strength. Important factors during cable design and manufacture
include:

- Grading of paper thickness through the dielectric, to optimize both electric and mechanical
    performance
- Control of paper storage and lapping enclosure humidity
- Butt gap registration
- Control of drying and impregnation: to avoid exposure to atmosphere and to provide low dielectric
    loss factor

Traditional “Kraft paper” as well as PPL may be used.

### 2.1.3.3 Impregnant

The papers are normally impregnated with an insulating fluid to prevent the formation of voids and the
associated ionization when energised. For SCFF cables, this is normally a low viscosity synthetic type
of the alkylbenzene chain like, for example, dodecylbenzene (DDB).

This has also exceptional gas absorption properties over the range of cable operating temperatures.

Mineral oil was used in the past but may be also used nowadays.

Thermal ageing of fluid impregnated paper causes the slow evolution of certain gases and the use of
dodecylbenzene (DDB) imparts a greatly improved resistance to thermal degradation as compared to
earlier mineral oil impregnants. In 1990 a more biodegradable (linear chain) type of DDB was introduced
which is environmentally advantageous in the event of fluid spillage or leak.

Other work associated with traditional fluid filled technology has led to the development of a non-
halogenated fluid having non-fire propagating properties. Cables impregnated with this “low flam” fluid
offer a safer alternative in installations having an inherent fire risk such as in tunnels or vertical shafts.

Heat and vacuum treatment during manufacturing removes air and moisture from the papers.

### 2.1.3.4 PPL insulation

Of all the product improvements, the introduction of polypropylene paper laminate (PPL) insulation
systems is the most significant improvement in the recent history of self-contained fluid filled cables.

The main advantage of PPL is derived from its low dielectric loss factor, which significantly reduces the
losses generated within the insulation, as compared to cellulose paper.

In conventional paper fluid insulation, particularly when operating at 400kV, a significant part of the
permissible cable temperature rise during operation may be used up by heat generated through
dielectric losses, with a consequent restriction on current carrying capacity. The use of polypropylene in


a laminate tape form together with conventional cellulose paper, results in a dielectric having both lower
tanδ and ɛr than conventional paper/fluid. The result is a similar dielectric loss performance to XLPE.

### 2.1.3.4.1 Rating and dielectric losses

The dielectric losses in A.C. cables are a function of voltage squared. The losses therefore become
more significant as the system voltage increases. The reduction in the dielectric loss leads to an increase
in current rating for the same conductor size. A reduction in the dielectric losses of around 67% is
achieved by adopting PPL. This is offset by an increase in the conductor (I^2 R) losses at the higher load
current. The total losses are, however, 5% less even at the higher rating. Ratings are comparable to
those obtained with XLPE cable.

2.1.3.4.2 Further benefits of PPL

A further advantage with PPL arising from the lower value of relative permittivity is that for the same
cable size (in terms of dimensions and electrical design stress) the capacitance of the cable is reduced.

This, in turn, reduces the charging current and the amount of reactive compensation required particularly
in networks where there is a large quantity of underground cable installed. Today, with more focus on
the cost of losses, this is becoming a significant factor.

Further benefits in terms of external factors include:

- PPL allows cables to be installed at greater depths
- Closer cable spacing in conditions of higher values of the thermal resistivity and of higher ambient
    temperatures
- less need for external cooling

### 2.1.3.5 Metallic sheath

SCFF cables requires an impervious metallic barrier to contain the insulating fluid. This can be
either an extruded lead sheath or an extruded corrugated aluminium sheath (CAS).

The main functions of the metallic sheaths are:

- Protection against the ingress of moisture
- To give mechanical protection, thus preventing damages against external actions
- To withstand the single-phase fault current
- To carry the charging current
- To provide the earth or near-earth potential reference for the cable insulation
- To contain the insulating fluid

Consideration of the optimum sheathing system will usually depend on the specific application
(installation technique) and sometimes from specific Customer's requirements.

### 2.1.3.6 Reinforcement

SCFF cables operates with an internal pressure. When sheathed with lead alloys, the cables need to
be provided with a tape reinforcement layer (typically copper, bronze or stainless steel) in order to avoid
lead sheath mechanical failure.

### 2.1.3.7 Outer sheath

Generally PE (polyethylene) is used as the sheathing material for buried cables. This is normally
medium-density polyethylene (MDPE) or alternatively high density polythene (HDPE), both of which
provide good mechanical protection, good corrosion protection and good resistance to abrasion. PVC
(polyvinyl chloride) may also be used for buried cables, especially in areas where hydrocarbons are
present.

Cables laid in air (gallery, shaft, tunnel, etc.) normally have either a flame retardant PE or a PVC sheath.
Both systems are widely used.


### 2.1.4 Mass impregnated cables (MI)

Classical Paper Mass Impregnated (MI or MIND) and, more recently, PPL-MI (with use of polypropylene-
paper laminate tapes as a replacement for of Kraft paper tapes) have been considered for lapped
insulation materials.

The independence of this cable type from a hydraulic feeding system, has made it the preferred solution
for long D.C. interconnection systems.

MI cables were first used for an HVDC application in 1954 for the Gotland 1 project and have proven to
be highly reliable for both underground and submarine LCC or VSC HVDC applications.

MI cables comprise of special kraft paper insulation wrapped under high tension around a conductor,
vacuum dried, impregnated with high viscosity insulating fluid, sheathed with lead alloy, and jacketed.
These cables are non-draining in the event of cable damage or failure. MI cables can operate with a
maximum conductor temperature of 55°C.

New MI cable designs for HVDC applications have been developed by the industry using Polypropylene
Laminate Paper (PPLP) with a layer of polypropylene placed between two layers of kraft paper. This
insulation design permits higher operating temperature compared to the traditional MI cables. MI-PPL
cables can operate with a maximum conductor temperature of 80°C.

Apart for the conductor and the insulation design, the rest of the construction is similar to those used for
the SCFF cables, except for the reinforcement (generally steel) which is applied between the inner
polyethylene sheath and the outer sheath.

### 2.1.5 Cables integrating fibre optic units

Integrated optical fibres may be provided in a HV cable mainly for cable monitoring and diagnostic
purposes (distributed temperature measurements, distributed acoustic sensing, etc.).

Optical fibres are typically included into one or more non-ferromagnetic tubes stranded together with a
wire screen or into flat elements, to be placed underneath a metallic sheath.

The installation of HV cables integrating such units require to be specifically treated in terms of handling
and length.

In terms of handling, it is necessary to check if additional constraints in terms of minimum bending radii
and maximum sidewall pressure are recommended with reference to a HV cable without integrated F.O.
units.

About shipping lengths, it is necessary to consider the additional F.O. length required to perform optical
splicing at terminations and joints. The extra F.O. length reflects into an extra HV cable length.

In terms of testing, some standards give additional requirements for cables and accessories with
integrated optical fibres in terms of testing at factory and on site.

Please refrer to the work from WG B1.73 “Recommendations for the use and testing of Fibre Optic
Cables used in Land Cable Systems” for inclusion of fibre optic cables in land cables.

These are mainly attenuation measurements.

## 2.2 Accessories

Joints and terminations are fundamental devices in insulated cable system and must have the same
performance as the cable on which they are installed. The basic function of the joints is to connect
pieces of cable together, while that of the terminations is to seal the end of the cable and provide the
connection with other components of the network.

### All the operations that contribute to the accessories packaging have their own specifications. Experience

has shown that only qualified personnel can guarantee reliable accessories assembly. The procedures
for the qualification of the necessary personnel and equipment, which are specific to each operation,
are subject to specific procedures. The quality of the jointing/termination activity is strictly linked to the
scrupulous respect of the procedures developed during the development of the individual processes.

The reliability and quality of the system proposed is proven by type testing programs.


### 2.2.1 Compatibility of the accessories with the cable

It is essential to select the design of accessory to be compatible with the particular cable type and the
particular service application. Compatibility should be validated and be supported by appropriate tests,
or service experience. In particular the performance of the accessory is dependent on the quality, skill
and training of the jointing personnel in the installation conditions and on the use of the specialised tools
required for a particular accessory.

The itemised sub-headings below form the basis of the information that is needed by the manufacturer
and installer of the cable and accessories. For many applications the cable manufacturer also
manufactures, supplies and installs the accessories as part of the complete cable circuit, thus the
information is immediately available in-house. In the event that the user purchases the accessories
separately from the cable, then the following items from the basis of the questions that should be asked
to the manufacturers of the cable and accessories to ensure that the accessories are suitable for the
projects where they will be installed. As the design of the cable can depend upon the civil construction
and cable installation technique, the accessory design or selection must be made accordingly.

### 2.2.1.1 Number of cable cores

The user should determine whether the cable construction is of single, three core or triplex construction
(i.e. three single core cables twisted together). The design of the accessory and the method of assembly
is dependent upon the number of cable cores.

### 2.2.1.2 Cable constructional details

For satisfactory service performance it is most important that the correct size of accessory is selected
to suit the particular cable. The outer diameter of the cable insulation, its tolerance and shape are
particularly important in the selection of an accessory employing a pre-moulded component, such as an
elastomeric stress cone or an elastomeric joint moulding. Such components are designed to fit a specific
range of diameters of prepared cable insulation, (that is with the insulation screen removed and the
insulation smoothed and shaped). The components must not be used outside this range. The minimum
diameter is determined by the need to achieve sufficient pressure to eliminate voids at the interface with
the cable insulation. The maximum diameter is determined by such considerations as:

a) preventing damage by over stretching during assembly

b) limiting the maximum pressure at the interface such that compression set of the cable insulation
and moulded insulation is minimised.

The diameter and tolerance of the conductor and of its compaction (the ratio of the effective cross-
sectional area of the metal to the total area occupied) are needed in selecting a connector that will
exhibit stable conductivity and high mechanical strength.

The diameters and tolerances of the cable metallic barrier and over sheath are needed to ensure that
accessory metallic flanges and other components can be passed back over the cable during assembly.

The detailed cable construction should be obtained from the cable manufacturer, which includes the
following information as a minimum requirement. Diameters, maximum and minimum tolerances,
eccentricity dimensions, construction and material need to be obtained for each of the following cable
components:

- Conductor and special features (e.g. water blocking), if any
- Conductor screen
- Insulation (tolerances)
- Insulation screen
- Screen wires, if any
- Longitudinal water blocking, if any
- Metallic barrier, if any, for example whether an extruded sheath, a welded sheath, or a laminated
    foil barrier. Also whether of cylindrical or corrugated shape
- Over sheath
- Armour, if any


- Special features (e.g. presence of integrated optical fibre or pilot wires)

### 2.2.1.3 Conductor area and diameters

The user should ensure that the accessory has been designed and tested for the particular cable
conductor size. The electrical performance of an accessory design can become critical on large
conductor cables because of the high cable insulation screen stress.

The user should ensure that the conductor connections in the complete kit of components are supplied
to suit the particular conductor construction. The conductor connection must be capable of carrying the
same current as the cable conductor and must be capable of withstanding the cable longitudinal
thermomechanical forces, depending on the installation design, these being proportional to the cross
sectional area.

### 2.2.1.4 Operating temperature of the cable conductor and sheath

The operating temperature of the cable conductor and sheath under continuous, short term overload
and short circuit current loading have to be taken into account properly.

The materials of the accessory must be capable of operating satisfactorily at the operating temperatures
specified for the cable.. The short term overload temperatures depend upon the type of cable and
application. The temperature of the conductor under short circuit is dependent on the insulating material.
IEC 61443 Standard may be taken as a reference The permitted short circuit temperature of the cable
extruded metallic sheath or screen wires is determined by the type of metallic sheath and thermoplastic
over sheath, this temperature usually being significantly less than that what the cable insulation can
withstand.

### 2.2.1.5 Compatibility of the accessory with the type of cable insulation and semi-

### conducting screens

Physical compatibility with the extruded cable

The insulation of the polymeric cable must be identified by the user. There are significant differences
between the electrical and mechanical characteristics of extruded insulation. The usual insulants for
extruded polymeric cables in the voltage class of 60 kV and above being XLPE (crosslinked
polyethylene), LDPE (low density polyethylene), HDPE (high density polyethylene) and EPR (ethylene
propylene rubber) or HEPR (High Modulus or Hard Grade Ethylene Propylene Rubber).

Chemical compatibility with the extruded cable

The type of insulating liquid or lubricant used in joints and terminations should be identified to ensure
that these do not affect the properties of the polymeric insulation and semi-conducting screens
employed in the cable and accessories. For example:

Hydrocarbon liquids at elevated temperature can cause swelling of XLPE and EPR insulation and
reduction of the conducting properties of screens;

Silicone liquids can have an effect on silicone rubber components.

Compatibility with the paper insulated lapped cable

In the case of transition joints between polymeric cable and paper insulated cable it is important to
establish whether the cable is of the internally or externally pressurised type and whether the fluid
dielectric is a gas or a liquid; these details will determine the performance requirements of the barrier
plate that segregates the two cables. In the case of mass impregnated non pressurised cables it is
important to determine the type of impregnating compound and whether it is of the liquid type or of the
non-draining type; these details will determine the chemical suitability of the materials employed within
the joint to segregate the impregnating fluid from the insulation of the polymeric cable and joint.

Penetration of a hydrocarbon impregnating fluid into the polymeric cable can result in swelling and
modification of the electrical characteristics of the semi-conducting screens and insulation of both the
cable and accessory components, thereby reducing their electrical performance. Loss of the
impregnating fluid into the polymeric cable can result in eventual electrical failure of the paper cable.

HVDC transition joint reference is included into TB 622.


### 2.2.1.6 Cable electrical design stresses to be withstood by the accessory

The user is advised to obtain the magnitude of the cable stresses at the conductor and insulation
screens, or obtain the dimensions of the cable, thereby permitting the stresses to be calculated. The
unit of stress is kV/mm calculated at U 0 voltage. There are significant differences in the magnitude of
the electrical design stress employed in cables, these being dependent upon the type and thickness of
insulation, the conductor size, the system voltage and the transient voltages. It is essential that the
accessory has been designed and tested to operate at the particular cable design stress.

The stress at the cable insulation screen is of particular significance because this normally determines
the maximum design stress in the accessory. The insulation screen stress is usually of higher magnitude
in those cables designed for high system voltages and large conductor diameters.

### 2.2.1.7 Mechanical forces and movements generated by the cable on the accessory

The magnitude of the forces and movements generated by the cable on the accessory depends upon
the cable materials, the method of cable manufacture and the type of cable installation design (i.e. rigid
or flexible installation).

The following mechanical strains are dependent on the cable construction:

- Insulation retraction (shrink back) for extruded insulation
- Insulation radial thermal expansion
- Oversheath retraction (shrink back)

The following forces are dependent upon the cable construction, current loading, operating temperature,
method and type of cable constraint and accessory design

- Conductor thermomechanical thrust and retraction
- Sheath thermomechanical thrust and retraction

### 2.2.1.8 Short circuit forces

Electromagnetic forces are present during a short circuit between the individual conducting components
of the accessory and between the adjacent cables and the accessory. The following information is
applicable:

- Method of restraint of the accessory and cable
- Method of restraint and the spacing of adjacent cables

### 2.2.2 Compatibility of the accessory performance with that of the cable system

### 2.2.2.1 Circuit performance parameters

The current rating and optimum circuit economics are dictated by the cable conductor size, cable
material costs and the method of installation. To achieve the optimum economical solution it is important
that the accessory design is not allowed to limit the performance of the cable. The accessory must
therefore match the following cable performance:

- Rated voltages (Nominal system voltage U and maximum Um)
- Current rating (Current magnitude)
- Continuous, cyclic and short time overload (Current magnitude, time and temperature)
- Short circuit rating, “phase to earth” and “phase to phase” (Current magnitude, asymmetry, time and
    temperature)
- Basic impulse level (Withstand voltages for lightning impulse and switching surge), (Flash over
    voltage for the system insulation co-ordination of outdoor terminations, if specified)

### 2.2.2.2 Circuit life required

The accessory should match the design life specified for the particular cable circuit. This is typically
requested to be from 20 to 40 years, however some cable circuits are installed as temporary links, for


example in an overhead line circuit or for temporary transformer replacement. Such accessories may
be designed to be suitable for quick assembly with a reduction in performance and service life.

### 2.2.2.3 Metallic screen bonding requirements

The information relevant to the type of bonding leads (concentric or single conductors), their conductor
size and overall dimensions, as well as the type of cable bonding scheme, for example solidly earthed
or specially bonded metallic screens are needed to correctly design the cable system, as well as the
following data:

- Magnitude of induced sheath or screen wire voltage under normal and short circuit current
- Magnitude of circulating sheath or screen wire current under normal loading
- Magnitude of short circuit current
- Magnitude of specified over sheath lightning withstand voltage and DC withstand voltage

It is important that the accessory design incorporates means of connecting the cable screen wires,
metallic tapes or sheath and joint shell to the insulation screen.

### 2.2.2.4 Earth fault requirements

Some utilities require that short circuit currents be returned within the cable system. The user should
ensure that the accessory is also able to withstand this current.

### 2.2.3 Compatibility of the accessory with the cable system design and operating conditions

The user is advised to ensure that accessory design is:

- Compatible with the particular cable installation design, as this determines the mechanical loading
    applied
- Capable of being assembled in the site environmental conditions
- Capable of a satisfactory service performance under adverse climatic conditions

### 2.2.3.1 Type of cable installation design

- Rigidly constrained (cable laid direct in the ground or close cleated)
- Flexible unconstrained (cable horizontally snaked or vertically waved)
- Semi-flexible (cable constrained but permitted to exhibit a controlled deflection, for example at a
    bridge crossing or adjacent to gas immersed switch gear)
- Unfilled duct

### 2.2.3.2 Standard dimensions for cable termination

The user is advised to ensure the following dimensional compliance.

Outdoor and indoor terminations

Harmonisation with existing equipment of the overall height of the off-going bus bar connector and of
the bottom metalwork fixing arrangements to the support structure.

GIS and transformer terminations

Harmonisation of the cable termination with both the design of the metal clad switch gear (internal
diameter, overall length, off-going bus bar connector, bottom metalwork sealing arrangements and
pressure) and the design of the support structure (fixing arrangements for the particular cable constraint
selected).

### 2.2.3.3 Types of accessory installations

- Buried in the ground (laid direct)
- Jointing chamber
- Tunnel


- Above ground
- Bridge
- Tower
- Shaft

### 2.2.3.4 Jointing limitations in restricted installation locations

- Space limitations
- Time limitations (for example arising from road or rail traffic influences)
- Tolerance limitations of assembly personnel (for example arising from extremes of temperature,
    humidity, vibration, noise and induced voltage)

### 2.2.3.5 Mechanical forces applied to the accessory

- Thermomechanical forces
- Earthquake, landslides
- Vibration
- Off-going bus bar at terminations
- Wind loading on bus bars at terminations
- Ice loading on bus bars at terminations
- Short circuit loading on bus bars at terminations
- GIS pressure
- Angle of installation of terminations
- Hydraulic or pneumatic pressure forces at transition joints

### 2.2.3.6 Climatic conditions

Accessories require to be suitable for the extremes climatic conditions expected both in service and
during assembly. Some types of accessories are required to be assembled under controlled
environmental conditions.

- Altitude (reduction of electrical strength of air)
- Air pollution (reduction of electrical strength of outdoor insulator surface)
- Precipitation (reduction in electrical strength of air and outdoor insulator surface)
- Salt fog (reduction in electrical strength of outdoor insulator surface)
- Moisture condensation (reduction in electrical strength of insulator surface)
- Temperature
- Atmospheric humidity
- Ice formation

### 2.2.3.7 Type of accessory outer protection required

The accessory protection is required to provide corrosion protection and, for a specially bonded cable
circuit, insulation from ground.

- Joint box (laid direct in the ground or in air)
- Pedestal insulator (in air)
- Moulded sheet insulation (in air, to protect personnel against electric shock)
- Metallic fences or screens (in air, to protect personnel against electric shock)


### 2.2.3.8 Situations requiring special accessory protection

- Submerged under water
- Fire risk
- Rodents
- Termite infestation

### 2.2.4 Terminations

The function which a termination must meet is to make the connection of a cable to another element of
an electrical system. More precisely, the termination is necessary to seal the end of the cable and
provide for the passage of the conductor from the insulating of the cable to another insulating medium,
generally air, as well as providing for the electrical connection (for earthing or other purpose) of the any
screens or metallic sheaths.

To perform these functions, each type of termination must provide for:

- A high current connection between the cable and the external busbar or overhead conductor
- An adequate current connection to the local earth to allow the short circuit current to flow from the
    metal sheath or cable shield
- An insulation between the cable conductor and the ground with the same performance of the cable
    insulation
- An external profile suitable to comply with the required creepage distance
- A corona shield to redistribute electrostatic lines of force
- Supporting the cable and resisting thermo-mechanical loads and external stresses, such as wind,
    ice and bar loads
- Protect the insulation and the cable shields from the water inlet; from the entry of pressure dielectric
    fluid coming from adjacent prefabricated armoured systems
- Protection of metalwork against corrosion
- Ability to withstand cable thermomechanical loads and external forces such as wind, ice and busbar
    loading

In addition, as an additional task, the termination must provide for disconnecting (isolating) the metal
sheath / shield from the ground potential, in the case of special shield connections, or in case it is
necessary to check the integrity of the external protection sheath.

The following termination types exist:

- For outdoor or indoor use, to connect the cable to an aerial busbar or to a line in an aerial cable
- Entry into an armoured system that connects the cable to an armored cabin isolated in pressurized
    gas (GIS metal enclosed switchgear)
- Entry into a transformer that connects the cable to a bar in armoured system with oil insulation
    (Metalclad bus bar oil insulated)
- Temporary terminations

As far as outdoor terminations are concerned, particular care shall be paid to the design of terminations
for temporary installations, which are normally of the "flexible" dry type.

Outdoor terminations can have porcelain or composite insulator and are filled with an insulating fluid (oil
or gas) or, for certain voltage classes, they can be of dry type.

The thermo-mechanical thrust of the cable conductor is generally discharged on the support structures
through the insulator. In the case of internal/external terminations, which have a long and free conductor
section to expand linearly, to reduce this thrust and avoid deflection by tip loading inside the insulator,
it is provided to enter the termination with an appropriate curvature in the air, placed under the
termination. Different criteria are possible with regard to the clamping of the cable under the terminal:


- Generally, for SCFF cables the cable is rigidly clamped to a suitably curved beam to control the
    curvature, so as to avoid the risk of fatigue failure of the metal screen and therefore of the loss of
    the dielectric fluid
- For the extruded cables instead, generally the cable is curved and suspended under the terminal,
    so that a limited thrust is transferred to the terminal

SCFF cable terminals must withstand the dielectric fluid pressure coming from the cable insulation and
must also allow reverse feed of the dielectric fluid from the termination to the cable. For extruded cable
terminations filled with dielectric liquid, it is necessary to allow the expansion of the liquid due to
temperature variations. The expansion volume can be accommodated using either an air space at the
end of the insulator or external expansion tanks.

In order to prevent uncontrolled loads from being transferred to the termination by the GIS or transformer
bar, a flexible connection should be used.

In areas of high seismic risk it is necessary to verify the design of the outdoor termination and its
supporting structure.

Reference should be made to the work of WG B1.54 “Behavior of cable system under large
disturbances” (TB is imminent).

### 2.2.5 Joints

The design requirements common to each type of joint are:

- A high current connection between conductors,
- Low resistance;
- Joint insulation which meets the same performance standard as the cable;
- A high current connection to permit the flow of short circuit current between the two cable sheaths
    or screen wires
- A metallic joint shell or screen wire connection electrically insulated from earth potential to match
    the insulating integrity of the cable oversheath;
- Protection of the joint and cable insulation against the ingress of water;
- Protection of the joint metalwork against corrosion;
- A tough protective sleeve/casing against mechanical aggressions;
- A heat dissipating device to ensure that the joint is not a hot spot along the power link.

There are different types of joints for extruded cables; the following will be main ones:

- Pre-moulded or prefabricated;
- With taped insulation (field vulcanised);
- With extruded insulation (field moulded).

For SCFF systems, an additional joint is required to isolate adjacent hydraulic sections of the cable route
to ensure the system hydraulic pressure limits are not exceeded. This is referred as a stop joint. These
can have an additional feature of being able to feed oil into the system at the intermediate location.

One of the major objectives in the field of extruded cable systems is the need to develop reliable
couplings that can be mounted in the field with the use of a relatively simple technology. The reliability
of a buried system, in fact, is strictly related to the formation of the joint on the cable with extruded
insulation and this process is a very delicate step in the installation phase of a new circuit. For extruded
cables it is important that the accessory is compatible with the type of extruded cable and with the
particular application for which it is required to be installed. This compatibility must be validated by type
tests and confirmed by the service experience. In particular, the performance of the accessory depends
strictly on the quality of the components, on the technical expertise and training of the joint staff and on
the use of tools specifically prescribed for the type of accessory.


Depending on the specific system design, the joints could be electrically continuous or sectionalized.

Many types of joints are provided with interruptions of the shields/metallic sheaths of the cables on the
two sides of the joint, so as to ensure an electrical insulation between them and to be used in
transposition systems of the sheaths (cross-bonding type) or other special bonding systems. These
joints must ensure the interruption of the electrical continuity of the metal case of the joint, together with
that of the metal or semi-conductive shield on the joint insulation (which constitutes the continuity of the
screen/semi-conductive cable).

Depending on the specific application, the joints can be:

- Transition (for coupling between different cable design technologies, e.g. paper to extruded);
- Asymmetric/bi-metallic (for coupling cables of different sizes and/or conductor size and material);
- Y branch.

Finally, the type of external protection can also be chosen as a function of the specific installation
environment. The following types of external protections are commonly used:

- Taped;
- Heat-shrinkable tubes;
- Plastic or fibreglass coffin box with appropriate type of filling material to deal with dry or wet
    environments.

### 2.2.6 Link boxes

Link boxes are required to interconnect the metallic screens of the cables at the junction points where
special bonding or grounding is necessary and to connect to ground the screens at the terminations.
Depending on the electrical sheath bonding method planned for the specific system, various types of
single-way or three-way link boxes may be used.

In case of special bonding connections, the link boxes are provided with SVL’s (surge voltage limiters).

Particular attention shall be paid to the sizing of the SVL’s in relation to the induced voltages deriving
from short circuits and fault conditions.

Link boxes design shall take into account watertightness properties as well as corrosion aspects.

Note

The design of the correct SVL (and the bonding leads type and length) should also take into account
the possible increase in the EPR (earth potential rise) due to voltage transients. This voltage rise adds
to the SVL’s one thus increasing the voltage at the joint/termination insulating flange.

Please refer to CIGRE TB 283 (Special bonding of high voltage power cables) and CIGRE 797 (Sheath
bonding systems of AC transmission cables - design, testing, and maintenance) for details.

### 2.2.7 Anchoring devices

Anchoring devices are used to mechanically lock the armour of a submarine cable at the shore end,
before jointing to the land cables through a sea-land joint or before the termination after the end of j-
tube in offshore platforms (or hang-off devices).

The device normally consists in a double bolted flange where the armour wires are bent and locked,
fixed to a buried concrete basement and provided with an earth connection.

Different designs are available for steel or copper armour.

### 2.2.8 Hydraulic equipment

The operation of SCFF cables requires the use of special equipment such as:

- Oil tanks
- Gauges (read only, electrical contacts, transducers)
- Pipe insulating connectors


### 2.2.8.1 Oil tanks

Oil tanks can be divided into constant pressure and variable pressure systems.

2.2.8.1.1 Gravity fluid tanks

Constant pressure systems are characterised by a feeding system composed of the so called “gravity
tanks”; tanks without internal gas cells that absorb pressure variations. Since this type of feeding system
was the first to come into service, to this category belong generally very old SCFF cable systems where
the original fluid was mineral oil and cable routes were short with flat profiles. Since the tank pressure
cannot vary, the tank must be installed in a position where the tank is higher than the highest system
point. The tank must contain all fluid expansion from the cable and the accessories under maximum hot
conditions and return the fluid to the system by gravity in the worst cooling conditions whilst maintaining
positive pressures. Small changes in pressure due to changes in fluid levels in the tank are usually
ignored.

2.2.8.1.2 Pre-pressurized fluid tanks

SCFF cables in service today are most likely to use pre-pressurised tanks as their fluid reservoirs. These
tanks contain a number of sealed flexible expanding cells that contain pressurised gas, usually carbon
dioxide or nitrogen. Expansion of the cable fluid is compensated by the compression of these cells. The
tank capacity and the pressure setting must be such that the minimum static pressure is higher than the
highest system point and the maximum static pressure does not cause unacceptable pressure values
for cable and accessories at the lowest system point. The relationship between fluid volume variations
and the corresponding pressure variations of a cable fluid reservoir, of a stated type, is obtained either
by means of a suitable experimental curve or by means of calculations according to the "Ideal Gas Law".

### 2.2.9 Quality assurance scheme for accessory installation

Assembly of the accessories onto cable with extruded insulation is the most vulnerable part of a project
involving the manufacture. Accessories and cables are manufactured and tested under controlled
factory conditions, whereas the in-service performance of the accessory is dependent upon the training,
skill and reliability of the personnel, who are often required to work under adverse site conditions.
Recommendations as to the requirements are described fully in TB 476 Chapter 7 (Cable accessory
workmanship on extruded High Voltage cables).

For many project applications one company will manufacture the cable and accessories and undertake
to complete the installation of the circuit. In other applications, the installer may complete the circuit
using cable and accessories supplied by different manufacturers. In some applications, the installer may
only assemble the accessories. For each application, the requirements of the Quality Assurance (QA)
system are equally rigorous:

### 2.2.9.1 Quality assurance approval for installation

The user should ensure that the installer provides evidence of an approved quality assurance system
for installation to an internationally recognised standard.

### 2.2.9.2 Quality plan

The installer is required to produce a Quality Plan for each project, this includes the project time
schedule together with the requirements for suitably qualified personnel, training, on-site storage of
components and accessories, tools, testing equipment, constructing materials, assembly instructions,
preparation of the jointing environment and records of the assembly work. It is important that the records
of assembly are traceable to the location of each accessory in the cable circuit. If purchasing separately,
the user is advised to ensure that, for the purposes of traceability, the quality systems of the cable
manufacturer, accessory manufacturer and installer are compatible.

### 2.2.9.3 Training of personnel

When selecting the designs of accessories, the user should ensure that up to date training courses are
available for the jointing and supervisory personnel. It is strongly advised that personnel receive training
on the particular designs of accessories and cable. The company should keep an up to date record of
the jointing competencies tested and certified. The certificate should also indicate:

- Voltage class applicable;
- Accessory cover;


- List of skill-sets covered;
- Mechanism used for Certification

The methodology of assessment should also be stated.

Examples of the elements of a training course for assembly personnel are:

- General training at specific system voltages with the standard range of accessories required by the

```
user;
```
- Theoretical understanding of WHY a particular aspect is important;
- Observation that a jointer has understood and executed the instructions correctly;
- Electrical and mechanical testing of the final accessory assembly;
- Repeat training after a defined period for those personnel who have completed general training

```
and where up skilling may be required;
```
- Specified training on a new accessory or cable design for those personnel who have completed

```
general training.
```
At the end of the training course the proficiency of the assembly personnel is normally assessed, for
example, by a verbal or written examination, by a practical test and preferably by performing on the
assembled accessories an electrical partial discharge test and voltage withstand test.

The jointer has to demonstrate his skill-set under the supervision of a jointing supervisor, experienced
in the type of accessory being assembled and in the skill-set required. Quality assurance checklists
provide a useful tool for on the job training verification. It is recommended that each jointer keeps a
logbook of all the accessories assembled (type and voltage class).

Proficiency is recognised at the completion of training by the issue of a certificate, which should be
checked by the user as part of the quality plan for a specific project.

### 2.2.9.4 Assembly instructions

The accessory manufacturer is required to supply a complete set of assembly instructions together with
drawings of the particular accessory.

The instructions should also include lists of the specified assembly tools, the specified consumable
materials and the health and safety precautions. Recommendations for the preparation of the assembly
environment should also be given.

It is important that the user studies the instructions before work begins to ensure that the workplace is
correctly prepared and that all the tools and consumable materials are available and that specific
instructions are supplied to match the cable construction.

### 2.2.9.5 Special assembly tools

Most designs of accessories, particularly those operating at higher system voltages, require special
tools which are purchased or hired from the accessory manufacturer. The user should ensure that full
instructions are provided and that the personnel are trained in their use. These tools may take the form,
for example, of:

- Hydraulic compression presses or welding equipment for connecting the conductors;
- Cutting equipment to remove the insulation screen and to shape the cable insulation;
- Assembly machines which stretch and position pre-moulded elastomeric components;
- Taping machines that apply tape;
- Heated mould tools and mobile extruders for field moulded joints.

### 2.2.9.6 Preparation of the assembly environment

It is strongly recommended that the assembly area for both joints and termination to be enclosed within
a tent or temporary building, with the objective of providing a clean and dry environment. The enclosure


should be well lit to facilitate accurate preparation of the cable insulation, provided with a sound floor
and lined with sealed materials to facilitate cleanliness.

In extremes of climate it is good practice to provide control of temperature and humidity to ensure a
consistent performance of the personnel and consistent properties of the polymeric materials.

For joint assembly, an appropriately sized joint bay or chamber shall be considered, as well as the
provision of a temporary and/or permanent support for the completed joint.

For termination assembly, a permanent support structure and a temporary weatherproof structure during
assembly shall be installed. A suitable means of lifting the cable and insulator into position shall also be
provided.

### 2.2.10 Compatibility of the accessory with specified after laying tests

When the installation of the cable and accessories has been completed it is standard practice to perform
electrical tests to demonstrate that the assembly of the accessories is of satisfactory quality and that
mechanical damage to the cable and accessories has not occurred during installation.

The following tests can be performed:

### 2.2.10.1 Voltage test on main insulation

The commissioning voltage test source may differ from HVDC to HVAC cable systems and insulation
type.

Here below, a brief summary is reported. Please refer to TB 841 “After laying tests on AC and DC cable
systems with new technologies” for recommendations.

2.2.10.1.1 HVDC systems

Both for extruded and lapped insulations, a DC test is recommended.

2.2.10.1.2 HVAC systems

While for lapped insulations the use of a DC source is recommended, on cable with extruded insulation
this is not recommended. Experience has shown that the DC voltage test is not always sufficiently
sensitive to detect damaged cable insulation or incorrectly assembled accessories and hence prevent
them from entering service. Experience has shown that DC test could cause damage to HVAC extruded
insulations and may lead to life reduction. In particular, the electrical stress distribution under DC voltage
in an accessory is usually significantly different from that under AC voltage in normal service.

The application of an AC voltage is therefore nowadays recommended for extruded insulations. This
can be applied either as voltage from the transmission system or by the application of test voltage from
mobile test equipments.

2.2.10.1.3 Transition joints

Tests on newly installed transition joints are carried out when the installation of the cable and its
accessories has been completed.

For HVAC systems an AC voltage source, directly from the grid or through mobile test equipment, is
applied.

For HVDC systems a DC voltage source is applied.

If required the new cable section with extruded insulation may be tested separately according to its
relevant standard before the transition joint is installed.

Please refer to TB 415 “Test Procedures for HV Transition Joints for Rated Voltages 30kV up to 500kV”
and TB 622 “Recommendations for testing DC transition joints for power transmission at a rated voltage
up to 500 kV” for recommendations.

### 2.2.10.2 Partial discharge detection

Partial discharge detection techniques are available to check for the absence of damage to the cable
during installation and incorrect assembly of the accessories. Methods for detecting PD may be of the
following types:

- Embeddedd sensors, installed inside the accessories


- HFCT (high frequency current transformers) and coils for signal synchronism, installed in close
    proximity of the accessories
- Sensors in direct contact with the cable, installed in close proximity of the accessories

PD measurements are carried out during commissioning either applying the voltage from the grid or
through mobile test equipment.

### 2.2.10.3 Voltage withstand test on the cable over sheath and joint protection

It is usual for specially bonded cable systems, including their accessories, to be subjected to an after
laying test comprised of the application of a DC withstand voltage applied to the metallic sheath or
screen wires to prove the external sheath electrical integrity.

Test voltage value depends on circuit age and it is normally defined for new systems, while for systems
already in service it could be stated from some national standards or agreed between owner and
supplier.

### 2.2.10.4 Current balance test on the cable sheath and screening wires

This test is performed on cross bonded cable systems at or adjacent to accessory positions to confirm
that the bonding connections of the accessory are correct and the cable lengths and spacing are
symmetrical, such that the magnitude of residual circulating current is of an acceptably low magnitude.

### 2.2.11 Maintenance requirements of the accessory

The user should ensure that adequate maintenance tests and checks have been recommended by the
cable and accessory suppliers, for example:

### 2.2.11.1 Monitoring of fluid insulation

Liquid and gas levels: some types of termination, straight joints and transition joints are filled with
insulating liquid or gas and may require to be regularly inspected or monitored in service to ensure that
neither the liquid or gas have escaped.

### 2.2.11.2 Voltage withstand tests on the over sheath and joint protection

These tests are similar to the after laying tests, but are usually performed at reduced voltage levels.

### 2.2.11.3 Shelf life of accessories for emergency spares

The user should ensure that information is provided on the shelf life of the components in an accessory
for long term storage as these may vary according to the type of material, the way they are packed and
the appropriate temperature and humidity conditions of storage.

### 2.2.11.4 Availability of accessory kits for emergency spares

The user is recommended to obtain either a sufficient stock of spare accessories or to have an
agreement with the manufacturer to supply accessories at short notice. The design of an accessory for
emergency use may be different from that installed.

### 2.2.12 Economics of accessory selection

A comparison of the relative costs of different designs of accessory kits should not be undertaken without
giving due consideration to the total costs of installation and assembly. The following are the main items
of cost:

### 2.2.12.1 Cost of the accessory complete with all components

The accessory design should be checked to ensure that it is a complete kit and will be supplied with all
the components and assembly instructions for the particular application. Some components that may
not necessarily be supplied by all accessory manufacturers are for example the conductor connections
and anti-corrosion protection for joints and bus bar take-off connectors and support metalwork for
termination.

### 2.2.12.2 Cost of guarantee and insurance

At the higher system voltages, it is more usual for the cable and accessories to be supplied, installed
and guaranteed as a “ turn-key ” project. Under such circumstances the guarantee will usually extend
to a specified number of years in service. If the user decides to divide the supply and installation of


accessories between companies, it is recommended that the cost of financial self-insurance be
considered, because the responsibility for an accessory failure in service can be difficult to apportion
between the accessory manufacturer, the cable manufacturer and the installer.

### 2.2.12.3 Cost of assembly time

The jointing time required to assemble accessories can differ dependent on their design. Similarly the
time required to assemble the anti-corrosion protection and the final mechanical support to the
accessory can be the over-riding factors in determining the jointing time.

### 2.2.12.4 Cost of preparing the installation environment for the accessory

Accessories require the provision of a weatherproof enclosure together with the environmental
conditions necessary for jointing (e.g. good lighting, cleanliness and, when necessary, air conditioning.
The supply of electricity and gas may be required).

### 2.2.12.5 Cost of safe working conditions

In addition to the cost of constructing the installation environment to comply with the regulations for safe
working practices , the provision may be required for temporary and permanent protection to the
installer's personnel from electric shock during assembly and the user's personnel when the accessory
is in service.

### 2.2.12.6 Cost of special jointing tools

There may be significant differences in purchase cost and hiring charges of the tools required for
different accessories.

### 2.2.12.7 Cost of training

Qualified jointers who are trained to assemble the particular accessory should always be employed.

The user should decide whether it will be more cost effective to employ qualified and experienced
personnel to assemble the accessories or to employ qualified and experienced personnel to install the
cable and assemble the accessories as part of a turn-key contract or incur the on-going costs of training
and regular repeat training for his own personnel.

### 2.2.12.8 Comparative cost of cable and accessories

The design of the cable can influence the cost of the accessory design. Thus a reduction in the cost of
the cable construction may result in an increase in the cost of the accessories. Similarly an increase in
the cost of installation by laying longer lengths of cable may achieve a reduction in overall costs by
requiring fewer joints.

### 2.2.12.9 Cost of verification of accessory performance

If a type test report is not available for the particular cable and accessory in combination, then the user
is advised to allow for the cost of performing a type approval test. This cost may be born by the supplier,
in the case of a turn-key project, but this is less usually so in the case of separately supplied cable and
accessories.

### 2.2.13 Accessories for cables integrating fibre optic units
