Particular accessories have been developed to accommodate cables with integrated fibre optic units.

Due to the particular care needed for splicing and terminating of cables with integrated F.O. units, special
attention must be paid to the personnel employed in such activities, which must be trained and qualified
to manage these specific operations.

At joints, the optical fibres are accumulated into loops, spliced and protected by the same joint structure.

At terminations, optical fibres are separated and taken out of the termination through insulating elements
and spliced to the land optical cable inside a splice box or joint placed on or very close to the termination
structure.


## 2.3 Retrofitting

The need for replacing old three-core pipe-type cables, realized as high-pressure oil-filled cables
(HPOF), internal gas-pressure cables (gas-filled cables) and external gas-pressure cables (gas-
compression cables), installed into steel pipes and approaching the end of their life, has brought the
opportunity to use the existing steel pipes as infrastructure for a possible retrofitting with extruded
cables.

Nevertheless, due to different design criteria, paper insulated cables result in a smaller overall size with
respect to extruded cables, thus reflecting in the need for suitably design cables with reduced sizes to
be pulled into the existing pipes.

Extruded cable designs required for complying with this requirement need to have reduced sizes
associated with sufficient mechanical robustness to be pulled into pipes.

Pipes for retrofitting are generally made of ferromagnetic steel. As a consequence, all the three phases
need to be pulled in the same pipe to dramatically reduce the hysteresis losses and the associated
increase in the temperature of the system.

All the above leads to a cable design in which the insulation thickness is reduced and consequently the
voltage stress increased.

The accessories for this kind of cable also experience a working stress higher than that normally
considered. Therefore, special care shall be considered in their selection.

A more detailed discussion about this topic is included into TB 606 "Uprating and upgrading of cable
systems".


## 3. Civil construction and cable installation techniques

The design phase of the cable route starts with the preliminary study of one or more possible paths and
the choice of the optimal path defines the maximum design. Estimating costs and time of supply and
realization allow the insertion of the work in the financial programs and in the specific intervention
programs (budget) that will make the implementation program for macro activities operative by defining
the times and phases of the execution of the work. The preliminary study follows the construction design
phase in which all the technical parameters are defined, also in reference to the surveys carried out
along the laying path, and all the documentation for the various phases of the authorization process is
prepared. In urban itineraries, the accurate survey along the entire layout of the HV cable laying, carried
out with the Georadar technique, allows to highlight with appreciable precision the presence of
underground services and structures not shown on the maps of the service providers (gas,
telecommunications, water, electricity, collectors and sewer drains etc.) as well as any presences of
archaeological sites not previously listed. The measurements of the thermal resistivity of the ground will
allow to establish some laying characteristics to guarantee the power that the power links will be able to
transport in the various operating conditions. The main phases for carrying out site work, related to the
type of system and the voltage of the connection to be implemented, can be summarized as follows:

- Site opening
- Execution of civil works
- Cable laying
- Accessories assembly
- Tests after laying
- Backfilling, warning tape placement
- Construction site closure

After laying and before starting the line, the insulation tests shall be carried out in accordance with
international standards.

It must be underlined that failures of underground cables and accessories can result in the release of
toxic gases and large amounts of energy. These failures include smoking manholes, manhole fires, and
manhole explosions. These failure events can damage underground infrastructure and pose a risk to
the maintenance crews and the public.
For these reasons, sometimes supported by local requirements, preventive methods to mitigate these
phenomena need to be considered.

IEEE P2417/D7.7 “Draft Guide for Early Detection, Mitigation, Preventative Measures, and Response
to Smoke, Fire, and Explosions in Underground Electrical Structures” offers some methods that can
help preventing or mitigating such events.

## 3.1 Cable reel handling..................................................................................................................................

At the end of the production, the ends of the shipping length are sealed by means of heat shrinkable
caps. For the transport of HV cables, steel reels with external protection consisting of wooden battens
treated against rotting or with metal tape barriers or with plastic coils are generally used. When required,
the pull head can be installed directly at the factory. The reels are stored in such a way that the axis is
in a horizontal position, avoiding overlapping and respecting the direction of rotation indicated on the
flanges. the maximum shipping length and weight are identified during the design phase of the project
according to the maximum loads and dimensions the roads, bridges,etc. When large reels are used for
shipping, it is necessary to consider the impacts on transport, especially when the dimensions are such
as to require transport on special vehicles, if not as exceptional transport, thus also influencing transport
logistics.

For the loading and unloading of the coils it is necessary to use suitable equipment such as:


- forklift trucks
- tower crane

The correct positioning of the coils is illustrated below.

```

```
By applying these measures, possible damage to the cable or coils is avoided, which instead inevitably
leads to the methods illustrated below.

## Figure 2 – Wrong lifting position

Finally, with reference to the operations of winding and unwinding the cables from and on a reel, figures
below show the correct operating modes and the methods to be avoided.

Cable drum unwinding can also takes place from the installation trailer if local conditions allow (e.g.,
position of the trailer from the joint bay).


## 3.2 Direct burial

### 3.2.1 Description of the technique

This technique is the oldest and easiest one to implement. It’s been used for centuries to install utility
services such as water, sewer or gas pipes. This method consists of digging and leveling a trench in the
ground such that cables can be laid on the bottom of the trench prior to backfilling. The use of this
technique is declining and our survey indicates that only 17 % of all underground transmission circuits
have been directly buried as it needs to have the trench between two joints open during all the
sequences described below.

The cables are normally pulled into position within the trench using rollers and pulleys located at regular
intervals along the bottom of the trench. Once the cables are in their final position in the trench,
backfilling is then carefully performed using either original or new materials. The figures below show
typical sections of directly buried cables and the construction sequences.

## Figure 4 - Direct burial with shoring arrangement and trefoil cable formtion


## Figure 5 - Direct burial with sloping arrangement and flat cable formation

Installation Sequence

- Surface cutting (if necessary)
- Trench excavation and securing
- Bedding material placement and compaction
- HV cables placement by individual pulling onto rollers
- Fiber optic cable placement
- Low thermal resistivity backfilling and compacting
- Placement of protective slab (or tiles)
- Controlled backfill and warning tape placement and compaction
- Top layer reinstatement (top soil, road pavement, etc...)

Application

This technique is a “main” installation technique as opposed to a special crossing technique which is
designed to avoid a special obstacle.

This technique is best suited for the following areas:

- Agricultural areas
- Rural areas
- Sparsely urbanized areas
- Highway or Railroad right of ways (if owner approves)


Table of Pros and Cons

```
PROS CONS
```
```

The trench must remain open until all cables are pulled
and is a longer nuisance compared to other installation
modes
```

```
ready (i.e. construction schedule coordination)
```
```

locate with fault locating apparatus
```
```

be carried out with extreme caution
```
```

to air filled duct banks for the same cable
design and axial distance between cables
```
```

on the protective slab design and materials used
```
```

```
```
It is difficult to remove the cables from the ground for
replacement or after the cables useful life
```
```

cross sections due possible limitations on the
temperature at the contact cable-soil to avoid any soil
drying out
```
### 3.2.1.1 Civil work description

The depth of the trench will typically vary from a little less than a meter up to several meters depending
on local requirements. The cables can be installed in trefoil or in a flat formation as shown in the previous
figures and pictures.

Direct buried sections require that cable trench remains open for the complete section length until the
cables are pulled into place. On completion of excavation works the trench should be appropriately
shored, dewatered, and cleaned prior to start of the cable installation works. The base of the trench
needs to be inspected to insure it is firm, relatively smooth and free of rocks, rubble, or sharp objects
which could damage the cables during pulling operations.

Bedding materials with low thermal resistivity are usually placed at the bottom of the trench and
compacted. On completion of cable laying, backfill materials with low and controlled thermal resistivity
are carefully placed around the cables and compacted. Care must be taken not to use compacting tools
too close to the cables. A protective slab is then placed above the cables and the rest of the trench is
backfilled and compacted with suitable material. The warning elements are normally incorporated in the
final backfill layer, above the protective slab. The cable jackets should be tested shortly after backfilling
to ensure that the cables were not damaged either during the laying or during backfilling.

The adequate compaction of all backfill materials is vital in order to remove as much air as possible as
air is a poor thermal medium. The protective slab is either a concrete slab (precast or poured in place)
or a series of concrete or polymeric tiles.

Cables in flat or trefoil formation

Cables can be laid in flat or trefoil formation. In flat formation, spacers are generally used whereas in
trefoil, non-metallic straps or ropes are wrapped around the cables to keep the trefoil formation during
the back filling. The flat formation is typically used for bigger cables or when larger current carrying
capacities are required.

Use of fiber optic cables


The use of fiber optic cables for telecommunication and protection services is now common. Fiber cables
are also used for thermal, acoustic and other types of monitoring is and the use for these purposes is
increasing. These cables are usually installed within small ducts and be placed within the low resistivity
material zone around the cables. Alternatively, they could be included in the metallic screen of the
cables.

### 3.2.1.2 Duration of civil works

The duration of the civil works will depend on the depth of the trench, soil conditions and the number of
obstacles that may be encountered. Progress could be as high as several hundred meters per day.

With this technique, the civil works cannot be carried out separately (weeks apart) from the cable laying
works due to the fact that the trench cannot be backfilled before the cable pulling is completed. If the
trench were to remain open for an extended period, it would be a public nuisance, present as a safety
risk, and the trench would be subject to collapse during adverse weather conditions or land subsidence.

Furthermore, in urban situations, management of traffic issues becomes a major factor. The opening
of a trench several hundreds of meters long may give rise to numerous problems. Accordingly, local
authorities may restrict the maximum length of open trenches as well as the daily working hours
according to job locations, number of hours per day and days of the week, etc...

The figures below show the impact of a trench excavation in a city. In the top left figure, the parking lane
is temporarily unavailable for the residents.

## Figure 6 - Excavation impact

When the route of the cable trench intersects a crossroad, it must be temporarily covered with steel
plates in order to reduce the impact on traffic. Typically this requires milling of the road to recess the
plates or the use of pins to minimise the problem of traffic dislodging the plates.


### 3.2.1.3 Cable Route selection

The selection of a suitable cable route should be based on a number of factors including, but not limited
to the following:

- Acceptability by local governments/municipalities
- Space for cables with sufficient side clearance with other services or buildings
- Availability of permanent space for joint bays and temporary space for the cable laying equipment
    and cranes
- Making the route as short as possible to reduce costs
- Reduce the impact to the community by selecting lesser trafficked streets
- Possibility of repairing the cables and/or installing a repair joint/bay along the route
- Reliable conditions (stable soil, low risk of third party intervention, etc...)
- Flat elevation, as high slopes may increase complexities in cable laying, splicing pit and back filling
- Environmental sensitive areas from wildlife and naturalistic viewpoints

It is highly recommended that all potential obstacles along the cable route are identified and documented
at the design stage. With this information in hand, the cable route designer will try to avoid, as far as
possible, these obstacles. If avoiding obstacles is not possible, the cable route designer will adjust the
depth of the trench, the trench profile or change the cable installation technique accordingly. Exploratory
pits may also be required to check for existing services when reliable information is not available from
municipalities or other asset owners.

With the help of government or other agencies, studies should be carried out in order to determine soil
characteristics along the selected route, the potential for soil contamination, the possibility of the
presence of archaeological artifacts, presence of shells, and other significant elements.

Actual geotechnical and environmental surveys can and should be carried out to confirm the data
highlighted by the above studies. Such surveys are also helpful to measure the water table depth and
define the possibility of acid sulfate soils. Soil thermal resistivity along the route should also be
measured.

Thermal resistivity and temperature measurements can also be made in conjunction with the above
investigations, taking advantage of the excavation activity already planned.

At special crossings, other installation techniques such as Ducts or Duct banks, trenchless construction
methods, dedicated supporting structures, or even tunnels may be used to overcome obstacles.
However, any change in the construction technique will require adjustments in the cable laying
technique and grounding arrangement and will create an interface with new challenges. It is also
important to perform ampacity studies for all unique cable route installation cross-sections in order to
confirm that the systems power transfer requirements are met.

Existing services

It is likely that HV cables will be installed near other services such as water pipes, gas pipes, other
electrical cables, steam pipes, telecommunication cables, etc.

A minimum clearance, specified by each third party asset owner, will be required in order allow them to
properly maintain and repair their infrastructure without interference. In some cases, electrical
interference studies may need to be performed to confirm no adverse effects to neighbouring utilities.

It is important that dialogue with all other third part asset owners affected by the installation, be started
as early as possible during the project life cycle.

Steam pipes

Steam pipes are among the services that could have the greatest impact on the performance of HV
cables because of the amount of heat they can induce in the ground. The older steam pipes are of even
greater concern as they were not as well insulated.

The thermal impact of steam pipes on the current carrying capacity of the HV cables must be evaluated.
Solutions to overcome this problem could be to insulate the steam pipe from the cables by means of


thermal insulating sheaths running across the two services up to the surface. This insulation must not
however prevent the heat from the HV cables to flow upwards.

Electrical cables

When the route of the HV cables comes close to other electrical services, it is also necessary to evaluate
their impact on the current capacity of the HV cables and also the impact of the HV system on the
performance of existing electrical cable systems as they also could be impaired.

Gas

In the case of a gas leakage, it is important that the leak is repaired quickly and efficiently as it could be
a risk of explosion. This is why, gas owners specify safe minimum distances. Should gas services be in
close proximity to joint bays, there is also the possibility of explosion should "hot works" be done within
the joint bay.

Telecommunication cables

The performance of telecommunication cables (made of copper) running closely and parallel to HV
cables could potentially be disturbed by induced voltages. This is why telecommunication will specify
minimum clearances with electrical cables.

Nowadays telecommunications cables are made of fiber optic cables which are not affected by induced
voltages.

Water and sewer pipes

Water pipes may run beside banks of HV cables. Water pipes are typically shallower and smaller
compared to sewer pipes.

When a pressurized water pipe breaks, it often causes localized potholes which could have an adverse
effect on the cables or joint bay stability.

Since sewer pipes are not as pressurized and are located deeper in the ground, they do not constitute
a similar risk. However, sewers are often very old structures and can be easily damaged by the trench
work.

Trees

Most cities want to increase their plant cover as an important measure against global warming. As a
result, new lines of trees may be installed around existing HV cables.

Lines of trees should be installed outside the tree route protection zone from directly buried cables as
the roots could possibly become entwined with the cables especially when cables are shallow. Large
trees can also have an impact on the thermal resistivity of the native soil by removing moisture from the
soil.

Railways

Railways (or tramways) could be an important source of stray currents (DC) into the ground. This can
lead to the corrosion of metallic pipes carrying either water or gas. The installation of cathodic protection
systems with sacrificial anodes will prevent degradation of metallic pipes.

HV cables are less susceptible to this phenomenon as they are insulated from ground by a continuous
polymeric jacket. However, stray currents could still potentially find a way into cables through nearby
grounding points.

### 3.2.1.4 Equipment and spaces

In the case of rural areas, an area from 10 to 20 meters in width could be required to dig a trench. This
includes space for the excavator on one side while the other side is used to store the excavated
materials and/or new backfilling materials. If the excavated materials are not reused, they are normally
dumped into trucks and taken away from the site. The top soils (organic soils) are also normally
separated from the main excavated soil.

In urban areas, the excavated materials are normally taken away from the site due to severe space
limitations and the working zones are therefore reduced in width.


## Figure 7 - Excavation phases of direct burial with sloping arrangement

## Figure 8 - Excavation phases of direct burial in agricultural environment

Once the trench is ready for cable pulling, the cable reel machinery and the winch will be located at
opposite ends of the trench to carry out the pull.

### 3.2.2 Limits of the technique

### 3.2.2.1 Drying of the soil

Direct burial is certainly the most sensitive installation mode with regards to soil drying because the
cables are in direct contact with the ground.

Soil drying can be attributed to natural processes as well as by induced changes. The rate of drying
depends on the location, precipitation, soil type, natural water table, air temperature, surface
temperature, wind speed, type of cover (grass, asphalt, concrete), vegetation (moisture depletion by
trees), etc. For underground power cable environment, the heat generated by the energized cable
(W/m) drives the moisture away at the cable/soil interface, and it has a significant impact on the thermal
resistivity and thus the cable rating.


In certain regions, soils may be naturally dry. Various solutions can be considered:

- Use of controlled backfilling material
- Increase the installation depth in order to reach more thermally stable soils
- Use of a flat formation instead of trefoil formation to distribute the heat over a larger area and thus
    improve the rate of heat dissipation of each cable
- Install fiber optic cables either attached to the power cable jacket or embedded inside the power
    cables and use them as Distributed Temperature Sensor (DTS) in order to detect hot spots before
    any irreversible damage to the cable insulation occurs

A famous case of soil drying happened in Auckland, New-Zealand in February 1998 when the last circuit
(out of 4) finally failed from thermal runaway, leaving the Central Business District of the city without
electricity for at least 5 weeks.

It is worth noting that air and soil conditions were at records high levels during that summer and that the
peak load had also switched from winter to summer since a few years.
The lessons that were learned from this incident is the importance of validating that design and “in-situ”
parameters such as soil temperatures and thermal resistivities are consistent. The maximum measured
soil temperatures were at least 7°C higher than assumed and the measured soil thermal resistivities
reached values as high as 6°K.m/W as opposed to a 1.2°K.m/W assumed value.

Even though the first 2 circuits did not fail for thermal reasons, it was demonstrated that the additional
load placed on the remaining circuits led to thermomechanical-failure of one circuit and ultimately,
thermal runaway of the last circuit.

Retrospectively, it was stated that all 4 circuits had not been installed in a way they could really achieve
their specified ratings in the summer.

### For more information on dry out, see the Cigré TB714 “Long term performance of soil and backfill

### systems“ (WG B1.41, published in 2017).More specific details on moisture migration are in chapter 5

### 3.2.2.2 Water drainage

Conversely to the soil-drying phenomenon, wet soil may seem more favourable to laying of HV cables
because it constitutes a natural cooling system. This is partly true. However, in those cases, the trench
cannot be backfilled as effectively with thermal backfill.

This is of concern if the soil or the backfill material is loose (granular with high voids content, poor
compaction). It should be addressed during the construction phase if it affects the stability of the soil or
the backfill around cables. The execution of trenches in wet soil often requires means of drainage
(pumps, etc.) in order to avoid erosion of the trench walls and to enable proper cable laying and
controlled backfilling.

Washout of fines in the backfill material may adversely affect the stability and thermal resistivity of the
backfill and such cases have been documented.

Geotextile of various types are commonly used in civil engineering applications when working on very
soft and unstable ground conditions, especially in flooded areas. When a static or dynamic load is
exerted on the Geotextile mat, it allows water to filter through thus, consolidating the sub-soil and
preventing settlement (sinking).

If cables are directly buried in areas where the natural water table fluctuates (tidal changes) or if the
area is prone to flooding, the backfill around the cables (especially granular type) may lose (washout)
fines such as fine sand and silt. This will impact not only the mechanical but also the thermal
characteristics of the backfill. If the cable trench is provided with appropriate Geotextile, it will allow the
water to permeate through but will prevent wash out of the fines.

Please refer to item 8.2 of TB 714.

### 3.2.2.3 Temperature of the soil/environment

At a given location the temperature of the sub-surface soil is a function of the latitude, depth (cables or
duct-bank), time of the year and surface cover (grass, asphalt, concrete). Natural changes in
temperature follows a sinusoidal curve (Hillel, 1982) and is fairly constant at a depth of about 10m. Local
environmental and metrological agencies as well as airport authorities are good sources of temperature


records; especially for depths up to 2m. Agronomy records in general provide temperature and moisture
data for shallow depth of less than 1m. For cable rating calculations, the ground temperature at the
burial depth must be taken into consideration.

Summer conditions are naturally the hottest conditions of the year.

A tree of thermocouples can be installed at various locations and depths so that ‘hot-spots’ are
monitored over several cycles to obtain the true temperature values and their changes as a function of
depth and time of year. Alternatively, as stated previously, fiber optic cables and Distributed
Temperature Sensing (DTS) systems can be used to monitor temperatures and better understand the
effect of ground temperatures. Fiber optic cables will probably provide more accurate results over the
years as they are passive sensors and will not suffer from drifting or corrosion problems over the life of
the cable system.

### 3.2.2.4 Hardness of the soil

Hard soil, hardpan and rock can pose difficulty, delays and extra costs for excavation and construction.

It is therefore advisable to conduct a geotechnical survey along the cable route. The borehole logs or
the test-pit logs can give a good indication of the soil stiffness/hardness and depth of rock (if
encountered). Geotechnical tests are conducted in-situ and/or in the laboratory on samples collected
during investigation.

If excavation into rocky soils cannot be avoided, project schedule and costs must be adjusted
accordingly.

### 3.2.2.5 Stability of the soil

Soil types that are non-cohesive, such as sandy and gravelly and soil conditions, are generally very soft
and sensitive and have poor mechanical stability, posing problems during excavation; especially at
locations with a high water table.

Stability must then be addressed during the construction phase by de-watering, trench shoring,
mechanically or dynamically compacting the area or by the use of grout injection techniques.

Stability must be achieved for the successful operation of the work as well as for the safety of the
workers. The figure below shows a shoring technique to secure trench walls that is used in Spain.
Metallic sheets and adjustable telescopic poles are being used. Alternatively, sheet plywood, timber
struts and whalers can also be used.


## Figure 9 - Example of securing a trench with shoring technique

### 3.2.2.6 Thermal resistivity of the soil

For cable rating (ampacity) calculations, the three most important parameters are:

- The ambient temperature at the cable burial depth;
- The earth surface temperature;
- The thermal resistivity of the soil/backfill around the cables up to the ground surface.

Soil gives the largest single thermal resistance and thermal capacitance of an underground power cable
system. It has the greatest variability with distance along the cable route and with time. The thermal
resistivity can easily vary threefold or more on a specific circuit, and the cable must be rated for the
worst-case condition. Better understanding of the physical and thermal properties of soils and backfills,
and the use of controlled backfills can result in significant increase in ampacity.

Thermal resistivity depends on the soil (or backfill) type, water content, density and various other factors
to a lesser degree.

Typical thermal resistivity values are suggested in IEC 60287- 3 - 1 specification for various countries,
however, these remain very approximate. Variations in these informative numbers are widespread,
particularly in large countries with significant variations in soil and weather conditions. This approach
could advantageously be replaced by a cable route thermal survey where in-situ and laboratory thermal
tests are carried out at various times of year to understand the thermal behavior of a particular soil and
design the backfill material accordingly.

### 3.2.2.7 Agricultural environments

Installing directly buried cables into agricultural environments can lead to legitimate public concerns
about impacts on productivity from the land owners associated with civil works as well as increased heat
in the ground. Various actions can be taken by the utilities to address these concerns.

It is important to discuss with land owners how they plan on using the land after cable system installation.
The post installation land use is used to determine the required cable system protection methods.

For construction through agricultural environments, consideration should be given to preventing undue
compaction of soil from the construction equipment. As an example, the use of wooden beams and
planks can be used to spread the weight of construction vehicles out over a larger area. Consideration
should also be given to ensuring that different soil layers are stockpiled separately and that trench
backfilling ensures that soil layers remain the same as they were prior to construction activities.

Deep rooted vegetation should not be permitted overtop of the cable system and should only be
permitted a certain horizontal distance away from the directly buried cables as the deep roots can
potentially cause damage to the cable system. It is common for the cable system owner to implement a
vegetation monitoring and clearing program.

It is to be noted that it is in the best interest of both parties that the presence of cables does not lead to
soil disturbances or dry out:

- The utility does not want current carrying capacity of the cables to drop and
- The land owner does not want the production of his land (located near the cables) to decrease

The measures taken by utilities could be the following:

- Minimally, install the cables at an adequate depth in agreement with the owner depending on the
    type of culture and the working depth of the agricultural machinery
- As far as possible, maintain as far as possible the original “nature” and “degree of compaction” of
    the soils above the cables
- As far as possible, minimize damage induced by cable installation machinery on surrounding areas
- The cables could be a barrier to the water flow within the ground. The designer should be sure that
    the water table or the water flow won’t be disturbed by the cable link


These measures are deemed to avoid disturbing land drainage and enabling the crops to grow normally
as fast as possible.

In recent European projects, such an approach proved to be successful as the impacts of the civil works
went unnoticed and the crops grew normally after only a few months delay.

### 3.2.2.8 Seismicity

Direct burial is certainly the most vulnerable installation mode against earthquakes as the cables are
not free to move (as in a duct for instance) and they are not protected by any other structure.

As a result, cables directly laid in seismic areas could potentially be subjected to high stresses generated
by soil movements. A number of cables were severely bent and damaged following an earthquake and
subsequent soil shearing events in Christchurch, New Zealand in 2011.

### 3.2.2.9 Frost (temperature limits, provisions for installation in cold environments)

Carrying out civil works in frosty environments (winter conditions) remains possible, with certain
precautions. The work will be more demanding on the workers due to the lower temperatures as well as
on the machinery due to the increased hardness of the ground. The increased hardness of the ground
is still significantly less than that which may be encountered when excavating in rock.

As far as cable laying is concerned, cable manufacturers do not normally recommend laying of HV
cables below 5°C temperatures without cable pre-warming. This is because the cables get stiffer and
loose flexibility and this could lead to higher pulling tensions and stability problems with the cable head.

The pre-warming will consist in either storing the cable reel indoor a heated room near the pulling site
or installing a heating shelter around the cable reel at the site and maintain the air temperature within
the shelter at 10°C or more for at least 24 hours prior to pulling.

The freezing of the soil itself does not constitute a risk for the cables providing the following criteria are
met:

- The cable trench must be adequately drained in order to avoid water freezing and differential uplift
    around the cables;
- If possible, the trench bottom must not be too shallow (below freezing line) in order to avoid
    differential ground movements during thaw period especially near fixed structures such as joint bays
    and terminations foundations.

### 3.2.2.10 Archaeology

If the cable route crosses an area of potential archaeological interest, it must be borne in mind that, that
due to local and environmental authorities requirements, any finds made during excavation will make it
mandatory to stop the work, at least in the portion where the find has been made.

Work at the site is then delayed until the archaeologists have investigated the find. The length of the
portion affected and the duration of the stoppage will depend on what further discoveries are made.

Another way of coping with this issue is to investigate the archaeological potential along the cable route
at the design stage. If the potential is sufficiently high, an alternate route can be found. If not, a daily
surveillance by an archaeological expert firm could be carried out during the civil work, and any finding
can be examined and extracted as they are met, in collaboration with the civil contractor. The most
extensive archaeological work can even be planned (ahead of time) to take place after the working
hours of the civil contractors.

Another solution could be to execute an HDD below the archaeological area. However, this solution
may not always be technically feasible and would have a strong impact on the cost and schedule of the
project.


## Figure 10 - Archaeologial findings during excavation

### 3.2.2.11 Presence of rodents and termites

In the presence of rodents or termites, various solutions can be used such as:

- Placing anti-termite chemical in or around the outer covering of the cables
- Using nylon 12 or double brass tapes in the make up of the cable sheath/serving
- Pouring a sufficiently strong backfill (low strength concrete or FTB) around the cables
- Increase the cable laying depths where the pests will not be active

### 3.2.2.12 Laying in national park

Open trench work may not be permitted in some national parks due to the potential impact on wildlife.
When the cable route cannot avoid such areas, other technique such as horizontal directional drilling
(HDD) may be used. However, as explained earlier, such a technique could have a strong impact on
the cost and schedule of the project.

If open trench work is allowed, it is likely that during the backfilling, the restoration of the original soil
conditions will be required possibly including the degree of compaction.

Laying the cables into already disturbed areas such as road or road shoulders could be the best
approach for such locations.

### 3.2.2.13 Land registration

The rights of way are usually settled by joint agreement between the utility and public authorities
(municipalities, highways, railways, bridges, etc.) or between the utility and a private owner.

When using public land, the construction techniques are normally agreed with between the various
parties. The responsibility for the construction and its impact (whether it is the utility or the public
authority) must also be identified.


The work carried out in public land does not generally lead to compensation being paid. However, in the
case of a private owner, the compensation could be agreed according to the width of the right of way.
In some countries, the compensation is based on the actual width of the trench multiplied by a factor.

In the left picture below the factor would be 2. In the right picture, the factor would be 1.5. In some
countries, the compensation could be based on a minimal width required for future repair work (typically
3 to 5 m). Compensation may also be paid for the use of temporary additional width for the work.
Permanent construction or stockpiling of any material will normally not be allowed above the cables on
these right of ways.

## Figure 11 - Examples of right of ways

### 3.2.3 Environmental impact

With the direct burial method, natural environments may be substantially altered when compared with
“trenchless” techniques (horizontal drilling, tunnel “boring”, etc).

It is important not to underestimate the environmental impact and local environmental laws during the
construction itself, and therefore it is wise to consider such implications at the preliminary study stage,
before it becomes an issue for the population.

The objective is to mitigate the impacts and restore the area, as much as possible, to the original
condition. The impacts related to the construction and operation of a power link, can be classified as
follows:

Soil

- Direct damage due to the excavation (material displacement)
- Possible contamination of soils due to excavated materials being mixed with surrounding land
    materials

Water

- Possible contamination of water ecosystem due to excavated or backfilled materials being
    displaced/lost in water bodies.
- Disturbances of the ground whether acid sulfate soil affected water is encountered

Air

- Release of pollutants to atmosphere during the construction work


- Noise generated by machinery during the work

Flora

- Direct destruction of the vegetation cover
- Indirect destruction of the nearby plant communities
- Damage to unique or endangered species

Fauna (embedding)

- Direct or indirect disturbance of natural fauna habitat

Socio-economic aspects

- Difficulties caused to parking and access to shops etc. during working hours
- Temporary effects on tourism trade during the construction work
- Rights of way affected during the construction work
- Traffic disruption

Landscape

- Alteration of the landscape during the construction phase.

It clearly appears that most of the disturbances can be prevented or mitigated at the design stage. The
major impact arises most of the time during the construction phase and can be mitigated by the
implementation of construction environmental management plans and consultation with the local
authorities and population, by using less noisy equipment and by working during less sensitive hours.

### 3.2.4 Safety.................................................................................................................................................

### 3.2.4.1 During Construction

During digging, cable laying and jointing operations, it is vital that considerable attention is paid to the
safety and health of the workers and the public. Among the items that should be considered are:

- Risks to other electrical services, both underground and overhead
- Risks to other types of services such as gas, water, sewers, etc...
- Ground subsidence
- Prevention of falls or tripping into the trench
- Prevent workers from induced voltages from other circuits
- Traffic management issues
- Etc...

The jointing work normally take place in joint bays. Such structures are normally open (no cover/roof
during the work) and may also constitute similar risks.

### 3.2.4.2 During Operation

Owners of underground infrastructure normally offer a free consultation and inspection service to all
companies intending to carry excavation work. Such services are aimed at damage prevention to
underground infrastructure or other structures.

Visible markers can be used to help reduce the risks. These could be located in the pavement, on kerb
and gutters or beside the road with an indicated offset.

Buried devices such as warning tapes or nets could be placed above the cables to help reducing the
risk of damage.

Sidewall protection is not compulsory because of the cables depth. For Joint bays, a brick or concrete
enclosure will normally protect the joints all around.


### 3.2.5 Legal requirements

When cable routes are located in public lands, there is normally no acquisition cost for the easement. It
also eases the approval process because projects are submitted only to the public authorities who are
normally accepting of these kinds of projects as it is seen to be of public value.

Open trench works for direct burial installations will cause some disturbances to the population because
the entire length of the trench is open until the cables are installed and the trench backfilled. The impacts
will be more or less severe depending on the location of the project and the urbanisation level.

When the cable route runs on private properties, utilities must submit their project to every owner along
the route and take all necessary mitigation measures. Several years can be needed before an
agreement is reached with all. In the absence of an agreement, some utilities may have the right to
expropriate or resume the land with financial compensation.

### 3.2.6 Maintenance and repair processes

Once the cable link is commissioned, it has to be accessible for maintenance along the entire route,
whether at the ends (terminations in substations) or adjacent to the joints through ancillary pits next to
every joint bay.

The maintenance work will consist of performing some or all the tests listed below in a periodic manner
typically ranging from annually to periods of up to several years as recommended by the cable
manufacturer and according to the owner’s experience:

- Jacket tests (integrity tests on the outer layer of the cables)
- On-line or off-line Partial discharge tests (quality tests on cable and accessories insulation)
- High voltage tests (integrity of main insulation)
- Checks on sheath voltage limiters (SVL’s)

Additionally to the electrical checks mentioned above, a visual inspection of the right of way can be
achieved to check for presence of possible vegetation around inspection areas, such as link box pits,
etc. and for ground displacements.

In the case of cable damage, either for intrinsic reasons or due to a third party, excavation work will be
required to expose the damaged cable. If the cable cannot be saved, a bigger trench will have to be
opened and secured to allow for a new piece of cable and 2 repair joints to be installed which will be
buried above or beside the remaining sound cables.

It is worth noting that the backfill around the cables must be designed on the basis of both adequate
thermal and mechanical characteristics. As an example, its compression strength must be sufficiently
low (below 3 MPa) to allow for a safe digging without pneumatic hammers and the associated risks of
damage to the cables.

Several weeks could be required to complete this task once the damaged area is located.

### 3.2.7 Commissioning and de-commissioning..............................................................................................

Before the cable circuit is commissioned and connected to the power transmission system it has to
undergo a series of tests to confirm its suitability for connection to the Network.

The tests vary across the industry but generally include HV tests on the primary insulation and on the
cable outer sheath. Further tests are carried out such as phasing confirmation, insulation resistance (IR
tests), conductor resistance, capacitance, bonding and earthing tests.

The use of AC or DC test sources for testing the primary insulation is subjected to the type of electrical
system (HVAC or HVDC) and the type of cable insulating material.
Please refer to TB 841“After laying tests on AC and DC cable systems with new technologies”.

With the availability of mobile on-site AC test sets, of the resonant frequency type, there is a general
move to testing extruded cable on site by AC voltage only. These systems generally referred to as


Resonant Test Sets (RTS). IEC standard states a minimum range frequency of 20 Hz and it generally
prevails. However though, in specific cases and particularly long cable system testing a frequency down
to 10 Hz could be considered as indicated in CIGRE TB 841.

Very low frequency test systems such as 0.1 Hz have also been developed having advantages such as
reduced equipment complication, less cost and greater portability. However, the voltage levels must be
adjusted (raised) to compensate for the lower frequencies used.

Further developments in partial discharge testing have shown that the combination of a PD test with a
high voltage AC test is the most reliable means of confirming the integrity of extruded cable systems
prior to connection to the power transmission grid. Circuit impedance meassurements, zero sequence,
+ve and -ve sequence measurements and bonding verification can also be carried out.

Cable removal at the end of life is a greater challenge with the direct burial method since the cables are
virtually “stuck” into the ground and could not be removed at the cost of significant excavation work
which would cause major inconvenience for the surrounding population and have major traffic impacts.

In several cases, cables have been left in the ground and, in the case of SCFF cables, attempts are
made to remove the bulk of the insulating fluid. Most of the fluid is eliminated by pumping high pressure
gases into the conductor central channel. This eliminates the risk of a future fluid leak into the soil.

### 3.2.8 Specific monitoring

In the case of Self Contained Fluid filled cables, the pressure of the fluid is constantly monitored from
the ends (terminations) of the circuits and/or at other oil feeding positions along the cable route. An
abnormal loss of pressure will trigger an alarm as it could mean a leak of fluid into the environment. The
operator will react quickly in order to locate and fix the leak. This monitoring has to do with the type of
cable and is not specific to direct buried circuits. In some countries, tracers are used and can make the
location of the leak much easier as they can be detected from the surface with specialized sensors.

With extruded cables, there is no need for such a pressure monitoring as the cables do not contain fluid.

To ensure the expected cable systems life and enable maintenance and prevent operational problems,
there are different monitoring systems that can be useful.

Distributed Temperature Sensing (DTS) is a technique that could be used to make sure the cables
perform as planned and can therefore safely carry their full load. DTS systems use fiber optic cables
installed along the power cables (in the metallic screen or as a separate cable) to directly monitor the
heating of the cable at every meter along the route. This tool, coupled with a Real Time Thermal Rating
(RTTR) servers and software, will inform the operator about the real time heating and also about
possible temporary overload schemes.

This kind of monitoring could prove useful in avoiding cable overheating and failures that could result
when soils, directly in contact with cables, are subject to drying out.

Distributed Acoustic Sensing (DAS) is a technique which also uses fiber optic cables to monitor the
noise and vibration around the cables at every meter along the route. It can prove useful in detecting
and pinpointing situations such as cable failures, unauthorized excavation work, traffic, etc...


## 3. 3 Ducts & Duct Banks

### 3.3.1 Description of the technique

The use of these 2 techniques combined (“Ducts” and “Duct banks”) represents more than 50% of all
reported transmission circuit kilometers according to our survey. This compares to 21% for the 2nd
mostly used technique “tunnels” and 17% for the 3rd most used technique “Direct burial”. These
techniques have been grouped together in this section because they are similar as they both refer to
the installation of non-metallic ducts where cables can be subsequently pulled.

The “Duct banks” technique refers to ducts being “encased in concrete” and accounts for 34.2% of all
reported underground transmission circuit kilometers. Such a figure makes the “Duct banks” technique
the most used technique among all techniques.

The “Ducts” technique however refers to ducts being backfilled typically with “Sand” or “Controlled
thermal backfill” as opposed to “Concrete”. These materials are also the ones used in the case of directly
buried cables and this is why, this technique also refers to ducts being “Directly buried”. This technique
accounts for 16,3% of all reported underground transmission circuit kilometers which is about two times
less important compared to its counterpart “Duct banks”.

In both cases, the cables are pulled into the ducts by means of steel or synthetic wire ropes with the use
of lubricants to help reduce the pulling tensions.

In the next paragraphs both techniques will be simultaneously addressed and distinctions will be made
when required.

## Figure 12 - Duct bank arrangement with flat cable formation


## Figure 13 - Duct bank arrangement with trefoil cable formation


## Figure 14 - Various sections of duct bank arrangement (directly buried)

## Figure 15 - Various sections of duct bank arrangement (encased in concrete)....................................

Installation Sequence

- Surface cutting (if necessary)
- Trench excavation and securing
- Bedding material placement and compaction
- Ducts placement
- Concrete or thermal backfill placing around the ducts
- Controlled backfill and warning tape placement and compaction above the ducts
- Top layer reinstatement (top soil, road pavement, etc.)
- HV cables pulling into ducts
- Fiber optic cables pulling into dedicated ducts
- Joints and terminations assembly

Application

This technique is a “main” installation technique as opposed to a special crossing technique which is
designed to avoid obstacles.

This technique is best suited for the following situations:

- Urbanized areas (under streets, sidewalks, etc...)
- Under or in the vicinity of roads (as opposed to highway) crossings
- In all areas where an easy cable removal is requested


It is to be noted that “ducts use” is also expanding in country side areas, where environmental restriction
are more and more demanding, limiting the execution of the civil works in well defined periods or where
hydrogeological risks are present.
