### 3.3.1.1 Civil work and Electrical work “De - coupling”

Most cities have strict regulations about traffic obstructions and this technique allows for a complete
separation between “civil work” and “cable work” which is also referred to as the “electrical work”. Both
types of works can be done in a timely manner according to applicable regulations. In addition, it
becomes possible to limit the impact of the civil works to few hundred meters at a time since the trench
can be backfilled swiftly after the ducts are installed reducing the amount of trench open at a given time.
“Non-duct” technique such as “direct burial” requires the trench to remain open over the total cable
length and until the cable installation is completed.

### 3.3.1.2 Civil work description

A layer of well compacted bedding material having low thermal resistivity could be placed on the bottom
of the trench prior to the installation of the ducts to provide a smooth surface but is not mandatory.

The number of ducts required for the given cable system, each having the proper diameters and wall
thicknesses, are placed in a trench at the pre- determined depth and configuration. Thinner wall ducts
can be used when the ducts are encased in concrete. Ducts could be stacked in two or more layers to
accommodate the required number of cables to be installed.

Ducts can be used for single cable systems, such as HVAC or HVDC, possibly with dedicated ducts for
ancillary telecom cables, or grouped for multiple cable system with other services in the so called duct
banks.

Special spacers are used to ensure the exact configuration and to allow concrete to flow between ducts.
A warning tape (or band) is usually placed about 30 cm or less above the top of the duct bank to signal
the contractors about the danger. An additional warning tape could be placed closer to the surface for
added safety.

Spacing between ducts and the depth of the ducts below final grade is determined through the design
process with the calculation of cable ampacity taking into consideration mutual heating effects between
operating cables as well as external heat sources where required.

Reinforcing steel rods could be used in special cases such as railway or highway crossings in order to
increase the rigidity of the concreted duct bank against compression stresses from the surface or
vibrations or to increase its robustness when it has to be exposed and supported by third party work.


For ducts of single cable systems, steel pipes can also be installed beforehand as a reinforcement
measure, and the ducts pulled in.

It remains important however to avoid continuous metallic loops of rods too close to the power cables
to avoid unnecessary induced currents and associated heating effects. If loops cannot be avoided, they
should at least comprise all AC cables.

In some cable sections, the space between the outside diameter of the installed cables and the inside
diameter of the ducts could be filled using special materials to enhance cable current carrying capacity
or restrict its movement. This is recommended in excessively deep installation or when difference in
elevation between manholes is substantial. Alternatively, intermittent manholes could be used to clamp
the cable in place.

When ducts are not encased in concrete, they must be backfilled with low thermal resistivity materials
and gently compacted to prevent the ducts from being warped or damaged during the operation.

### 3.3.1.3 Manholes and/or joint bays

The ducts normally terminate at “manholes” or “joint bays” from which the pulling can be executed.
Manholes normally refers to structures that are “accessible” through chimneys whereas joint bays are
“not accessible” after construction as they are typically backfilled.

Manholes/ joint bays can be built in place using reinforced concrete or, more and more frequent, using
pre-cast concrete elements. This would reduce the time needed for on site works. Depending on
dimensions, a blinding layer is installed to provide for proper ground leveling, stabilisation and ground
water drainage.

Manholes

Manholes are underground chambers built to house the joints and other auxiliary equipment such as
fluid feeding tanks, sheath cross bonding cables and sheath protection surge arresters. Access to cables
and joints is provided by the use of fixed or removable ladders installed in two or more chimneys
depending on manhole design.

Manhole dimensions are dependent on the number of cables to be jointed as well as the cable system
rated voltage. Metallic structures are usually used inside the manholes to support cables and joints. All
metallic steel members inside manholes should be properly connected to a solid ground rod assembly
or bare ground cable loop.

Joints should be protected from mechanical forces due to cable expansion under load cycles either by
expansion loops and/or by a rigid clamping system. Manhole dimensions could be reduced if joints and
cables are rigidly clamped.

Manholes should be designed in accordance with local standards to withstand the traffic loads according
to the location (main road, footpath, etc.).

It is also worth mentioning that manholes could present a safety hazard in case of a joint failure. Care
must be taken in designing manholes to vent fault energy in a safe manner.

Joint bays

Joint bays are designed with the same parameters of manholes, but without permanent access. So they
are preferred in country side areas or where access is not needed or foreseen. Joint bays are normally
installed shallower compared to manholes as they do not require chimneys.

They then offer a more economical method to house and mechanically protect cable system joints,
however they do not permit easy access to cable joints after installation activities are completed. After
construction is complete, joint bays are completely covered with no infrastructure extending above
ground. Joint bays can be built in place or assembled using precast concrete.

Joints bays can make cable pulling easier compared to manholes because the roof of joint bays is
normally removable (or built after the cable pulling).

Depending on the thermal requirements of the cable system, joint bays may be unfilled or filled with
thermal backfill to aid in the dissipation of heat. After installation of cable joints, all surrounding areas
which were disturbed during construction are restored to a condition equal to or better than the state at
which they were found using the appropriate backfill material as required by site conditions and local
authorities.


A temporary shelter should always be used during jointing operations to protect workers, cables, and
joints from the elements and ensure a clean environment for jointing operations. These shelters often
also provide a controlled temperature environment. The shelters could be “tarpaulin” sheets attached to
steel scaffolds or adapted “ship containers”. Once the joints are completed, the joint bay is normally
filled with thermal back filling material and top slabs placed for the roof. Sheath testing is possible using
link boxes located either above ground or in below ground accessible pits where cross bonding cable
leads are connected.

### 3.3.1.4 Number of cable(s) or circuit(s) per duct

High Voltage cable circuits are normally installed one cable per duct. However, for economic reasons,
three cables could be installed in the same duct in case of lower class voltages.

Also, two or more circuits (six cables or more) could be installed in the same duct bank. However, a
network risk assessment has to be performed for these cases since they can lead to multiple circuit
losses during a single event such as a third party damage or ground settlement. Due to mutual heating
effects between cables, multiple cable circuits within the same duct bank reduces the current carrying
capacity for each circuit.

Installing cables in ducts banks is considered one of the safest types of installation regarding safety
against external “third party” damage.

It can be useful and economical to install additional and/or spare ducts for future needs especially when
these needs are confirmed in the short or medium term. This is even more true in the case of special or
difficult crossings where it could be very costly and disturbing to re-open a trench in the same vicinity.

To this effect, our survey indicates that about 57% of the respondants do install extra ducts. Among
these, 42 % are used for “potential repairs” and 42%, for telecommunication circuits. See questions
2.2.1 and 2.2.2 of the survey in Annex C3.

### 3.3.1.5 Duration of civil works

The duration of the civil works will mainly depend on the depth of the trench, the number of ducts and
the number of obstacles along the cable route. Being naturally used in urban areas, this kind of work is
submitted to a large number of constraints such as:

- Permitting;
- Traffic and pedestrian signaling and securing
- Limited working hours due to rush hours
- Cutting into the pavement
- Slow (safe) digging for obstacles identification
- Management of excavated and backfill materials mostly by trucking
- Safe shoring of the trench for the workers
- Duct assembly and concreting
- Setting time of the concrete (or of the low strength, low resistivity backfill)
- Trench backfilling in order to respect a maximum length of open trench at once

Taking these factors into consideration, the duration of the civil works could vary from tens of meters up
to a few hundred meters per day for the most favorable conditions.

### 3.3.1.6 Cable Route selection

This process is very similar to the one used for direct buried cables. For completeness, the description
is repeated and adapted to “ducts” technique.

The cable route should be based on several factors such as:

- Acceptability by local governments/municipalities (for instance, avoiding very busy streets and
    environmentally sensitive areas)


- Space for cables with sufficient side clearance to other services or buildings
- Availability of permanent space for joint bays and temporary space for the cable laying equipment
    and cranes with smallest impact on traffic
- Making the route as short as possible to reduce costs
- Possibility of repairing the ducts/cables and/or installing a repair joint/bay along the route
- Reliable conditions (low risk of third party aggression, low flood risk, etc...)
- Selecting a route and a depth allowing minimal weaving across obstacles in order to reduce the
    cable pulling efforts
- Environmental sensitive areas from wildlife and naturalistic viewpoints

It is highly recommended that all potential obstacles along the cable route be identified and documented
at the design stage. With this information in hand, the duct bank route designer will try to avoid, as far
as possible, these obstacles. If avoiding obstacles is not possible, the cable route designer will adjust
the depth of the trench accordingly. Exploratory pits may also be required to check for existing services
when the information is not available from municipalities.

With the help of government or other agencies, studies should be carried out in order to determine soil
characteristics along the selected route, potential for contamination, possible presence of archaeological
artifacts, presence of shells (unexploded ordinance - UXO), and other significant elements.

Actual geotechnical and environmental surveys (including soil thermal resistivity measurements) can
and should be carried out along the final cable route to confirm the data highlighted by the above studies.
Such surveys are also helpful to check for the presence of rock and measure the water table depth.

Thermal resistivity and temperature measurements can also be made in conjunction with the above
investigations, taking advantage of the excavation activity already planned.

At special crossings, other installation techniques such trenchless construction methods, dedicated
supporting structures or even tunnels may be used to overcome obstacles. However, any change in the
construction technique will require adjustments in the cable laying technique and grounding
arrangement and will create an interface with new challenges. It is also important to perform ampacity
studies for all unique cable route installation cross-sections in order to confirm that the systems power
transfer requirements are met. Please see the interface section of this brochure.

The duct route shall be continuously recorded during duct laying: sometimes the field works differs from
the planned one, for example for unforeseen crossings with other services, or route changes for
obstacles. The cable pulling calculations may be significantly affected by such changes and should be
reviewed taking into account the final duct profile.

### 3.3.1.7 Existing services

In urban areas, it is likely that duct banks of HV cables will be installed near other urban services such
as water pipes, gas pipes, other electrical cables, steam pipes, telecommunication cables, etc.

A minimum clearance, specified by each underground owner in order to properly maintain and repair
their infrastructure without interfering with others, should exist between all services. In some cases,
electrical interference studies may need to be performed to confirm no adverse effects to neighbouring
utilities.

It is important that dialogue with neighbouring or crossed utilities is started as early as possible during
the project life cycle.

Steam pipes

Steam pipes are among the services that could affect the most the performance of HV cables into duct
banks because of the amount of heat they can induce in the ground. The older steam pipes are of even
greater concern as they were not as well insulated.

The thermal impact of steam pipes on the carrying capacity of the HV cables must be evaluated.
Solutions to overcome this problem could be to insulate the steam pipe from the duct bank by means of


thermal insulating sheaths running across the two services up to the surface. This insulation must not
however prevent the heat from the HV cables to flow upwards.

Electrical cables

When other electrical cables banks are concerned, it is also necessary to evaluate their impact on the
current capacity of HV cables and vice-versa as both performances could actually be impaired.

Gas

In the case of a gas leakage, it is important that the leak is repaired quickly and efficiently as it could be
a risk of explosion. This is why, gas owners specify safe minimum distances.

Telecommunication cables

The performance of telecommunication cables (made of copper) running closely and parallel to duct
banks could potentially be disturbed by induced voltages from HV cables. This is why telecommunication
will specify minimum clearances with electrical cables.

Nowadays telecommunications cables are made of fiber optic cables which are not be affected by
induced voltages.

Water and sewer pipes

Water pipes may run along duct banks of HV cables. Water pipes are typically shallower and smaller
compared to sewer pipes.

When a pressurized water pipe breaks, it often causes localized potholes which could have an adverse
effect on the cables or joint bay stability.

Since sewer pipes are not as pressurized and are located deeper in the ground, they do not constitute
a similar risk. However, sewers are often very old structures and can be easily damaged by the duct
bank trench work.

Trees

Most cities want to increase their plant cover as an important measure against urban warming. As a
result, new line of trees are getting installed around existing duct banks.

Line of trees may be installed fairly close to concreted duct banks and vice-versa as they are very robust.
The roots will hardly damage the concrete nor the ducts themselves. However, the duct bank trench
work must not severely damage the root structure of the trees.

Duct banks which are backfilled with lower strength materials may not be as robust and roots could
possibly become entwined with the ducts especially when duct banks are shallow. As a result, a greater
distance to a line of trees is advised.

Railways

Railways (or tramways) could be an important source of stray currents (DC) into the ground. This can
lead to the corrosion of metallic pipes carrying either water or gas. The installation of cathodic protection
systems with sacrificial anodes will prevent degradation of metallic pipes.

HV cables into ducts are not susceptible to this phenomenon as they are insulated from ground by their
external polymeric jacket.

### 3.3.1.8 Equipment and spaces

During trench excavation and manhole / joint bay installation

With ducts and duct banks, the native soils have to be removed from the site as they are not typically
reused. Trucks are normally used for this task and will require one circulating lane. The need for trucks
and a circulating lane will increase the horizontal space required for construction. It is common for the
excavator to be located in line with the trench. Please refer to the picture below which provides an
example of the described situation.


## Figure 16 - Site occupation during manhole installation

As a result, an area reaching a typical width of 7-8 meters is required to dig the trench and a wider area
for the manhole/joint bay installation. Depending on the street lengths, traffic may be completely
blocked.

During cable pulling

Once the trench is ready for cable pulling, the cable reel machinery and the winch will be located at
opposite ends on the duct bank to carry out the pull.

During cable pulling, several specialized vehicles or trailers are required. The main winch is required on
the pulling side (left below) while the cable reel stand (unwinding machine) is required on the cable
payout side (right below).

The winch should be set to have a cut out at or approaching the maximum pulling tension of the cable.

Cable drum unwinding can also takes place from the installation trailer if local conditions allow (e.g.,
position of the trailer from the joint bay).

During preparation, a crane will be required to offload the cable reel from the transport truck to the
unwinding stand.


## Figure 17 - Arrangement and site occupation during cable installation

An area reaching a width of up to 7 meters in width and up to 50 meters in length is required to carry
such operations as shown above.

## Figure 18 - Installation equipment

### 3.3.1.9 Duct size, material and bend considerations

The duct must not restrict the cable during the pulling nor during its operation.

During pulling, if the clearance between the outside diameter of the cable and the inside diameter of the
duct is too small, the cable will be more restrained at the bends, leading to higher pulling tensions and


sidewall bearing pressures. Cables with larger outside diameters tend to be stiffer and require more
energy to bend. Brochure TB 669 could be consulted for more information on these topics.

During operation, if the clearance between the cable and the inside diameter of the duct is too small, it
will cause the cable to behave more like a straight bar as opposed to a flexible element and will not
allow cable snaking to take place as a result of thermal expansion. This will ultimately result in higher
thrust forces at the joints and terminations.

Technical Brochure 669 could be consulted for more information on these topics.

Several materials from various ducts manufacturer are available, such as:

- PVC (Polyvinyl Chloride)
- PE or HDPE (Polyethylene or High density PE)
- PP (PolyPropylene)
- ABS (Acrylonitrile Butadiene Styrene)
- FRE (Fiber reinforced Epoxy)
- PFP (Polyester Fiber Plastic)
- FRP (Fiber reinforced plastic)
- Reinforced Plastic composite duct
- Polyester Concrete FRP

The selection can be driven by technical or economical aspects. The duct material shall be compatible
with the temperatures to be experienced during the service life of the system without affecting its
mechanical performances. If the ducts surrounding material is the original soil, the duct must withstand
the expected loads, so the ducts thickness must be properly selected. Duct thickness is of paramount
importance also when the ducts must be pulled along the route, for example in case of an HDD
installation. In such case the ratio of the pulling force and duct area shall be within the working limits.

The size of the ducts is of paramount importance for the system design and performances; the smaller
is the duct the lower is the thermal resistance of the air gap and the larger is the thrust at joint positions.

Another aspect to be considered are the joints between duct sections. The joint can be of different types,
requiring different materials, expertise and costs. Each type of joint can have different issues, especially
during cable pulling.

For PVC ducts spigot joints are normally used. The skill of the personnel is very important to ensure that
the joint is properly sealed, with a smooth inner interface. When such joints are glued using a solvent
cement, application of the product is important as too much quantities can introduce problems as well
as too small quantities.


## Figure 19 - Conduit adhesive for PVC pipes

The use of these joints could dictate that the direction of cable pulling is determined by the direction of
the conduit installation, to ensure that the leading end of the cable does not consistently encounter the
lip of the inner conduit. These factors need to be determined in the early design stages for each cable
section.

## Figure 20 - Conduit connection

If the duct is provided with an inner semi-conducting layer, then the joint fittings shall also be provided
to ensure the electrical continuity of this layer.

For HDPE ducts the most commonly used jointing method is the electro welded joint: a specially
designed socket with integrated resistance is installed. The jointing process is fully automatic, with self-
calibrating welding equipment in accordance to material and duct size. The operator must only check
the cleanliness of the ducts.

Socket joints with sealing rings are also used, especially when the backfill is strong enough to prevent
duct displacement, such as lean concrete. The joint provides a smooth inner surface, but the jointing
operation requires special care to avoid damage or deformation to the sealing rings. In such case water
or sand/mud could seep into ducts.

Butt welded joints are used when the duct shall be subject to pulling forces, for example the HDD ducts
installation, and the full mechanical characteristics of the duct must be available. Butt welded joints are


also used when the increase outer diameter of the socket joints are not acceptable or suitable. As for
the electro welded joints, the jointing procedure is fully automatic, and special tools have to be used.
The jointing process leaves a significant bead inside and outside the duct: such bead shall be removed,
at least inside, with special in order to provide a smooth inner surface.

## Figure 21 - Butt welding of PE pipe

Where bends are required, the usual practice is to have the ducts either bent on site or delivered as pre-
formed unit from the factory. These practices were confirmed by almost 80% of the respondents to our
survey. A minority are using short couplers of either 5° or 11.5°.

If bent on site, the manufacturer recommendation as far as the minimum bending radius of the duct shall
be strictly followed.

FRE ducts are also used but are not as common. This type of duct is very light and robust making their
installation in the field easier. However, due to their increased rigidity, they cannot be bent on site. They
could be factory bent but it would be very difficult to plan enough pieces at various radii to cover the
whole cable route. As a result, several low angle couplers (5°) are used to “create the bends”. Quality
control must be very stringent during construction in order to make sure that these couplers remain
sufficiently distanced one from another to avoid introducing too many low radii bends along the route.

in general, the bending radius of the route shall consider not only the cable, but also the duct bending
radius and preformed bends, which typically allow for smaller radius compared to ducts bent on site. In
case of PE / PP ducts, the ambient temperature during ducts installation has to be considered. Lower

### temperatures requires higher bending radius.

If ducts are used to cross bridges, the supports must be calculated considering the forces and/or
deformations generated by the duct thermal expansion as well as the one of the cable.

Please refer to section 4.5 “Cable pulling” for more information on these topics.

### 3.3.1.10 Duct cleaning and proving

The duct(s) should be thoroughly cleaned and tested with mandrels to assure a suitable passageway
for the cable to be pulled safely without damage.

Water Jetting can be used as the first step to clean and remove debris prior to using the applicable
mandrels. Alternatively, a sponge mandrel can be used either using a draw rope or compressed air to
move the mandrel through the conduits.


A mandrel is basically a cylindrical tool designed for either cleaning and boring or testing a duct.
Mandrelling is used to check for deformations in the ducts and acts as a “go/no-go” test.

Some underground service owners specify a maximum tension for duct cleaning and proving. Should
this tension be exceeded or the mandrel moves with sudden bursts even though the max tension is not
exceeded , the repair of the ducts may be called for.

Mandrelling is the final operation preparatory for cable pulling and should be done regardless of the
installation date of the duct system.

The mandrelling procedure can be done as a function of the cleaning procedure or can be performed
as a separate operation just prior to the scheduled cable pull. If the duct is mandrelled at the time of
cleaning then large impassable obstructions can be determined and corrective action, such as
excavation and duct reconstruction can be done or if other passable ducts are available the duct
assignment can be changed.

To mandrel a duct, the mandrel will be attached to the cleaning line or a pilot line in the manhole and
pulled to the next adjacent manhole (or termination). The mandrel having pulling eyes on both ends will
have attached a trailing line to allow it to be pulled back to the original manhole in case of jamming due
to an obstruction.

Cleaning, boring or test mandrels should be pulled back and forth through the duct several times before
acceptance to again assure the proper duct surface or configuration for a safe cable pull.

After the mandrelling operation is complete a pilot line should be left in the duct to permit the cable
pulling rope to be pulled into the duct for the pulling operation.

#### MANDRELS

The types of mandrels can be classified as cleaning or testing categories. The cleaning mandrels are
normally used after the cleaning operation to provide a clear and clean duct for cable pulling. This type
of mandrel dislodges and removes the debris. A boring mandrel is also classified in this category which
in addition to removal of debris, rebores a duct in removing duct blisters, roots, concrete stalactites at
duct joints, etc.

Test mandrels are required and used as the final inspection of the duct preparation for cable pulling.
These types of mandrels check for excessive duct ovality, surface roughness, and inadequate bend
radii that could ultimately damage the cable during the pull. If project requires pulling through duct runs
of changing inside diameters, such as increased wall thicknesses through trenchless sections, cleaning
and testing requirements need to be addressed accordingly.

Cleaning Mandrels. There are three main categories of cleaning mandrels, brushes, swabs and
sponges. Each is discussed in more detail below.

- Brushes:

A wire brush having flat, stiff, nylon or steel bristles is used to remove light obstructions, sand or silt
adhering to the inner surface of the duct. Dirt loosened by the wire brush is removed from the duct by
pulling a washer cleaner, conduit or cloth swabs through. The brush should be the same diameter of
the duct to be cleaned. Caution should be used from excessive use of wire brushes to prevent the
introduction of scratches to the inner duct surface.


## Figure 22 - Wire brush cleaner

- Swabs:

Swabs are used for a finished wiping and cleaning of the duct. The conduit swab can remove residual
water and lubricate the duct surface as it is pulled.

The swab shall consist of a ball cotton rags or series of rag balls on a pull line, sized to fit snugly within
the duct or constructed from a series of hard foam / rubber discs with steel pull loops.

If moisture and/or foreign materials are evident on the swab after being pulled through the duct,
additional swabbing must be performed until swab appears clean and dry. If ripping is evident on the
swab, swabbing must be carefully repeated and inspected to ensure duct or weld defects do not exist.
If any defects exist, they should be investigated further and repaired if necessary.

## Figure 23 - Duct swabs

- Sponges:

Just like rags, sponges are used to remove any remaining small debris and water. Sponging will be
carried out few times until no sign of water and debris are coming out.

The sponge shall be made of a single body of absorbent high density material sized to fit snugly within
the duct, retained by side rigid disks with steel pull loops.


## Figure 24 - Sponges

Test Mandrels: There are three categories of test mandrels, Rigid, Flexible and Cable mandrels. Each
is discussed in more detail below

- Rigid:

Rigid mandrels are typically constructed of maple wood or similar seasoned hardwood, in some cases
steel or aluminum or painted polystyrene. Rigid mandrels should have a diameter of 1/4 inch (6mm) to
1/2 inch (13mm) less than the inside duct diameter according to local practices and standards.

They should be tapered at each end or at a minimum have rounded ends. The length of the full-diameter
portion of the mandrel should be at least 1.5 times the diameter of the mandrel.

The mandrels should be finished with a two-tone finish, typically painted first with a red coat then finished
with a white coat to aid in identifying and scratches.


## Figure 25 - Rigid test mandrel

Any evidence of scratching deeper than 1/32 inch (0.8 mm) or obstruction of the test mandrel shall be
investigated, and any corrective action taken. The process will be repeated until a satisfactory result is
obtained.

- Flexible:

Flexible Mandrels consist of discs of graduated or uniform diameter strung on a flexible steel cable with
eyes at both ends. A flexible type mandrel will normally traverse any duct bend radius: therefore, the
mandrel diameter could be sized to match the inside diameter of the duct and the length could be any
reasonable length capable of being handled in the manhole. The grooves or spaces between the discs
can collect loosened dirt and can facilitate cleaning of ducts where bends inhibit passage of a rigid
mandrel.

## Figure 26 - Flexible test mandrel

- Cable Mandrel:

This mandrel is made by cutting a length of the cable to be installed (or a piece of scrap cable of the
same or larger diameter) and fitting each end with a pulling eye. The condition of the cable mandrel after
being pulled through the duct will indicate if the cable to be installed can be safely pulled. A cable
mandrel is normally 4 feet (1.3m) or longer. If results are unsatisfactory, duct cleaning can be repeated.

#### OTHER STEPS

- Log sheet

Upon completion of the duct cleaning and mandrelling the contractor should fill out a log sheet for each
duct. Some owners may require that the ducts be videoed to provide a visual record of the ducts prior
to use.

- Pilot Line Installation

In most cases, after the duct(s) has been rodded, cleaned and mandrelled a pilot line (rope or nylon
mule tape) is left in the duct to enable the cable pulling rope to be pulled into the duct by the pulling
crew. This pilot line is installed trailing the last mandrel pass through the duct and is of sufficient length
to allow the cable pulling crew to make the attachment to pull the cable pulling rope through the duct.
This added pilot line slack is trained and pushed back into the duct (duct is then plugged to maintain a
clean condition) for storage until the cable pulling operation.

- CCTV

In some countries the conduits undergo further inspection by using a closed circuit television camera to
further verify the conduit of the conduits. This is done by using a small mechanical crawler housing a
video camera which allows inspection of joins and any conduit deformation as a result of backfilling
materials.

### 3.3.1.11 Typical duct problems

Some of the typical duct problems encountered during cleaning and test mandrelling are:

- A heavy wall of silt and debris clinging to the inner duct surface


- Burrs and foreign protrusions at duct joints. This could be concrete from construction, roots, broken
    duct ends, excess of adhesive compound combined with debris, etc.
- Collapsed or misshapen (oval) ducts due to construction practices when encasing the duct in
    concrete or from surface pressure such as heavy vehicular traffic
- Duct misalignment at the duct joints. Caused by earth movement or construction practices primarily
    with tile or concrete ducts
- Short radius duct bends (horizontal and vertical) wherein the cable pull could be difficult or possibly
    destructive to the cable. Cause is basically incorrect construction practices during installation or the
    use of too closely installed low angle couplings
- Excessive abrasion of the duct couplings at the bends due to high pulling forces. They could be
    caused by the presence of too many low angle couplings along the duct route due to a low radius.
    This type of damage to the duct may lead to damage to the high voltage cable jackets

## Figure 27 - Picture revealing intense abrasion at a duct coupling (up to concrete)


### 3.3.2 Limits of the technique

For matters of clarity and completeness, the texts have been repeated from other installation modes
when they apply and adapted to “ducts” technique as required.

### 3.3.2.1 Drying of the soil

“Ducts” is not the most sensitive installation mode with regards to soil drying because the cables are not
in direct contact with the ground. They are contained within ducts which are then covered with a
homogeneous and compact material such as concrete or thermal backfill which is placed around the
ducts in a fluidised state to eventually to set into a solid state.

This layer of well compacted material helps carry the heat generated by the cables from the outer
surface of the “duct bank”. It is still required, however, that the soil surrounding the bank carries this
heat further away from the cables, however the requirement is not as imperative as it is for direct burial
cables.

It is to be noted, however, that the use of ducts will reduce the current “rating” of the cables compared
to a “direct burial” arrangement because of the higher thermal resistance of the air trapped around the
cables. In short, the current carrying capacity is smaller into ducts (compared to direct burial) but it
remains less susceptible to the risks of soil drying out.

Soil drying can be attributed to natural processes as well as by induced changes. The rate of drying
depends on the location, precipitation, soil type, natural water table, air temperature, surface
temperature, wind speed, type of cover (grass, asphalt, concrete), vegetation (moisture depletion by
trees), etc. For underground power cable environment, the heat generated by the energized cable
(W/m) drives the moisture away at the cable/soil interface, and it has a significant impact on the thermal
resistivity and thus the cable rating.

In certain regions, soils may be naturally dry. Various solutions can be considered:

- Use of controlled backfilling material
- Use of a larger conductor size
- Use of bigger installation depths in order to reach more thermally stable soils
- Use of a flat formation instead of trefoil formation to distribute the heat over a larger area and thus
    improve the rate of heat dissipation of each cable. Further consideration may be done about the
    sheath bonding system
- Install fiber optic cables either attached to the power cable jacket or embedded inside the power
    cables and use them as Distributed Temperature Sensor (DTS) in order to detect hot spots before
    any irreversible damage to the cable insulation occurs

### 3.3.2.2 Water drainage

Water table level varies with location. In some areas, abundant surface water could hinder civil work
progress. Water seeping through the ground during construction should be pumped out, using
appropriate equipment, to ensure personnel safety as well as quality of work. Once properly installed,
ducts should not be affected by surrounding water. Water can penetrate the ducts, however clean water
ingress is not a significant issue. Problems arise when other materials are brought inside with the water,
such as sand, mud, etc. This may create significant difficulties in the case of cable replacement or cause
the cables to become stuck inside the duct.

In case of manholes, and if possible, ducts should be installed with a continuous slight slope towards
manholes. Manholes would be connected to city sewage or storm draining systems through an anti
pollution (water treatment) arrangement particularly in the case of fluid filled cables. Local regulations
should be followed and authorization should be obtained for these connections.

Conversely to the soil-drying phenomenon, wet soil may seem more favourable to laying of HV cables
because it constitutes a natural cooling system. This is partly true. In such cases the trench cannot be
backfilled as effectively with thermal backfill.

This is of concern if the soil or the backfill material is loose (granular with high voids content, poor
compaction). It should be addressed during the construction phase if it affects the stability of the soil or
the backfill around the ducts. The washout of fines in the backfill material can adversely affect the
stability and thermal resistivity of the fill material and such cases have been documented.


Geotextile of various types are commonly used in civil engineering applications when working on very
soft and unstable ground conditions, especially in flooded areas. When a static or dynamic load is
exerted on the Geotextile mat, it allows water to filter through thus, consolidating the sub-soil and
preventing settlement (sinking).

If cables are directly buried in areas where the natural water table fluctuates (tidal changes) or if the
area is prone to flooding, the backfill around the cables (especially granular type) may lose (washout)
fines such as fine sand and silt. This will impact not only the mechanical but the thermal characteristics
of the backfill. If the cable trench is provided with appropriate Geotextile, it will allow the water to
permeate through but will prevent wash out of the fines.

Please refer to item 8.2 of TB 714 for further details.

### 3.3.2.3 Temperature of the soil/environment

At a given location the temperature of the sub-surface soil is a function of the latitude, depth (cables or
duct-bank), time of the year and surface cover (grass, asphalt, concrete). Natural changes in
temperature follows a sinusoidal curve (Hillel, 1982) and is fairly constant at a depth of about 10m. Local
environmental and meteorological agencies as well as airport authorities are good sources of
temperature records; especially for depths up to 2m. Agronomy records in general provide temperature
and moisture data for shallow depth of less than 1m. For cable rating calculations, the ground
temperature at the burial depth must also be taken into consideration.

Summer conditions are naturally the hottest conditions of the year.

A tree of thermocouples can be installed at various locations and depths such as ‘hot-spots’ are
monitored over several cycles to obtain the true temperature values and their changes as function of
depth and time of the year. Alternatively, as stated before, fiber optic cables and Distributed
Temperature Sensing (DTS) systems can be used to monitor temperatures and better understand the
effect of ground temperatures. Fiber optic cables will probably provide more accurate results over the
years as they are passive sensors and will not suffer from drifting or corrosion problems with time.

While ducts could be installed in all type of soils, for some ducts materials the environmental temperature
must be considered, both during construction and service. Lower temperatures may affect the flexibility
of the ducts, while high temperatures may influence their mechanical properties.

### 3.3.2.4 Hardness of the soil

Hard soil, hardpan and bedrock can pose difficulty, delays and extra costs for excavation and
construction.

It is therefore advisable to conduct a geotechnical survey along the cable route. The borehole logs or
the test-pit logs can give a good indication of the soil stiffness/hardness and depth of bedrock (if
encountered). Geotechnical tests are conducted in-situ and/or in the laboratory on samples collected
during investigation.

If excavation into rocky soils cannot be avoided, project schedule and costs must be adjusted
accordingly.

### 3.3.2.5 Stability of the soil

Soil types that are non-cohesive, sandy and gravelly and soil conditions that are very soft and sensitive
have poor mechanical stability and can pose problems during excavation; especially at locations with a
high water table.

Stability must then be addressed during the construction phase by de-watering, trench shoring,
mechanically or dynamically compacting and/or grout injection techniques.

Stability must be achieved for the successful operation of the work as well as for the safety of the
workers. The figure below shows a shoring technique to secure trench walls. Metallic walls and welded
channels are being used. Alternatively, wooden structures can also be used.

The duct solution provides a better protection in comparison with direct burial technique in case of local
soil instabilities or for example soil washing out after flooding or heavy rains.


## Figure 28 - Securing of a trench with shore technique

### 3.3.2.6 Thermal resistivity of the soil

For cable rating (ampacity) calculations, the three most important parameters are:

- The ambient temperature at the cable burial depth
- The earth surface temperature and
- The thermal resistivity of the soil/backfill around the ducts up to the ground surface

Soil gives the largest single thermal resistance and thermal capacitance of an underground power cable
system. It has the greatest variability with distance along the cable route and with time. The thermal
resistivity can easily vary threefold or more on a specific circuit, and the cable must be rated for the
worst-case condition. Better understanding of the physical and thermal properties of soils and backfills,
and the use of controlled backfills can result in significant increase in ampacity.

Thermal resistivity depends on the soil (or backfill) type, water content, density and various other factors
to a lesser degree.

Typical TR values are suggested in IEC 60287- 3 - 1 specification for various countries, however, these
remain very approximate and are informative only. This approach could advantageously be replaced by
a cable route thermal survey where in-situ and laboratory thermal tests are carried out at various times
of year to understand the thermal behavior of a particular soil and design the backfill material
accordingly.

### 3.3.2.7 Seismicity

“Ducts” is not the most sensitive installation mode with regards to seismicity because the cables are not
in direct contact with the ground. The cables are contained within ducts which are then covered with a
homogeneous and compact material such as concrete or thermal backfill which are placed around the
ducts in a fluidised state and the material eventually sets into a solid form.

Nevertheless, ducts and duct banks could potentially be subjected to high stresses generated by soil
movements. Several events of this type were experienced in Japan and led to the damage/separation
of the ducts. It also led, in a very few cases, to the damage of the cables themselves.


As opposed to the direct burial technique, measures can be taken with the ducts to help withstand the
seismic stresses such as flexible and expansion joints in the duct system as well as free cable loops in
the manholes. These elements will absorb the stresses of the earthquake while protecting the cables.

### 3.3.2.8 Frost (temperature limits, provisions for installation in cold environments)

Carrying out civil works in frosty environments (winter conditions) remains possible. The work will be
more demanding on the workers due to the lower temperatures as well as on the machinery due to the
increased hardness of the ground. The increased hardness remains however much lower than the one
of rock.

As far as cable pulling is concerned, cable manufacturers do not normally recommend pulling HV cables
below 5°C temperatures without cable pre-warming. This is because the cables get stiffer and loose
flexibility and this could lead to higher pulling tensions and stability problems with the cable head.

The pre-warming will consist in either storing the cable reel indoor a heated room near the pulling site
or installing a heating shelter around the cable reel at the site and maintain the air temperature within
the shelter at 10°C or more for at least 24 hours prior to pulling.

The freezing of the soil itself does not constitute a risk for the cables providing the following criteria are
met:

- The cable trench must be adequately drained in order to avoid water freezing and differential uplift
    around the duct banks;
- If possible, the trench bottom must not be too shallow (below freezing line) in order to avoid
    differential ground movements during thaw period especially near fixed structures such as joint bays
    and terminations foundations. It is to be noted however that no such events have been reported.

Duct banks remain a very robust solution against differential ground movements due to frost (in a
manner similar to earthquake stresses) because they form a very compact assembly into which the
cables remain free to move.

In extreme cases where ground is permanently frozen, special arrangements should be considered
since the heat dissipated from cables may cause soil instability.

### 3.3.2.9 Archaeology

If the cable route crosses a location of potential archaeological interest, it must be borne in mind that,
due to the requirements of local and environmental authorities, any finds made during excavation will
make it mandatory to stop the work, at least in the portion where the find has been made.

Work at the site is then delayed until the archaeologists have investigated the find. The length of the
portion affected and the duration of the stoppage depend on what further discoveries are made.

Another way of coping with this issue is to investigate the archaeology potential along the cable route
at the design stage. If the potential is sufficiently high, an alternate route can be found. If not, a daily
surveillance by an archaeological expert firm could be carried out during the civil work and any finding
can be examined and extracted as they are met in collaboration with the civil contractor. The most
extensive archaeological work can even be planned (ahead of time) to take place after the working
hours of the civil contractors.

Another solution could be to execute an HDD below the archaeological area. However, this solution
may not always be technically feasible and would have a strong impact on the cost and schedule of the
project.

### 3.3.2.10 Presence of rodents and termites

Cables should be designed to have an anti-termite protection and ducts can provide an additional
protection. Duct ends should be properly sealed or blocked to prevent their entrance.

### 3.3.2.11 Laying in national park

Open trench work may not be permitted in some national parks due to the potential impact on wildlife.
When the cable route cannot avoid such areas, other techniques such as horizontal directional drilling
(HDD) may be used. However, as explained earlier, such a technique could have a strong impact on
the cost and schedule of the project.


If open trench work is allowed, it is likely that during the backfilling, the restoration of the original soil
conditions will be required possibly including the degree of compaction.

Laying the ducts into already disturbed areas such as road or road shoulders could be the best approach
for such locations. The duct technique also provides benefit in terms of flexibility to plan the works in the
allowed periods of the year.

### 3.3.2.12 Land registration

The rights of way are usually settled by joint agreement between the utility and public authorities
(municipalities, highways, railways, bridges, etc.) or between the utility and a private owner.

When using a public land, the construction techniques are normally agreed with between the various
parties. The responsibility under who’s the work is to be carried out (whether it is the utility or the public
authority) must also be identified.

The work carried out into public land does not generally lead to compensation being paid. However, in
the case of a private owner, the compensation could be agreed according to the width of the right of
way. In some countries, the compensation is based on the actual width of the trench multiplied by a
factor.

### 3.3.3 Environmental impact

With the ducts method, natural environments may be substantially altered when compared with
“trenchless” techniques (horizontal drilling, tunnel “boring”, etc).

It is important not to underestimate the environmental impact and local environmental laws during the
construction itself, and therefore it is wise to consider it already at the preliminary study stage, before it
becomes an issue for the population.

The objective is to mitigate the impacts and restore as far as possible the original environment. The
impacts related to the construction and operation of a power link, can be classified as follows :

Soil

- Direct damage due to the excavation (material displacement)
- Possible contamination of soils due to excavated materials being mixed with surrounding land
    materials.

Water

- Possible contamination of water ecosystem due to excavated or backfilled materials being
    displaced/lost in water bodies.

Air

- Release of pollutants to atmosphere during the construction work
- Noise generated by machinery during the work

Flora

- Direct destruction of the vegetation cover
- Indirect destruction of the nearby plant communities
- Damage to unique or interesting species

Fauna (embedding)

- Direct or indirect disturbance of natural fauna habitat

Socio-economic aspects

- Difficulties caused to parking and access to shops etc. during working hours
- Temporary effects on tourism trade during the construction work
- Rights of way affected during the construction work

Landscape


- Alteration of the landscape during the construction phase.

It is clear that most of these disturbances can be prevented or mitigated at the design stage. The major
impact arises most of the time during the construction phase and can be mitigated by informing and
consulting with the local authorities and population, by using less noisy equipment and by working during
less sensitive hours.

### 3.3.4 Safety.................................................................................................................................................

### 3.3.4.1 During Construction

During digging, duct placing, cable pulling and jointing operations it is vital that considerable attention
be paid to the safety and health of the workers and the public. Among the items that should be
considered are:

- Risks to other electrical services
- Risks to other types of services such as gas, water, sewers, etc...
- Ground subsidence
- Prevent falls or tripping into the trench
- Prevent workers from induced voltages from other circuits
- Traffic management issues
- Etc...

The jointing normally takes place in joint bays or manholes. Joint bays are normally open (no permanent
cover/roof during the work) and this may also constitute a risk.

If the case of a manhole, accessible only through chimneys, it becomes work carried out in a “confined
space” and additional safety rules will apply:

- Before entering a manhole, sensors must be used to make sure of the absence of hazardous gases
- Every worker must be accompanied by at least one companion into the manhole
- Provisions must be made to be able to “extract” an injured or unconscious worker up to ground level.
    Safety harnesses or specially designed stretchers are used

Please consult relevant local documentation regarding safety in confined spaces for more information.

### 3.3.4.2 During operation

Owners of underground infrastructure normally offer a free location service to all companies intending
to carry excavation work. Such services are aimed at damage prevention to third party underground
infrastructure or other assets.

Visible markers can also be used to help reduce the risks. These could be located in the pavement, on
kerb and gutters or beside the road with an indicated offset.

Buried devices such as warning tapes or nets are also generally placed above the cables and the top
of the duct banks could be colored red to help reducing the risk of damage. The tapes and nets can be
manufactured with a metallic strip making them easier to be detected.

For manholes or joint bays, bricks or concrete enclosures will normally protect the joints from third party
damage, especially boring.

### 3.3.5 Legal requirements

When cable routes are located in public lands, there is normally no acquisition cost for the easement. It
also eases the approval process because projects are submitted only to the public authorities which are
normally favorable to these kinds of projects as it answers to the needs of many.

Open trench work for duct installation will cause some disturbance to the population but to a smaller
degree when compared to direct burial installation because not all the route between joints must be
opened at once. It can be backfilled and restored within a few days by a second team while the first


team progresses with the digging. The impacts will be more or less severe depending on the location of
the project and the urbanisation level.

When the cable route runs on private properties, utilities must submit their project to every owner along
the route and take all necessary mitigation measures. Several years can be needed before an
agreement is reached with all. In absence of agreement, some utilities have the right to expropriate with
financial compensation.

### 3.3.6 Maintenance and repair processes

Once the cable link is commissioned, it will be accessible for maintenance at the ends (terminations into
substations) and at the joints (at the manholes or at link box pits next to every joint bay).

The maintenance work will consist of performing some or all the tests listed below in a periodic manner
typically ranging from annually to periods of up to several years as recommended by the cable
manufacturer and according to the owner’s experience:

- Jacket tests (integrity tests on the outer layer of the cables)
- On-line or off-line Partial discharge tests (quality tests on the main insulation)
- High voltage tests (integrity of main insulation)
- Checks on sheath voltage limiters (SVL’s)

In the case of a third party damage leading to a cable fault, excavation work will be required to expose
the damaged cable. The cable damage assessment will either lead to a:

- Long length cable replacement up to adjacent joint bays; or
- Medium length cable replacement between repair bays (for one joint) built of either side of the
    damage location; or
- Short length cable replacement within a longer repair bay allowing for the installation of 2 joints

These tasks would then be followed by the restoration of the duct bank and the rest of the trench.

However, in the case of a cable internal failure, fault localizing tools will be required to firstly pre-locate
the fault and then, proceed with its pinpointing. The pinpointing of a fault within ducts could be more
difficult due to the fact that they act as an insulation between fault and ground. However, filling the duct
with water, using a semiconducting cable outer sheath or even a semiconducting duct’s inner layer can
be used to potentially overcome this issue.

Once the fault is localized, the repair scheme will be one of the three options described above. Several
weeks could be required to complete this task once the damage is located.

### 3.3.7 Commissioning and de-commissioning..............................................................................................

Before the cable circuit is commissioned and connected to the power transmission system it has to
undergo a series of tests to confirm its suitability for connection to the Network.

The tests vary across the industry but generally include HV tests on the primary insulation and on the
cable outer sheath. Further tests are carried out such as phasing confirmation, insulation resistance (IR
tests), conductor resistance, capacitance, bonding and earthing tests.

The use of AC or DC test sources for testing the primary insulation is subjected to the type of electrical
system (HVAC or HVDC) and the type of cable insulating material.
Please refer to TB 841“After laying tests on AC and DC cable systems with new technologies”.

With the availability of mobile on-site AC test sets, of the resonant frequency type, there is a general
move to testing extruded cable on site by AC voltage only. These systems generally referred to as
Resonant Test Sets (RTS). IEC standard states a minimum range frequency of 20 Hz and it generally
prevails. However though, in specific cases and particularly long cable system testing a frequency down
to 10 Hz could be considered as indicated in CIGRE TB 841.


Very low frequency test systems such as 0.1 Hz have also been developed having advantages such as
reduced equipment complication, less cost and greater portability. However, the voltage levels must be
adjusted (raised) to compensate for the lower frequencies used.

Further developments in partial discharge testing have shown that the combination of a PD test with a
high voltage AC test is the most reliable means of confirming the integrity of extruded cable systems
prior to connection to the power transmission grid.

Cable removal at the end of life could be a challenge if the cables are stuck in the ducts but in general,
it remains possible even if the duct bank has to be cut open at the middle of the run to ease the removal
of the cables from both ends.

In the case of fluid filled cables, the cables would initially have their insulating fluid removed before they
are pulled out, in order to minimize the risks of fluid loss into the ducts.

### 3.3.8 Specific monitoring

Various monitoring systems are used with the following high voltage cables types.

In the case of Self Contained Fluid Filled (SCFF) cables, the pressure of the fluid is constantly monitored
from the ends (terminations) and at any intermediate oil feeding points along the route of the circuits.
An abnormal loss of pressure will trigger an alarm as it could mean a leak of fluid into the environment.
The operator will react quickly in order to locate and fix the leak. This monitoring is associated with the
type of cable and is not specific to direct buried circuits. However, with such circuits, the risk of a spill in
the environment is probably higher because the leak is more difficult to find compared to other
installation modes. In some countries, tracers are used and can make the location of the leak much
easier as they can be detected from the surface with specialized sensors.

With extruded cables, there is no need for such a pressure monitoring as the cables do not contain fluid.

Distributed Temperature Sensing (DTS) is a technique that could be used to make sure the cables
perform as planned and can therefore safely carry their full load. DTS systems use fiber optic cables
installed along the power cables (in the metallic screen or as a separate cable) to accurately monitor
their heating at every meter along the route. This tool, coupled with a Real Time Thermal rating (RTTR)
software, will inform the operator about real time heating and allow for the formulation of temporary
overload schemes.

This kind of monitoring could prove useful in avoiding cable overheating and failures that could occur
when soils, in contact with the duct bank, are subject to drying out.

Distributed Acoustic Sensing (DAS) is a technique which also uses fiber optic cables to monitor the
noise and vibration around the cables at every meter along the route. It can prove useful in detecting
and pinpointing situations such as cable failures, unauthorized excavation work, traffic, etc...


## 3. 4 Troughs

### 3.4.1 Description of the technique

This technique has been used for about 12% of all reported underground transmission circuit kilometers

Troughs are generally used within the confines of outdoor switchyards or in open country where there
are few or no other obstructions, such as other services. In some countries, above ground troughs are
installed within railway easements, with the permission of the local rail authority. Troughs are also
sometimes used in tunnel situations to assist with fire performance and they are unfilled.

## Figure 29 - Direct buried trough


## Figure 30 - Filled surface trough

## Figure 31 - Unfilled surface trough

## Figure 32 - Unfilled surface trough with hanged cables

Although the above pictures represent flat cable formations, it must be specified that cable trefoil
arrangement is also used, both with filled or unfilled troughs.


Installation Sequence

- Surface cutting (if necessary)
- Trench excavation and securing (if necessary)
- Bedding or blinding material placement and compaction
- Trough sections assembly (pre-cast) or construction (poured in situ)
- HV cables installation into trough (generally pulled on rollers)
- HV cables cleating (if required)
- Fiber optic cables placement in the trough (if any)
- Controlled backfilling around the cables in the trough (if necessary)
- Trough covers assembly or construction
- Backfill and warning tapes placement and their compaction above the trough
- Top layer reinstatement (top soil, road pavement, etc...)

Application

This technique is a “main” installation technique as opposed to a special crossing technique which is
designed to avoid obstacles. This technique is typically used within secure locations such as the
perimeter of substations or generating stations.

Some electricity companies are also using troughs into sandy areas, along railways or highways or into
tunnels where the public access is normally restricted.

BURIED TROUGHS

```
```

```

ground after their useful life.

(lockable lids could be used to prevent these
actions)
```

after their useful life
```

direct sunlight exposure
```
```

in areas with freezing temperatures
```

### 3.4.1.1 Civil work description

A trough system is most often a prefabricated concrete U-shaped (culvert) with a concrete cover or lid
and is intended to protect the installed cables from mechanical damage. More recently, the use of
polymer-based trough material has become more popular, particularly within rail corridors and generally
are smaller in section than their concrete equivalent.

The trough may either be a continuous cast in situ construction or a series of single elements composed
of precast sections of up to 2.5 meters in length installed serially. Covers, composed of concrete, steel
or fiberglass reinforced plastic (FRP) are installed over the “U-shape” sides, providing mechanical top
protection for the cables installed inside.

The troughs may be backfilled with a thermally stable backfill (generally flowable) or other thermally
stable material requiring compaction that allows the cables to effectively dissipate heat and hence
perform at their optimal capability. Alternatively, the trough may have no backfill and if it houses power
cables, the cables are suitably secured with cleats at selected intervals.

Generally, if the troughs are used to house cables other than power cables (such as control and
communications cables) no backfill material or cable fixing is used.

In situations where the troughs are being installed in ground, once a long enough trough path has been
established, the cables may be installed as in an open trench using rollers suitably spaced and pulling
the cable between joint bays or from a joint bay to a terminal point. The covers are then placed once
backfilling or cleating of the cables has been completed (where appropriate).

The factors that determine the internal dimensions of the trough are as follows:

- Width sufficient to cater for the required phase spacing and cleats;
- Ensure there is sufficient spacing between the outer phases and the internal walls of the trough
    (usually one-half cable diameter) to ensure that the thermal backfill can be effectively installed
    between the cable and the walls of the trough;
- Be of sufficient width to allow for installation of the cable rollers in the through;
- Be of sufficient width to allow for cable snaking if required.

### 3.4.1.2 Buried troughs

For buried troughs, civil work includes the excavation of a trench and shoring when required by ground
conditions and local regulations.

The troughs generally made of reinforced concrete are then installed in the open trench.

After installation of the cable and sealing of the trough, the trench is backfilled, and layers of backfill
material (possibly the native soil) are compacted (where required). Care should be taken to leave no
voids between the outer surface of the trough and the trench wall. Limits are the same as for directly
buried cables with an additional limit concerning the trough bending radius which is generally 70 times
the cable outside diameter.

Additional civil works may be required if the excavated ground is unstable in any way. The installation
of a blinding layer of concrete, stabilized sand or road base may be necessary to provide stability.

From a thermal point of view, the buried troughs method is better than direct buried method because of
the improved heat flow provided by the use of concrete trough. Troughs material and backfilling
materials inside and outside of the troughs can be selected to take into account the thermal resistivity
of the native soil.


## Figure 33 - Buried troughs

### 3.4.1.3 Surface troughs

Surface troughs are defined as troughs installed at ground level or slightly recessed to provide stability
without any backfilling. They can be backfilled or not depending on the ampacity requirements. They
are typically made of concrete either precast or cast in situ.

They are often installed on a stabilized sand or crushed road base bedding (blinding) when slightly
recessed to ensure that one section of the trough does not subside in relation to the adjacent unit. Again,
the cables are installed in the trough in the same manner as for the direct buried troughs.

Where the troughs are filled with a thermally stable material having no cementitious component, there
is a possibility that cable movement in the vertical plane may take place, resulting, in the lifting of the
trough lids. In such cases care should be exercised to ensure that the lids are either heavy enough, or
fixed to the trough, to resist such forces.

In those installations where the troughs are unfilled, it is likely that the cables will need to be snaked
(generally in the horizontal plane) and cleated to cater for thermomechanical effects. The
recommendations of TB 669 should be followed in such cases.

This kind of trough in commonly used for low or medium voltage, control or telecommunications cables
in substations and occasionally for HV cables where the utility is the owner.


## Figure 34 - Surface troughs

### 3.4.1.4 Unfilled troughs in air

Troughs in air may be made of metal or composite materials such as “Fiber Reinforced Plastic” (FRP)
and are designed to provide additional protection to the cables both mechanically and with respect to
fire propagation.

They are typically installed into tunnels where the use of other techniques is too difficult due to space
constraints.

Exceptionally, they are also used on land, in remote areas where the access is difficult and the risk of
vandalism is low.Metallic structures (generally non-magnetic) are normally used to support this type of
trough.


## Figure 35 - Unfilled trough in air

### 3.4.1.5 Rigid or flexible type trough

Cable installation using troughs is classified with regard to installation method, either;

- Rigid type (where the troughs are filled or cleated); or
- Flexible type (where the cables within the troughs are in air)

Rigid type

The bottom of the trough is filled with a layer of compacted thermally stable sand or stabilized sand
before laying the cable. If the cables are in trefoil, then after laying the lower cables, the trough is partially
backfilled to the top of the installed cables to eliminate any voids and in preparation of the final cable to
be installed.

After installation of all three cables, the trough backfill is completed using a thermally stable material.
Finally, the protective covers are installed, sealed and fixed, if required.

Flexible type

When single core extruded cable is installed in the straight line in an unfilled surface trough or in unfilled
trough in air, irregular thermal cable movement occurs due to longitudinal thermal expansion. To
overcome these forces, “snaking” of the cable is required.

Snaked installations are where the cables are laid in waves at a certain frequency and amplitude to
assist in the absorption of the thermal cycling of the cable, which leads to expansion and contraction.
The physical dimensions of the snaking, which by definition takes place in the horizontal plane for trough
installations, is determined by considering the cable occupied space, the axial forces at the end of the
snaked section, workability of the snaking and a number of other factors that are addressed in TB 669
(WG B1.34). Short circuit straps are also sometimes applied at intermediate locations between the
clamps in these installations to absorb electrodynamic forces during short circuit events.


### 3.4.1.6 Duration of civil works

3.4.1.6.1 Buried trough

The duration of the civil works will depend on the depth of the trench, the size of the trough and the
number of obstacles that will be encountered and could reach several tens to a few hundred meters per
day.

With this technique, the civil works cannot be carried out separately (weeks apart) from the cable laying
works due to the fact that the trench/trough cannot be backfilled before the cable pulling is completed.
If the trench/trough were to remain open for extended periods, it would cause significant disruption to
vehicular and pedestrian traffic, present a safety risk to the wider community and it could get damaged
by adverse weather conditions or land subsidence.

Furthermore, if this technique were used in urban situations, opening trenches hundreds of metres long
may give rise to problems. Accordingly, local authorities may restrict the maximum length of open
trenches as well as the daily working hours according to job locations, days of the week, etc.

Additionally, when a trench is dug to cross a road, it must be temporarily covered with steel plates in
order to reduce the impact on traffic.

3.4.1.6.2 Surface or above ground trough

By definition, this kind of trough will be used within secured locations. As a result, the impact of this type
of installation will be limited as they are either located into substations or in the right of ways of railroads
and highways.

This kind of trough is much quicker to install (compared to buried troughs) because they do not require
the digging of deep and long trenches waiting for cables to be pulled in.

This type of trough installation will still require some digging either to house the surface trough or for the
foundations of the surface troughs. In both cases, some digging may also be required for drainage
purposes.

On average, the duration of the civil works can vary from tens of meters per day (when installed along
right of ways) up to hundreds of meters (when installed in a less restricted area such as a substation).

### 3.4.1.7 Cable Route selection

3.4.1.7.1 Buried trough

Due to the nature of the installation, the route of an in-ground trough installation needs to be flat and
shallow. The route also needs to be ostensibly straight with any changes of direction being achieved
using a large radius. Similarly, it is important to be aware of any service crossings at an early design
stage as crossing under services takes a significant amount of planning to allow the system to ramp
down or up. Installation of buried troughs could also quarantine access to any services crossing
underneath.

3.4.1.7.2 Surface or above ground trough

As far as surface and above ground trough are concerned, the cable route should be based on the
following factors:

- Space for cables with sufficient side clearance to other services or buildings
- Availability of permanent space for joint bays and temporary space for the cable laying equipment
    and cranes
- Making the route as short as possible to reduce costs
- Reliable conditions (low risk of third-party aggression, low flood risk, etc.)

It is highly recommended that all potential obstacles along the cable route be identified and documented
at the design stage. With this information in hand, the trough route designer will try to avoid obstacles,
as far as possible, adjusting the route accordingly. Exploratory pits may also be required to check for
existing services when the information is not available from services data bases.


### 3.4.2 Limits of the technique

For matters of clarity and completeness, the texts have been repeated from other installation modes
when they apply and adapted to “trough” installation mode as required.

### 3.4.3 Drying of the soil

“Trough” is not the most sensitive installation mode with regards to soil drying because the cables are
not in direct contact with the ground. They are contained within concrete housings which are often filled
with low thermal resistivity materials.

This assembly helps carrying the heat away from the cables to the outer surface of the “trough”. It is still
required, however, that the soil surrounding the trough carries this heat further away from the cables
but it is not as acute as for direct burial cables.

It is to be noted, however, that the use of troughs will reduce the current “rating” of the cables compared
to a “direct burial” arrangement when the trough is not backfilled, because of the higher thermal
resistance of the air trapped around the cables.

On the other hand, the current rating of the cables will be similar or even higher when the trough is filled
and it will be less susceptible to soil dry out. This is due to the fact that the trough concrete is a better
heat conductor and enables a better heat flow away from the cables.

Soil drying can be attributed to natural processes as well as by induced changes. The rate of drying
depends on the location, precipitation, soil type, natural water table, air temperature, surface
temperature, wind speed, type of cover (grass, asphalt, concrete), vegetation (moisture depletion by
trees), etc. For the underground power cable environment, the heat generated by the energized cable
(W/m) drives the moisture away at the cable/soil interface, which has a significant impact on the thermal
resistivity and ultimately, the cable rating.

In certain regions, soils may be naturally dry. Various solutions can be considered:

- Use of better controlled backfilling material
- The use of additional controlled fill around the cables
- Use of bigger installation depths in order to reach more thermally stable soils
- Use of a flat formation instead of trefoil formation to distribute the heat over a larger area and thus
    improve the rate of heat dissipation of each cable
- Install fiber optic cables either attached to the power cable jacket or embedded inside the power
    cables for use as part of a Distributed Temperature Sensor (DTS) system in order to detect hot
    spots before any irreversible damage to the cable insulation occurs

### 3.4.4 Water drainage

Conversely to the soil-drying phenomenon, wet soil may seem more favourable to laying of HV cables
because it constitutes a natural cooling system. This is partly true. However, in those cases, trenches
cannot be backfilled as effectively with thermal backfill.

This is of concern if the soil or the backfill material is loose (granular with high voids content, poor
compaction). It should be addressed during the construction phase if it affects the stability of the soil or
the backfill around the troughs. The execution of trenches in wet soil often requires means of drainage
(pumps, etc.) in order to avoid erosion of the trench walls and to enable proper trough laying and
controlled backfilling.

Washout of fines and in backfill material adversely affecting the stability and thermal resistivity has been
well documented.

Consideration also needs to be given to the drainage of any unfilled troughs, the impact that a buried
trough may have on the surrounding water courses and the natural drainage of the immediate area. It
may be necessary to install subsurface drainage systems consisting of corrugated and slotted plastic
pipes or gravel drains.

### 3.4.5 Temperature of the soil/environment

At a given location the temperature of the sub-surface soil is a function of the latitude, depth (cables or
duct-bank), time of the year and surface cover (grass, asphalt, concrete). Natural changes in


temperature follows a sinusoidal curve and is fairly constant at a depth of about 10m. Local
environmental and meteorological agencies as well as airport authorities are good sources of
temperature records; especially for depths up to 2m. Agronomy records in general provide temperature
and moisture data for shallow depth of less than 1m. For cable rating calculations, the ground
temperature at the burial depth must be taken into consideration.

Summer conditions are naturally the hottest conditions of the year.

A thermocouple (T/C) tree with several T/Cs can be installed at various locations and depths such as
‘hot-spots’ and monitored over several cycles to obtain the true temperature values and their changes
as function of depth and time of the year. Alternatively, as stated before, fiber optic cables and
Distributed Temperature Sensing (DTS) systems can be used to monitor temperatures and better
understand the effect of ground temperatures.

### 3.4.6 Hardness of the soil

Hard soil, hardpan and bedrock can pose difficulty, delays and extra costs for excavation and
construction.

It is therefore highly advisable to conduct a geotechnical survey along the cable route. The borehole
