logs or the test-pit logs can give a good indication of the soil stiffness/hardness and depth of bedrock (if
encountered). Geotechnical tests are conducted in-situ and/or in the laboratory on samples collected
during investigation.

If excavation into rocky soils cannot be avoided, project schedule and costs must be adjusted
accordingly.

### 3.4.7 Stability of the soil

Soil types that are non-cohesive, sandy and gravelly and soil conditions that are very soft and sensitive
have poor mechanical stability and can pose problems during excavation; especially at locations with a
high-water table.

Stability must then be addressed during the construction phase by de-watering, trench shoring, over
excavation, mechanically or dynamically compacting and grout injection techniques.

Stability must be achieved for the successful operation of the work as well as for the safety of the
workers.

If the soil is not stable, it may be necessary to anchor surface troughs on a concrete foundation.

Designs should also take into consideration factors such as ground stability and impacts of unstable
ground and seismic events.

### 3.4.8 Thermal resistivity of the soil

For cable rating (ampacity) calculations, the three most important parameters are:

- The ambient temperature at the cable burial depth
- The earth surface temperature and
- The thermal resistivity of the soil/backfill around the ducts up to the ground surface

Soil gives the largest single thermal resistance and thermal capacitance of an underground power cable
system. It has the greatest variability with distance along the cable route and with time. The thermal
resistivity can easily vary threefold or more on a specific circuit, and the cable must be rated for the
worst-case condition. Better understanding of the physical and thermal properties of soils and backfills,
and the use of controlled backfills can result in significant increase in ampacity.

Thermal resistivity depends on the soil (or backfill) type, water content, density and various other factors
to a lesser degree.

Typical TR values are suggested in IEC 60287- 3 - 1 standard for various countries; however, these
remain approximate and are generalisations. This approach could advantageously be replaced by a
cable route thermal survey where in-situ and laboratory thermal tests are carried out at various times of
year to understand the thermal behavior of a particular soil and design the backfill material accordingly.

Trough material and backfilling inside and outside the troughs should then be selected to take account
of the local soil conditions.


### 3.4.9 Seismicity

“Trough” is not the most sensitive installation mode with regards to seismicity because the cables are
not in direct contact with the ground. They are contained within the trough, covered and backfilled with
a homogeneous thermal backfill up to the ground surface.

Nevertheless, troughs could potentially be subjected to high stresses generated by soil movements.
These stresses will even be amplified in the case of an above ground system.

When compared to the direct burial technique, measures can be taken with the trough installation to
help withstand seismic stresses, such as flexible expansion joints between trough sections and free
cable loops in the joint bays. These elements will absorb the stresses of the earthquake while protecting
the cables.

### 3.4.10 Frost (temperature limits, provisions for installation in cold environments)

Carrying out civil works in frosty environments (winter conditions) remains possible. The work will be
more demanding on the workers due to the lower temperatures as well as on the machinery due to the
increased hardness of the ground. The increased hardness of the ground due to icy conditions is still a
lesser impediment to excavation than the one of encountering rock.

As far as cable pulling is concerned, cable manufacturers do not normally recommend pulling HV cables
below 5°C temperatures without cable pre-warming. This is because the cables get stiffer and lose
flexibility and this could lead to higher pulling tensions and stability problems with the cable head.

The pre-warming procedure will consist of either storing the cable reel indoors in a heated room near
the pulling site or installing a heating shelter around the cable reel at the site and maintaining the air
temperature within the shelter at 10°C or more for at least 24 hours prior to pulling.

The freezing of the soil itself does not constitute a risk for the cables providing the following criteria are
met:

- The cable trench must be adequately drained in order to avoid water freezing and causing
    differential uplift around the trough
- If possible, the trench bottom must not be too shallow (below freezing line) in order to avoid
    differential ground movements during the thaw period, especially near fixed structures such as joint
    bays and terminations foundations. It is to be noted however, that no such events have not been
    reported

Troughs remain a very robust solution against differential ground movements due to frost (in a manner
similar to earthquake stresses) because they form a very compact assembly into which the cables
remain partially free to move.

### 3.4.11 Archaeology

If the cable route crosses a potential archaeological area, it must be borne in mind, that due to local and
environmental authorities, any finds made during excavation will make it mandatory to stop the work, at
least in the area where the find has been made.

Work at the site is then suspended until the archaeologists have investigated the find. The length of the
portion affected, and the duration of the stoppage will depend on what further discoveries are made.

Another way of coping with this issue is to investigate the archaeology potential along the cable route
at the design stage. If the potential is sufficiently high, an alternate route can be found. If not, a daily
surveillance by an archeological expert could be carried out during the civil work and any findings can
be examined and extracted as they are encountered in collaboration with the civil contractor. The most
extensive archeological investigation work can even be planned (ahead of time) to take place after the
working hours of the civil contractors.

Another solution could be to construct an HDD below the archaeological area. However, this solution
may not always be technically feasible and would have a strong impact on the cost, schedule and cable
rating of the project.


### 3.4.12 Presence of rodents and termites

Cables installed in open troughs can potentially be affected by rodents and termites. Cables should then
be designed with an anti-termite protection (see direct burial section) and the trough should be properly
sealed to prevent their entry.

### 3.4.13 Laying in national park

Open trench work may not be permitted in some national parks due to the potential impact on fauna and
flora. When the cable route cannot avoid such areas, other techniques such as horizontal directional
drilling (HDD) may be used. However, as explained earlier, such a technique could have a strong impact
on the cost, schedule and cable rating of the project.

If open trench work is allowed, the trench would be backfilled with an engineered material and any
compaction would be a function of the TR requirements and hence ratings. It is more likely that the last
200mm of the trench backfill would use native material and the area restored to the original condition.
The planting of large trees would not be permitted close to the trench alignment.

Laying the trough in already disturbed areas such as road or road shoulders could be the best approach
for such locations.

### 3.4.14 Land registration

The rights of way are usually settled by joint agreement between the utility and public authorities
(municipalities, highways, railways, bridges, etc.) or between the utility and a private owner.

When using public land, the construction techniques are normally agreed to between the various
stakeholders. The responsibility for carrying out various aspects of the work (whether it is the utility or
the public authority) must also be identified.

The work carried out in public land does not generally lead to compensation being paid. However, in the
case of a private owner, compensation could be agreed according to the width of the right of way. In
some countries, such compensation is based on the actual width of the trench multiplied by a factor.

Compensation may also be paid for the use of temporary additional width for the work and/or access to
the work area. Permanent construction or stockpiling of any material will normally not be allowed above
or adjacent to the troughs on this right of ways.

### 3.4.15 Environmental impact

With the “Trough” method, natural environments may be substantially altered when compared with
“trenchless” techniques (horizontal drilling, tunnel “boring”, etc).

It is important not to underestimate the environmental impact and local environmental laws during the
construction itself, and therefore it is wise to consider these factors at the preliminary study stage, before
it becomes an issue for the environment and general population.

The objective is to mitigate the impacts and restore as far as possible the original environment. The
impacts related to the construction and operation of a power link, can be classified as follows:

Soil

- Direct damage due to the excavation (material displacement)
- Possible contamination of soils due to excavated materials being mixed with surrounding land
    materials.

Water

- Possible contamination of water ecosystem due to excavated or backfilled materials being
    displaced/lost in water bodies.
- Possible diversion of natural water courses

Air

- Release of pollutants to atmosphere during the construction work
- Noise generated by machinery during the work

Flora


- Direct destruction of the vegetation cover
- Indirect destruction of the nearby plant communities
- Damage to unique or interesting species

Fauna (embedding)

- Direct or indirect disturbance of natural fauna habitat
- Disturbance of natural pathways for movement of animals

Socio-economic aspects

- Difficulties caused to parking and access to homes, shops, businesses etc. during working hours
- Temporary effects on tourism trade during the construction work
- Rights of way affected during the construction work

Landscape

- Alteration of the landscape during the construction phase.
- Most of these disturbances can be prevented or mitigated at the design stage. Most of the major
    impacts arise during the construction phase and can be mitigated by informing and consulting with
    the local authorities, the community, and other stakeholders. An example of mitigation measures
    would include the use of silenced equipment and working at times that have less community impact.

### 3.4.16 Safety.................................................................................................................................................

### 3.4.16.1 During Construction

During excavation, trough installation, cable laying and jointing operations, it is vital that considerable
attention is paid to the health and safety of the workers and the public. Among the items that should be
considered are:

- Risks to other electrical services
- Risks to other types of services such as gas, water, sewers, low and medium voltage cable etc.
- Ground subsidence
- Prevent slips, trips and falls into the trench
- Safeguard workers from induced voltages from other circuits
- Traffic management issues

The jointing process normally takes place in formed joint bays. Such structures are normally open (no
cover/roof during the work) and may also constitute a risk. The provision of a controlled environment
with effective means of egress is highly recommended at Transmission voltages.

### 3.4.16.2 During Operation

Owners of underground infrastructures normally offer a free localization service to all companies
intending to carry excavation work. Such service is aimed at damage prevention to underground
infrastructures or other structures.

Visible markers can also be used to help reduce the risks. These could be located in the pavement, on
the kerb and gutters or beside the road with an indicated offset.

Buried devices such as warning tapes, or nets are also generally placed above the cables to help
reducing the risk of damage.

Sidewall protection is not compulsory because of the cable’s depth. For joint bays, a brick or concrete
enclosure will normally protect the joints from side intrusion. Such protection may be included in
situations of reduced cover.


### 3.4.17 Legal requirements

When cable routes are located in public lands, there is normally no acquisition cost for the easement,
however this may depend on the jurisdiction. It also eases the approval process because projects are
submitted only to the public authorities which are normally favorable to these kind of projects as it
answers to the needs of many. Location of installations in parklands etc., also reduces the impact on
traffic and eliminates the need for road restorations, which is often seen as a positive thing by road asset
owners.

Open trench work for trough installation will cause some disturbances to the population because the
entire length of the trench is open until the troughs and cable are installed. The severity of the impact
will depend on the location of the project and the urbanisation level.

When the cable route runs on private properties, utilities must liaise with all of the affected stakeholders
and undertake all necessary mitigation measures. Several years may be required to reach agreement
with all the affected parties. In the absence of an agreement, some utilities have the right to appropriate
or “resume” the property providing financial compensation.

### 3.4.18 Maintenance and repair processes

Once the cable link is commissioned, it will be accessible for maintenance at the ends (terminations into
substations) and near the joints through handholes next to every joint bay.

The maintenance work will consist of performing some or all the tests listed below in a periodic manner
typically ranging from annually to periods of up to several years as recommended by the cable
manufacturer and according to the owner’s experience:

- Jacket tests (integrity tests on the outer layer of the cables)
- On-line or off-line Partial discharge tests (quality tests on cable and accessories insulation)
- High voltage tests (integrity of main insulation)
- Checks on sheath voltage limiters (SVL’s)

In the case of damage caused by a third party, which may lead to a cable fault, excavation work will be
required to open the trough where the cables have been damaged. A bigger trench will then be required
for the installation of a repair joint bay which will possibly have to include the remaining sound cables
and be of sufficient size for the damaged cable to be repaired. Typically, a new piece of cable and 2
repair joints will have to be installed in the bay beside the other cables.

After the repair, the cables will be backfilled or cleated, and the joint bay secured, following satisfactory
electrical testing.

Several weeks could be required to complete this task once the damaged area is located.

### 3.4.19 Commissioning and de-commissioning..............................................................................................

Before the cable circuit is commissioned and connected to the power transmission system, it has to
undergo a series of tests to confirm its suitability for connection to the Network.

The tests vary across the industry, but generally include HV tests on the primary insulation and on the
cable outer sheath. Further tests are carried out such as phasing confirmation, insulation resistance (IR
tests), conductor resistance, capacitance, bonding and earthing tests.

The use of AC or DC test sources for testing the primary insulation is subjected to the type of electrical
system (HVAC or HVDC) and the type of cable insulating material.
Please refer to TB 841“After laying tests on AC and DC cable systems with new technologies”.

With the availability of mobile on-site AC test sets, of the resonant frequency type, there is a general
move to testing extruded cable on site by AC voltage only. These systems generally referred to as
Resonant Test Sets (RTS). IEC standard states a minimum range frequency of 20 Hz and it generally
prevails. However though, in specific cases and particularly long cable system testing a frequency down
to 10 Hz could be considered as indicated in CIGRE TB 841.


Very low frequency test systems such as 0.1 Hz have also been developed having advantages such as
reduced equipment complication, less cost and greater portability. However, the voltage levels must be
adjusted (raised) to compensate for the lower frequencies used.

Further developments in partial discharge testing have shown that the combination of a PD test with a
high voltage AC test is the most reliable means of confirming the integrity of extruded cable systems
prior to connection to the power transmission grid.

Cable removal at the end of life depends on the cable installation mode, i.e. if the trough is filled or if it
is not filled.

In the first case, cable removal requires to remove firstly the filling compound (typically cement mortar
or sand).

In the second case, cable removal requires to disconnect all fittings (cable cleats).

In the case of fluid filled cables, the cables would initially have their insulating fluid removed before they
are removed, in order to minimize the risks of fluid loss.

Where existing asbestos structures need to be removed (partially or totally), please refer to local laws
and restriction for dismantling of the structure.

### 3.4.20 Specific monitoring

Various monitoring methods are used with the following high voltage cables types.

In the case of self-contained fluid filled (SCFF) cables, the pressure of the fluid is constantly monitored
at the ends of hydraulic sections and oil feeding points that constitute the circuit. An abnormal loss of
pressure will trigger an alarm as it could mean a leak of fluid into the environment. The operator will
react quickly in order to locate and fix the leak. This monitoring has to do with the type of cable and is
not specific to direct buried circuits. However, with such circuits, the risk of a spill in the environment is
probably higher because the leak is more difficult to find compared to other installation modes. In some
countries, tracers are used and can make the location of the leak much easier as they can be detected
from the surface with specialized sensors.

With extruded cables, there is no need for such a pressure monitoring as the cables do not contain fluid.

Distributed Temperature Sensing (DTS) is a technique that could be used to make sure the cables
perform as planned and can therefore safely carry their full load. DTS systems use fiber optic cables
installed along the power cables (in the metallic screen or as a separate cable) to accurately monitor
their heating at every meter along the route. This tool, coupled with a Real Time Thermal Rating (RTTR)
software, will inform the operator about real time heating and also about possible temporary overload
schemes.

This kind of monitoring could prove useful in avoiding cable overheating and failures that could occur
when soils, in contact with the trough, are subject to drying out.

Distributed Acoustic Sensing (DAS) is a technique which also uses fiber optic cables to monitor the
noise around the cables at every meter along the route. It can prove useful in detecting and pinpointing
situations such as cable failures, unauthorized excavation work, traffic, etc...


## 3.5 Tunnels / Culverts....................................................................................................................................

### 3.5.1 Description of the technique

The tunneling technique is normally used when one or all of the following conditions are encountered:

- There are a significant number of cables required within a relatively confined area
- The number of circuits required need to have a high rating that cannot be achieved by having a
    large number of cables in close proximity, as they would be in a duct bank or direct laid situation
    (electrical requirement)
- Where the civil requirements of constructing duct-lines through urban environments using traditional
    techniques becomes cost prohibitive (civil requirement)
- The selected route for the cable is already too congested with other services; and
- The construction of a duct bank is not feasible due to the location, resulting in unacceptable traffic
    congestion and general public disruptions

Where a tunnel option is adopted, generally the selection of the preferred tunneling technique is based
on a combined assessment of ground conditions, electrical requirements, required tunnel profile and
the length of the required cable route.

Since the tunnel construction method is much more expensive than construction of ducts by open cut
method, it is necessary to carefully evaluate the construction cost. Construction of a tunnel is
economically unfavorable when there are only a few circuits to be installed.

Tunnels can be constructed through virtually any type of ground condition, so in instances where it has
been assessed that the required cable route cannot feasibly be constructed using traditional techniques
(direct buried, pit and duct etc.), the key consideration then becomes a combined assessment of what
type of tunnel will meet the electrical requirements of the cables and which technique should be utilized
given budget constraints.

Budget assessments should always include allowances for future maintenance activities, given that
these costs can differ considerably, depending on the tunneling technique chosen

The early use of power cable tunnels to house cables over short distances, for example, beneath rivers
and railways, has largely been replaced by alternative and less expensive techniques such as horizontal
directional drilling or pipe ramming and micro tunneling techniques (reference TB 770). These
alternative methods, although less expensive and generally requiring less maintenance than a tunnel
accessible by personnel, have practical restrictions on length, tunnel cross section and cable heat
dissipation (necessary for power transmission). For long circuit lengths, tunnel cross sections which
provide tunnel accessibility to personnel and space to accommodate in-situ joints require differing
installation techniques.

By installing cables in long tunnels deep below the surface a number of advantages are achieved
compared to traditional methods, especially when utilised in densely urban areas:

- Avoidance of major disruption to the road network
- Overall disruption to public and road users during construction is significantly reduced as the
    majority of the works take place under ground
- Future repair and maintenance work can be carried out without disrupting traffic, businesses and
    residents
- Possibility for cables can be installed in the tunnels to meet future demand; and
- Secure operating environment with respect to third party damages

Tunneling in its various forms has been used to cross busy transport corridors such as rail tracks and
motorways, beneath rivers or other natural obstructions such as mountainous terrain. Tunnels can also
provide a secure cable environment and influence an investment decision where this is of particular
concern. Due to the cost of tunnel construction, a new cable tunnel is normally considered only as a last
resort where alternatives are either unavailable or unsuitable.

Tunnel designs and construction techniques are defined by such factors as the prevailing ground
conditions, cable system needs, safety, geology, hydrology, cost, constructability, access and the


presence of other underground services. Environmental factors will also have an influence including
construction noise, vibration, pollution, light and dust creation.

The length and cross section of an accessible cable tunnel is determined by:

- The number of power conductors to be accommodated, their mechanical supports, and ancillary
    cabling
- The selection and requirements of the cable cooling system to be used
- Tunnel transport system requirements during construction and operation
- Foreseeable upgrade requirements; and
- The investment, engineering and safety limitations

The expected life of the main structure (100 years or more) is expected to exceed the service lifespan
of the cables (40 years) and thus cable system replacement should be considered in the design.

The construction of a new tunnel to contain power cables is a significant investment and is much more
expensive than the construction of ducts by the open cut method, so it is necessary to carefully evaluate
the construction cost. Construction of a tunnel is less cost effective when there are only a few circuits to
be installed.

If an existing tunnel, which can contain cables, has a useful alignment then the cost of installing cables
within such a tunnel may offer an attractive cable route option. The use of a multipurpose tunnel can be
expected give rise to additional complexity, particularly if it will contain services having differing and
possibly conflicting requirements (such as inductive coupling effects on metallic telecommunications
cable). TB 403 is dedicated to the discussion of cable systems in multi purpose or shared structures
and is an additional CIGRE reference for the reader on this topic.

### 3.5.2 Tunnel Types

The most common techniques used are;

- Open Cut (cut and cover type)
- Immersed Tube Method
- Pipe Jacking
- Tunnel boring machine (TBM)
- Road Header Excavation; and
- Soft Ground Tunnel Boring Machine (Earth Pressure Balance Machine)


## Figure 36 - “Open cut” tunnel technique

## Figure 37 - “Shielded” tunnel technique

### 3.5.2.1 Open cut method

Shallow tunnel designs using open cut and cover techniques are available for installations where a
surface excavated trench is feasible to construct the tunnel. This technique usually involves the


installation of culvert sections, one in a “U” installation in the trench followed by the application of an
inverted section on top of this, resulting in a rectangular tunnel section.

Short lengths of precast tunnel section may be lowered onto a prepared base at the bottom of the trench.
These short lengths are sealed together to form a continuous tunnel. It is possible to join several tunnels
together to form an underground tunnel network.

In cases where the ground is unstable the sides of a tunnel may be formed by the driving of piles. A row
of piles are installed either side of the tunnel line and the ground between the piles removed to allow
tunnel installation. The piles installation may also be dressed to form the tunnel walls. Following
construction of the tunnel the tunnel trench is filled.

## Figure 38 - Cut and cover tunnel

### 3.5.2.2 Immersed Tube Method

Immersed tube tunnels are used on harbour crossings for road and rail transport. An underwater trench
is dredged from the surface to accommodate the tunnel. The bottom of the trench is made level and if
necessary a gravel base installed to assist with positioning of the immersed tunnel section.

Lengths of precast concrete tunnel are fabricated inside a flood-able construction basin which, when
flooded, permits the entry of tugs for the removal and transport of the tunnel sections. The length of the
prefabricated section is determined by the fabrication basin capacity, transport, handling, access,
pressure resistance and buoyancy requirements of the prefabricated section. Each precast section may
itself consist of several subsections which are cast and sealed together to make one complete
prefabricated section. Each tunnel section is fitted with water tight bulkheads before the construction
basin is flooded and the tunnel sections floated. The buoyancy of the tunnel sections is adjusted by
internal ballast tanks to permit safe transportation of the concrete section to the tunnel location.

When in position and attached to adjustable length mooring wires the tunnel section is made negatively
buoyant and slowly immersed into the pre-excavated tunnel trench. The newly installed terminal tunnel
section is then placed against the previously installed tunnel section. The water between the bulkheads


of the touching tunnel sections is evacuated and the water pressure outside of the new terminal section
forces closure of the compressible gasket (GINA gasket) seal between the adjoining tunnel sections.
This provides a water tight tunnel seal. Rock is placed above the tunnel and in the gap between the
trench wall and the tunnel installed by rock placement vessels. The rock is provided to both secure the
tunnel sections in place and provide mechanical protection. The tunnel cross-section can consist of
several longitudinal compartments designed to contain road, rail, utility and emergency escape services.
Please refer to TB 403 “Cable systems in multi purpose or shared structures” Chapter 2.1.1 for more
details.

### 3.5.2.3 Pipe Jacking

Pipe jacking (or shield method) is another tunneling method typically used in softer ground conditions
and where the tunnel length is relatively short (generally less than 2000 metres). While this is a
considerable length, it is more likely to have restricted length due to other issues, such as cable length,
available space for joints and personnel entry and egress.

Pipe jacking using shielded machines is usually adopted in situations where open cut methods cannot
be adopted due to existing civil constraints. For example, pipe jacking under a railway line in situations
where trains are still required to run; in which case open cut methods would not be suitable.

It is first necessary to construct vertically shafts for the sending and receiving of the boring equipment.
Pipe jacked tunnels can be up 3 metres in diameter.

## Figure 39 - “Pipe jacking” tunnel profile (six cable circuit)

### 3.5.2.4 Traditional Tunnel Boring Machine (TBM)

There are essentially two types of TBM open-face and closed-face. Open-face machines may be used
in stable non-water-bearing ground such as firm clays. They comprise a cylindrical steel shell, with either
a mechanical excavator arm or a rotating cutter head at the front, and various hydraulic rams at the rear.


Some rams push the TBM forward, whilst others erect the precast concrete segmental tunnel lining
behind the machine. Closed face machines are known as earth pressure balance machines (EPBM).

Prior to the tunnel boring, the parts of the TBM are transferred underground at the TBM launch shaft.
The machine is assembled within the launch shaft. As it bores, the TBM and its operators continually
install the precast segmental lining to make a permanent tunnel wall. At the same time, excavated
material is transported to the back of the machine along the lined tunnel to the launch shaft and raised
to the ground surface for disposal or reuse. Upon completion of tunnel construction, the TBM may be
disassembled and removed from a retrieval shaft at the tunnel’s end. The TBM may then be maintained
at the surface and made ready for re-use.

Traditional tunnel boring machines (TBM’s) are usually used for tunneling through self-supporting hard
rock, where a standard circular tunnel profile is required, and for typically longer tunnel routes. The
equipment consists of a large rotating cutting head at the front of the shield and is equipped with
hardened teeth which can drive a tunnel through soil, rock or a mixture of both. This multi functional
construction tool can excavate and remove excavated materials, and at the same time install the
permanent reinforced concrete lining of the tunnel as it progresses.

Generally, the tunnel is lined with pre-cast concrete segments, which are installed immediately behind
the tunnel boring machine, as it proceeds with excavation. The selected precast lining design comes
from a combined consideration of essential structural requirements and desired waterproofing
outcomes. TBM tunnels for electrical tunnels are typically in the range of between 3 and up to 16 metres
in diameter, although larger equipment is available for other purposes. The equipment is often
articulated to construct large radius curves along the tunnel route. To construct such a tunnel, it is
necessary to first construct a shaft, generally vertically down to the desired level. This shaft, typically
around 15 metres in diameter, is excavated at the drive site in order to set the depth of the tunnel. A
tunnel boring machine (TBM) is then lowered down the shaft and starts tunneling along a pre-determined
route. Rates can average between 50-120 metres per week. The TBM terminates at a reception site
where another shaft is sunk and the TBM may be removed.


## Figure 40 - Typical TBM tunnel profile (segmental lining)

Intermediate shafts with head houses are required at key points along the route of the tunnel. The TBM
will link these points as it progresses along the routes. The shafts and head houses are needed for the
following reasons:

- It is essential to have access and egress points to ensure the safety of the workforce during
    construction and operation
- The shafts are used to access the tunnel for maintenance work once the tunnel is commissioned
- When used, ventilation fans remove stale air, cool the cables and joints, and regulate the
    temperature inside the tunnel; and
- To provide cable access points to intermediate substations or other points of network connection

### 3.5.2.5 Road Header Excavation

Generally, road headers are used to excavate through hard rock. For electrical transmission purposes,
the road header technique is normally chosen where varying or non-standard tunnel profiles are required
to account for systems such as ventilation caverns, drainage sumps, other services, etc.

Usually, the tunnel profile consists of vertical tunnel walls, with a curved roof. After excavation, road
header tunnels are usually lined with reinforced shotcrete or a cast-in-situ concrete lining. The lining
selected is a combined consideration of essential structural requirements and desired waterproofing
outcomes.

## Figure 41 - Typical road header tunnel profile.....................................................................................

### 3.5.2.6 Soft Ground Tunnel Boring Machine (Earth Pressure Balance Machine)

An Earth Pressure Balance TBM achieves the same end result as a traditional TBM but is used for
tunneling through softer ground conditions (usually soil and clay), as opposed to rock and is often
referred to as a closed face type machine. The TBM is designed to support the weaker soft ground


conditions, using earth pressure balance techniques, until the concrete lining segments (whether
segments or pipes) can be installed behind the TBM. Additives are used to turn the excavated material
into a soil paste that is used a pliable plastic support medium and allows the removal of the spoil from
the head of the equipment. As above, the selected precast lining design comes from a combined
consideration of essential structural requirements and desired waterproofing outcomes.

## Figure 42 - Typical earth pressure balance machine configuration

### 3.5.3 Cable installation

### Once the tunnel is complete and the cable supports installed, the cables are pulled through

### the tunnel either from cable drums lowered down the shaft at the drive site or alternatively the

### cable drums are located at the surface and the cable pulled from the drum and guided down

### the shaft and into the tunnel where they are maneuvered into position.


## Figure 43 - Example of a surface drum with cable being guided into tunnel

There are a number of methodologies that have been adapted to install the cables, which often requires
snaking of the cable to cater for thermal expansion. Such installation techniques generally use similar
techniques to those used in direct laid situations and may additionally use powered rollers, caterpillars
or innovative techniques involving a cable train, which has the capability of pulling the cable through the
tunnel with minimal cable strain as well as lifting and snaking the cable into position. The cleating and
jointing processes follow the installation and fixing of the cables.

Special caution shall be taken when any semi-conductive layers are applied on cable system
installations. The semi-conductive layers are usually applied to the bonding and earthing design to
mitigate the risk of any capacitive coupled steady state or transient overvoltages. When the user requires
an extruded outer semiconducting layer over the insulating sheath for tunnel installations, it is required
to solidly bond the semi-conductive outer sheath layers to the metal structures along the entire tunnel
length in order to prevent any voltage difference between the semi-conductive outer sheath and the
tunnel metal structure. The voltage difference could lead to sparking. It is advisable that any personnel
contact with the cable system semi-conductive outer sheath and racking system in the event of switching
operations should be prevented.

## Figure 44 - 420 kV London tunnel project

Once the cables are installed, any shafts which are not required for access or ventilation may be
backfilled. In general, when a cable system is installed in a tunnel, multiple circuits are installed, rather
than a single circuit. Due to the nature of most tunnels being sealed structures, it is difficult to achieve
a local earthing.

All principles of safe work, work procedures and earthing methods which described in TB 801 801
"Guidelines for safe work on cable systems under induced voltages or currents" are also applicable in
tunnels. Consequently, it is necessary to ensure a proper local earthing system at the working site. If
necessary, a temporary earthing conductor should be installed. The temporary earthing conductor which
is laid parallel to the cable systems in the tunnel, requires that special attention is given to the presence
of induced voltages.

It is possible for long cable sections to ground the working area and isolating the cable system next to
the working area. This can be achieved by connecting to the local metal structures in the tunnel systems.


Cable pulling in tunnels

During the cable installation, all exposed parts of the cable should be earthed where they may be
exposed to induced voltages or currents caused by adjacent in service cable systems. In fact all metallic
parts like the conductor, metallic sheath, armoring and any ECC (Earth Continuity Cable) should be
earthed at a common point.

Consideration of earthing requirements should also be given to other cable pulling equipment, such as
cable rollers. If during the cable pulling process a metallic draw wire is used, the metallic wire will be
touching the rollers which are used along the cable route for the cable installation. Equipotentiality is
normally achieved by connecting all the metallic structures to a common bare ground conductor. In case
where no proper earthing possible, the rollers can be kept insulated from earth.

The cable outer sheath will also be touching the earthed rollers, so care must be taken to ensure there
is no capacitive charge on the outer semi-conductive layer or graphite during installation. From a
practical point of view this is not always possible to earth all rollers in the tunnel, so it is sufficient to
earth one roller at each end of the tunnel.

Where the cable is being installed down a steep incline or vertical shaft, particular care needs to be
given to the securing of the cable drum. Consideration should also be given to earthing the drum and
the inner end of the cable to the common earthing system.

The conductor and the metallic screen should be electrically connected through the pulling eye and
connected with the pulling wire where a metal rope is used. The pulling wire is then connected to the
rollers and the cable winch at the end.

The placing of multiple circuits in a tunnel should take into account the possibility that a single could
lead to the failure of multiple circuits. It is therefore sensible to avoid a tunnel becoming an unnecessary
pinch point where multiple essential circuits providing mutual redundancy are located in the same
location (e.g., two very close circuits) introducing the possibility of simultaneous damage.

Jointing in tunnels

The jointing work in a tunnel may be done under conditions where standing voltages or currents are
present. This means that the working area will be need insulated. Alternatively, it can be performed
under earthed conditions or ensuring that no induced voltages or currents are present. For earthed
working conditions, where induced currents or voltages may be present, particular care must be taken
as to the quality of the earthing point. In a tunnel situation, it is not always possible to achieve a
satisfactory local earthing.

If necessary, a temporary ECC can be used in order to provide a proper earthing point. It should be
pointed out that an induced voltage will also appear on this earthing connection, for the length which is
parallel to the cable section.

Additional factors to be considered for the tunnel cable installation include:

- All work in tunnels should be performed under the relevant national occupational, health and safety
    regulations
- Provision of adequate rescue measures, evacuation procedures and due consideration to possible
    fire hazards
- A working agreement between the asset owner and the contractor which details the situation before
    commencement each day
- All parties must be informed about any existing energized systems, the expected level of magnetic
    field and other working conditions
- All employees, including employees of other companies, which may also be working in the tunnel,
    must be trained in evacuation procedures in the case of emergency; and
- All employees should be also be trained in the rescue procedures and any associated equipment
    required for rescue or evacuation

### 3.5.4 Tunnel Shafts

In general, there are three types of shafts associated with tunnels:

- a working shaft


- a reception shaft; and
- an intermediate shaft

The working and reception shafts need to be of an adequate size to:

- Facilitate safe installation and removal of the TBM and associated equipment; and
- Accommodate the plant/equipment required to remove the excavated soil and transport construction
    materials

Intermediate shafts are generally used for ventilation or safety egress, although they can also be used
to lower equipment. A number of different methods are used to construct shafts and tunnels but the
method chosen is largely dependent on whether the prevailing ground conditions are classified as soft
ground or rock.Additional equipment for the cable pulling can be lifted in to the tunnel by using the
working shaft. It may be advantageous to install the cable winch permanently at a designated location
within the tunnel, purpose designed for the pulling of the intended lengths into the tunnel cable sections.
If a cable pusher is needed to assist with the cable pulling, these should also be positioned and installed
permanently in the tunnel also.Circular shafts in water-bearing soils are often constructed using precast
concrete segmental linings using a Caisson technique. The Caisson technique for excavating tunnel
shafts involves building the shaft lining in incremental rings at ground level, and forcing each complete
ring into the ground with additional temporary weight or hydraulic jacks as the ground is excavated from
within the shaft.

The Head House may contain:

- Control room
- Communications room
- Maintenance storage
- Shaft access; and
- Access to ventilation, fire control and cable cooling systems

The cables at ground level are terminated into Air Insulated Switchgear (AIS), Gas Insulated Switchgear
(GIS) or Trasformer oil immersed terminations in a manner identical to cables used on surface
installations.

### 3.5.4.1 Ventilation and heat dissipation

Ventilation may be provided to remove stale air, smoke and noxious or flammable gas build-up within
the tunnel. Ventilation is also able to regulate the tunnel temperature which can rise due to the tunnel
air being warmed by cable heat losses. This cooling method can increase the transmission capacity of
the cable tunnel when compared to a naturally cooled cable tunnel design. Ventilating facilities are
necessary for both open cut tunnel and shielded tunnel types.

In cable tunnels housing transmission type cables, the heat generated by the cables operating at full
load varies, but an indication may be taken from an existing EHV cable tunnel which requires the
removal of up to 180W/m per circuit. The heat generated by the cables must be extracted to avoid
overheating of both the cable and the tunnel air, which otherwise may restrict personnel access. Tunnels
have been installed with cables cooled by separate-pipe water cooling. Separate water pipe cooling in
tunnels are in use in Japan and Europe. As a result of experience, water cooling systems are not
favoured by some utilities due to the maintenance requirements and the lower availability of the cooling
system when compared with air assisted cooling. Water cooled fluid filled cable joints are currently in
service and available.

However, XLPE insulated cable joints in tunnels all rely on heat transmission using air convection and
radiation. At least one double circuit fluid filled cable exists where the cables are installed within water
pipes and is known as integral water pipe cooling. The water is circulated around the water pipes and
through water/air heat exchangers to cool the water before re-entering the tunnel.

A tunnel has a limited capacity to dissipate heat generated by the cables within the tunnel into its
surroundings. The tunnel structure and the surrounding ground will absorb heat energy, but the rate of
natural heat dissipation away from the tunnel is low and the transport of heat out of the tunnel may be
necessary if the cables within are not to overheat. Natural ventilation may be designed into a short tunnel
using a chimney effect. Here ambient air is drawn into the tunnel as warm tunnel air from the cables


rises through the chimney causing a pressure drop between the tunnel’s air inlet and the air pressure at
the base of the chimney. Natural draughts through a tunnel assist with the tunnel environment for
accessibility as fresh air is drawn into the tunnel. Naturally ventilated tunnels have the advantage of not
requiring the cost or maintenance of an assisted cable cooling systems such as air movement fans or a
water cooling systems. If natural ventilation is either inadequate or impracticable, then a tunnel will
include a means of extracting heat from the cable and joints (or any other heat generating service within
the tunnel) by a method of assisted cooling.

Air or water, or a combination of the two, have been employed to transport heat from within transmission
cable tunnels. Assisted cooling of the cable system allows the transmission of more power than would
otherwise be possible by means of a naturally cooled arrangement. In the case of air, electric fans create
an air pressure drop along the tunnel from an open inlet to a point of extraction. As cool air travels along
the tunnel it collects heat from the tunnel’s contents and cools the cable system. It is common for air to
be blown out of, rather than blown into, a tunnel as this avoids the heat generated by the fans when the
air is pressurised from entering into the tunnel.

The presence of fans which can manipulate air movement within the tunnel can also assist with
personnel safety. In case of a fire it is possible to design an air ventilation system whereby the direction
of airflow within a tunnel can be reversed to ensure that smoke may be directed along the tunnel in a
direction away from any personnel inside of the tunnel system. The extraction of heat from the tunnel
by pumping cooled water over submerged cables in a ‘trough and weir system’, or through pipes along
side, or containing, cables have all seen service. In such cases the water is circulated in a closed loop.
The water circulation loop includes heat exchangers or chillers to remove heat from the water and
transfer it to the atmosphere. The most common means of water cooling in a long tunnel is separate
pipe water cooling. In separate pipe water cooling the pipes containing circulated cooled water are in
close proximity to the power cables. In Europe, fluid filled cables have been installed, alongside water
filled and pressurised polythene pipes, inside of concrete troughs filled with a thermally stabilised sand
cement mixture. Heat transfer from the cable to the water pipe takes place by means of thermal
conduction. This is not the most efficient means of heat transfer between the cooling water and the cable
but does significantly reduce the risk of a fire, and its spread, in the event of a cable fault. All assisted
cooling systems introduce complexity into the cable system design. The preferred assisted cooling
system for solid dielectric cables and joints in Europe, is forced air cooling.For tunnels with a shorter
route which have not been designed to exclude water, the issue of cable overheating is dramatically
reduced.

### 3.5.5 Limits of the technique

### 3.5.5.1 Drying of the Soil

The ambient temperatures in tunnels are often considerably less than the ground temperature at 1 metre
depth of cover, as often experienced by cables in duct banks. Such low temperatures in tunnels, often
less than 20oC, result in there being no need to take into account the drying of the native soil, when
performing ampacity calculations.

### 3.5.5.2 Temperature of the Soil / Environment

Transmission capacity is decreased if the surrounding soil temperature is high or if the air temperature
within the tunnel is high as is often the case with large numbers of circuits in relatively small tunnels. As
a result, ventilation systems over and above those that may be required for the safety of personnel
accessing the tunnel, may be required to reduce the temperature within the tunnel. These systems have
ongoing maintenance costs which need to be considered.

### 3.5.5.3 Thermal Resistivity of the Soil

Thermal resistivity of the soil has impact on the transmission capacity for naturally ventilade tunnels,
while it has little impact when forced ventilation is considered..

### 3.5.5.4 Seismicity

In countries with known earthquake activity, such as those countries around the Pacific rim, tunnels are
designed to have stability against earthquakes with an acceleration of 0.3G and with a design safety
margin greater than 2.

Historical evidence would suggest that tunnels have a high resistance to earthquake activity. Reference
should be made to the work of WG B1.54 (TB is imminent)


Earthing System

Tunnels usually require the inclusion of counterpoise earthing conductors, earth bars and associated
interconnections, the electrical bonding of Concrete Steel Reinforcing at key locations and the electrical
isolation of conductive elements where necessary.

This is particularly important where joints are installed as a part of a sheath bonding system as large
tunnels are often constructed in rock at considerable depths. As rock generally has a high electrical
resistivity, localised earthing using drilled and driven earth rods may not be practical to cater for the
requirements of local earthing at joint bays.

## Figure 45 - Conceptual elevation of a tunnel earthing system

### 3.5.5.5 Cable Configuration

Cables in tunnels may be installed in either a rigid configuration (normally when there is not much space
available) or, more commonly, in a flexible configuration. In the latter case, both vertical and horizontal
snaking installation techniques are used, depending on the practical considerations.

Unfilled troughs with horizontal snaking may also be adopted as well as situations where the circuits are
buried within the floor of the tunnel to provide a trafficable platform.

### 3.5.5.6 Fire Hazards

If an electrical fault occurs on a fluid filled cable installed in air there is a resultant risk of fire. This risk
of fire is enhanced in an air filled tunnel as the burning fluid leaking from the damaged cable is pooled
within the tunnel increasing the concentration of heat. If the resulting fire has the severity to damage
adjacent fluid filled cables the ignition of these cables will increase the fire’s intensity further. Fire may
then propagate along the tunnel consuming all flammable material within.

An example of fire prevention method as a consequence of a fire experience on a 275 kV SCFF circuit
is mentioned in paper ref. JIC07_C527.

Although not at such a high risk of ignition, the polythene contained within the solid dielectric XLPE
insulated cable has a high calorific value (similar to that of petroleum) and thus whilst these cables


present a lower fire ignition risk than a low pressure fluid filled cable, the potential fuel loading by the
placement of multiple circuits in a tunnel has resulted in more than one significant fire containing solid
dielectric cable.

It is not certain that a cable failure will inevitably result in a fire. However, the risk of ignition of flammable
cable materials installed in air increases significantly if the system operator closes a breaker onto an
already faulted cable system. The intense heat of a cable tunnel fire has the potential to seriously
damage not only the cable system but also the tunnel structure itself. This is a primary reason not to
use a breaker auto-re-close system onto cables and accessories installed in air.

The use of fire retardant cable over sheathing compounds and intumescent cable coatings will assist
with limiting fire propagation along a tunnel. However, should the severity of a fire exhaust or exceed
the fire retardant properties of the over sheath and/or intumescent coating the fire may be expected to
propagate along the tunnel so long as air and fuel is available.

Further information are mentioned in TB 720.

### 3.5.5.7 Frost

No need to be considered.

### 3.5.5.8 Presence of Termites and Rodents

Termite activity is generally limited to a depth of five metres, so cables are unlikely to be affected by
termite activity. Protection against vermin migration and population of the tunnels is necessary and the
use of poison at the entrances of the tunnel is a common practice. A removable vertical barrier at the
entrance of the tunnel is also commonly used to prevent vermin intrusion and does not restrict access
when required. Hygiene procedures such as avoiding food or waste storage are important in the
prevention of vermin Intrusion.

### 3.5.5.9 Civil Aspects

Water Drainage

Tunnels can be designed as either tanked structures (essentially waterproof), or drained structures, in
which case groundwater is allowed to seep into the tunnel. In the case of a drained tunnel, drainage pits
and pumping systems are required to collect and dispose of the water.

Depending on the quality of the groundwater, water treatment facilities may also be required to treat the
water before it is deposited into local stormwater systems or waterways. Most city or state authorities
do not allow the pumping of untreated groundwater into stormwater systems.

Tunnels designed using pipe sections are often allowed to flood, particularly if there are no joints in the
tunnel. Evacuation of the water is only undertaken when access to the tunnel is required.

Water treatment is one of the most significant ongoing costs associated with cable tunnels. As such, a
key consideration is assessing the cost/benefit of the higher capital costs of constructing a tanked tunnel
against the ongoing maintenance costs associated with a drained tunnel.

Archaeology (prehistoric sites)

Investigation into the possibility of discovering prehistoric items should be done prior to any construction
work commencing.

In the case of deep tunnel construction where prehistoric building ruins may be uncovered, any such
discovery should be reported to the relevant authorities and a site investigation should be undertaken
before the construction continues.

Cut and cover tunnels are subject to uncovering ancient artifacts as would duct bank and direct laid
installations.

Statutory Approvals

As with all other types of installation methods, it is necessary to get permission from relevant authorities
before commencing construction works.

Environmental Impacts


As with all large scale civil projects, there is an impact on the environment, albeit less than that
experienced as a result of other methods. Land is required for the send and receive areas and often
these may coincide with substation sites. There is still the issue of land acquisition, noise and duct
control and well as tipping of spoil. If the project is in a built up urban area, vibration may also be an
issue and often dilapidation surveys are required before commencement.

Tunnel Routes

In most countries, tunnels are required to be designed so as to not traverse beneath building structures,
however this is dependent on local regulations. This can often require that tunnels follow established
road reserve routes, which may prove difficult when altering direction due to the turn radius of the
tunneling equipment. The profile of the tunnel will generally be guided by the capacity of the tunneling
equipment to change direction. Inclines and declines are often of a gentle grade only.

When constructing a tunnel using a shielded method, while only small areas of land are required for for
send and receive areas, land for shafts is is still required.

Duration of the Work

The duration of the construction period can vary greatly, depending on the size of the tunnel, the depth,
the ground conditions, and general complexity. The amount of time required to construct various
components of the project are as follows:

- Shaft: 6-9 months (depth 30 metres)
- Tunnel Driving :10 - 15 m/day
- Tunnel lining, Cable Supporting Material, Lighting: 15- 20 m/day
- Maintenance and Repairing Process

Maintenance and Repair

By monitoring for cracks produced in the tunnel wall, erosion rates may be estimated, and the most
suitable repair method determined. Repair methodologies vary from simple repairs such as filling cracks
to large construction projects such as building a steel reinforcement to support the tunnel itself from the
inside.

Entry/Egress

Tunnels are a confined space and the safety of personnel in the tunnel system is the most important
design safety consideration. Access to a tunnel system should be subject to safety protocols with
mandatory communication to control the risks of entering the confined spaces of a tunnel or shaft. The
safe evacuation of all personnel at risk within a tunnel complex will require knowledge by tunnel
controllers as to the number and location of people within the tunnel complex. Systematically good
communication between personnel located within the tunnel and the tunnel’s controllers located at a
safe control point.

Tunnel controllers should have, for example, adequate and appropriate training, a tally count and details
of those within the tunnel, a record of where they are located, and the means to contact and co-ordinate
with tunnel occupiers and emergency rescue responders should they be called upon.

Understanding the philosophy of the state’s emergency responders, if called upon, should be
investigated at the design stage. The emergency responders of some countries will not attempt the
rescue of personnel trapped in a tunnel which contains burning or energised cables. It may be necessary
that personnel entering the tunnel have the means, equipment and individual training to successfully
achieve a self-rescue if called upon to do so. The tunnel and cable system designers should consider
the escape prospects for personnel in the tunnel including acceptable distances from any point in the
tunnel to the nearest place of safety in case of a variety of hazardous Scenarios.

Specific Monitoring

Many tunnels today are equipped with CCTV, communications systems and/or thermal imaging and/or
fibre optic systems to monitor for fire or other abnormal conditions.

Cable Removal after Operation


The design life of a civil structure such as a tunnel may well greatly exceed the anticipated design life
of a cable circuit. As such, when it is time for cables to be replaced, and where flexible installation
systems have been employed, removal and replacement is relatively straightforward and often much
simpler than the work required to be performed for cable replacement in a duct bank. Rigid cable
installations require significantly more cable support steel work and cleats and tunnel securing points.

### 3.5.6 Adaption of the technique to the cable system design

Planning

During the planning phase there are a considerable number of factors that need to be addressed that
will affect overall design of the tunnel. These factors include but are not limited to the following:

- the number of circuits
- existing ground conditions
- installation methodology
- thermal design
- mechanical design
- earthing requirements
- drainage
- ventilation and cooling requirements
- working space
- maintenance and repair of the tunnel and the cable systems
- access and road condition
- countermeasures in the event of a fire or explosion; and
- environmental impact of the construction of the tunnel

Basic Design

The height of the tunnel needs to be such as to allow adequate space for installation and maintenance
work. The height may also influenced by the need to have additional services in the tunnel such as
communications, fire suppression and monitoring systems.

Joints are generally necessary in tunnel installations and it is normal for the distance between the joints
to be as long as is possible. The length of each cable section based on the longest length of cable that
can be transported to site and installed. This will often depend on where the drum of cable being installed
is sited.

Ventilation is provided by shafts along the route as required to satisfy the air flow requirements for
personnel access, safety requirements and to ensure the capacity of the circuits. These ventilation
systems are normally part of a fire management system as continuation of the operation of the ventilation
system in its normal mode during a fire episode may be dangerous for personnel and equipment already
in the tunnel.

During the design process consideration should also be given at an early stage to the reasons and
frequency of access that will be required. Typical reasons for access will include short duration
inspections of both the tunnel and its assets. Longer term access for cable system repairs or cable
removal and replacement should be considered. The availability and frequency of access may be such
that the installation of permanent lighting, power supplies and fire detection along the tunnel’s length is
deemed necessary.

However, the installation of light current cables, fixtures and light fittings can in themselves dictate the
frequency and cost of maintenance within the tunnel. Such services will also need to be capable of
withstanding the rigours of impressed voltages and earth currents introduced by the power cable
system. Health and safety restrictions, such as the risks associated with confined space working and
EMF levels may limit the frequency of access. At least one long cable tunnel installation in Europe
contains 400kV cables where video inspection using a remotely controlled vehicle equipped with


cameras and on-board mobile lighting are employed. The electric vehicle is provided with a point of
tunnel insertion and extraction for recharging and maintenance.

Mechanical design

This is an important consideration in the design of a tunnel. There are a number of factors which need
to be evaluated to determine the way in which cables will be supported, the distances between supports,
clamping methodologies and whether the cables are installed and supported as single cables or in a
trefoil arrangement.

There are two types of installation solutions available, namely rigid and flexible.In particular, a decision
is required as to whether the cleating system will be rigid or flexible.

Rigid cleating restrains the cable from movement. In order to achieve this, cleats are installed at
frequencies typically in the order 1 metre and more frequently (typically 0.6 metre) on bends. The
distance between cleats is calculated to restrain the cable in such a manner that lateral movement (Euler
bending) is not possible. This method of cleating is designed to prevent bending fatigue.

A flexible installation technique enables cable thermal expansion and contraction to be accommodated
by lateral displacements of the cable. The cable is initially installed in waves at a calculated pitch, offset
and separation. Additional mechanical restraints may be required to restrict cable movement during a
short circuit event. Short circuit forces are an electromechanical interaction force between current
carrying cables which cause the circuit cables to mutually repel. The short circuit forced may be
restrained by straps or linkages between cables of the same circuit. When a flexibly cleated cable
system is installed in a tunnels, the most common type is using a vertically sagged arrangement. Cable
snaked installations, horizontal or vertical, are seldom installed in tunnels where the technique has found
to be more suitable for shafts, troughs and bridges.

In all variants of the flexibly cleated cable system the cable joints are rigidly restrained.

The cable system in the tunnel may be fixed with cleats to achieve a snaking design, if this is the case
then it is recommended that:

- The cleating distance is such that the cable is installed without sagging; and
- Cleats for vertical runs must be designed to support the weight of the cable

## Figure 46 - Vertically sagged system with saddle cleats spaced

Having considered the ratings of the circuits, it is also necessary to review the weights of the cables and
the behaviour of the cables under fault conditions. This latter factor has a large impact on the design
and strength of the supporting steel work and fixings, support and clamp spacing as well as the cleating
arrangement.

Thermal design

It is very important to evaluate the thermal expansion of the cables. To cater for the inevitable thermal
expansion and contraction of single-core cables installed on supports in tunnels, a snaking regime of
“cable snaking” usually employed.


This technique enables thermal cable expansion and contraction to be absorbed by lateral
displacements of the cable initially laid in waves at a certain pitch and width. The format for this snaking
may take the form of either horizontal or vertical arrangements.

The decision as to which option is best suited to the installation is made by considering the site
conditions, available space, economy, etc. Horizontal snaking installations have been widely used for
fluid-filled cables and both snaking installations are used for XLPE cables. Reference is made to TB 669
which covers this facet of design in more detail.

### 3.5.7 Maintenance and Repairing Process

Long tunnels need to provide means of entry and egress for maintenance crews and cable jointers. For
AC power cables where the control of screen losses is required, the tunnel will be designed to
accommodate the installation and placement of joint bonding and link equipment. The methods of cable
screen earthing may also require the installation of earth connections and earth conductors.

During the design process, consideration should be given at an early stage, to the reasons and
frequency of access that will be required. Typical reasons for access will include short duration
inspections of both the tunnel and its assets. Longer term access for cable system repairs or cable
removal and replacement should be considered (the tunnel is usually expected to outlast the cable
system). The availability and frequency of access may be such that the installation of permanent lighting,
power supplies and fire detection along the tunnel’s length is deemed necessary. However, the
installation of lighting cables, fixtures and light fittings can in themselves dictate the frequency and cost
of maintenance within the tunnel. Such services will also need to be capable of withstanding the rigours
of impressed voltages and earth currents introduced by the power cable system. Health and safety
restrictions, such as the risks associated with confined space working and EMF levels may limit the
frequency of access. At least one long cable tunnel installation in Europe contains 400kV cables where
video inspection using a remotely controlled vehicle equipped with cameras and on-board mobile
lighting are employed. The electric vehicle is provided with a point of tunnel insertion and extraction for
recharging and maintenance.

As mentioned before all principles of safe work, work procedures and earthing methods which are
described in TB801 should be considered in the event of repair. It is necessary to ensure a proper local
earthing at the working site.

### 3.5.8 Commissioning and decommissioning

When the flexible installation is applied, it is relatively straightforward to remove the cables. However,
rigid cable installations require significantly more cable support steel work, cleats and tunnel securing
points. During the planning phase cable pushers and winches should be permanent installed at specific
points depending on the pulling force calculations.

The HV-test can be carried out at the end of each circuit, normally located at substations. In order to
perform an synchronous partial discharge measurement on all installed accessories, a concept should
be developed to reduce the conversion times of the test equipment. Due to the very complex and
regulated tunnel access requirements, the installation and calibration of the measuring devices which
have to placed temporary at the accessories becomes more difficult.

A fibre optic cable could be installed permanently in the tunnel for PD measurement and a junction box
at all accessory positions be provided. In each accessory, temporary HFCT could be installed in the
cable screen. During the HV-test the cable screens at the joints (if used) should be through connected.


## Figure 47 - HFTC with acquisition unit and optical fibre connection at termination (left) and joint (right)

In tunnel cable installations the removal of the cable system may be necessary in some situations. One
of the most common cases is when the cable system reaches the end of its service life.

When a cable system has to removed that may be adjacent to another in service cable system with
metal screens connected to the same earthing system, extreme care needs to be taken when
disconnecting the screens from the common earthing system as potential difference could be transferred
to the metal screen in case of failure on the another cable system.

Furthermore a quality check on the steel structure in terms of both static and material fatigue should be
performed regularly to determine whether the supporting structure may be reusable for a replacement
cable system.

### 3.5.9 Specific monitoring

To monitor tunnel security, condition and personnel safety video cameras should be installed at strategic
locations. Furthermore, the installation of other monitoring systems to assess air quality, humidity,
temperature, fire and water levels (as applicable), should also be considered.

Monitoring systems can provide an early warning signal in case of fires or in case of breakdown of the
cable system. Integrated fibre optics in the cable design can indicate overheating of the cable systems
or installed sensors in the tunnel can indicate the presence of fire and smoke.


## 3.6 Description of the shaft technique

Shafts are generally used in hydro electric generation plants where the power generated from the
underground equipment has to be brought up to a switch yard from where they can be connected to
aerial lines. Shafts also form part of cable tunnel systems, whether constructed using a cut and cover
method or a shielded method to provide ventilation, personnel access, cable entry or egress and
material handling access.

The shafts may be bored from ground level using large scale machinery designed to drill to depths of
up to 2,000 metres and a diameter of 12 metres. Shafts as part of a tunnel system can also be bored
from the tunnel to surface level.

When bored from the surface, the same technique used for creating shafts for the lowering of shielded
boring equipment can be used to support the shaft during construction. In ground that is slightly
unstable, the use of caissons is widespread. In rock conditions, the use of shotcrete through welded
mesh is more common.

Cables within shafts may be fixed with clamps at the shaft walls or to metallic structures, which are in
turn fixed to the shaft walls.

Several circuits may be installed in the same shaft; in this case walls or special separating structures
are used to reduce the possible damage in case of an adjacent cable failure or fire.

Joints, if present, are normally installed in a horizontal configuration in specially constructed joint
chambers.

## Figure 48 - Typical cable arrangement into a shaft


### 3.6.1 Limits of the technique

In the case of extruded cables particular consideration should be given to the thermo-mechanical
properties of the cables, in particular to limit the restraining force that each clamp may transfer to the
cable. Additional design effort may be required for the design of the supporting steel work and cleating
system. Depending on whether the installation is rigid or fixed in nature, will determine whether there is
a need to snake the cables in the shaft. There may be a requirement for the use of special clamps in
this situation.

For fluid filled cables the limits of this technique are further complicated by the hydraulic requirements
of the system. Particular care is required during the design phase, not to exceed the maximum static
and dynamic fluid pressures for the system. Stop joints are required to limit these pressures and the
design and installation techniques of the joints must be carefully evaluated. The hydraulic pressure limits
will determine the maximum length of cable that can be installed in a shaft.

Also the supporting structures along the cable route and at the terminations may also have an impact
on the design of the system.

For both types of cables, but especially for fluid filled types, the laying operation requires the use of
special procedures and tools. The design of the installation should carefully consider the laying aspects
and whether there is sufficient space and working areas. Any special installation equipment and
ancillaries structures will need to remain in place and be available for the whole of the service life to
allow the recovery and replacement of a faulty phase or circuit.

Laying of the cables into shafts is normally carried out from the top of the shaft and sufficient enough
space for the drum and cable laying equipment handling has to be allowed. Transportation of the cables
inside the terminal station may be also a critical aspect if for example the station is completely enclosed.

In the shaft the safety aspects play a significant role because short circuits, fire or explosion may lead
to failure of one or more circuits.

Special structures are often incorporated into the design of the shaft supports to minimise the impact of
fire inside the tunnel.

### 3.6.1.1 Civil works

Shafts are often designed as part of the power plant or tunnel structures and the cable installation design
should consider any existing facilities as well as form an integral part of a new design. Where the shaft
is a part of a tunnel system, the shaft can be constructed by using raise boring methodology without the
need for additional equipment.

In the case of fluid filled cables, depending on the allowable maximum cable length, joint chambers may
be required along the shaft to house stop joints. The size of the chambers need to take into account the
size of the joints, the number of cables, any bonding and earthing requirements and the structures to be
installed.

### 3.6.1.2 Water drainage

Water may permeate through the shaft walls, depending on the design. In such cases, evacuation of
the water from the lowest point of the shaft, whether it be a sump or as part of the water evacuation
system of a tunnel, will need to be designed for. Condensation on the cables and support structures is
also likely over time and this may ultimately lead to corrosion issues for the support structures and
clamping system. Consequently, the selection of materials for the supports and cleats is most important.

### 3.6.1.3 Temperature of the soil/environment

Transmission capacity is decreased if the surrounding soil temperature is high or if the air temperature
within the shaft is high as is often the case with large numbers of circuits in relatively small shafts. As a
result, ventilation systems over and above those that may be required for the safety of personnel
accessing the shaft may be required to reduce the temperature within the shaft. These systems would
normally be a part of a tunnel system or in the case of a dedicated tunnel at a power plant, normally rely
on natural convection.

### 3.6.1.4 Hardness of the soil

The hardness of the soil will determine the type of equipment and methodology to be used when
constructing and supporting the shaft.


### 3.6.1.5 Thermal resistivity of the soil

The thermal resistivity of the soil has little impact on the transmission capacity because heat from the
cables is mainly transferred to the air within the shaft.

### 3.6.1.6 Seismicity

Accelerations imposed by earthquakes should be considered when designing shafts and the cable
supporting structures. Where the shaft is a part of a generating station or tunnels civil works, the civil
structures design should have already been considering accelerations due to seismic events.

### 3.6.1.7 Frost

This phenomena will only be apparent at the very top of shafts in very cold areas. It is not normally a
consideration for the structure itself, only for the installation of the cables.

### 3.6.1.8 Archaeology

Investigation into the possibility of discovering prehistoric items should be done prior to any construction
work commencing. In the case of deep shaft construction where prehistoric building ruins may be
uncovered, any such discovery should be reported to the relevant authorities and a site investigation
should be undertaken before the construction continues.

### 3.6.2 Installation techniques

Standard installation methods are normally applied for vertical shaft cable installation. Snaking is
normally applied for large size cables with the view of managing thermal expansion and contraction.
Installation methods include the nose pulling method, bond pulling method and hauling method. The
construction of full height scaffold is normally required to assist with cable installation, cable snaking
and fixing.

### 3.6.2.1 Rigid type
