Rigid cleating restrains the cable from movement. In order to achieve this cleats are installed at
frequencies typically in the order 1 metre and more frequently (typically 0.6 metre) on bends. The
distance between cleats is calculated to restrain the cable in such a manner that lateral movement (Euler
bending) is not possible. This method of cleating is designed to prevent bending fatigue.

### 3.6.2.2 Flexible type

A flexible installation technique enables cable thermal expansion and contraction to be accommodated
by lateral displacements of the cable. The cable is initially installed in waves at a calculated pitch, offset
and separation. Additional mechanical restraints may be required to restrict cable movement during a
short circuit event. Short circuit forces are an electromechanical interaction force between current
carrying cables which cause the circuit cables to mutually repel. The short circuit forced may be
restrained by straps or linkages between cables of the same circuit.

When a flexibly cleated cable system is installed in a shaft the system used is invariably a horizontally
sagged arrangement. In all situations where joints are installed they are rigidly restrained.

### 3.6.3 Equipment and spaces

The shaft should be so designed to allow for the construction of large scale scaffold with intermediate
platforms to allow for access to the cables both during installation and maintenance events. The shafts
are often used as entry and egress points during the movement of personnel and equipment during
construction as well as evacuation purposes and should be designed to accommodate all of these
eventualities.

### 3.6.4 Environmental impact

As with all large scale civil projects, there is an impact on the environment, albeit less than that
experienced as a result of other works. In general, the construction of a shaft during the construction of
a hydro-generation plant would be catered for in the environmental assessment for the overall project.
The same would apply for shafts as a part of a tunnel project, forming a part of the overall approval,
albeit that a number of shafts would be required for a tunnel project and they would be spread over a
wider area. If the project is in a built up urban area, vibration may also be an issue and often dilapidation
surveys are required before commencement.


### 3.6. 5 Safety...............................................................................................................................................

The main safety factor relating to shafts is that of falls. Shafts can range in depth from a few metres to
many hundreds of metres. Shafts are normally used as entry and egress points on generating station
and tunnel projects, so the means of escape is already available.

### 3.6.5.1 Communications

This is normally achieved via the installation of a "leaky" coaxial cable which allows for two way radio
communications. This is a coaxial cable with the outer screen removed at intervals along the cable, this
facilitating communication along the length of the cable, essentially functioning as an extended antenna.

### 3.6.5.2 Fire risk

If an electrical fault occurs on a fluid filled cable installed in air there is a resultant risk of fire. This risk
of fire is enhanced in an air filled shaft as the burning fluid leaking from the damaged cable is pooled
within the tunnel increasing the concentration of heat. If the resulting fire has the severity to damage
adjacent fluid filled cables the ignition of these cables will increase the fire’s intensity further. Fire may
then propagate along the tunnel consuming all flammable material within.

Although not at such a high risk of ignition, the polythene contained within the solid dielectric XLPE
insulated cable has a high calorific value (similar to that of petroleum) and thus whilst these cables
present a lower fire ignition risk than a low pressure fluid filled cable, the potential fuel loading by the
placement of multiple circuits in a tunnel has resulted in more than one significant fire containing solid
dielectric cable.

The use of fire retardant cable over sheathing compounds and intumescent cable coatings will assist
with limiting fire propagation along a tunnel. However, should the severity of a fire exhaust or exceed
the fire retardant properties of the over sheath and/or intumescent coating the fire may be expected to
propagate along the tunnel so long as air and fuel is available.

The hazard may be further exacerbated by the natural draft effect within the shaft.

### 3.6.5.3 Ventilation

In shafts the cables are installed in air, so air can circulate from the bottom to the top of the shaft.
Generally, there is no requirement for the installation of forced ventilation unless it is already a part of a
tunnel system.

### 3.6.6 Environmental impact

Tunnel /Culvert are large-scale civil projects which have a particularly strong impact on the environment.
In general, the construction of a tunnel/culvert would be included in the environmental impact
assessment for the overall project.

If the tunnel will built-up in urban area, vibration may also be an issue and often dilapidation surveys are
required before commencement. Therefore it is wise to consider these factors at the preliminary study
stage for the Tunnel design itself.

The impacts related to the construction and operation of a power link, can be classified as follows:

Soil

- Direct damage due to the excavation (material displacement)
- Possible contamination of soils due to excavated materials being mixed with surrounding land
    materials.

Water

- Possible contamination of water ecosystem due to excavated or backfilled materials being
    displaced/lost in water bodies.

Air

- Release of pollutants to atmosphere during the construction work
- Noise generated by machinery during the work”


### 3.6.7 Maintenance and repair processes

Maintenance for cables in shafts may consist of a periodic visual inspection on:

- the cable sheath
- supporting structures
- fixing devices; and
- joints (where applicable).

In a shaft, the repair of a faulty phase will involve the recovery of the faulty cable and the laying of new
cable. Repair of an existing cable by installing joints in the run of a cable length, is generally not possible.

Maintenance requirements for shafts themselves is similar to that required for tunnels. By monitoring for
cracks produced in shaft walls, erosion rates may be estimated, and the most suitable repair method
determined. Repair methodologies vary from simple repairs such as filling cracks to large construction
projects such as building a steel reinforcement to support the shaft itself from the inside.

### 3.6.8 Commissioning and de-commissioning............................................................................................

Cable removal is possible but with difficulty due to access restrictions caused by access stairways and
other features installed after the original cable installation was undertaken. Removal is in the same
manner as installation but precautions must be taken to ensure personnel safety and that no damage
can occur to the remaining cables in the shaft. Please also refer to chapter tunnel for more details.

### 3.6.9 Specific monitoring

Shafts can be monitored either by CCTV or by using distributed temperature systems to monitor cable
and shaft temperatures and in particular alarm if temperatures are abnormal as may be the case when
a fire has broken out.


## 3.7 Horizontal Directional Drilling

### 3.7.1 Description of the technique and methods

Horizontal Directional Drilling (HDD), sometimes referred to as guided boring, is a trenchless
construction technique used for the installation of pipeline services such as water, gas and electricity,
under natural or artificial obstacles, such as large infrastructures (such as metro lines), roadways,
highways, slope drillings, train tracks, rivers, sea-land approach, etc. It minimizes the environmental
impact and causes less disruption to the surrounding urban and suburban areas.

The normal HDD process consists of drilling a pilot borehole in the ground, and then back reaming to
open the pilot borehole to install directly the conduits (without casing) or to take a casing pipe. In this
last case, conduits may then be pulled into the casing pipe and cables may then be pulled into these
conduits. Generally casing is recommended only in soil critical condition where the coating of pipe could
be damaged, like in gravel, cobles and rock formation. If the pull-back includes more than one pipe it is
enough to connect all the pipes in one suitable pulling head.

HDD may have one conduit per phase or, depending on length, pulling tensions or specific requirements,
all three phases may be installed directly in one pipe. HDD casing pipes are normally 200 – 1800 mm
diameter, whereas conduits are usually 200 – 300 mm diameter, and depending on the geology and
cable design, lengths up to 2000 m may be achievable.

The Horizontal Drilling equipment is composed of the drilling rig, drilling bits, drilling rods, the drill
guidance system, drilling fluids, mud pumps, and recycling/ancillary equipment.

A number of construction techniques are possible for a HDD installation and the type selected will
depend on a range of characteristics, e.g. the rating required from the cable, the depth of the cable, the
size of the cable, etc. as well as geology, geography, logistics, environmental constraints, etc.

The four main techniques are as follows:

```
i. Drill the borehole, enlarge and/or condition the borehole and pull the cable/s directly into the
borehole
ii. Drill the borehole, enlarge and condition the borehole, install a casing large pipe into the
borehole and pull cables directly into the pipe
iii. Drill the borehole, enlarge and condition the borehole, install a large casing pipe into the
borehole, pull a bundle of conduits into the casing pipe and pull a cable into each conduit
iv. Drill the borehole, enlarge and condition the borehole, pull conduits into the borehole without
using a large casing pipe and pull the cables into each conduit
```
Alternatively, it may be possible or necessary to drill individual boreholes for each phase, one cable
(with conduit) per borehole. The type of material selected for the casing pipe and conduits will depend
on electrical and HDD engineering factors; the most commonly used materials are PVC, HDPE and
steel.

Technique (i) would typically be used where individual drills are planned for the individual phases of the
high voltage cable circuit. This may be specified where the required rating of the cable is such that a
large separation distance is required between phases of the cable circuit. This solution is not normally
encouraged due to the high risk involved.

Where technique (ii) is used the pipe provides more protection for the cable or cables than technique
(i). Thermal grout may be required to completely fill the annular space of the casing pipe surrounding
the cables. This technique may also be necessary where the type of soil does not guarantee sufficient
stability to the borehole.

Technique (iii) gives added protection to the cable, but it also allows for easy retrieval of a single phase
if required. Thermal grout is generally required to completely fill the annular space of the casing pipe
surrounding the conduits.

Technique (iv) may be used where the size of the borehole is limited and there is not enough space to
accommodate a large pipe, but there is a requirement that individual conduits be installed for each
phase. If conditions allow, special backfill may be injected into the conduits and/or grouting applied
between the conduits and the borehole.

TB 770 provides more details of this technique.


### 3.7.2 Site set up and working areas

Horizontal Directional Drilling involves the operation of mobile drilling rigs/machines and requires some
space to allow for working around it. Depending on the project, some generic items may also be required
on site such as welfare facilities, meeting rooms, site offices etc.

The drilling site should be carefully chosen by first surveying the area to identify its boundaries. Legal
issues, including landowner agreements, environmental exclusion zones, and adequate space for the
working areas should be considered when determining if the location is feasible for drilling the required
installation. Positioning of the entry and exit sites is determined by the radius of the drilling and the depth
of the drilling. It may be necessary to prepare the land by levelling/grading it to allow access or to allow
the machines to be made stable in the area. The quantity of water that will be required for the
construction should also be considered. It is estimated that 1000 l/m is required for construction in rock
and approximately 400 l/m is required for soil.

At the entry side, the site requires space for the entire drill rig spread, the entry pit, and a pit or containers
for capturing waste materials.

## Figure 49 - Typical site setup at launch position

At the exit side, space is needed for the exit pit and a pit or containers for capturing drilling fluids and
cutting from the drill.

Large working areas must also be planned to include the temporary location of the equipment and pipe
fabrication (welding of pipe sections) during the drilling works and during the installation of the pipes or
cables.

### 3.7.3 Limits of the technique

### 3.7.3.1 Civil works

Civil limitations to drilling are fairly obvious. The following limit the effectiveness or are the cause of
problems during job performance:


The necessary borehole diameter exceeds the capability of the available equipment. The depth of
installation doesn’t allow enough cover to prevent the drilling fluids from percolating to the street surface.

Segment lengths between pit locations are too long to be done in a cost-effective manner.

The underground environment contains flowing sands with high water table, or contains a rocky
environment not conducive for the proper directional controls needed.

Attempting to drill around tight bends or intersection corners in a street.

Many of the above can be overcome using lesser known drilling methods, however in some cases
the better approach to take would be to use microtunnelling.

### 3.7.3.2 Drying of the soil

The design of the installation shall consider the possibility of drying out of the soil at the external surface
of the pipe. This might result in a thermal runaway causing cable insulation failure, if the system is
heavily and continuously loaded. Drying out of the soil is not of any importance, if the permanent
groundwater level is above the pipe installation. Therefore, the groundwater level must be verified at the
geological survey stage before the detailed design of the HDD installation is completed. Additionally,
the soil characteristics (thermal resistivity and drying out performance) must also be clarified during the
geological survey.

It must be noted that relatively large diameter of many casing pipes associated with trenchless methods
would result in a relatively low heat flux (heat per unit area) on the surface of the casing to cause drying
in the environments that are often below the water table as compared to conventional, smaller diameter
conduits and cables that are installed at shallower depths where soil drying may be a factor.

### 3.7.3.3 Temperature of the soil/environment

Depending on the drilling depth, temperature of the ambient soil or rock where the cable circuit would
eventual reside, can actual be more constant and at a lower temperature than for shallower installations
done using open cut methods. Because of the greater installation depths, the circuit ampacity may need
a de-rating. This disadvantage can be overcome through the use of dynamic monitoring on the circuit
or through the use of a larger conductor size.

### 3.7.3.4 Hardness of the soil

As previously mentioned under civil limitations, the hardness of soil can affect the drilling effectiveness.

Geotechnical and geophysical investigations are critical to the success of trenchless operations, and
should be carried out by specialist firms at the conceptual design stages of a project. Ground conditions
will have a significant influence on the length and accuracy of the HDD borehole. Geophysical testing,
trial pitting, static cone penetration testing and percussive or rotary drill bore holes may be used where
appropriate. Boreholes may be adequate for characterising soils, but occasionally test pits are
recommended, for example to characterise size and extent of cobbles. There are many other
investigation techniques (probing and geophysics) that could also be considered where less
homogenous soil conditions are expected.

Boreholes should not be drilled in the line of the HDD profile. All boreholes should be properly backfilled
and sealed.

### 3.7.3.5 Stability of the soil

Unstable soil conditions such as flowable sands and cobble mixed with softer soil materials are probably
the worse conditions for drilling operations to be effective. In the case of flowing sands, or very sandy
soil conditions where the bentonite slurry cannot stabilise and maintain the drill hole, a washover
technique can be performed (for small diameters). This is where sections of a larger diameter pipe are
connected and pushed into the borehole during the pilot drilling operation. Once the drill rod has gone
the desired length, it can be removed from the washover pipe and also be used to pull in the cable. The
washover pipe is sacrificial and it stays in the ground to act as a conduit. For larger diameter holes, a
technique known as forward reaming is used. During the pilot hole drilling, reamers are attached every
so often onto the pilot drill rod faced in the forward direction (smaller reamers first, eventually having the
last reamer at the desired diameter). Behind the last reamer is attached a sleeve or large pipe, which is
pushed into the large hole in parallel with the pilot hole drilling and reaming operations. This sleeve or
large pipe provides a path for the cuttings and slurry back to the drill rig. The fact that it is pushed or
spin into the hole during the drilling results in a stable hole. This however does limit the advance rate of


the drilling and as a result dramatically increases to cost. This same method may also be needed where
cobbles and covered river bottom rock is encountered.

### 3.7.3.6 Thermal resistivity of the soil

Thermal resistivity of the rock or native soil outside the pipe or conduits needs to be taken into account.
Different soil layers with different thermal properties may be crossed during horizontal directional
drillings. Geological data may provide some initial guide. The most common approach to handling non-
homogeneous thermal properties is the use of the finite element or conformal mapping technique as
outlined in CIGRE Technical Brochure 640, A Guide for Rating Calculations of Insulated Cables.

In the case of conduits crossing multiple layers of soil, if there is a fluid inside these conduits (water,
bentonite), the possibility of axial heat transfer along the conduit may be considered. In addition, a
deeper layer of soil with unfavourable thermal properties may have an effect at another (higher) location
along the drilling. Conversely a layer of soil with good thermal properties may assist in removing heat in
a longitudinal direction and help the cable’s rating. Simply taking the radial heat transfer may not be
sufficient to correctly model the installation. Multiple soil layers are discussed in TB 640 “Guide for Rating
Calculations of Insulated Cables”.

Soil boring and sampling must be conducted in order to classify the type and mechanical properties.
The investigation borehole locations must be offset from the centerline of the drill path. This will
eliminate/minimize the possibility of fraking during horizontal drilling. Fluid pressures must be kept within
the limits and not to exceed the overburden pressure at a given elevation. In-situ thermal testing should
be considered during this stage of operation. This will give the temperature and thermal resistivity profile
along the drill path and for various elevations.

### 3.7.3.7 Seismicity

Accelerations imposed by earthquakes may have to be considered especially in cases where the HDD
exit points terminates into joint bays.

In this case, suitable cable anchoring devices shall be considered, as well as cable snaking, in order to
avoid the transfer of the tensile force to the joints.

### 3.7.3.8 Subsidence

Subsidence can be minimised by inserting a steel pipe into the bore to provide hole stability. In the case
where the drill bit is separate to the drill pipe it may be run a few meters (up to 9 m) ahead of the drill
pipe and so the ground conditions can be assessed prior to the drill pipe reaching that location. This
allows the operator some time to alter the drill direction to avoid hazardous areas

### 3.7.3.9 Archaeology

In general, trenchless techniques are the preferred methods for installing cable/s where archaeological
sites have been noted. This has as a result of less surrounding soil being removed during drilling and
the drill path can be planned to go deeper under these sites.

Local authorities or actors must always be consulted in advance about the rules and regulations.

### 3.7.4 Benefits in using HDD

Some of the factors listed below have resulted in an increase in the use of HDD in the last years:

- Trenching in areas with many existing services can be quite costly, because the work must be
    done slowly and carefully in order to avoid damaging existing services. Otherwise it may be
    necessary to relocate the existing services to facilitate trenching
- Traffic disruptions can be restrictive and authorities may insist on HDD methods being used to
    avoid road closures
- Depth of the trench versus cost. If deep trenches are required, they can be costly from an
    excavation and shoring point of view
- Environmental aspects; no surface trenching is required when using HDD, so the environmental
    impact is less
- Aesthetics; trenchless construction generally leaves no surface trace and regulators are
    becoming more sensitive to public concerns in this area

HDD is also commonly used to build land-sea interface connections. HDD technology avoids digging
trenches by and through the seashore and offshore areas, where it is much more difficult to work. This


avoids working in difficult weather conditions and also avoids working in environmentally sensitive or
protected areas.

The main difference compared to an HDD land installation is that one end of the pipe/drill will be at sea.
This requires the mobilization of a marine spread for the assistance at sea and two different possibility
for pipe installation once the borehole has been drilled.

### 3.7.5 Equipment

The main components of any HDD installation are the drilling rig, drilling pipes, drill bits, drilling guidance
systems, drilling fluids, mud pumps, recycling/ancillary equipment, HDD pipes and conduits. A brief
description is given below.

Drilling rig

Horizontal drilling rigs can be classified into four equipment sizes based on their capability:

- service tools
- mini-rigs
- midi-rigs
- maxi-rigs

Depending on the installation, the size of the tool needs to be chosen carefully for maximum economic
benefit and to ensure a successful installation.

Service tools are used for very small installations of gas and underground residential distribution (URD)
services. These are easily transportable and require very little set-up space. Service tools can drill small
diameter holes for about 60 m effectively. Many are "dry drills", meaning that they use no slurry to act
as a coolant or for hole stabilization.

Mini-rigs currently represent the largest market segment for drill rigs. These are small versatile tools that
employ a minimum amount of slurry for drilling in relatively easy to moderate soil conditions. Mini-rigs
come in various sizes and capabilities just as back hoes. In many respects, these small machines are
the equivalent of backhoes, while midi-rigs, which are approximately the same physical size, are
equivalent to excavators.

Table 1 below shows the indicative performance ranges of the four categories of drilling rigs as a function
of the pulling force. Precise information shall be obtained after an accurate design.

```
Type of rig Pulling force
[kN]
```
```
Maximum borehole diameter
[mm]
```
```
Maximum length
[m]
```
```
Service 25 125 50
```
```
Mini 100 400 150
```
```
Midi 500 800 500
```
```
Maxi 2500 1400 2000
```
(*) 1800 mm can be obtained with a bigger equipment

```
Table 1 - Rig type vs. pulling force
```
Drilling pipe

The drill pipe (also known as drill rod) is the critical link between the drill rig and the drill bit, reamer or
pipe/cable. It is used to transfer torque to cut the hole, pull back to install the pipe/cable and also serves
as the conduit to deliver the drilling fluid to the drill bit. The drill pipe provides the trade-off between
flexibility for steering and strength for power and durability. Small diameter pipes are flexible and provide
the most steering and the best control for small diameter boreholes. The drawback is that the small rods
are weak and susceptible to fatigue. Large diameter drill rods are strong and can transmit the most
power for fast drilling of large diameter holes; the drawback is less flexiblity for steering and control.


Drill pipes usually wear out from abrasion on rock and from fatigue by being bent and rotated. If the drill
pipe breaks during the reaming or drilling process, the loss is expensive and sometimes unrecoverable.
The most common failure mode is material fatigue and it most often occurs at a joint between pipes or
between a pipe and the bit. Contractors inspect drill pipes frequently and always before the
commencement of each project.

Reamers

Reamers are tools that allow the enlargement of the pilot hole with a defined diameter according to the
diameter of cable/s to be laid.

The reaming tool and relative accessories are secured to the pack of pull rods at the point of exit. The
reamer is then rotated and at the same time pulled by the drill rig, thus enlarging the pilot hole. As the
reamer proceeds, the new pull rods are attached immediately behind it to ensure continuity of
connections inside the hole.

Depending on the diameter of the pipe casing or conduit/s, the terrain crossed and the length of the
crossing, the reaming phase may be repeated several times, progressively increasing the diameter of
the reamer, to reach the required hole dimensions.

Drill guidance systems

A tracking system may be used to help guide and monitor the route of the pilot borehole. Tracking
systems include some arrangement of magnetic sensors. Four types of guidance systems are
commonly used; a Walkover Locator, Wire-line, Magnetic Guidance System (MGS) and Gyro Steering
Tool (GST).

The drill guidance system is also known as BHA (Bottom Hole Assembly).

A Walkover Locator system, is feasible up to approximately 40 m depth, depending of the soil
characteristics and disturbance from other electro-magnetic sources. The system uses a battery-
operated transmitter (sonda), located in a housing near the drilling bit or motor. This transmits a
continuous magnetic signal picked up by a portable hand-held receiver located above the drilling. The
sonda also sends data about the inclination of the drill bit to the drilling rig. This inclination data and the
location of every joint of the drilling rods obtained by the walkover/receiver system, communicates the
real location of the drilling bit and allows the user to adjust the routing, if necessary. This system is
limited because it can only be used where the surface over the drill is accessible, therefore this technique
often cannot be used with crossing of large infrastructure, such as highways, railroads, waterways, etc.

The Hardwire guidance system (also called “Wire-line” guidance system) utilizes a cable running
through the drill string to transmit data from the drill head beacon to the control console in the cab of the
drill rig. As such, it overcomes the depth limitation of the walkover system. It also has a higher degree
of accuracy as the information sent to the control console is not interrupted by the different ground
conditions or the depth of the borehole. This system can be used in any environment, providing the
beacon is durable and shock and vibration resistant.

MGS (Magnetic Guidance System) is a system which uses two magnetic fields. The earth’s magnetic
field is used to measure the orientation of the drill bit with respect to the North-South axis. An artificial
AC magnetic field is then created by laying electric cables over the area where the drilling is going to be
performed. The AC cables present the “known” magnetic field and help to determine the perpendicular
distance of the drill bit. The accuracy of this system is jeopardised in urban areas where the presence
of other power cables, steel pipes, etc will disrupt the magnetic fields.

GST (Gyro Steering Tool), provides an accurate 3D (longitude, latitude and height) reference for the
position of the drill via an optical fibre connection between the tool and the control centre. It uses the
GPS position of the launch pit and continuously measures the angle of the drill with reference to the
launch pit, to control the approach of the drill towards the exit pit. Measurements may be taken even
when the drill-string is rotating at high frequency.

GST guidance system is limited by the radius. Often a radius of at least 100-180 m is required to used
GST, depending on the size of HDD.

Drilling fluids

Drilling fluids have four important functions, which are remove soil cuttings from the hole, cooling the
drill bit, preventing the soil from collapsing on the drill rod and lubricating the drill pipe and thereby


reducing friction and drag. Drilling fluids usually consist of a water based mixture with either polymer
and/or bentonite clay (with additives) and their characteristics are compared in Table 2 below.

```
Drilling fluid summary
```
```
Type Cuttings transport Cooling Hole stabilization Lubrication
```
```
Bentonite Excellent Excellent Excellent Excellent
```
```
Polymer Excellent Poor Excellent Excellent
```
```
Water Poor Excellent Poor Poor
```
## Table 2 - Drilling fluid summary

The fluid, by penetrating the soil surrounding the hole and mixing with this, especially in loose soil,
modifies the structure, which then tends to act like an artificial clay. The walls of the borehole thus
become more stable, ensuring operation success.

The use of the appropriate drilling fluid depends also from other factors such as the geological conditions
of the soil and, for example, close to points where there is the presence of water collection plants.

Mud pumps / mud mixing / recycling

In order to get the drilling fluids to the drill bit a drilling mud system is required. The primary drilling mud
system consists mainly of a mud mixing unit and mud pumps. The system can also include a mud
cleaning/recycling unit that pumps the returning mud from the HDD pit, cleans the mud and then pumps
it back to the mixing tank. The mixing tank mixes the drilling fluid to the required rheological properties
(viscosity, weight, etc). The cleaned and conditioned mud can then be temporarily stored in another tank
or pumped directly downhole through the drill-string.

Pipes and conduits

Pipes are also referred to in industry as “casing pipes”. They may be steel, non-magnetic stainless steel,
PVC, HDPE, concrete, fibre-glass reinforced polymer mortar, or clay.

The most commonly used are those made of HDPE and steel.

Conduits are usually of polyethylene or polyvinyl chloride construction, similar to that laid in trenched
installation, but with a slightly greater wall thickness. Wall thickness is normally a function of mechanical
aspects. Additionally, it provides further mechanical protection for the cables.

The casing pipe and conduits are usually welded or butt-fused at the launch/exit pit. This welding may
be done on site and the working area of the site should take into account any space requirements for
this work. This means a continuous string of a casing pipe or conduits must be laid out on site, as once
the pipe or conduit installation operation commences it must not be interrupted or stopped because the
pipe /conduit could stick in the drilled hole due to collapse; this is less likely if the pipe/conduit is kept
moving in the drilled hole until the full installation length is completed.

When PVC is used, the jointing between segments is carried out through “non-slip action”, which is a
mechanical connection. Due to the lower tensile strength compared with that of HDPE joint technique,
the maximum allowable length must be carefully considered at design stage.


## Figure 50 - Casing pipe and conduits

## Figure 51 - Drill section showing casing pipe and conduits


### 3.7.6 Environmental impact

Where cable/s may need to be installed in National Parks, golf courses, old forested areas, etc., HDD
is the preferred construction method.

Local archaeology authorities must always be consulted.

It must be ensured that any pollutant run-off from the site is managed in such a way that the special
area is protected from the impact of the run-off.

When and HDD approaches a sea-land area, in order to protect the coast line and protect the cable, a
HDD installation is often carried out from behind the natural or man-made sea defence area to an
appropriate place in the surf zone. A pipe is pulled through the horizontal drill, and the cable is pulled
from the sea through the pipe.

During HDD construction, suitable methods are used to limit the dispersion of bentonite fluid into the
sea (water) and to recover as much as possible the drilling fluid from the sea bottom

### 3.7.7 Safety...............................................................................................................................................

It is advised that all works should be carried out in a safe manner with respect to all aspects of the work,
including but not limited to; site set up, fencing, handling of complex equipment, ponds, support
equipment, large numbers of personnel on site, working near launch and exits pits, high pressures,
moving mechanical parts, working in the vicinity of water, working in the vicinity of busy roads, heavy
machinery traffic, livestock, animals, etc.

Activities involved in the laying of network services infrastructures using HDD technology shall be carried
out in full compliance with current national regulation governing occupational health and safety.
Provisions specified by local administration and authorities shall also be observed with regard to
authorisations and site opening times.

### 3.7.8 Legal aspects

It can be observed that national authorities or the utilities hardly ever forbid the proposed civil
construction method, unless there are very particular reasons.

It must be borne in mind that in certain countries the law is such that the owner of the land also owns
the subsoil under that land. During link construction requiring for instance horizontal drilling, it is in those
cases absolutely necessary to secure the authorization from the person that owns the land under which
the link construction will take place.

An alternative option may be to build the link under the public roadway, in which case only authorization
by the public authorities is necessary (but this may entail a longer route for the link).

Also, magnetic field calculations more and more dictate, through guidelines, or even through standards,
the choice of the route and, above all, the depth at which the cables will have to be buried.

### 3.7.9 Maintenance and repair processes

The preferred practice for installing cable in HDD is in a conduit which may be unfilled or filled. In the
event of a fault on a cable in a conduit the normal practice is to pull the cable out and pull a new cable
in. For filled conduits, this is dependent on the ability to remove the bentonite with water sprays. If it is
not possible to remove the cables, then they may be left in-situ. In the case of oil filled cables if being
left in-situ it may be necessary to drain and treat the cables.

In an unfilled conduit, it should be possible to remove the cable, if it has reached the end of its life or it
needs to be replaced.

Alternatively, the installation of a spare conduit may be considered at the initial design stage.

### 3.7.10 Specific monitoring

HDD installations represent sometimes the circuit hot spots.

As with any buried cable installation, greater depth generally results in lower ratings. This may be
mitigated somewhat by lower summer time ambient earth temperatures and possible better earth
thermal resistivities (TR) at the greater depth. It is very difficult to predict the values of TR and
temperature at high depths and specific measurements are, very often, not performed.


Under the abovementioned uncertainties, a Distributed Temperature System (DTS) may offer some
more confidence in trenchless technology installations where the cable rating has to be determined.

Optic fibres, either integrated within a cable structure or placed close to the active phases, are now
common features in transmission cable installations for the purpose of DTS and to take advantage of
other sensing opportunities including partial discharge detection (PD) where optic fibre is both the
"sensor" and the "data transmitter".


## 3.8 Microtunnelling/Pipe Jacking

### 3.8.1 Description of the technique and methods

Pipe jacking is generally referred to as microtunnelling below 1.2 metres diameter. The term
microtunnelling is also used for fully automated pipe jacks in larger diameters. Pipe jacking is a method
of installing pipes by using a thrust system at the entry position, which is usually located in a shaft. A
small tunnel boring machine or mechanized shield is used to excavate the route for the pipe, Hydraulic
cylinders/jacks normally power the thrust system. These hydraulic jacks are used to push specially
designed casing pipes through the ground behind a mechanized shield and at the same time as
excavation is taking place within the shield. This method provides a flexible, structural, watertight,
finished casing pipe, as the tunnel is being excavated.

Casing pipes may consist of the following material types:

- Concrete
- Steel
- mortar-filled
- glass fibre reinforced plastic
- clay
- polymer concrete

The cables may be installed directly in the casing or installed in conduits which have been pulled into
the casing. Pipe jacks can range between 150 mm and 3000 mm in diameter to accommodate
transmission power cables. Mainly due to safety considerations, access by personnel may be limited in
smaller microtunnels.

Pipe jacking can be used in most ground conditions, including rock, water bearing, and mixed face
conditions. Where the ground is unstable the appropriate selection of machine is used to control the
face, or alternatively geotechnical processes can be used to stabilize the ground.

When used in urban areas, pipe jacking is generally used at depths which will avoid conflicts with
existing services. This installation method is usually selected when ploughing in is not a viable option
and where HDD may pose a risk of conflict with existing services or where ground conditions are
unsuitable for HDD. Microtunnelling/pipe jacking is generally used for the installation of cables and
conduits where a strong structural lining is required e.g. in heavily trafficked urban areas or under
strategic roads, motorways, railways, rivers and canals.

Differently from pipe-jacking, microtunnelling may be done below the water table or without de-watering.

Six inter-dependent components are incorporated into a pipe jacking system. They are as follows:

a) Jacking System including thrust wall, jacking rig, guide rail, and thrust rings.

b) Shield/cutter head

c) Spoil Removal System

d) Laser Guidance and Remote-Control System

e) Pipe Lubrication System

f) Pipe jacking pipe

In order to install a casing pipe using this technique, it is first necessary to construct thrust and reception
pits. The dimensions and construction of thrust pits vary according to the specific requirements of the
drive, with economics being a factor. Pit sizes will vary according to the excavation methods employed,
although these can be reduced, if required by special circumstances.

A thrust wall is constructed to provide a reaction against which to jack. In poor ground, piling or other
special arrangements may have to be employed to increase the reaction capability of the thrust wall.
Where there is insufficient depth to construct a normal thrust wall, e.g. through embankments, the
jacking reaction has to be resisted by means of a structural framework having adequate restraint
provided by means of piles, ground anchors or other such methods for transferring horizontal loads.


To ensure that the jacking forces are distributed around the circumference of a casing pipe being jacked,
a thrust ring is used to transfer the loads. The jacks are interconnected hydraulically to ensure that the
thrust from each is the same. The number of jacks used may vary because of the pipe size, the strength
of the jacking pipes, the length to be installed and the anticipated frictional resistance.

A reception pit of sufficient size for removal of the tunnelling machine is normally required at the remote
end. The initial alignment of the pipe jack is obtained by accurately positioning guide rails and/or the
jacking frame within the thrust pit. It is essential that the alignment is accurate, as otherwise it will be
very difficult to achieve a successful installation.

When the microtunnel is constructed below the water table it is usual to incorporate a headwall and seal
assembly within each thrust and reception pit. The use of these items prevents ingress of ground water
and associated ground loss, and retains annular lubricant.

Consideration must be given to ground stability around the pipe jack entry and exit eyes in shafts. This
can be by a number of methods, for example, the use of gland assemblies, pressure grouting or localized
dewatering.

Entry and exit shafts can readily be converted into permanent access facilities.

A range of pipe jacking excavation methods are available. In most cases, the choice of method will
depend on the type of ground and the selection of the appropriate ground support techniques.

In the smaller diameters, generally below 1.2 metres diameter, microtunnelling systems are used. These
systems combine excavation methods that provide full face support, specifically TBMs and pressurised
slurry, with a purpose designed spoil removal system. These fully guided microtunneling machines,
which are likely to be applicable to the majority of cable installation requirements are auger
microtunnelling and pressurised slurry microtunnelling machines. A similar technique using guided
augers is also available for use.

In the larger diameters, generally above 1.2 m diameter various cutting heads and excavation systems
are available to suit a broad range of ground conditions.

Fully automated microtunnelling systems and guided augers and the similar larger diameter automated
pipe jacks are applicable to most cable applications. A range of full face excavations methods are
available dependent on ground conditions and any requirement for face stabilization. These are tunnel
boring machines or pressurized slurry. Additionally, earth pressure balance machines can be used for
larger diameters generally above 1.4 metres.

A number of open face systems are also available but these are only suitable for use above 1.2 metres
diameter, owing to limitations on safe operator access, and where ground conditions permit. These are
cutter booms in rock and stable ground, backacters in semi-stable to stable soils, and for very short
drives where no alternative exists, open hand shields.

Jacking lengths

The length over which a pipe jack can be installed is dependent upon many interrelated and variable
factors; the stability and friction characteristics of the geology to be tunnelled through, the self-weight
and strength of the pipes, the diameter of pipe, the type of excavation method and the available jacking
reaction. The major constraint will be the nature of the ground and the ground water characteristics. In
small diameters (up to a metre) jacking lengths of up to several hundred metres, in a straight line or on
a radius, are achievable. In larger diameters, lengths in excess of 1 km are possible, depending on
ground conditions and excavation methods.

Intermediate jacking stations

In order to reduce the total required jacking force on the full pipe length, intermediate jacking stations
are frequently used between the launch pit jacking rig and the tunnelling machine.

The interjack itself consists of a steel cylinder which matches the outside diameter of the jacking pipe
and is fitted, either as a tolerance fit to the leading pipe on site or in some cases comes factory fitted
from the pipe manufacturer. It must be designed to cater for ground, hydrostatic and thrust load
conditions. A trailing interjack pipe recessed to slide into the interjack shield is positioned behind
hydraulic jacks. These are used to move the forward train of pipes independently of the trailing pipes
using the friction resistance of the trailing pipes to reduce the load on the thrust wall. On completion of
the stroke of the interjacks the main jacks in the thrust pit advancing the rear pipeline to its original
position relative to the leading pipeline and thereby closing the intermediate jacks. The sequence is then


repeated for the duration of the pipe-jack and on completion the jacks and fittings are removed and the
inter jack closed up.

Inter jack stations are not only used to increase the jacking lengths achievable, but also to reduce the
loads that are transmitted to the shaft structure. This is useful where ground conditions at the drive shaft
are poor or of low inherent strength.

TB 770 provides more details of this technique.

### 3.8.2 Site setup and working areas

Access for equipment needs to be considered when designing the site set up and working areas, with
particular care given to equipment size and weight, the local road structure and impact on traffic.

The pipe jacking/microtunnelling launch pit/shaft needs to be sufficiently dimensioned to facilitate the
jacking and drilling equipment, the installation of casing pipes and conduits, if required, and the
installation of cables. To minimise the size of excavation, in particular the length of the pit/shaft, it may
be possible to form a narrow sloping entry ramp to help guide the conduits into the pipe. However,
launch and exit pits/shafts may potentially be quite large in size, depending on the amount of material
to be excavated, the required lining, local water levels, soil type, thrust pressure and the potential for
flooding. Working areas must be kept dry from rain and ground water.

The size of launch and exit working pits/shafts generally depends on the requirement to install the
jacking rig, the length of the jacking pipe and the space required to remove the excavation machinery
on completion of the drive. Depth, site requirements and ground conditions will also influence the choice
of pit/shaft. Pits/shafts can range from 2.4 – 4 m in diameter or can be constructed to meet specific site
requirements.

Around these pits/shafts an area must be available for storage of material, decanting of mud, machines,
cranes, control and other equipment as well as welfare facilities, parking, site offices, wheel washing
and other elements that are demanded by the particular project. An area in the range of 5 m x 50 m to
10 m x 80 m may be required for this surface area. Consideration should be given during design to the
overall plan area and depth. Safe handling and storage of pipes should also be considered when
planning the site setup design.

### 3.8.3 Limits of the technique

### 3.8.3.1 Civil works

This technique has a higher initial capital cost than HDD, as the equipment is more complicated.
Microtunnelling can have difficulties in soils containing boulders with sizes greater than 30 % of machine
diameter due to the inability to crush them as well as the unability to make rapid changes in alignment
or level. Auger type microtunnelling machines are usually limited to tunnelling less than 3 m below
ground water levels. During thrust boring, if the cutting head becomes damaged, and if its diameter is
larger than the casing pipe, it may be impossible to withdraw it, resulting in costly and time consuming
rectification works.

The greater risk is frack-out of the lubricant injected along the jacked pipe. This tends to happen when
extreme pressures are used particularly when frictional forces have increased to the point that the
jacking might be stopped. It is possible to heave/hump the surface with a TBM but this is an extremely
rare event.

For this reason, the installation depth must be carefully taken into account.


## Figure 52 - Drill section showing casing pipe and conduits

Pipe jacking below the water table in unstable ground can and often does lead to catastrophic ground
loss and damage to the structures above. In a sink hole area, it may be possible to use slurry
microtunneling in lieu of pipe jacking with dewatering that may not be so effective in preventing further
subsidence.

With microtunneling in extremely soft soils it is difficult to control alignment and grade without soil
improvement. The TBM cannot develop enough side force using its articulation steering system to
deflect the TBM in the correct direction. In larger TBMs (>1.5m), their weight is so great that they tend
to sink 50-100 mm in spite of best efforts to steer them.

Rock microtunneling is limited normally to the rock hardness being less than 200 MPa due to the small
size of the cutting tools used on the cutter head.

Microtunnel boring machines must be 1.5 m or larger O.D. to have “face" access to the back of the cutter
head in order to change the cutting tools. This is critical when microtunneling in granitic rock where disk
cutters can fail or wear out in as little as 10 m of tunnelling. These type conditions require a face access
TBM or run the risk of failure to complete the drive and require rescue shafts or tunnels or retraction of
the TBM.

### 3.8.3.2 Drying of the soil

The design of the pipe jacking installation shall consider the possibility of drying out the soil at the
external pipe surface which might result in a thermal run away causing an insulation cable failure if the
system is heavily and continuously loaded.

Drying out of the soil is not of any importance if the permanent groundwater level is above the pipe
installation. Therefore the groundwater level must be verified at the geological survey before the detailed
design of the pipe installation is completed. Additionally the soil characteristics (thermal resistivity and
drying out performance) must also be clarified at the geological survey.

Drying out of the soil can be expected to start at a continuous temperature of 50 °C depending on the
soil characteristics. (In sand material the drying out phenomena will most properly start at a lower
temperature). Soil drying is a function of the cable load, its duration and the heat-flux.

Cable installation with pipe jacking will always result in that all three phase conductor cores are pulled
through the same concrete or steel pipe. The pipe surface temperature varies (among others) with the
current, phase distance, depth of the pipe, and the outer diameter of the pipe.

Drying out of the soil must be considered in particular for cable systems designed for a continuously
load and with a conductor size being equal along the whole cable route. In such a case a large depth


(above 3m) might result in a bottleneck for the current capacity of the whole link, since available
measures against the possibilities of the drying out of the soil is limited. Spreading out of the cables
beyond the inner pipe diameter is impossible. Improving the soil characteristics by substitution of soil
with better thermal performance is not possible. Injection of a "specific developed fluid with excellent
thermal properties" within the pipes will only have a minor effect, since the major temperature raise
appears in the soil.

Above mentioned heating problem can be prevented if a cooling arrangement is applied. Natural or
forced air circulation in the cable pipes can remove the heat generated by the cable cores. Alternatively
a water cooling system can be adopted. A cooling system will result in additional installation costs and
require supervision and maintenance on a larger scale than the cable system, which is practically
maintenance free.

The thermal situation of a power cable system within a microtunnel is determined by a large number of
different components:

- cables are typically laid in filled or unfilled PE or PVC ducts (sometimes, passive fire protection

```
pipes may be used)
```
- these ducts are installed inside the common tunnel tubes, either steel or concrete
- interstices between ducts and tunnel tubes are filled with special slurry
- tunnel tubes are ”welded” to surrounding subsoil by special fillers (bentonite, cement grout)
- surrounding subsoil can be of great variety with regard to geological composition, equality along

```
the route, homogeneity and, last but not least, thermal resistivity
```
- the position of the microtunnel in the ground will be different with each installation, especially with

```
regard to depth, ground water table, distance to foreign heat sources etc. The calculation of
admissible thermal ratings of the cable system has to adequately consider these parameters to
guarantee for stable thermal conditions rather than thermal runaway and drying out of soil
```
### 3.8.3.3 Water drainage

For pipe jacking, water drainage shall be considered for the implementation period. Permanent water
drainage is not recommended.

For microtunnelling, the robotisation of the mechanism allows work to be carried out in the groundwater
table without lowering the water level. This is possible up to 20 m water pressure but only for
microtunnellers which remove the earth hydraulically. The only problems can arise at the work shafts,
but there are methods to seal these watertightly.

### 3.8.3.4 Temperature of the soil/environment

Pipe jacking will be performed at a depth more than 2,5 meter in almost every installation. The
temperature at the upper surface layers (depths 0,2-1,0 m) varies during the day and week depending
on the sun heating, wind, and air temperature. This fluctuation in the soil temperature is less distinct at
1 - 3 m depth and vanishes at a larger depth. The soil temperature at large depth is almost constant and
equal to the yearly average air temperature at the particular location. This implies that a different soil
temperature can be applied for the calculation of the cable temperatures.

As microtunnels typically will be positioned in greater depths the average yearly ground temperature of
the soil will be dominant. This will be especially true, when the tunnel is below the ground water table.
If drying out of soil is avoided, stable soil temperatures can be assumed.

### 3.8.3.5 Hardness of the soil

Pipe jacking in soil consisting of rock, granite or similar soil can not be performed.

The microtunneller does not have problems in hard soils because its tools can be adjusted to the type
of soil.


Of course, there are limits to the hardness and/or the abrasiveness of the tools, e.g. with extremely hard
rocks. Screw machines are well adapted to clay conditions and therefore are used in any sort of soil
from sand to soft rocks.

Hydraulic and pneumatic earth removal machines are efficient in groundwater tables and very sticky
soils.

### 3.8.3.6 Stability of the soil

The microtunneller is badly adapted for heterogeneous soils. Once the microtunneller type and the
drilling head are chosen, a change is very difficult and costly.

Blocs or obstacles that exceed about 30 % of the drill’s diameter can not be dealt with and it is therefore
impossible to use this method in soils containing large blocks.

Certain clay soils, which are too sticky, should be avoided either.

### 3.8.3.7 Thermal resistivity of the soil

The detailed knowledge of the thermal characteristics, especially the thermal resistivity of the soil along
the microtunnel is a must before such a cable system can be designed. If changes of these parameters
cannot be excluded over the life time of the cable system a real time temperature monitoring system
with temperature sensors along the cables can be installed to identify changes and to adjust cable
ratings accordingly.

### 3.8.3.8 Seismicity

The tunnel tubes, whether steel or concrete, provide a certain mechanical protection for the cable
system inside. The degree of seismic impact, which can be withstood without damage is hard to define.

### 3.8.3.9 Archaeology

Pipe jacking can be applied close to archaeology sites within the cities. If large depths is required and
the available area for a sloping horizontal drilling to the maximum depth is insufficient a pipe jacking can
be performed.

Microtunnelling shafts can be placed closer to the edge of the item to be passed under, thus reducing
the length of the tunnel significantly compared to a HDD installation. HDD installations by their nature
require entry and exit pits positioned in locations that allow gradual approach of the HDD under the “item
to be passed”. For this reason this technique has a lower impact on areas considered sensitive for
archaeology.

### 3.8.3.10 Presence of termites

Intrusion of termites and other vermin must be prevented if an air installation is chosen for large pipe
diameters.

### 3.8.3.11 Frost

Since these technologies are performed at depths generally much larger than 1.5 m, frost does not
affect the performance of the pipe jacking operation.

### 3.8.3.12 Laying in National Parks

Due to the the extension in crossing such sensitive areas, microtunnelling and pipe jacking are not the
preferred solutions, since intermediate shafts should be foreseen frequently, thus increasing the costs
and impact on the environment.

### 3.8.4 Benefits in using microtunnelling/pipe jacking

1) Microtunnelling is a versatile technique that can achieve a very high degree of alignment accuracy
(usually with a deviation of less than 20 mm over 100 m).
2) Many microtunnelling methods have been designed that are able to deal with a variety of ground
conditions. The drilling heads can be designed to crush boulders with a diameter of up to 20 % of
the machine diameter and for tunnelling through hard rock.
3) Microtunnelling can reduce the length of a link compared to open trench, since it could avoid
longer deviations, thereby reducing associated labour costs and personnel risks. Unlike standard
trenching methods, a large increase in depth typically only results in small increases in relative
cost.


4) Microtunnelling requires less cover to sensitive structures than HDD due to its slurry pressure
control.
5) Microtunnelling shafts can be placed closer to the edge of the item to be passed under, thus
reducing the length of the tunnel significantly compared to a HDD installation. HDD installations
by their nature require entry and exit pits positioned in locations that allow gradual approach of
the HDD under the “item to be passed”.

### 3.8.5 Equipment

A range of pipe jacking excavation methods are available. In most cases, the choice of method will
depend on the type of ground and the selection of the appropriate ground support techniques.

In the smaller diameters, generally below 1.2 metres diameter microtunnelling systems are used. These
systems combine excavation methods that provide full face support, specifically TBMs and pressurised
slurry, with a purpose designed spoil removal system. These fully guided microtunneling machines,
which are likely to be applicable to the majority of cable installation requirements are auger
microtunnelling and pressurised slurry microtunnelling machines. A similar technique using guided
augers is also available for use.

In the larger diameters, generally above 1.2 m diameter various cutting heads and excavation systems
are available to suit a broad range of ground conditions.

As noted, fully automated microtunnelling systems and guided augers and the similar larger diameter
automated pipe jacks are applicable to most cable applications. A range of full face excavations
methods are available dependent on ground conditions and any requirement for face stabilisation.
These are tunnel boring machines or pressurised slurry. Additionally, earth pressure balance machines
can be used for larger diameters, generally above 1.4 metres.

A number of open face systems are also available but these are only suitable for use above 1.2 metres
diameter, owing to limitations on safe operator access, and where ground conditions permit. These are
cutter booms in rock and stable ground, backacters in semi-stable to stable soils, and for very short
drives where no alternative exists, open hand shields.

These excavation method options are described below.

### 3.8.6 Microtunnelling machines and guided augers

Fully guided microtunnelling machines are remotely controlled from the surface. They have an internal
diameter below 1.0/1.2 m and access by personnel is not permitted. These microtunnelling machines
are generally of two types, both having face support capability and characterised by their soil removal
method: pressurised slurry and auger machines:

### 3.8.6.1 Pressurized slurry microtunnelling machine;

Where a pipe jack or microtunnel is to be installed using a pressurised slurry system, the design control
and management of the slurry and the separation equipment that removes the excavated soils from the
slurry is critical to the success of the pipe jacking or microtunnelling operation.

A slurry system uses water based fluid (slurry) to transport excavated soils from the tunnelling machine
to the surface where the excavated soil is removed from the slurry enabling the re-use of the slurry for
further excavation. When slurry tunnelling process are used slurry separation plant will be required.

### 3.8.6.2 Auger microtunnelling machine;

It may also be possible to use a ‘full-face’ tunnel boring machine in which the excavated material is
transported from the face by a balanced screw auger or screw conveyor. The face is supported by
excavated material held under pressure behind the cutter head in front of the forward bulkhead.
Pressure is controlled by the rate of passage of excavated material through the balanced screw auger
or valves on the screw conveyor.

### 3.8.6.3 Guided auger boring machine.

This is also used for pipe installation where ground conditions are deemed to be suitable.

### 3.8.7 Excavation systems and cutter heads..............................................................................................

There are six types of Pipe Jacking excavation methods:

a) Tunnel Boring Machine (TBM)


b) Pressurised Slurry

c) Earth Pressure Balance Machine (EPBM)

d) Cutter Boom Shield

e) Backacter Shield

f) Open Hand Shield.

To maintain accuracy of alignment during pipe jacking, it is necessary to use a steerable shield, which
must be frequently checked for line and level from a fixed reference. For short or simple pipe jacks,
these checks can be carried out using traditional surveying equipment. Rapid excavation and remote-
control techniques require sophisticated electronic guidance systems using a combination of lasers and
screen based computer techniques.

### 3.8.8 Adaptation of the technique to the cable system design

Depending on the size of the concrete/steel pipe two different installation methods can be applied:

### 3.8.8.1 Air installation

If the concrete pipe is large (above 1,5m inner diameter) the cables can be suspended at the inner pipe
surface either as a flexible or rigid installation in air. In this case the cable installation design practice
used for cables in tunnels can be applied taking into consideration the mechanical forces during short
circuit and the thermal design (heating up of the soil and the cable insulation). A proper sealing of the

pipe must be considered in order to prevent intrusion of water, insects etc.

### 3.8.8.2 Cables laid in pipes

If a small pipe diameter is designed the cables must be laid in cable plastic pipes (made of PE or PVC)

in order to avoid damage of the cable sheath during the pulling operation. Depending on the dimensions

and weight of the cables one or three cable pipes can be used. Small cable sizes can be pulled out in
one pulling operation if the three phase conductors are attached together with a suitable tape.

Unintended overheating of cables must be prevented by substitution of the residual air volume with a
material with improved thermal characteristics. Bentonite or a similar pumpable mixture is injected
between the cable and the plastic pipe. A slurry mixture with a thermal resistivity less than 1 Km/W in
dry condition is normally selected for use between the plastic pipes and the concrete pipe.

If a trefoil formation is essential for the cable system design all three plastic pipes must be attached
firmly in trefoil and pulled through the large pipe in one operation. Increasing the phase distance to the
maximum possible can reduce the thermal mutual heating between the phase conductors. In such
installation (with pipe diameter above 1.5 m) it is necessary to adapt clearance wedges between the
cable plastic pipes.

It is not mandatory that the cable cores are located in a true trefoil formation. The current and voltage
of the screen depend on its bonding, the location of each phase conductor and the length of the whole
cable system. If close trefoil is applied in the standard trench a short length with flat formation will result
in an unbalanced screen bonding system. The screen voltage at the opened end for single point bonded
systems and the circulating current for cross bonded & two point bonded systems will increase a little
compared with a true balanced system. This however is of negligible importance if the total length of
pipe/cable installation is relatively short compared with the whole cable system length.

If the space is available more than one cable system consisting of three phase conductors can be
installed in the same concrete pipe. However, consideration related to the mutual heating and the
possibility of a cable failure caused by one system to the other system must be taken.

### 3.8.9 Environmental impact

The volume of material removed from the site in this technology type is relatively substantial. This
impacts on traffic and spoil removal, waste licenses etc. For a 1 km microtunnel of 2.5 m diameter truck
movements may be in the order of 400 movements.

Due to the large working area required at entry shaft and receiving shaft, in case of route crossing
sensitive environmental areas, it is unlikely that microtunnelling/pipe jacking technology is the best
trenchless installation compared to HDD.


### 3.8.10 Safety...............................................................................................................................................

It is advised that all works should be carried out in a safe manner with respect to all aspects of the work,
including but not limited to; site set up, fencing, handling of complex equipment, support equipment,
large numbers of personnel on site, battering of launch and exits shafts, high pressures, moving
mechanical parts, working in the vicinity of water, working in the vicinity of busy roads, heavy machinery
traffic, livestock, animals, etc.

Activities involved in the laying of network services infrastructures using microtunnelling/pipe jacking
technology shall be carried out in full compliance with current national regulation governing occupational
health and safety. Provisions specified by local administration and authorities shall also be observed
with regard to authorizations and site opening times.

In addition, consideration should be given to the presence of very deep open shafts, as well as the noise
levels during construction.

With reference to item 3.10.6.1, provisions shall be taken to consider emergency conditions that could
occur in working in confined areas, in terms of personnel evacuation, rescuing and communication.

### 3.8.11 Legal aspects

In addition to what specified in item 3.7.8, as far as magnetic field is concerned, attention must be paid
to the presence of multiple circuits in the same main pipe.

### 3.8.12 Maintenance and repair processes

Once the microtunnel and the cable installation is completed no maintenance is required.

As the tunnel tubes provide a strong mechanical protection, damages from outside are very unlikely,
thus excluding the need of mechanical repairs. If the cable should fail internally it can easily be pulled
out of the duct for exchange. Repair by cable joints in the section of the tunnel is not possible due to the
limited diameter of the ducts.

The microtunnel provides the mechanically well protected tube for the cable system. Typically one PVC
or PE duct for each cable phase is either thrust or pulled into the tunnel and fixed by drowning in injected
cement (slurry). A fourth duct can be laid and used as a reserve in the event of a problem with one of
the other phases. The position of the ducts is maintained by clearance wedges.

Putting in place the slurry is a delicate operation. This material must be spread in all the volume in order
to avoid the presence of air bubbles, which could create hot spots. The injection of the slurry must be
done without damaging the ducts.

To avoid ovalisation during injection of the slurry it is recommended to strengthen the plastic ducts by
air, water or helium pressure.

Lastly, the slurry should have a low and consistent thermal resistivity , typically around 1 Km/W.

Once this installation of ducts and filling is completed, the three single core cables can be pulled into
the ducts within the microtunnel. The ducts can be left unfilled or be filled with e.g. bentonite to improve
thermal heat dissipation.

Cable pipe with bentonite

No maintenance is required for the cable installation if the plastic pipes and the concrete/steel pipes are
injected with bentonite. In case of cable failure within the pipe installation the bentonite can be flushed
out and the cable core redrawn from the pipe.

Cable suspended in air

If the pipe installation can be accessed a regular visual inspection (each second year) can be
recommended in order to detect any deterioration of the cable suspension arrangement or to inspect if
objects or animals has intruded the installation and caused any damage. Cable clamps can be easily
removed in case of system failure.

### 3.8.13 Specific monitoring

As for HDD, even microtunnelling/pipe jacking installations could represent sometimes the circuit hot
spots.


As with any buried cable installation, greater depth generally results in lower ratings. This may be
mitigated somewhat by lower summer time ambient earth temperatures and possible better earth
thermal resistivities (TR) at the greater depth. Differently from HDD in this case it is possible to measure
the values of TR and temperature at any shaft location.

A Distributed Temperature System (DTS) is however recommended to monitor the temperature profile
along the cables/pipe environment.

Optic fibres, either integrated within a cable structure or placed close to the active phases, are now
common features in transmission cable installations for the purpose of DTS and to take advantage of
other sensing opportunities including partial discharge detection (PD) where optic fibre is both the
"sensor" and the "data transmitter".

## 3.9 Pipe Ramming

### 3.9.1 Description of the technique and method

There are a number of techniques generally more suited to shorter lengths (less than 100m), which can
offer practical solutions, sometimes with limitations on accuracy. In many circumstances, this is not
necessarily an issue for cable installation, but could impact on other infrastructure. These techniques
include pneumatic hammers used to physically thrust a steel casing through the ground, piercing tools
suitable for very small diameter installations that can be used to drag a service installation behind it,
hammers driven by compressors using rotary-percussion to provide a pilot borehole and auger boring,
which thrusts a steel casing whilst simultaneously excavating the ground using a screw auger. The latter
technique is a basic form of installation and is not to be confused with the fully steerable guided augers
and auger based microtunnelling machines, as described in the pipe microtunnelling/jacking section.

In general pipe ramming and auger boring are not fully engineered solutions and are simple in
application. They are based on very preliminary designs for shorter uncomplicated lengths.

Since this technique applies to relatively low depths, it is of high importance to accurately investigate
the existing of sub-services (pipes, cables, etc.) for all the area interested by the crossing.

TB 770 provides more details of this technique.

### 3.9.2 Limits of the technique

This technology is applicable in sandy soils, gravel, stones and wet soil and it is generally more suited
to shorter lengths (less than 100m). During this operation, all stones, with a diameter up to the casing
diameter, are engulfed into the casing. Debris will be removed once casing installation has been
completed. It must be reminded that pipe ramming is a non-steerable technique.

### 3.9.2.1 Civil works

As mentioned above, pipe ramming is possible up to about 100 m length. The site set-up consists of a
launch pit with a strong and flat laying basement, normally concrete, which holds a double T steel slide
strong enough to hold the casing weight and size. This slide is set up to be perfectly aligned with the
required bore path.The positioning of the starting pit shall enable easy insertion of the pipe elements to
be planted, also in consideration of the fact that they sometimes may be previously welded for the entire
length of the route. As for the thrust pit size, the length and the diameter of the pipe to be installed and
the overall dimensions of the complete thrust jackhammer, pipes and accessories shall be considered.
Besides the overall shape of equipment and the pipe shall be carefully assessed their operational
facilities and above all the space required for the execution of welds. It is normally appropriate to dig a
pit deepening of at least one meter below the pipe so as to allow the welding in the bottom of the jacket
tube.Once the mucking of the material present inside the pipe has been completed, the alignment of the
new section of pipe shall be carried out and the welding between the two successive sections is then
carried out. It is therefore important to have adequate space to ensure the operation described given
that, in the case of non steerable thrusts, the initial alignment is essential for the correct execution of
the laying of the entire pipe string. Equally important is the fastness of the support base which will not
move during the thrust. The larger the diameter, and consequently the weight of the pipe to be buried,
is, the greater the solidity of the supporting structure shall be. Therefore, the concrete pad on which


guide beams are fixed shall be carefully constructed, species under the water table where it will be
necessary to first create a drainage layer.

### 3.9.2.2 Drying of the soil

The design of the pipe ramming installation shall consider the possibility of drying out the soil at the
external pipe surface which might result in a thermal run away causing an insulation cable failure if the
system is heavily and continuously loaded.

Drying out of the soil can be expected to start at a continuous temperature of 50 °C depending on the
soil characteristics (in sand material the drying out phenomena will most properly start at a lower
temperature).

Drying out of the soil must be considered in particular for cable systems designed for a continuously
load and with a conductor size being equal along the whole cable route. In such a case a large depth
(above 3m) might result in a bottleneck for the current capacity of the whole link, since available
measures against the possibilities of the drying out of the soil is limited. Spreading out of the cables
beyond the inner pipe diameter is impossible. Improving the soil characteristics by substitution of soil
with better thermal performance is not possible. Injection of a "specific developed fluid with excellent
thermal properties" within the pipes will only have a minor effect, since the major temperature raise
appears in the soil. Above mentioned heating problem can be prevented if a cooling arrangement is
applied (mainly water cooling). A cooling system will result in additional installation costs and require
supervision and maintenance on a larger scale than the cable system, which is practically maintenance
free.

### 3.9.2.3 Water drainage

Pipe ramming techniques are not suitable for lay under water table.

### 3.9.2.4 Temperature of the soil/environment

Pipe ramming is performed at a depth very similar to that of open trench technologies in almost every
installation. The temperature at the upper surface layers (depths 0,2-1,0 m) varies during the day and
week depending on the sun heating, wind, and air temperature. This fluctuation in the soil temperature
is less distinct at 1-3 m depth and vanishes at a larger depth. This implies that in most of the cases the
same soil temperature used for open trench sections can be applied for the calculation of the cable
ratings.

### 3.9.2.5 Thermal resistivity of the soil

The detailed knowledge of the thermal characteristics, especially the thermal resistivity of the soil along
the pipe ramming section is a must before such a cable system can be designed. As above said, since
the installation depth is similar to that of the open trench sections, no big differences in thermal resistivity
figures are expected, unless for all portions crossing railroads, where the material used for railroad
construction could be of different nature.

If changes of these parameters cannot be excluded over the life time of the cable system a real time
temperature monitoring system with temperature sensors along the cables can be installed to identify
changes and to adjust cable ratings accordingly.

### 3.9.2.6 Seismicity

The pipes, whether steel or concrete, provide a certain mechanical protection for the cable system
inside. The degree of seismic impact, which can be withstood without damage is hard to define.

### 3.9.2.7 Frost

Since pipe ramming is performed at depths larger than 1.5 m frost does not affect the performance of
the pipe jacking operation.

### 3.9.2.8 Archaeology

Pipe ramming is normally applied to cross existing services at relatively shallow depths. It is supposed
that an archaeological survey has already been carried out along the existing service , so the results of
