the survey will be used.

Anyway, if crossings are foreseen inside cities, investigations are strongly recommended.


### 3.9.3 Equipment

A range of pipe ramming excavation methods are available. They are described below.

### 3.9.3.1 Pneumatic percussive casing thrust

With this technology, a pneumatic hammer thrusts a steel casing under streets, railways, rivers, etc.

The open-ended steel casing engulfs the soil, minimising external soil compression, avoiding voids, soil
volume changes or fractures on the surface.

This technology is applicable in sandy soils, gravel, stones and wet soil. During this operation, all stones,
with a diameter up to the casing diameter, are engulfed into the casing. Debris will be removed once
casing installation has been completed.

The site set-up consists of a launch pit with a strong and flat laying basement, normally concrete, which
holds a double T steel slide strong enough to hold the casing weight and size. This slide is set up to be
perfectly aligned with the required bore path.

Following the above the next steps are reinforcing the entry hole edge and installing a lubrication line.
The hole edge reinforcing is obtained by cutting two sections of the casing of about 30 cm depth, which
are then welded internally and externally at the hole edge. These sections can be placed in a staggered
manner, depending on where one mainly wants the debris to be pushed during ramming operations.

Subsequently a lubrication line is installed, which externally facilitates lubrication for reduced casing
friction and internally it facilitates better slurry extraction.

Lubrication is obtained by pumping water and polymer, which create both internal and external films on
the casing during ramming installation. Big water/polymer volumes at high pressure are not needed,
because they may destabilise the surrounding soil and create problems related to stability and
maintenance of the direction of the ramming operation. Lubricant reaches the open end of the casing
through a small hose, and it runs both internally and externally to the casing itself. A flexible hose is
mostly preferred to a rigid hose, because it can absorb all percussive shocks and vibrations. This line is
meant only for lubrication, not for slurry.

At this point the hammer/ramming tool is physically connected to the casing to be installed. The ramming
tool has to be perfectly aligned, so as to correctly direct and discharge all the impact energy along the
casing. For this purpose, collets and collars are made. Collets and collars distribute the percussive
energy on the entire casing circumference. Collets keep the hammer locked in position.

Once the first casing section has been pushed into the ground, subsequent casing sections can be
welded on and rammed until the requested exit point is reached.

Once the installation is done, or after every section has been installed, the casing shall be internally
cleaned of all debris. For a diameter, smaller than 0.7 m the cleaning is done with air and/or water, while
for bigger diameters manual cleaning by jet washing is employed. Table 2 below gives some idea of the
casing diameter and the maximum thrust length that might be expected to be achieved for that diameter.

```
Casing diameter [mm] Thrust length [m]
```
```
1000 ≤ 80
```
```
2500 ≤50
```
```
4500 ≤20
```
## Table 3 – Thrust length vs. casing diameter

### 3.9.3.2 Auger boring

This technology consists of casing installation by pushing the casing and simultaneously drilling the
ground through a rotating drilling head. This is an open drilling head and all spoil evacuation is done by
internal auger, which transports it out of the boring.

The first step involves the construction of a thrust chamber, of an appropriate size to hold the auger unit.
Subsequently the auger unit is placed in position, taking advantage of prepared thrust reference points.


The first pipe section to be installed is placed on the auger unit frame, and this holds internally the first
auger, and that in turn functions both to drill the hole (by a cutting front plate) and remove the spoils. As
the augur advances the trust system pushes the casing into the hole.

The process then proceeds onto pipe installation by welding at the first buried section the following one,
and so on. During the thrust operation, it is necessary to constantly monitor the thrust pressure, auger
rotation speed, effective installation length, extraction soil examination. At the end of the installation all
augers are recovered.

This technology allows drilling installation for a casing up to 1.4 m.

The thrust chamber size is essentially determined by the auger unit size, but also by pipe section length
to be installed. For pipe sections of 3 m we need to think of a thrust chamber size of 9.5 m x 4 m.

### 3.9.3.3 Piercing tool

Piercing tools or moles can be used for small diameter pipes installations up to 180 mm, especially in
street and railways crossing, domestic services like gas, water and drains, telephone and fibre optic.

The new pipe can be installed directly if connected to the piercing tool or it can be pulled in at a later
time if the hole is stable. The tool is placed and aligned by eye sight and the bore accuracy can be
considered reliable for lengths between 10 m and up to 40 m.

Because the installation process is percussive, the application of such technology is only suitable for
installation depths at least 10 times the utilised tool diameter. Failure to comply with this requirement
may cause ground heave.

It is possible to also carry out side by side installations, as long as a distance of at least four times the
drilling diameter is kept between the installations. This is to nullify the effect of compaction that the mole
itself generates in the ground.

The percussion takes place at the base of the pipe to be installed with a tool fixed to the pipe itself and
is driven by a power compressor with an adequate power. The set up depends on the size of the pipe
to be laid, the length of the crossing and the characteristics of the ground on site.

### 3.9.3.4 Down the hole hammer

In the case of the hammer technique the bore hole is pushed simultaneously through the ground using
a hammer driven by a compressor which, by tapping on the digging face, disintegrates the material
(normally rock) and this is then extracted and transported externally by a screwdriver system.

With these systems one can make straight bores at any angle (including horizontal) at depth and length
up to 100 meters. The main components of the system are:

- an internal pilot drill, which drills the central part of the hole and guides the drill string;
- a circular outer crown shoe to the pilot bit to which the pipe sleeve is welded.

The drilling takes place by means of rotary-percussion with the possibility of advancing the pilot bit
separately from the drilling shoe; the pilot bit can be extracted from the pipe casing. Cooling/lubrication
fluids (water) is during drilling used. These systems are able to handle casing pipes from 76 mm up to
1200 mm.

### 3.9.4 Environmental impact

An evaluation report of inclusion and environmental impact is normally required and shall contain the
feedback on the modifications of the hydrological state of the underground during the excavation
(evaluation on hydrogeological modifications at thrust ended regarding the aspects of the project in
general and not only to the aspects concerning the installation technique of pipelines), the feedback on
the management of spoils with particular reference to the results of drilling materials that come into
contact with lubricants or stabilizing fluids used during the pipe thrust and the feedback on the equipment
for any noise pollution (in particular of the generator).

### 3.9.5 Safety...............................................................................................................................................

Activities related to the laying of pipes by jacking methods shall be carried out in compliance with current
national regulations on health and safety in the workplace. Operators and contractors shall also comply
with ordinances imposed by local government agencies and authorities regarding permits and duration
of site activity.


### 3.9.6 Legal aspects

As for pipe-jacking, even in this case it is necessary to check for the respect of EMF requirements
dictated by national laws.

Ratings shall be checked to take into account the presence of further power sources inside the pipe, as
well as in case of steel pipes that, in spite to an optimum EMF shielding effect, could have a big impact
on the derating of the circuits.

### 3.9.7 Maintenance and repair processes

Once the pipe and the cable installation is completed no maintenance is required.

As the pipe provide a strong mechanical protection, damages from outside are very unlikely, thus
excluding the need of mechanical repairs. If the cable should fail internally it can easily be pulled out of
the duct for exchange. Repair by cable joints in the section of the pipe is not possible due to the limited
diameter of the ducts.

This technology provides the mechanically well protected tube for the cable system. Typically one PVC
or PE duct for each cable phase is either thrust or pulled into the tunnel and fixed by drowning in injected
cement (slurry). An extra duct can be laid and used as a reserve in the event of a problem with one of
the other phases.

Putting in place the slurry is a delicate operation. This material must be spread in all the volume in order
to avoid the presence of air bubbles, which could create hot spots. The injection of the slurry must be
done without damaging the ducts.

The slurry should have a low and consistent thermal resistivity of preferably 1 Km/W.

Once this installation of ducts and filling is completed, the three single core cables can be pulled into
the ducts. The ducts can be left unfilled or be filled with e.g. bentonite to improve thermal heat
dissipation.

Cable pipe with bentonite

No maintenance is required for the cable installation if the plastic pipes and the concrete/steel pipes are
injected with bentonite. In case of cable failure within the pipe installation the bentonite can be flushed
out and the cable core redrawn from the pipe.

Cable pipe unfilled

No maintenance is required for the cable installation if the plastic pipes and the concrete/steel pipes are
left empty. In case of cable failure within the pipe installation the cable core can be redrawn from the
pipe.

### 3.9.8 Specific monitoring

As for HDD and microtunnelling/pipe jacking, even pipe ramming installations could represent
sometimes the circuit hot spots.

Differently from HDD in this case it is possible to measure the values of TR and temperature in the area
where the pipe ramming is planned to be installed.

A Distributed Temperature System (DTS) is however recommended to monitor the temperature profile
along the cables/pipe environment.

Optic fibres, either integrated within a cable structure or placed close to the active phases, are now
common features in transmission cable installations for the purpose of DTS and to take advantage of
other sensing opportunities including partial discharge detection (PD) where optic fibre is both the
"sensor" and the "data transmitter".


## 3.10 Mechanical laying

### 3.10.1 Description of the technique

With ploughing, cables or conduits are installed in a direct buried environment. It may also be possible
to include backfill sand, protective polymeric cover strips and/or warning tapes simultaneously with the
cable or conduit during the installation process. By creating a narrow temporary opening in the ground,
the environmental impact is considerably reduced, when compared with traditional methods, which
require wide trenches and longer working times. This method can be applied across open terrain at an
accelerated rate compared to traditional trenching techniques.

## Figure 53 – Trench excavation with mechanical lay technique

The plough works best in soils that can be easily displaced and reused as fill. The mole plough pushes
the spoil removed by the plough back into the opening as the new utility line is fed into the opening.

TB 770 provides more details of this technique.

### 3.10.2 Limits of the technique

The ploughing method is mostly used for the laying of lightweight cables, optical fibre cables, gas pipes,
water pipes, but can also be used for medium and high voltage cables. Currently, worldwide there are
not many
experiences with this method in built-up areas.

However, the practice has application in wide open spaces such as in 'out-back' Australia, where
ploughing has application associated with terrestrial wind farms and coal seam gas projects in the
installation of cable, conduits
and gas pipes. The use of lightweight cables facilitates the installation of longer lengths.

Experiences for this techniques for HV cables are available up to 150 kV voltage level.

It can be difficult to plough with the very large cables/drums required for high voltage cables and it can
prove difficult to install more than one cable at the same time.


A major disadvantage is that the final compaction only occurs after a long time, as the smallest particles
must first be washed in by water. As a result, the final strength of the soil is not yet given after the
protective pipes have been inserted and the immediate subsequent insertion of cables into the newly
laid pipe can lead to great difficulties.

### 3.10.2.1 Civil works

This technique does not require additional civil works, since cutting of soil and cable lay is carried out
simultaneously.

It is expected that for MV cables a working area of 6 m wide would be required along the route and for
HV cables up to 10 m may be required. This is necessary to handle all equipment including the possibility
of 3 cable drums.

### 3.10.2.2 Drying of the soil

Soil drying can be attributed to natural processes as well as by induced changes. The rate of drying
depends on the location, precipitation, soil type, natural water table, air temperature, surface
temperature, wind speed, type of cover (grass, asphalt, concrete), vegetation (moisture depletion by
trees), etc. For underground power cable environment, the heat generated by the energized cable (W/m)
drives the moisture away at the cable/soil interface, and it has a significant impact on the thermal
resistivity and thus the cable rating.

Although this technique is used mainly in rural areas, it could happen that the use of controlled backfilling
material may be considered. In this case a lot of equipment has to be managed at the same time –
backfill truck, conveyor belt, hopper for backfill, plough, pulling excavator, cable drum/s.

Differently from other open trench technologies, the cable formation is typically a trefoil one and the
increase in the installation depth in order to reach more thermally stable soils is limited by the size of
the excavating tool.

### 3.10.2.3 Water drainage

Conversely to the soil-drying phenomenon, wet soil may seem more favourable to laying of HV cables
because it constitutes a natural cooling system. This is partly true. However, in those cases, the trench
cannot be backfilled as effectively with thermal backfill.

This is of concern if the soil or the backfill material is loose (granular with high voids content, poor
compaction). It should be addressed during the construction phase if it affects the stability of the soil or
the backfill around cables.

Washout of fines in the backfill material may adversely affect the stability and thermal resistivity of the
backfill and such cases have been documented.

### 3.10.2.4 Temperature of the soil/environment

As for any other laying operation, special care should be given to cable temperature during laying
(minimum as well as maximum temperatures).

### 3.10.2.5 Hardness of the soil

The plough works best in soils that can be easily displaced and reused as fill. The mole plough pushes
the spoil removed by the plough back into the opening as the new utility line is fed into the opening.

The conditions must be right for ploughing with respect to the soil not being too hard. There must also
not be too many other obstacles, which would prevent ploughing. The ground must be sufficiently soft
for the plough to operate.

In common with other cable installation methods, there should be a full investigation of the ground
geology to ensure that the various characteristics are known like thermal resistivity, hardness,
homogeneity, water content, hidden underground obstacles (bombs, big stones, etc...). Subsidence
should not arise with ploughing.

### 3.10.2.6 Stability of the soil

Stability of the soil is not normally a concern, both in terms of trench stability and in terms of safety,
since the excavation size is very small.


### 3.10.2.7 Thermal resistivity of the soil

In general, no extra backfill material is foreseen for ploughing although sometimes it is applied above.
The trench is immediately closed by the ground that is pushed away by the ploughing machine. The
disadvantage with ploughing is that the backfill is normally not controllable, as is the case with other
direct burial technique.

### 3.10.2.8 Laying in national park

In National Parks, golf courses, forested areas, where services may need to be installed, there is much
less chance of having environmental damage if ploughing is used over open cut methods.

### 3.10.2.9 Existing services

Other utilities in the ground may interfere with the routing of a plough, as the installation has to be
interrupted so they can be crossed.

### 3.10.2.10 Land registration

For ploughing technique, the ground must be relatively level. Slopes higher than about 25°, ditches, and
other obstacles etc., are difficult to manage.

### 3.10.3 Installation technique

For trefoil laying, the cables must be bound together, if the cable guides in the plough cannot maintain
the cables in the trefoil position until the backfill covering is in place. Conduits must be installed in
bundles.

One must also ensure that the maximum bending radius of the cable is not exceeded during the
ploughing process.

Note with this technique it may be difficult to install mechanical protection slabs and warning tapes, so
the installed cable may be more liable to damage at a later stage. In the most countries, due to the local
electrical regulations, mechanical protection slabs may be required above cables.

Achievable depths for ploughing extend to 2.0 m.

Of course, it is necessary to have a very clear route without obstacles in order to use the ploughing
technique. While some ploughing machines can cope with uneven ground and shallow waterways each
obstacle like other services & roads etc will interrupt the laying operation.

### 3.10.4 Equipment and spaces

The equipment is composed of:

- Conveyor belt feeding controlled backfill into hopper
- Hopper for feeding controlled backfill into ploughed opening in ground and around cable/s being
    laid
- Plough for opening the ground and feeding cable/s into the ground
- Tracked excavator for pulling the plough
- Cable drums


## Figure 54 – Typical equipment for mechanical lay technique

Note controlled backfill may be required for ploughing because

a) The ground contains stones, which could damage the cable serving over time and lead to cable faults

b) The in-situ soil has a high thermal resistivity, which would decrease the potential rating of the circuit

### 3.10.5 Cable design

The cable design must take into account the uncertainty regarding the thermal resistivity of the backfill
material and whether it needs to be replaced. In addition, if special backfill is not installed, there is a risk
that potential cavities can be created, when the soil falls back around the cable. Generally special sand
with good thermal characteristics, is used, if the native backfill needs to be replaced.

The outer serving of the cable represents an important factor, when considering the use of a ploughing
system. This is due to the fact that the outer serving has to resist the external environment i.e. possible
damage during installation or damage at a later stage due to penetration of stones in the cable.

The metallic sheath and cable serving design may also be adapted to provide a lightweight cable design,
which will allow longer cable lengths to be transported and installed. Of course, the design must be
robust enough to withstand the rigours of this installation technique, without affecting the performance
of the system in the long term.

### 3.10.6 Environmental impact

Depending on location e.g. beside a road there could be considerable traffic implications.

### 3.10.7 Safety...............................................................................................................................................

In comparison to other trenchless technologies, space is required along the entire route during the
installation process for all the required equipment.

Depending on location e.g. beside a road there could be considerable traffic implications.


### 3.10.8 Maintenance and repair processes

If a hard mortar-cement is used as a backfill, it’s difficult to repair or remove the cable/s. In general, no
special backfill is used with ploughing, so it is relatively easy to remove/repair the cable/s.

## 3.11 Bridges and water crossings

The crossing of waterways, roads or railways is commonly achieved by the use of a drilled duct or pipe
installation within which one or more cables may be installed. However, underground cable circuits may
need a means of traversing obstructions where an underground crossing is either not possible,
environmentally acceptable or would require excessive expenditure. An example would be the crossing
of a river located at the bottom of a narrow but steep sided gorge. Here a cable within a duct installed
by horizontal directional drilling may significantly de-rate the power cable due to the depth at which the
drill must be installed beneath ground level at various positions along the drill profile. The use of two
shafts and an interconnecting ventilated tunnel, to avoid cable derating, may be an option but this is
likely to be at significant additional expense. In such cases, a bridge capable of carrying the power
cables may provide a cost effective solution.

### 3.11.1 Description of the technique

Bridges carrying power cables may be divided into two broad types, either dedicated structures or
shared structures:

- A dedicated cable bridge is one which is constructed to carry one or more cable circuits on the same
    structure.
- A shared bridge structure usually and primarily provides a crossing for other services such as road
    or rail but is also able to carry power cables. Footbridges may also be able to carry cables. A shared
    bridge structure is generally owned by a third party and the possibility of installing high voltage
    power cables on the structure may not have been considered within the original bridge design. Cigre
    technical brochure 403 "Cables in Multipurpose or Shared Structures" may prove a useful reference
    for information on the installation of cable on a multi-utility bridge.

It is necessary to avoid any interaction, deliberate or otherwise, between the cables and the general
population. Cables can be mistaken for more benign utilities such as water pipes or drainage ducts and
therefore the use of notices and warnings of electrical hazards are additional precautions that are
recommended.

Where a dedicated cable bridge is to be constructed, the most significant engineering effort will be
required from the civil and structural engineering disciplines who must ensure that the bridge has
suitable foundations and is mechanically robust. The design must consider both the forces the cable
system will impart onto the structure as well as the natural and transient forces anticipated such as
seismic disturbance and severe climatic conditions. For the crossing of a navigable river, canal, road or
railway it must provide sufficient safe vertical and horizontal clearance for traffic travelling beneath its
span, if any. To formulate a bridge design which is fit for location and cable use it must be constrained
by the requirements of the power cable system and by environmental considerations and stakeholder
requirements; such as sympathetic design and construction. The bridge design must also comply with
any statutory requirements.

In addition to the spanning of physical obstructions on a cable route, dedicated cable bridges may also
be deployed to span across thermal impediments, for example the crossing of: a large number of cable
circuits where the ground is thermally saturated or over a heat source such as a large pipe carrying hot
fluids. In these cases a bridge may be used to elevate the cables over the thermally saturated ground
or heat source providing a sufficiently naturally ventilated air gap between the heat source and the
underside of the bridge.

It is sometimes possible to utilise an existing structure, such as a road or rail bridge to support power
cables. A common example of a shared bridge structure is for cables to be laid in the deck of a road
bridge or attached by cleats (directly or in ducts) to the bridge structure. Bridge owners or other
occupiers may offer opposition to the passage of power cables across their bridge particularly if the
cables inhibit or obstruct the current or future use or maintenance of the bridge structure.

The installation of cables into the shallow deck of a road bridge will be a candidate for additional physical
protection and visible warnings to reduce the risk of cable damage and alert the unwary to the cable’s


presence. The practice of burying cables is to afford mechanical protection to them when the land they
occupy is put to additional use. It is also likely that there are statutory requirements, particularly on public
rights of way, regarding protection of the public from the hazards that HV power cables can present.
The requirements of land use, hazard mitigation and cable protection significantly influence the selection
of a minimum depth of burial for power cables at different voltages. During bridge maintenance or the
excavations performed for other utilities, both above ground/bridge deck warnings and additional
mechanical protection of the cables may prevent damage and risk to life. One UK national engineering
recommendation (ENA Engineering Recommendations (EREC) C98 Issue 1) recommends the use of
steel plates above the cables as a means of additional mechanical protection. Consideration must also
be given to the mechanical protection and warning notices provided at the approach to the bridge
particularly where the cables transit from their ‘standard’ depth of burial to the reduced depth of cover
on the bridge deck.

When installing power cables alongside tramways or railways powered by AC or DC supplies,
consideration of the cable system earthing system design to avoid the unintentional passage of traction
return current on the power cable screen can avoid both a potential, if transient, derating of the cable
circuits. These traction return currents may be present on the cable screen and the bonding leads
whether the power cables are not in service or not and only appear when a train is in a track section
loosing return current to the power cable screen. This hazard is present for all cable systems laid in
proximity to electrified railways, whether on bridge structures or not.

Where cables are installed on long bridges where significant inductive coupling and impressed voltages
may appear on parallel services, such as telecom cables, studies considering the impact of such
interactions should be considered. Mitigation of inductive coupling may be provided by; avoidance of
cable installations alongside other current carrying services, passive or active cable screening methods,
the selection of a geometric cable laying arrangement (e.g. trefoil) which reduces the inductive coupling
and the selection of a suitable power cable screen bonding and earthing arrangement (calculation of
inductive coupling and impressed voltages and currents are discussed in Cigre Technical Brocure TB
283 "Special bonding of high voltage power cables").

Cables when installed on road or rail bridges in particular will be subject to vibration due to the traffic
travelling across the bridge. This vibration can adversely effect a cable’s sheath, particularly on self
contained fluid filled cables, and result in the pressure retaining sheath cracking with a subsequent loss
of fluid to the environment. The use of aluminium or copper sheaths with their higher fatigue
performance, rather than lead sheaths, can significantly improve cable sheath performance. Where
vibration fatigue performance of a particular cable sheath design is unknown then physical fatigue
testing of the cable design should be considered to simulate the life service performance.

There are a variety of means of by which a cable system may be secured to a bridge. These include
attachment using cleats, passage through ducts (fixed to or embedded within the bridge) and
containment within bridge trough-work. The forces produced by the cables, and to be withstood by the
bridge, will impact on the bridge design.

If the bridge requires a significant change in elevation or is of sufficient length the pressurised hydraulic
fluid system design may require additional stop joints or feed tanks to be installed off the bridge to avoid
these more bulky items being installed on the bridge structure.

Bridge structures will expand and contract with ambient temperature and solar radiation. If the bridge
deck is sufficiently long it will require bridge expansion joints to be located within the bridge design. The
bridge expansion joints allow relative movement between connecting bridge and deck sections and
between the end deck sections and the bridge abutments.

Where the cables on a bridge traverse an expansion joint, the cable installation design must
accommodate this movement without reducing the cable’s design service life. The relative thermal
movement of a bridge deck compared to the cable will be concentrated at the expansion joint positions.
The cable layer most susceptible to axial fatigue will be any metallic radial water seal, particularly on
pressurised fluid filled cables where metal fatigue due to localised and repeated axial strain can cause
the cable’s metallic sheath to crack. For pressurised fluid filled cables the crack develops into a leak
with a loss of pressurised fluid. There are several methods which may be employed for cables to safely
traverse an expansion joint:

- The use of cleating systems with flexible arrangements allowing the expansion to be accommodated
    within either vertical sagging/horizontal waving


- Ridged cleating systems with the cleats bolted to a shaped-plate former which deforms in a
    controlled manner as the expansion gap opens and closes. These methods have proven successful
    as they allow the bridge movement to be accommodated by cable bending rather than axial strain.
- Rigid cleat arrangements where the cleats are constrained laterally but axial movement (relative to
    the bridge) is permitted by installing the cleats on low friction bushings located between the bridge
    and the cleat. This design allows the bridge to expand or contract beneath the cables as they
    maintain their position relative to the ground.
- Cable sagging in chambers located at the ends of the bridge where cable slack feeds into the
    expansion joint on the bridge deck.
- For pipe type cables the use of rollers or bushings such that pipe may move independently of the
    bridge.

### 3.11.2 Water crossings

Apart from the use of existing or dedicated bridges or structure, the crossing of water courses can be
achieved with the use of trenchless technologies as mentioned in above chapters.

These construction technolofies are progressively replacing the “Embedding” technology.
This technique consists of excavating a river bed from a barge or with an amphibious machine, burying
the ducts or the cables and filling up the trench.
Trenching can be performed by:
a) Dredging

b) Blasting

c) Jetting operated by divers

Usually, the use of these methods is limited to rather shallow waters, practically up to depths of about
20 m, which is usually enough for river crossings. The environmental impact of such activity is by far
higher than those caused by trenchless technologies.

### 3.11.3 Limits of the technique

In general dedicated bridges are more expensive to design, construct and maintain than buried cable
alternatives where they exist. The bridge structure will invariably require maintenance over the circuit
life of a cable system, this is can be expensive as can the patrolling of the bridge to inspect any security
measures in place to limit vandalism and avoid cable theft.

Dedicated bridges may require an architectural design so as to limit the visual impact of the structure.
The installation of cables on the external visible surfaces of an existing bridge can also give rise to an
unacceptable visual impact.

The use of fluid filled cables on bridge structures is possible but can be more problematic particularly
where the installation causes strain to the cable sheath due to expansion, vibration and/or repetitive
movement The use of low pressure self contained pressurised fluid filled cables on a bridge may be less
desirable due to the risk of cable fluid leaks (for example over waterways) requiring the use of another
cable construction type.

### 3.11.4 Civil works

The contribution of the power cable engineer is to inform the bridge designers with regards to:

- The installation limitations of the cable such as the minimum bending radii permissible, with and
    without formers. For practicable purposes the largest cable bending radii should be used. The
    cable’s minimum bending radii should only be used when absolutely necessary. The use of the
    largest bending radii will reduce the installation forces required and the ease with which the cables
    may be installed and moved into their final position
- The cable or duct cleating requirements, in both type, size and spacing to be used on the structure
    both along straight sections and at bend positions
- The personnel access and installation space required to attend to the cables, and if necessary joints,
    to be installed on the structure. This access should also include the provision for any repairs or
    circuit maintenance (for example if link equipment is located on the bridge)
- The thermal requirements of the cables to maintain the rating for an appropriate cable design


- The magnitude of the thermo-mechanical and electro-mechanical forces which the cables may be
    expected to produce
- The nature of the safety and environmental hazards introduced to the design due to the presence
    of cables. This information should cover the hazards present during; construction, operation,
    maintenance, repairs and during decommissioning
- The vulnerability of the cables with regard to physical interference by third parties and the level of
    security required to avoid cable damage or harm to others. This should include physical protection
    and warning signage

### 3.11.5 Seismicity

The resilience of a cable bridge's structure to seismic activity is an obvious design consideration for the
bridge design engineer. If the bridge cannot survive a seismic event then the expectation that any power
cable on the bridge will survive the same event cannot be high. Bridge designs that allow movement,
both vertically and horizontally, between bridge members during seismic events will require cable
system to accept such movement. Where cables are secured by cleats any relative displacement of
adjacent cleats during a seismic event, must be survivable by the installed cable. Rigid cleating of power
cables at fixed cleat positions which do not permit the cable to flex but requires the cables to withstand
the high and rapid compressive and tensile loads of bridge movement may not survive. Power cables,
in particular fluid filled power cables with a pressure retaining sheath prone to cracking, are unlikely to
react well to such an endurance requirement. Flexible cable arrangements which allow some length
dilation and contraction or designs that can mechanically isolate the cleats from bridge movement (for
example by sliding) may improve survivability.

Similar constraints exist at positions where the cables transition between the ground and the bridge
support (e.g. buried direct or in ducts and the cable support method employed on the bridge). The
anticipated relative movement between the ground and the cable securing positions on the bridge will
be required to provide and allow for an acceptable level of relative movement if the cable is to survive.

It is common for cable installations on bridges to allow for slow thermal expansion and contraction of
the bridge due to climatic variations. The installation of cables on shared structure may also require the
cables to withstand some vibration (for example road traffic). In the case of a seismic event the bridge
movements can more rapid and larger in magnitude though occurrence usually less frequent. In New
Zealand, the crossing of the Auckland Harbour bridge required a thermal dilatation of +/-50mm but a
seismic allowance of +/-250mm and was achieved using a dilatation/expansion mechanism ("220kV
North Auckland and northland (NAaN) Project in Auckland, New Zealand", Rahman,N, B1-103, Cigre,
2016, Paris). A description of the design and testing of a bow spring device is described in a JICABLE
paper published in 2007 ("Verification of Mechanical Support For Cables Used On Bridge Structures at
Dilation Points", Rouillard V., B8-5, JICABLE 2007, Paris Further discussion of cable installation
arrangements on bridges can be found in Cigre Technical Brochure 403 "Cable Systems in Multi
Purpose or Shared Structures", TB 403, WG B1.08, Cigre, 2010, Paris).

In countries enduring regular higher seismic activity such as Japan, there is a preference to use cables
in tunnels due to a higher resistance to seismic events.

### 3.11.6 Frost.................................................................................................................................................

Where cables are exposed to extremely cold weather they must be capable of withstanding the sub zero
temperatures. Cable systems, including joints and terminations, should be tested to demonstrate that
they can operate within the thermal environment. The limits on thermal performance will include:

- shrinkage of components such as rubber bushings within joints and terminations.
- The hardening of seals and ‘o’ rings.
- The operating range of compression springs in dry type accessories at low temperatures.
- The low temperature effect on insulating fluids employed in cable an accessories.

The testing should include sustained periods of cold temperatures in the testing process to demonstrate
that the accessories when fully chilled throughout can withstand a sudden return to service.


### 3.11.7 Route mapping and topography

Where cables are installed on an existing structure, such as a road bridge, the position of the cable
within the structure, and any joints and link equipment should be indicated on the cable installation
records and on any shared structure drawings provided for updating by the bridge owner.

For new bridge structures the civil and mechanical design of the bridge will need recording and details
provided to the bridge owner for future reference. As a visible structure the location of the bridge should
be selected where possible to blend (where possible and desirable) with the natural environment, this
may include constructing a bridge with a low visual profile and positioning it within the topography such
that it does not break the skyline when viewed.

### 3.11.8 Installation techniques

For cable bridges which can be traversed using a pipe or ducted solution the cable may be installed on
the bridge in the same manner as that used for buried ducts or pipes using the same equipment and
duct lubrication (preferably) biodegradable.

Where cables are installed in pipes or ducts installed on the bridge these should undergo the same
proving and cleaning procedure as ducts installed underground.

### 3.11.9 Installation methods

If the permanent bridge design does not provide for a safe walkway where the cables or ducts may be
accessed, then a means of access will need to be to install cable installation equipment such as a bond
wire and cable rollers as well as cleats, cable and joints. For cleated installations some manipulation of
the cable from its pulled-in position to its final cleated will be required. The design should also consider
the means of dismantling and removal of the installation equipment and its possible later reinstatement
when/if the cable system requires repair, replacement or decommissioning.

On long bridges it may be necessary to use multiple drum lengths of cable to complete a traverse.
Theoretically the use of very long cable lengths (transported on a carousel or a coil by water or on very
large drums) may be considered either with flexible joints (which are assembled at the factory) or without
joints as a single length. Such cables would need to be installed by the use a distributed force cable
installation techniques such as motorised rollers as has been used on some tunnel installations in Japan.
However, most structures containing multiple cable drum lengths include rigid joints assembled in situ,
this is usually more practicable for an installation where a future repair would in any case include an in-
situ joint in preference to the extraction and replacement of an end to end cable length. Drum length of
cables may be pulled from a drum stand position on to the bridge and pulled along the bridge length
through joint bay positions until it arrives at its final position for jointing.

Where joints are installed on the bridge a preconceived joint bay design, with cable routing through
cleats, including any phase transposition and positioning of any link equipment and bonding leads
should be designed prior to cable installation commencing. If earthing is required this may be catered
for by connection to earth tape running along the structure and/or earth tape installed down bridge
support to a suitable earth mat. If earth tapes are installed along the bridge consideration of circulating
currents and their effects should be considered as should any currents induced into the bridge
metalwork.

Jointing shelters installed on the bridge should be weather proof and suitably secured to avoid bad
weather dislodgement. For HV and EHV cables both power and lighting supplies will be required within
the enclosure. It will also be necessary to safely transport the jointing materials and any flammable
gasses used to the jointing position. The health and safety of those on the structure must be considered
including means of evacuation should this be necessary (e.g. a fire).

### 3.11.10 Equipment and spaces

Where cables are fixed to the structure in air, a design risk analysis should include the hazards of cable
exposure. The cables should be sufficiently protected and operated to prevent harm to or by the cables.
This may include limiting access to some areas of the bridge by personnel when cables are in service if
necessary.

The metallic content of power cables can make them attractive for theft and their flammability a possible
target for vandalism or arson. The likelihood of deliberate interference with the cables by third parties
differs by location. Methods of deterring interference include: fully enclosing the cables with mechanical
protection, installing them within a secure bridge cavity, making access to the bridge structure secure


(for example by anti intruder fencing and cut proof locks). Where a cable asset is considered to be of
high importance the use electrified fencing and intruder alarms have also been used.

Areas of cable vulnerability include the cable entry points to the structure, these are commonly located
at or near an abutment or bridge support. At these locations the cables, if buried, will usually emerge
from the ground and ascend onto the structure. At these locations buried cables are at a shallower depth
and additional warning signs and mechanical protection both above and below ground level will lower
the risk of accidental mechanical damage due to uninformed excavations and will discourage intentional
interference.

Heat generated by cables in service must be effectively dissipated when the cables are on a bridge. By
far the greatest proportion of cables fixed to dedicated bridge structures are naturally air cooled. If the
cables are contained within an enclosure on the bridge, consideration of the needs of adequate
ventilation to remove the warmed air from the enclosure is required.

Solar radiation on an enclosed metal bridge can also add an oven-like effect which can significantly
increase the temperature of the air and the contents of a cable enclosure. The installation of multiple
circuits, or other heat generating services in an enclosure will add further to the difficulties of keeping
the temperature within the enclosure acceptable.

One method to achieve adequate ventilation is to fabricate the sides of the enclosure from metal sheets
with ventilation gaps between partially overlapping layers in a louvered effect. The use of grills to the
external environment will also assist, particularly when creating walkways on the underside of a bridge.
Ventilation studies using computational fluid dynamics (CFD) may be employed to assist with the
ventilation design. Natural ventilation is usually adequate to cool the cables where the cables have been
rated for installation in the thermally demanding underground environment.

For buried cable circuits that are installed with separate water pipe cooling systems, the water cooling
pipes can accompany the cables over the bridge and may assist with cooling a bridge enclosure.
Assisted cooling of cables using cooling water pipes or air fans on a bridge structure may also offer a
design solution. However, the installation of additional assisted cooling on a bridge is generally
considered undesirable due to the requirements for periodic maintenance and inspection of the cooling
system, its control and LV supply requirements to maintain adequate reliability over the circuit service
life.

The placing of multiple circuits on a cable bridge or within the same enclosure may prove undesirable
should it be possible for a single event to lead to the failure of multiple circuits. It is therefore sensible to
avoid a bridge becoming an unnecessary pinch point where multiple essential circuits providing mutual
redundancy are located on the same bridge introducing the possibility of simultaneous damage.

A significant hazard in a ventilated enclosure containing cables is fire. In such cases the cable materials
could fuel a fire which can consume all cables on the bridge and cause irreparable damage to the bridge
structure. The consequences of fire to cables fixed to bridge structures become increasingly more
severe with the fuel load offered by the cables themselves. Whilst cable coatings and coverings provide
some degree of fire resistance all current HV cable insulation materials are flammable and if the
exposure to fire is sustained and intense the cables will eventually add fuel to the fire. The damage to
heavily fuel loaded cable bridges are particularly susceptible to an arson attack. The use of auto-reclose
on circuits installed in air should be avoided due to the fire risk.

On short bridge traverses where joints are not required, the use of air filled ducts can ease cable
installation. The ducts will provide some mechanical protection and may provide an alternative to a
bridge enclosure. The use of ducts should be considered with regard to their additional fuel load should
a fire occur, even PVC will eventually burn in a fierce flame giving off noxious. Care must also be taken
to ensure that the cable installation allows sufficient cable dilation and contraction to prevent excessive
cable strain.

High current cables passing through the iron works of the bridge or cable supports should not be
constructed in such a way that a single phase of an AC cable is encircled by steelwork to form an
induction loop; where circulating currents will occur and cause additional heating of the cable
environment by the iron ring effect.

Where cable phases are widely spaced, it has been found that power cables carrying in excess of 2000
A can produce eddy currents generating significant heat in the steel bolts securing cable cleat halves.
At full load the heat generated was calculated to be sufficient to damage the fibre reinforced plastic


cleats if the cables operated at full and continuous load. The use of non-magnetic stainless steel bolts
in place of galvanised mild steel bolts can avoid such overheating where such a risk exists.

### 3.11.11 Environmental impact

A cable bridge is designed to provide a crossing over an obstruction to the cable route. Directional
drilling technology, affordability, lack of future maintenance requirements and low long term visual
impact, if any, has seen the requirements for cable bridge structures for cable installations has reduced.
However, there are still some projects where, due to existing underground structures or ground
conditions a cable bridge may still be required.

The construction of a new cable bridge and its environmental impact will largely depend on the sensitivity
of the location in which it is constructed and any perceived loss of visual amenity. Cable bridges are
generally purely functional although there are examples where some effort has been made to decrease
the visual impact of the structure by either improving its appearance, minimising its size or locating it
(where available by cable route selection) in an area of lower visual sensitivity. The obstruction to be
crossed by a bridge will also have some demands on the design and bridge construction methods.
Transport routes such as navigable water ways, railways and roads will all require minimum height
constraints. Water ways may contain sensitive species where the foundations of a bridge may no
encroach to within a given distance to prevent accidental pollution by construction run off. The
construction method for the bridge will need to consider the disruption to these transport routes which
may not be welcome by the owners, users and governance of the transport route.

### 3.11.12 Maintenance and repair processes

The inspection usually covers the following:

- Check for abnormalities (damage, corrosion, etc.) in ducts and cable supports from under the bridge
    via visual inspection using a binoculars. For rivers, visual inspections via boats or barges should be
    conducted as necessary. In recent years, the use of drones for inspections has been considered.
- Check the status of intrusion prevention equipment and check for signs of intrusion. Since cable
    installations on bridges are more susceptible to external damage and fire than cables buried in the
    ground, regular monitoring of their condition is important.

For important cable assets, it is desirable to use an aerial work vehicle or scaffolding to directly touch
the structure (ducts and cable supports) and check for abnormalities (corrosion, cracks, looseness, etc.).
Determine whether a detailed investigation or repairs are necessary based on the results of the
inspection.

The space for repairs is often very limited. If damage occurs, replacement of the cable along the whole
length of the bridge may be needed. If the bridge has space for installment, an extra duct should be
installed in case a cable fault occurs.

### 3.11.13 Commissioning and de-commissioning............................................................................................

If cables are laid along a bridge using a duct, it is normally easy to remove the cables if they have to be
replaced.

## 3.12 Innovative techniques

The purpose for this chapter is to describe some new innovative techniques for cable installation.
Two new installation methods have been considered mature enough to be included in this Technical
Brochure.
One is a hybrid method combining Direct burial and Mechanical laying - refered to as a Digging box.
The other is a new method for installing cables into pre-installed ducts - Flushing of HV-Cables

### 3.12.1 Digging box

### 3.12.1.1 Description of the technique

High voltage cable installation with a digging box is essentially a development of the open trench
technique. The two techniques require the same amount of space during installation and the same
installation equipment.


The big advantage for the digging box compared to the open trench however, is when the cables are to
be installed in an area which could be very wet or with some unstable soil conditions. Instead of having
the complete trench open for the cable installation the use of the digging box allows installation of the
high voltage power cable with only a fraction of the trench open. When using the digging box, the
complete cable installation from opening the trench, installing the cables, thermal backfilling and
installation of warning tiles are done in a single process, eliminating the risk of a collapsing trench.

This technique can also be used with protective tubes.

## Figure 55 – Principle of the digging box. Pictures taken during test of a digging box

### 3.12.1.2 Route selection

As for the open trench technique the use of the digging box is mostly practicable in areas with less to
none other infrastructures, and most preferably over open fields.

There are no limitations in the actual profile of the landscape, as the digging box just follows the surface
of the ground, and hence the cables are always installed in the designed depth.

As for the open trench the digging box cannot be used when crossing roads, water bodies or other
obstacles. These obstacles must be crossed with another technique e.g. horizontal directional drilling.

### 3.12.1.3 Civil works

The first thing to be done when the final cable route is in place is to prepare the crossing of all mayor
obstacles like roads, water bodies etc.

When these crossings are prepared the cables are ready to be pulled. The cables are pulled on rollers
and one by one placed on the ground just next to the actual cable route. Where the cables are required
to pass beneath a trench crossing service (such as a buried water pipe) the cables are pulled beneath
and crossing the obstruction and returned to the ground surface. The digging box construction allows it
to be dismantled and reassembled around the cables on either side of the obstruction.


## Figure 56 – Prepared crossing obstacle and pulled cables along the cable route..............................

After removal and storage of the top soil the digging box is ready to be put to use. The digging box is
lowered into an excavated starting position. The digging box is prepared with the correct cable
guideways from the front top entry to the rear bottom cable exit. The cables to be installed (including
any associated control cables to be placed in the same trench), are threaded through the digging box
in the correct spacing and exit the box at the designed depth. The rear half of the digging box is filled
with cable surround which has the required thermal performance. The material is then placed around
the cables at the rear of the digging box and finally the cable protective warning tiles are installed. The
digging box is attached to the excavator which excavates the trench and pulls the digging box along the
cut trench. After the placement of the cable warning tiles the trench is backfilled and the top soil replaced
and the cable installation is complete.

## Figure 57 – Digging box in use

With good planning this technique is proven as fast as installation with traditional open trench.

At the end of each cable section the jointing bays are excavated just as with the use of other techniques.
When the joints are then complete the bays are backfilled and the area is reestablished.

### 3.12.1.4 Limitations of the technique

Sharp bends

A limitation for the digging box is very tight bends, as it is quite a big metal structure. However, if it is
necessary to do a very tight bend the digging box is easy to dismantle and put together again. This
meaning that the limitations for the use of the digging box are the cable limitations being lowest
temperature for pulling and minimum bending radius.


Low temperature

The digging box method is not suitable when the temperature is below the lowest allowed temperature
for the cable. Normally it is possible to heat up the whole cable on the drum in a building or in a heated
tent. When pulling the cable quickly in the traditional open trench, the temperature can remain above
the lowest allowed temperature for the cable until the handling of the cable is finished. This is not
possible using a digging box, thus even a heated cable will cool down before handling during all burial
for 3 cables are complete.

Many crossings

The digging box is not suitable for a cable trench containing many existing crossing pipelines and many
HDDs. It takes much time for every crossing and every HDD, thus the digging box has to be separated,
the cables has to be stretched through the ducts, the digging box has to be moved and assembled at
the other side.

Cable handling at HDD

The cable manufacturer has to approve the way the civil worker handles the cable while stretching the
overlength at every HDD or any ducts. It has to be clarified how to attach to the cable surface and how
much it’s allowed to pull in the cable at this position.

### 3.12.1.5 Soil considerations

As stated one of the advantages by using the digging box is that it can be used in wet areas and in
areas with unstable soil conditions.

As the digging box is used with a large excavator there is no need for considerations for soil hardness
or stability provided that the excavator alone can open the ground. Drying of the soil is calculated in the
design phase as the cables are installed in the designed depth, with the designed spacing and with the
designed thermal backfill, hence no risk of further drying.

### 3.12.1.6 Hazards

Use of the digging box is a quite safe installation method. Compared to the open trench technique, the
risk of falling in to the trench is almost eliminated as there is no trench left open.

One major hazard for this technique is that the cables are left free on the ground. Here they are
vulnerable to damage by falling objects, being driven over, vandalized or stolen. This is a very important
aspect that must be taken into consideration.

Of course, there is also the normal risk to personnel when working with big machines and high voltage:

- Hit by excavator
- Hit by materials dropped.
- Risk of falling into trench or hole
- Risk of induced voltage in the cable
- Etc.

### 3.12.1.7 Interfaces

As for all other cable installation techniques there are several interfaces that must be considered. Some
of these are:

- Contractual responsibility for the cable
- Working under a live overhead line
- Termination of the cable at the substation.
- Etc.

For the digging box technique, the most critical of these interfaces are truly the contractual question of
responsibility for the cable. As the cable are left on the ground for some time waiting for installation
there is a big risk of damage during this time that must be handled prior to installation.


### 3.12.1.8 Environmental impact.

The digging box technique is both quite sensitive to the environment but also quite rough. The working
area needed have a width of 13-18 meters where the environment is influenced. In the actual cable
trench the soil is dug up in some depth and will hence experience a rough treatment, but the other areas
are only under a very limited influence.

If it is decided to cut down some trees or other vegetation along the way this will be seen in the
landscape. Most cable owners do not want any new trees or vegetation replanted on top of their cables,
but it is possible to find trees or shrubs with roots that do not go that deep into the ground to mitigate
the impact on the landscape.

### 3.12.2 HV-cable pushing -flushing

### 3.12.2.1 Description of the technique

This installation technique is an alternative to a normal wire-cable-pull in already installed ducts. The
technique uses pressurized water and simply flushes the HV-cable into the ducts as normal procedure
with a FO-cable, but in a much larger scale.

First the ducts are installed either in open trench or by HDD and then calibrated and controlled. After
this the cable is mounted with a pig fit for the size of the duct. It must fit perfectly to keep the pressurized
water behind it. The cable is guided into the ducts by the Flushing-unit which is already connected to
the duct and the unit is also able to push on the cable as it has a caterpillar included.

If the ducts are installed properly and the pig fits the duct the cable can be flushed into the ducts with
10 - 15m/min at 10-12bar. The pressure inside the ducts must not exceed the limit for the actual ducts.

## Figure 58 – Flushing unit connected to prolonged duct


### 3.12.2.2 Route selection

No major concerns must be considered using this technique. The limitations are more from the
installation of the ducts, which for instance doesn’t allow for very tight bends. Experience have been
made with Ø160 SDR 17 ducts with a Minimum Bending Radius (MBR) of 5,6 meter with no issues
regarding the flushing of cables.

The elevation of the route can be of consideration. It is easier to flush the cables downhill, but elevation
differences along the route - hills/valleys - are not more critical than by normal wire-pull

### 3.12.2.3 Civil works

The ducts are installed as normal procedure covered in another section, however the size of the ducts
can be considered. In principal the ducts should have the normal size of about 1,5 ratio inner diameter
vs cable diameter. As the ducts however will be filled with water for the cable flushing it can be
considered to minimize the duct size to a minimum – a ratio of approx. 1.45 has proven to work.

The ducts which normally ends in the bottom of the joint bay have to be prolonged temporarily during
the cable flushing. They must be long enough to come out of the joint bay and onto ground level. Here
the end is connected to the Flushing-unit guiding and controlling the speed of the cable. The unit also
has a flange on the duct which seals the duct and keeps the water pressure inside.

The cable drum must have a very straight line through the guide and into the duct for the installation to
be successful.

## Figure 59 – Straight line from the cable drum through the flushing unit

The technique uses water; hence it is necessary to have access to quite a lot of water. However, if the
flange and the pig connected to the cable fits the duct, the waste of water is very limited and hence the


water used is the volume of the duct minus the volume of the cable. The water could come from a fire
tap or be delivered to site in a container. If the pig on the cable fits the ducts properly, there will come
almost no water through at the receiving end. This means that the water used for flushing the cable will
remain inside the duct, and here also aid the thermal aspect of the instalaltion.

### 3.12.2.4 Limitations of the technique

Limitations of this technique are very sharp bends due to the MBR of the ducts and steep inclines in the
cable route.
The distance between the joints should be limited to 1200-1300m depending on the bends on the route.
The cable manufacturer has to approve the water pressure in the duct surrounding the cable. Endcaps
might have to be even tighter than for normal use.

### 3.12.2.5 Soil considerations

No needed considerations.

Drying of the soil will only be as calculated in the design phase as the cables are installed in the designed
depth, with the designed spacing and with the designed thermal backfill, hence no risk of further drying.

### 3.12.2.6 Hazards

Use of this installation technique is a quite safe method. Compared to the normal wire-pull the risk with
a steel wire under tension is removed

One major hazard for this technique is however that the cables can get stuck in the ducts. If this happens
the last solution will be to dig down once more and open the duct.

When the cable end comes out some soft material is needed to stop the speed of the cable. Workers
have to be aware of this issue.

Of course, there is also the normal risk when working with big machines and high voltage:

- Hit by excavator
- Hit by materials dropped.
- Risk of falling into trench or hole
- Risk of induced voltage in the cable
- Etc.

### 3.12.2.7 Interfaces

As for all other cable installation techniques there are several interfaces that must be considered. Some
of these are:

- Contractual responsibility for the cable
- Working under a live overhead line
- Termination of the cable at the substation
- Etc.

### 3.12.2.8 Environmental impact

As this is just an alternative cable installation method there is no impact on the environment except the
use of water, which in some areas are a very critical aspect.
Water can be recycled by transporting it from the end and back to the start point.

### 3.12.2.9 Requirements

Requirements for using this technique are an area of about 10x50m for installation of the cable drum,
guiding device, jointing bay, contatiner with water and the access to water.


## 3.13 Temporary installations

### 3.13.1 Description of the technique

The increasing dependence that the modern society has on electricity, makes it more necessary to
increase the reliability of the electrical system. In addition, breakdowns in transmission lines became
critical because any incident that involves leaving the transmission or distribution network out of service,
represents a market cut and a very demanding replacement need. The overall objective, therefore, is to
ensure at all times the power supply to all consumers with a stable and reliable system in any service
situation and equipped with the appropriate mechanisms for an efficient management before
unexpected service interruptions.

In order to be able to provide the right reaction in those emergency situations and restore the energy
supply in the shortest time, it is necessary to have a solution able to cover the critical points detailed as
follows:

- Availability, which should be guaranteed by the sum of two important factors such as the ubication
    of the elements to be mobilized and their integrity, to be able to provide the service quality expected
    from them
- Integration, in order to mobilize the minimum number of elements and simplify on this way the
    logistics required for the deployment and collection of the emergency links
- Installation simplicity, including additional benefits oriented to simplify even more the deployment
    and collection of the links
- Safety, developing a concept able to extend the functions of the different elements of the link, using
    them also to improve the security levels in Operation

For all the mentioned above, the main purpose of the links should be to connect, temporarily, two points
of the network in order to restart the service in the shortest time while maintenance or repair operations
are carried out. The way in which the solution adopted solves the critical points detailed in the previous
section will determine the level of efficiency achievable in the use of the Provisional Connections and
their influence on the service replacement time.

Temporary solutions should be engineered from the following design bases:

Easy Handling

- Pre-assembled metallic structures
- Conventional auxiliary resources for handling cable drums

Quick assembly:

- Factory pre-assembled components
- Market available components

Versatility

- Can be adapted to several types of Locations
- Can be adapted to many cable designs
- Can be adapted to several termination Technologies

Storage

- Minimum number of components
- Conventional packaging for transportation and storage

### 3.13.2 Limits of the technique

Temporary cable system installations can be considered under the following conditions:

- Emergency / breakdown conditions; Temporary cable systems installations can facilitate the
    replacement of faulted cables/lines or assist with a temporary connection of a transformer or reactor
    within a substation. The maximal possible cable lengths are limited by the cable drum.


- Planned temporary conditions: Temporary cable systems installations can facilitate planned
    upgrading of cables/lines, temporary supply of substations while the permanent cables/lines are still
    being constructed or can be used to facilitate upgrades within substations.

A temporary cable system circuit comprises of a single cable length with two terminations or a
termination and a joint or, it could comprise of a cable system circuit of multiple cable lengths and
intermediate joints. These pre-configured temporary cable system circuits can be pre-assembled at the
factory, routine tested and supplied as a set or system (as may be required), on drums which can
accommodate up to three cables lengths in some instances. The cable drums should be transported on
a conventional cable truck. This requirement is met when galvanized steel drums with a restricted flange
diameter are used. Due to the above, it is common for such temporary cable installations not to perform
a commissioning test. Refer to Chapter Commissioning and de-commissioning.

By definition, temporary installations must be quick to install and not be operated for extended periods.
It is for this reason that the cables and joints are installed on top of the surface of the surrounding
environment and it may be considered to make use of special termination designs that do not require
fixed steel support structure. The special termination should be ready-mounted and maintenance-free.
This requirement is fulfilled with a flexible termination or dry type termination design.

Here below an example of possible solutions is shown:

## Figure 60 – Possible solutions for temporary installations delivery.....................................................

The modular provisional links solution contains serval components which mean that cable drums will be
delivered on site separately. All required auxiliary elements will be stored and delivered in a container.
The auxiliary elements would include all relevant components which is needed to install the temporary
cable system such as cable support brackets with suitable clamps for the stabilization and fixation. With
the integrated provisional links solution all cable drums and all auxiliary elements will be stored and
delivered together in one container.

Additional factors to be considered for the temporary cable system include:

- Safety and risk of the temporary installed cable system must be carefully assessed and managed,
    and then be discussed between all parties that have access to, or come in close proximity to, the
    temporary installed cable system. Warning signs facing outwards and inwards from the temporary
    installed cable system can also be used to relay critical safety information. This can become also
    very important in instances where the temporary cable system may cross or block some areas,
    which can easily be overlooked. Additional lighting can also be considered in areas with poor lighting
    conditions


- Route planning; It is also important to plan the route in detail. As stated previously, the temporary
    installation might be used for connection of a line / cable / transformer / reactor that is still in
    construction and hence the temporary cable installation should not be in the way of the ongoing
    finalization work. Careful planning with the other works is therefore critical
- Installation; During installation, the mechanical parameters such as pulling force, minimum bending
    radius, sidewall pressure and correct design and layout of the installation equipment (e.g. roller
    spacing) are important aspects to keep in mind
- Forces; The forces related to the normal operation (e.g. weight), forces related to abnormal
    conditions (e.g. short circuit conditions) and other external forces on the temporary cable system
    must be considered in the installation. Cable cleats should be used in order to withstand the forces
    which can occur during operation as illustrated below
- Mechanical protection; Installation of mechanical protection (e.g. concrete culverts or PVC split
    sleeve pipes) may be required if operations requires on-going crossing of the temporary cable
    system installation
- Electrical clearance; Consider the impact on electrical clearance that the temporary cable system
    may have on the area where it is installed. (e.g. An area may have been defined to be safe for work
    to be carried out at ground level, however, after the installation of the temporary cable system, this
    may no longer be the case)
- Earthing of the cable system (including metallic parts / structures connected to or forming part of
    the temporary cable system) and the impact of the temporary cable system installation on the
    existing earthing system must be considered. In addition, new fault return paths through the cable
    sheath created by the temporary cable installation, temporary overvoltage's on the cable’s sheath,
    possible touch and step potentials created by the temporary cable installation and possible
    transferred potentials must be taken into account
- Derating; Consideration should be given to derating caused by solar radiation (if applicable)

The intention of a temporary cable system is to make use of it over a short period of time and then to
reuse it elsewhere. Thus, the temporary cable system must be carefully decommissioned and not be
damaged during this process to ensure it can be reused

### 3.13.3 Installation technique

Normally temporary cable systems will be installed directly on the ground surface. The surface onto
which the cables and joints will be installed must be free from external debris (e.g. stones) or any other
objects that could potentially cause damage to the exterior surface of the cables and joints. Sandbags
installed above the existing ground surface, or other means, can also be considered to create a suitable
surface to prevent external damage during installation and operation of the temporary cable system.

Stabilisation and fixation of the cables and joints can be achieved for example by the use of concrete
blocks, metal plates with welded fixation points or cable support brackets with suitable clamps. In case
of construction road crossing a cable bridge or a buried pipe system has to be established. The use of
temporary termination support shall also be taken into consideration.

For overhead lines applications, the temporary cable system which is connected to the overhead lines
should be protected against overvoltage's by considering surge arresters.


## Figure 61 – Supports for temporary terminations

### In order to prevent damage to the cables while in operation and also to protect people from

### contact with the cable, physical barriers sould be added, as well as signalling along the route

### to highlight the presence of the electrical line.

## Figure 62 – Supports for temporary joints


## Figure 63 – Temporary cable cleating (left) and application on OHL tower (right)

The metallic screens of the temporary cables are generally earthed at both ends. If single point bonding
is applied in order to increase the ampacity, link boxes should be provided at one end with sheath
voltage limiters as illustrated below. It is recommended to use an earth continuity conductor for single-
point bonded systems. Link Box with plastic housing can be used in order to have a lower weight.


## Figure 64 – Temporary cable system with single point bonding connection

## Figure 65 – Flexible termination combined with a surge arrester


### 3.13.4 Equipment and spaces

Depending on the length and cross section of the cables chosen, the size of the transport trucks and
the cranes needed for laying the cables will be small, medium, large or extraordinary. For very
lightweight cables it might not be necessary to use a cable winch. Instead the cable laying can be done
by hand. The trained staff can pull the cable direct from the drum. For temporary cable systems with
pre-assembled flexible terminations a protection bag should be used in order to avoid any damages on
the termination.

In case of large cross sections a cable winch equipped with a pulling tension limiter should be used. In
order to make sure that the cable is not over-tensioned. Depending on the length and cross section
chosen, cable rollers shall be placed along the cable route, approx. every 2 – 3 m. At least with such a
distance that dragging over the ground surface which can harm the cable is avoided.

The chosen phase distance is depending on the ampacity of the cable system. In order to increase the
ampacity is also possible to connect two temporary cable systems in parallel.

Additional space for a safety access should be considered in the planning phase. Consideration should
be given for the installation of temporary fences with lockable gates or other means to prevent access
to the temporary installed cable system in cases where the risk is high. Formally restricting access
through authorisation can be considered for lower risk Environments.

### 3.13.5 Environmental Impact

During the installation the environmental impact on the surrounding environment should be taking into
account such thermal impact, drainage, cable system running in parallel etc. In regards to fire protection,
it should be considered the possibility of fire emanating from the temporary cable system installation
and damaging nearby equipment or assets. Therefore ensure the necessary action is taken to lower the
impact and possibility of this event.

A further aspect with regards to environmental impact is Electro Magnetic Non-Ionizing Radiation
Protection. Ensure the temporary cable system does not result in exceedance of acceptable Electro
Magnetic Radiation levels for parties working in close proximity to the temporary cable system
Installation.

### 3.13.6 Commissioning and de-commissioning............................................................................................

Before the cable system is connected to the power transmission system a series of tests should be
performed. The purpose of test is to confirm the integrity of the cable system including the cable and
accessories.

As temporary cable system are a special case, it is recommended that for prefabricated temporary cable
systems with two terminations a commissioning tests do not have to be performed as the terminations
will pre-assembled and tested during an FAT at the factory.

In case of temporary cable systems with a termination and a joint, one end have to be jointed to another
cable piece. In this case a commissioning test has to be performed in order to avoid assembly errors.
