This should be also done for pluggable accessories. A reduced test voltage with a longer duration may
be agreed between suppliers and customer. Alternatively, a voltage of U 0 may be applied for 24 h as
minimum requirement. A partial discharge test should be carried out on the cable system in accordance
with IEC 60840 or IEC 62067 and IEC 60885-3.

A DC voltage test of the oversheath (according to IEC 60229) and a visual inspection on the cable and
accessories is also recommended.

It is recommended to perform the above mentioned tests on temporary cables systems after each use,
before dismantling and storing the temporary cable systems.

After a defined number of use the temporary cable system should be inspected in the factory in order to
maintain the cable system.


## 3.14 Interfaces

### 3.14.1 Interface with substations

Where cables interconnnect with other circuits, the transition is achieved through a termination. Outdoor,
oil and GIS terminations are required to withstand a combination of axial and bending moment forces
and sheath retraction forces in addition to the conductor axial thermo-mechanical forces.

### 3.14.1.1 Air insulated substations

In addition to the forces generated by the cable thermo-mechanical action, the outdoor termination is
required to withstand short circuit busbar loading and meteorological effects such as wind and ice
loading. It is recommended to fix the cable along the rise to the outdoor termination from cable bending
moment loads and sheath retraction loads, as well as electrodynamic forces by one or more cleats.

The air-ground transition will be done by pedestal support structures for terminals and lightning rods.
Below is an example of a pedestal support structure for terminals and surge arrester.

The necessary dimensions of the termination must be considered so that it is housed in the support
structure and the power cable is fastened as well as the accessories to be installed. The distances
necessary to maintain during the installation and maintenance process must be taken into account. In
the same way the surge arrester should be considered.

Typical requirements for analysis and design:

- types of charges
- own mass
- loads due to weather events: wind, extreme temperature and ice
- Earthquake action on structures and equipment

## Figure 66 – Typical pedestral support structure


## Figure 67 – Landing of a duct bank to an air insulated substation

### 3.14.1.2 Gas insulated substations

A gas insulated substation (GIS) uses equipment such as circuit breakers and disconnectors installed
inside a metallic container filled with sulfur hexafluoride (SF6) gas, which has high insulation
performance. Generally, GIS are installed in places where there are space restrictions, such as urban
areas. GIS are resistant to damage from salt, and so are also installed in coastal areas. The features of
GIS are as follows:

Compactness

A conventional air insulated substation (AIS) requires a larger insulation distance due to air insulation.
However, since GIS equipment is housed in a sealed metallic container filled with SF6, its use can
greatly reduce the volume and area compared with AIS.

GIS has merits such as lower site costs, thanks to its reduced land area and total construction costs
(e.g. reduction in development costs for mountainous areas).

Safety

All the live parts of gas insulated switchgears are fully enclosed in grounded metal enclosures filled with
SF6. Therefore, there is no danger of electric shock or damage caused by flying objects or birds.

Saving Resources and Improving Environmental Harmony

Since the equipment becomes smaller by using SF6 and the site area of the substation can be reduced,
the use of GIS can reduce the amount of resources needed.

In GIS, the current breaking parts are housed in a metallic container, which also reduces noise.

In addition, since the entire equipment can be made smaller, it is easy to harmonize it with the
surrounding environment in terms of aesthetics, together with measures for green spaces at substations.


It must be noted that SF 6 is a green-house gas. The study of new type of fluids are nowadays ongoing,
with the aim of having benefits on the environment by reducing CO₂ equivalent emissions, thus making
GIS-technology more sustainable.

Simplification and labor saving in construction work

Since GIS equipment is compact and can be transported as a unit, construction work at the site is
simplified.

In addition, the construction period can be shortened in areas where land development is necessary,
since the amount of land area can be reduced.

Improved Reliability

In the case of AIS, the majority of failures are caused by atmospheric issues such as pollution and salty
air, but for GIS, the portion affected by such issues is very small, thus improving reliability

## Figure 68 – Cable termination in a GIS substation

3.14.1.2.1 Pre-Consultations before Construction

It is necessary for substation departments and gas insulated switchgear manufacturers to undertake
pre-consultations and make any necessary adjustments during the design phase, considering also the
SAT (site acceptance testing). When new GIS are installed, the position of the cable termination should
be designed taking into account work space for post-installation tests and maintenance.

In designing the space at the bottom of the joint and the cable offset, the permissible bending radius
and current transformer (CT) installation space should be considered. In particular, for plug-in
terminations, care should be taken to ensure the permissible bending radius and working space.

When connecting cables to gas insulated switchgears, it is necessary to confirm that the cables are
consistent with the standards and specifications for gas insulated switchgears (Reference IEC 62271 -
209).

In CIGRE Technical Brochure 605, the feasibility of a common interface between gas insulated
switchgears and cables under 145kV is examined. Accordingly, the design of a common interface up to
145kV is described in TB 784.


As per the design pre-consultation, the construction procedure should be discussed in advance between
the substation department and the gas insulated switchgear manufacturer, and the construction process
and site attendance should be confirmed. When a cable enclosure is connected to a gas insulated
switchgear unit, it is occasionally necessary to move the cable gland for cable enclosure installation
work. Since the amount of movement for the cable gland is different for each manufacturer, a pre-
consultation is conducted before the construction, and the cable construction company will be present
when movement is required.

3.14.1.2.2 Cable Laying

Riser

It is necessary to secure excess length of cable in the bottom of the gas insulated switchgear when the
cable is connected to it. After lifting the cable to a sufficient height, an offset is formed while pulling back
the cable, and it is necessary to confirm that the bending radius is secured.

Cleat

At the cable rising portion, if the cable is restrained at the bending portion, the core may become
eccentric and affect the electrical performance due to an increase in the core axial force inside the
terminal connection portion caused by a rise in the conductor temperature. Therefore, when supporting
the bending portion below the termination joint, it is necessary to take care not to restrain the thermal
behavior of the cable and not to make the cable eccentric. When a cable below the termination joint
needs to be supported for a long time, a suspension device is an effective method for not restricting the
thermal behavior of the cable.

Cable Termination Assembly

The structure of the cable termination is classified into dry type and oil-filled type, and specific assembly
control corresponding to each of these is necessary. It is desirable to assemble the cable termination at
the installation position and avoid movement of the cable termination after assembly as much as
possible.

A sheath sectionalizing insulator is installed to electrically divide the earthing system of the cable sheath
and the gas insulated switchgear


### 3.14.1.3 Interface with overhead line

Several types of structures can be used for the transition between overhead lines and underground
cables, such as self-supporting steel, guyed steel, wood, concrete, laminate, etc. However, the most
common is self-supporting steel.

## Figure 69 – OHL transition

When designing the transition structure, at least these specific items should be considered:

- Design consideration should be given to possible personnel on the structures, loads due to weather

```
events and of the weight and cleating / protection structures
```
- The attachment plate for the terminations should have an open side to facilitate cable installation

```
and to eliminate circulating currents in the steel
```
- Use of special clamps below the termination to limit movement of the cable into the termination
- A careful “underground interference” study should be performed in the area of the structure

```
foundations
```
- New steel structures to support cables
- Study of the possible requirement of a spare phase cable
- Study of the possible requirement of two cables per phase
- Maintenance and operation requirements
- General cable system requirements (voltage, multiple circuits, etc.)
- Environmental and permitting considerations
- Topography: a flat area is needed around the structure to allow for initial installation and future

```
maintenance
```

- Unrestricted access to the transition site is essential both for initial construction, and for ongoing

```
operation and maintenance
```
## Figure 70 – Example of attachment plate for termination

Two different ways are used to install underground cable to a steel structure:

Install the cable inside the structure

- Provides a complete protection from external damage
- This type of installation is more costly to install and more difficult to maintain
- May require a larger diameter pole and foundation to allow internal access for clamping the cable
- Requires special foundation design to allow the cable to be brought inside the structure
- More difficult to restore the cable inside the structure in the event of a failure
- Pole ventilation may be required

Install the cable outside the structure.

- Less costly than installing cable inside of the structure.
- Ease of installation and restoration
- Smaller diameter pole and foundation
- Ease to suffer third party damage

A very important and delicate accessory, especially in dry cable installation is the termination. The two
common ways of installing an extruded dielectric termination on a transition structure are at the ground
level or in place on the riser structure


- In the first case, the cable end is laid horizontally, taking care not to exceed the minimum bending
    radius, and the termination is installed at the ground level using a suitable supporting structure. The
    structure including the cable and the termination is then carefully lifted into place. One of the
    advantages of this method is that most of the work is done at the ground level, reducing the need
    for special safety equipment while terminating the cable and the scaffolding structure
- Precautions shall be taken to avoid torsional phenomena between cable and termination

## Figure 71 – Termination lifted to the tower

- In the second case, installing the terminations requires either scaffolding or a man-lift which require
    safety equipment and harnesses for the workers. The design of the tower must allow this type of
    work and avoid time of load shedding

## Figure 72 – Termination made on the tower at final position


The path from soil to the termination shall be provided with cleats to support the cables. This system
should be rigid enough to limit both longitudinal and lateral movement of the cable from gravity, thermal
expansion and contraction, and from short circuit forces. For single core cables, clamps or cleats are
typically manufactured of plastic or non magnetic metals such as aluminium alloys or to a lesser extent
non-magnetic grade stainless steel. When metallic clamps are used, liners (typically elastomeric
materials) are generally used where the clamps will bear on the cable. These liners are used to minimize
the possibility of damage to the cable sheath, to accommodate limited thermal expansion, and to provide
the required restraint. Cable cleats may also be designed to provide thermal expansion by using bolts
with spring washers or partially compressed springs.

Cable manufacturers will provide recommendations for cleat type and spacing as well as the supporting
structures. Based on the clamp design, a transition structure may require vangs, or pre-drilled holes to
fasten the clamps to the structure.

## Figure 73 – Examples of cleats used for cable climbing on the OHL tower

It is often necessary to consider the need for additional protection for the cable and the structure when
completing the design. The structures may need this protection for deterring unauthorized individuals


from getting to the structure and damaging the structure or harming themselves. For unfenced transition
structures, barriers such as concrete posts, or steel guard structures may be necessary. Examples
where supplemental protection could be needed include when the structure is located in close proximity
to a road or in a parking lot.

## Figure 74 – Additional protection at tower basement

Finally, interfaces with overhead lines may also be designed as a so called transition yard, i.e.
terminations on individual support structures (as per Figure 6 6 ), and then connected by jumpers to the
OH line at the terminal support.


### 3.14.1.4 Interface with submarine cables

Landfall operations can be one of the most complex challenges of an offshore installation campaign.
The objective is the protection of the cable by installing it at a sufficient depth, which must be compatible
with the risks anticipated for each stretch of cable route. The constraints of the landfall often determine
the methodology used to bring the offshore cables onshore. For landfalls where there is a flat straight
beach the most common methods are direct burial or ducted installation.

## Figure 75 – Example of submarine installation with underground portions

## Figure 76 – Example of underground and submarine extruded cables


## Figure 77 – Open trench landfall civil works

One of the main challenges is working around the tidal constraints, the extreme shallow waters make it
difficult to complete installation work from offshore, and the intertidal area makes access from an
onshore side problematic. Often a jack-up barge is used close to shore to enable a stable working
platform in shallow waters and divers can be used to support. It is important that 24-hour working is
consented during construction, as the progress will depend heavily on favourable tidal windows. The
tides can also prematurely backwash sediment into the trench which reduces the effective burial depth
and must be monitored throughout construction. For this reason, it is common to use a ducted solution
for the intertidal area to ensure cable burial depth without having to keep the trench open for the cable
pull operation. Once the ducts are buried to depth the sediment is then reinstated. It may be required
that a separate barge is needed to store the sediment during this period as it may get washed away if
the trench is open for a significant period of time. This will leave insufficient ground to close the trench
and it would take a longer period of time for the trench to naturally backfill; separate backfill material
may be required in some cases.

Geotechnical samples and seabed surveys are essential to understand the ground conditions to
engineer the landfall correctly. It is recommended that samples are taken in advance every 100 – 150m
to assess the change in ground condition across the landfall.

Depending on ground conditions it may be necessary to use temporary works at a landfall, including
sheet piling and cofferdams. Sheet piling involves installing metals sheets into the intertidal area to
ensure the sides of the trench do not collapse during the excavation process. These can be installed
using a number of purpose built tools, generally pile-drivers and pile hammers are the two most
common.


## Figure 78 – Cable ducts being sunk into a pre-lay trench

If the landfall has environmental or physical barriers (for example it may be a protected area or there
may be a steep cliff), then a direct buried solution is not possible and alternative solutions must be
looked at. The most common alternative is to perform an HDD. This could either be performed from the
land side or the offshore side, although an onshore drill is more common. A series of controlled drills
are performed, starting with a pilot hole and then a series of larger drills to make the hole sufficiently
wide. Ducts are then pulled through the hole (floated in from offshore) to secure the excavation and
prevent collapse. This is often performed well in advance of the cable pull-ins, to ensure the landfall is
prepared. A separate drill is normally used for each cable, often with a smaller drill for fibre optic cables,
if required.


(^)

## joint basement Figure 79 – HDD drilling rig (left) and cable pulled through HDD duct onshore (right) with transition

```
basement
```
Once onshore, offshore cables will end at the landfall transition joint bay (TJB), where they are
connected to the onshore cables. The transition joint bay location should be carefully chosen to be a
convenient distance inland from the shoreline, in particular taking into account local coastal erosion.
Often the HDD exit point is offset by 10-20m from the TJB to allow for cable handling and site set-up for
the cable winch. Cable in this short area between the HDD duct and TJB is often directly buried and
surrounded in CBS, but an extension of the ducting may be used. A transition joint bay may be a simple
concrete base for the cables to be jointed on, or it may be necessary to construct a full concrete chamber
where the joint is fully encased.

The landfall transition joint bay is buried underground and provides the following functions:

- Providing a controlled environment to ensure the quality of the joint
- Providing a reliable anchoring point for the offshore cables and allows for thermo-mechanical forces
    of the joint and the cable armour
- Allow for suitable alignment and compatibility with the directional drilling operations and cable winch
- Creating a pulling ramp for the offshore cable installation
- Allows the provision of link boxes for the earthing configuration of the Cable System

The transition joint between the submarine cable and the land cable is normally a rigid joint consisting
of prefabricated or pre-moulded joint bodies, housed within a metallic casing with some form of
additional protection against mechanical damage and water ingress.

The mechanical demands on the Sea/Land Transition joint will differ from case to case. The specific
design requirements for the mechanical and thermomechanical properties of the cable, armour and
conductor need to be considered and the TJB civil design, armour clamping design, joint and joint
anchoring design selected appropriately.

For example: if the submarine cable is jetted into a level seabed and the Sea/Land joint is placed on the
shore the armour may just need to be terminated, whilst the situation on steep slopes is different and
there may be a need to fix the submarine cable by the armour. This issue needs to be discussed from
project to project.

If the armour needs to be fixed to hold the weight of the cables, various designs may come into
consideration. One possibility is to terminate the armour in a steel fixture connected rigidly to an
anchoring block made of concrete. This block or plate may be used as the floor of the joint system.


In countries where ice accretion can happen, protection against ice sheering should be considered
nearshore.

## Figure 80 – Armour clamp at the transition joint

### 3.14.1.5 Ancillaries interface

Outside of the connections of underground transmission cable terminations to substations/switchyards
and overhead transmission lines there are auxiliary systems that must interface with the stations or
overhead lines. These auxiliary systems are described in more detail below.

In each of the following interface types provisions should be made to properly secure the auxiliary
equipment and cables to the termination structure(s).

### 3.14.2 Grounding/Bonding

The underground transmission cable system requires grounding/bonding connections at the
terminations. These are typically done through link boxes and design in accordance with industry
standards [CIGRE TB 283][IEEE 575]. The link boxes can be single-phase or three-phase depending
upon preference and structure configuration. The link boxes must be designed with a proper protection
index (IP) to protect against dust and water ingress.

When three-phase link boxes are used at the terminations, provisions should be made to route bonding
cables from the terminations to the link box along the steel structure(s) or via underground conduits.

Care should be taken to coordinate with the station design or overhead transmission design to ensure
the structure grounding is adequately sized to prevent larger ground conductor sizes associated with
underground transmission lines from connecting to much smaller local grounds at the termination
location(s).

### 3.14.3 Communications

Communication fiber optic cables are often part of an underground transmission line. Provisions for
these cables need to be made at the termination of the underground transmission line, whether in a
substation or to overhead transmission lines.


Typically, a fiber optic splice box is placed on the termination structure, station structure or transition
pole, to connect the fiber optic cable associated with the underground transmission line to the station
control building or in the case of overhead transmission connection the OPGW (Optical Groud Wire) or
other fiber optic cable.

The fiber optic splice boxes should be rated for the appropriate environment, i.e. rain, dust, harsh
environment.

Similarly, an optical sea-land transition joint shall be provided at the same power transition joints location
and the armoured submarine optical cable provided with a specific anchoring device.

Should a fiber optic system not installed, a wireless communication could be established.

In this case it is necessary to carefully consider the wireless communication protocol (4G/5G) to have
proper reseption at the substation.

### 3.14.4 Distributed Temperature Sensing (DTS)

DTS sensor, whether internal or external to the power cable, will need connection to the DTS
equipment. Fiber optic splice box(es) should be mounted on the structure to splice the cable DTS fibers
to an external fiber optic cable routed to the control building and DTS equipment cabinet.

Note: fusion splices should be used on DTS fiber cables to minimize losses.

### 3.14.5 Partial Discharge (PD) monitoring

Partial Discharge (PD) monitoring for short underground transmission lines is typically done locally at
each accessory. However, longer lines typically have a dedicated fiber optic cable along the
underground transmission route to connect each of the PD sensors to the end point of the line, whether
station or overhead.

PD monitoring is typically done during the commissioning or maintenance testing of the underground
transmission line and at the completion of the testing the PD sensors are removed, and the fiber optic
cable left in place for future use. In the case of a permanent PD monitoring system, the PD fiber optic
cable will need to be routed from the terminations to the control building and the monitoring cabinet. The
sensor system for permanent PD monitoring system typically need some type of low voltage power
supply. This power supply can be dedicated from the station or use the local distribution network.

Currently, wireless systems are also available to transmit PD measurements.

### 3.14.6 Pressurization facilities

Fluid filled cable systems require pressurization equipment associated with the insulating fluid for these
underground transmission lines to operate.

Low pressure cable system only requires small tanks pressurized above atmospheric pressure at the
termination and accommodate expansion and contraction of the insulating fluid. High pressure cable
system requires a pressurization plant that includes large fluid tank(s), pressurization pumps and
controls to maintain the fluid pressure in the cable at specified working pressures and provide volume
for expansion/contraction of the fluid.

The piping from the pressurization equipment to the terminations are typically constructed utilizing
copper or stainless steel to prevent corrosion.

### 3.14.7 Current monitoring

Monitoring of system parameters, such as the current circulating in the conductor and in the metallic
sheaths, is also possible by placing sensors inside the link boxes and at the terminations.

Even in this case, a transmission system needs to be considered for transmitting the signals to the
remote collection point.

### 3.14.8 Fault detection systems

Fault detection systems are nowadays available.
These may be both online and offline fault location methods that require power and possibly the use of
a fibre optic bus for transmitting information or the use of wireless communication.


### 3.14.9 Contractual interface contracts

### 3.14.9.1 Procurement strategy and contractual interface management

At the onset of a procurement strategy, the project execution risks must be established and evaluated
against the relative size of the project or scheme of projects; timelines; availability of various civil and
electrical contractors, manufactures and suppliers to perform the project scope; network risks (including
risks at various voltage levels and supply requirements), various internal and external approvals and
other aspects. The outcome of this should entail a detailed plan to invite tenders, receive submissions
on combined (Turnkey) or spilt contract scope specifications, evaluation of submissions, contract award,
execution of the project, and close out of the project.

### 3.14.9.2 Turnkey Projects

Turnkey cable system projects are projects where a complete cable system solution is provided, that
include the cable system design, manufacturing, installation, testing and commissioning. In most cases
these turnkey solutions are offered by cable system manufacturing companies that also provides a
system guarantee for the completed cable system installation. These are known as Engineering
Procurement Construction (ECP) contracts.

The benefits of Turnkey projects include only managing and interfacing with one contracting company
to perform all contracted works for a complete cable system. This can reduce project management and
technical complexities during contract execution and construction. There is also the opportunity to give
the risks associated with the Subcontractor management to the Principal Contractor.

One of the disadvantages of Turnkey projects is that commercially they may be more expensive
compared to multiple individual contracts to allow for the Principal Contractor to manage the interface
and associated risks. As the Employer does not have a direct relationship with the subcontractor it can
also greatly reduce the control the Employer has on the works. There is a possibility that certain technical
and quality aspects may be overlooked between Principle Contractor and the Subcontractor, so the
Employer should be clear in the main EPC contract what the minimum expectations are with regards to
quality and site management, and emphasise the importance of control mechanisms such as: design
reviews, risk assessment and method statement documentation, quality inspection plans, inspection
testing plans, hold points and regular project progress stakeholder meetings.

### 3.14.9.3 Split contract projects

Splitting of contract scope of work can be done in various ways, of which the most common practise is
to split Civil and Electrical contract work. Civil contract work may comprise of civil engineering, civil
product manufacturing (where required), and civil construction. Electrical contract work may comprise
of electrical engineering, product manufacturing (such as cable, joints, terminations and other
accessories), installation, testing and commissioning. These two main categories can be split further
and various permutations exist. This splitting of contracts should be informed by the capability of the
contracting companies in the market to ensure competitive tenders, the ability to project manage the
various contracting companies scope of works, and the capability to ensure all scope of works are
executed in line with the interface requirements for the various manufacturing and installation contract
outputs.

The benefits of splitting the contracts may include reduced costs and competitive tenders by multiple
companies.

The disadvantageous of splitting contract scopes are that the scope definition and administering of
contracts can be more complex, and there is a significant interface between the contractors for the
Employer to manage. Increased technical skills, project management skills and sufficient previous
experience are required from the Employer to ensure the quality levels of the works. Care should also
be taken to not overlook any scope of works that may be required for the interface between contracts.
If the split and responsibilities of contract scope of works are not clearly defined, it could lead to
construction gaps and defects and may affect future guarantees of the cable system and its related
project scope of works performed by the various contracted companies.

Where the cable and accessories (joints and terminations) are sourced from separate manufacturers,
the relevant standard for range of approval - type test - and pre-qualification requirements shall be
carefully considered to ensure compliance for the cable system components procured and installed as
a system. Additional type test or extension of pre-qualification tests may be required to prove the cable
and accessories sourced separately as a combined system prior to installation.


Some contracting complexities may include, as an example, the cable manufacturer not accepting the
responsibility for the quality of the bedding and blanket soil layers installed by the civil contractor. The
cable accessories manufacturer may also be required to accredit or perform the cable accessories
installation to retain the guarantee form the cable manufacturer. Such complexities can be resolved
upfront in the contracting scope of works and project design review. Various testing and inspection
strategies can also be followed throughout the project construction to ensure quality of work between
contracting companies and the complete cable system solution to be achieved.

### 3.14.10 Transition with other cables

On the basis of agreements between the owner of the installation and contractor there could be several
testing scenarios in order to decide the responsibility.

The objective of this chapter is to define the tests that may be performed in actions on existing cables
that involve the manipulation and subsequent re-use of part of the cables currently in service to delimit
responsibilities between the owner of the installation, the contractor of the awarded cable to perform the
modification of the line and the company awarded the contract to carry out the civil works in the case
were necessary.

Tests mentioned under items A to D below are not mandatory and they are subject to agreement
between the owner and the installation contractor. Examples of this type of actions are:

- Cable that currently reaches a GIS position and changes to another position in the same substation

## Figure 81 – Change of a position in a substation

- Split a section (diversion) of a cable to reach another substation between two existing substations /
    positions

## Figure 82 – link a new substation between two existing substations


Tests recommended:

A) Prior to beginning of the modification of the line

The purpose of the following tests is to determine that the circuit is delivered by the owner in perfect
conditions to the cable contractor awarded the modification of the underground circuit or to the company
awarded the contract to carry out the civil works in case these were necessary.

## Figure 83 – Initial tests on existing circuit

A.1) Partial Discharge Measurements.

Before to any action on the circuit, the contractor will carry out an online Partial Discharge
Measurements on the section of the line tat will be reused. This test will be carried out with the circuits
with no load applied and it will be scheduled approximately 1-2 months before the programmed release
of the line [IEC 60141, 60840, 62067].

A. 2 ) First sheath test.

Before or after PD test, a sheath integrity test shall be carried out using a DC voltage source (typically
5 kV or another testing value by agreement between contractor and owner) over the cable section that
will be reused. Once this test has been carried out, contractor of the cable will inform the owner of the
results obtained.

- Results of the tests satisfactory

The certificate of the tests will serve as proof that the owner delivers the installation in perfect conditions.
From this moment, any damage in the cable will be responsibility of the contractor awarded of the next
phase of the modification of the circuit.

If contractor and owner consider it is necessary, they can agree to commission the circuit again in
service and carry out a new Partial Discharge Measurements on the section of the line will be reused.

- Results of the tests unsatisfactory

In the event that partial discharges were detected in any of the cables or failure in any of the sheaths,
the owner would be informed so that it could take the appropriate measures.


B) After civil works

The purpose of the following tests is to determine that the cables have not suffered any damage during
the execution of the civil works.

## Figure 84 – Tests after civil works

B.1) Second sheath test.

After civil works (opening of new and existing trenches) have been executed, a second sheath integrity
test shall be carried out using a DC voltage source (typically 5 kV) or another testing value by agreement
between contractor and owner) over the cable section that will be reused. Once this test has been
carried out, contractor of the cable will inform the owner of the results obtained.

- Results of the tests satisfactory

The certificate of the tests will serve as proof that the civil works contractor returns the installation in
perfect conditions. From this moment, any damage in the cable will be responsibility of the contractor
awarded of the next phase of the modification of the circuit.

- Results of the tests unsatisfactory

Civil works contractor will be responsible to remedy the damage caused during works.

After civil works and before moving and laying circuits, it is important to define the responsibility and
protection of the circuits from possible external damage.


C) After moving and laying circuits and before making accessories

## Figure 85 – Tests before splicing

C.1) Third sheath test.

After moving and laying circuits and before making accessories cable contractor will carry out a third
sheath test to verify the integrity of the cable over-sheath by applying HV DC voltage between metallic
screen of the cable and the ground. Once this test has been carried out, contractor of the cable will
inform the owner of the results obtained.

This test was not necessary in the case, cable contractor was also responsible of civil works.

- Results of the tests satisfactory

The certificate of the tests will serve as proof that the cable contractor has modified of the underground
line successfully.

- Results of the tests unsatisfactory

Cable contractor of the modification will respond appropriately to investigate and solve the possible
problems of the new installation.

D) Before commissioning

## Figure 86 – Tests before commissioning


After installation, several tests must be carried out and there are also some optional tests which may
also be performed depending on the system requirements and the internal regulations of the owner of
the installation. These tests carried out by the cable contractor or an independent laboratory, are
important for the safe energization of the cable and in order to obtain information about the
characteristics of the connection. This information will be used for protection, operation and performance
guaranties to ensure a long life with minimum interruptions.

The following table shows a list of proposed commissioning tests [TB 680 WG B1.47].

This procedure is only a proposed agreement between possible companies involved in the installation
and commissioning of a circuit.

### 3.14.11 Spare paper cables concerns

The paper type high voltage cables have a history of reliability and appear to continue their service as
long as possible provided they are correctly operated and maintained. They represent a significant
portion of installed HV and EHV underground and submarine cable systems. After decades of highly
satisfactory service performance their basic designs are mature and not a lot of effort is being spent in
developing this cable technology.

However, the use of extruded cables for transmission and distribution circuits is increasing in preference
to cables with paper insulation. The number of manufacturers of paper cable is also decreasing,
therefore the availability of such cables for repair works or re-routing could be very limited in the near
future.

### 3.14.12 Maintenance and operation of paper cables

For paper type cables, effective maintenance and operation practices are (from TB 652) the following:

- Ensuring the cables are operating within their design pressures and capacity
- Monitoring the condition of the cable fluid, particularly the presence of dissolved gases
- Controlled flushing for high DGA and RPG readings
- Ensuring the outer cable serving is in a good condition
- Detecting fluid leaks and repairing them before there is any deterioration to the electrical insulation
- Ensuring that sheath bonding of single core cables is working effectively
- Internal pressure reduction where necessary
- Making the best use of internal and external inspection opportunities
- Investigating and understanding the cause of faults
- The application of statistical ageing models

From an operational point of view, paper type cables often give a warning when something goes wrong
before the actual breakdown of the system. By means of the permanent or regular pressure, monitoring
of the fluid pressures in the system a problem can be found and actions can be taken to avoid the
breakdown of the system.

The total repair time is dependent of the time needed for:

- The type of repair
- Time to localize the fault and re-establishing healthy cable
- The availability of spare parts
- The time required for the repair, including the fluid treatment

### 3.14.13 Decommissioning of paper cables

There are various asset management approaches to be adopted and options to consider when a utility
is deciding what to do with fluid-filled cables deemed to be no longer required for operational purposes.
These decisions will implied several environmental implications.
The environmental implications for any appropriate solution should be a major consideration in any asset
management decision making. In some situations, a decommissioned fluid-filled cable remaining in-situ
can still pose an environmental risk, depending on its location and how it is managed following
decommissioning. However, for any of the options described below the environmental risk will always
be several orders less than cables in service. Although the complete removal of any buried assets that


are no longer required would appear be the ideal and permanent solution, this is not always the best
overall practical environmental option and not always practical to achieve. As such all of the following
solutions should be considered in the decision making leading up to decommissioning.
There are no known cases of significant groundwater pollution arising from decommissioned fluid filled
cables where one of the methods outlined below have been used.

1. Full removal of cables and joints by open cut trenches (full excavation)

With this solution all the cables and associated equipment are removed by excavation including ancillary
equipment such as oil tanks and link boxes, allowing member companies in some cases to release the
land from operational status.

This technique removes all long term risks associated with fluid-filled cables and associated systems.
However, removal of the cable and joints will cause major disruption to the highways and other land
users, and is costly and may have short term safety and environmental risks that need to be carefully
managed, especially so whilst adjacent circuits remain in service. There are also significant
environmental impacts from the energy used in the activity of recovering the cables, traffic congestion,
the materials required for the remaking of the roadways, and the disposal of any material that cannot be
reused or recycled.

2. Removal of cable joints, removal of fluid from cable and capping cable.

For this solution the cable joints and ancillary equipment are removed by multiple mini excavations. A
pusher compound (e.g. Hydrogel) is then introduced to the cable to push out/replace free phase fluid in
the cable by filling the cable fluid void. The cable will then be capped and left in the ground.

This technique reduces the disruption to the highways and other land users. However, if the space
occupied by the redundant cables is needed in the future then the old cable can be removed when the
new assets are installed, thus avoiding the unnecessary environmental impact and cost of opening the
ground twice. It should be noted that there will be a need to manage the risk of any remaining cable fluid
residue in the cable (which can emerge from the ends or a leak point in the sheath of the cable). The
risk of environmental damage is reduced by the removal of the majority of the cable fluid.

Some technologies using bacteria are also available to strongly reduce the amount of oil remaining
trapped into the paper by transforming it chemically into a stable compound.

3. Removal of cable joints and extraction of cable cores and cable fluid

For this solution the cable joints and ancillary equipment are removed by multiple mini excavations as
above. The cable is drained and purged of all fluids and the cores are pulled out of the cable, recovering
the conductor, paper insulation and residual fluid. The cable sheath remaining in the ground can be
cleaned and all risks of future oil spill removed essentially forming a new asset in the means of a duct.

Current technology is limited in this field and the effectiveness of this technique is heavily dependent on
the cable system and installation.

4. Purging by nitrogen and further draining

Free phase fluid can be pushed from the cable using nitrogen. Cable joints can either be left in the
ground but preferably removed as this gives easier access to the cables for purging. It is possible to fit
draining points to the cables at low points on the profile for the draining of saturated fluid retained within
the insulating papers at intervals, until free of all traces of any residual fluid. As above all ancillary
equipment should be removed.

5. Purging by detergent and further draining

Free phase fluid can be flushed from the cable using a detergent solution. Cable joints can either be left
in the ground but preferably removed as this gives easier access to the cables for flushing. It is possible
to fit draining points to the cables at low points on the profile for the draining of fluid retained in the


insulating papers at intervals, until free of all traces of any residual fluid. As above all ancillary equipment
should be removed.

All decommissioned assets remaining in the ground need to remain on the asset register, and where
appropriate an asset specific maintenance regime shall be set for the particular asset. This is extremely
important where they run through sensitive areas and also for third party searches.

With all of the above options, when they are effectively implemented, the risk of significant volumes of
fluid leaking from the cables is extremely low and ongoing monitoring would not normally be required.

For each decommissioning case a range of options should be considered before deciding on any one
specific solution or combination of solutions. The selected solution(s) should also take into consideration
the cost and disruption, both to member companies and any other land owners/users, together with the
health and safety risks and environmental impacts from the proposed option.

Identification of and consultation with interested parties at an early stage is essential, especially land-
owners and relevant regulators such as the Environment Agency and Local Authorities.

It may well be the case that a number of options apply to different parts of any one route and a mixture
of options may be necessary. From an environmental perspective it is important to understand the
environmental sensitivity of the land that the cable is running through taking into consideration such
things as local ground water, water extraction points and the various types of areas having special
conservation status etc. The commissioning of an Environmental Impact Assessment in proportion to
the scale of the project, in terms of resources and environmental sensitivities, is therefore strongly
recommended and may be a condition of the project.

In some cases it will be necessary to carry out research and development on a project specific basis to
assess the best available technology for a particular situation. New technology should always be
considered alongside existing and a full assessment of impacts considered. It should be recognized that
the list of options as given above may not be exhaustive and other solutions may exist or be under
development.

### 3.14.14 Commisioning test for integrated FO designs

Extruded dielectric cables may have a distributed fiber-optic installed in the cable construction under
the cable sheath. Temperature is measured along the optical fiber by injecting pulses of light into the
optical fibers. The light that is backscattered from impurities in the glass fiber is used to measure the
temperature at discrete locations along the cable.

Multimode fibers can monitor lines up to about 3 0 km long. Single-mode fibers may be used for
temperature monitoring on longer lines, up to approximately 10 0 km. However, temperature resolution
is worse.

The distributed temperature monitoring system is designed during the initial cable system design.
Distributed temperature monitoring is most commonly applied to extruded dielectric cable circuits
because of the relative ease of applying the fiber and because extruded dielectric cables do not have
the temperature averaging that is possible with circulation of fluid in HPFF cables.

It must be also said that this technology was not available in the past for paper insulated cable
technologies.

This temperature monitoring system has the following implications for the cable installer:

- Separate testing and commissioning are required for the distributed temperature monitoring system,
    versus the power cable system
- The fibers can be damaged during power cable splicing and terminating
- Care must be taken to avoid damaging the fiber during cable installation


- The optical fiber must be removed from the cable at monitoring locations and run to a separate fiber
    optic splice box

## Figure 87 – Extruded cable design with integrated optical units

Commissioning tests proposed:

Test_1: A power meter test is performed and the total system loss in dB at the stated frequencies (1310
nm and 1550 nm) must be less than the values determined based upon the cable length and number of
fusion splices.

Test_2: Optical time domain reflectometer (OTDR) tests are made on the completed cables, and the
contractor must remake any splice or termination that does not meet the required loss.

Please refer to WG B1.73 “Recommendations for the use and testing of Fibre Optic Cables used in Land
Cable Systems” for further recommendations.


## 3.15 Hazards caused by inappropriate civil works or installation design

This section lists some of the found hazards concerning high voltage cable installation. Each of the
hazards will not be described in detail as most of them are self-explaining.

A general aspect of the hazards is that most of them can be mitigated by a better and more detailed
planning in the design phase of the project. Many hazards are an effect of bad route planning; ground
conditions, landscape profile, sharp bends, crossings of obstacles etc. Another aspect that will mitigate
many hazards is the planning of the working space. To mitigate the hazards concerning the Quality,
Health, Safe and Environment (QHSE) enough space for the work is necessary and needs to be planned
from the beginning.

There is of course also the risk of poor craftmanship and choice of bad materials, but if the working
conditions are good these hazards are possible to properly mitigate.

A list of know hazards are shown here:

- Pipe ovalization/deformity
- Concrete intrusion into ducts
- Bad duct jointing
- Wrong cleating pitch
- Lifting by thrust of lids
- Inappropriate bending radius
- Buckling of cable
- Bad backfill compaction
- Fire hazard
- Excessive load bearing pressure
- Shallow installations
- Cable system installations which do not meet national legal requirements
- Scaffolding collapse
- Risk when working close to big machines
- Risk of being hit by material droppings
- Risk of falling into excavations
- Safety incidents (induced voltage due to parallelism with underground and/or OHL’s)
- Interface failures or delays due to mismatch of components or designs
- Use of incorrect backfill materials resulting in cable damage/overheating
- Damage to existing services
- Trench collapse
- Water ingress into joints
- Metallic sheath fatigue failure
- Environmental contamination
- Hydrology changes causing field flooding
- Disturbance to third parties (e.g. noise, dust, access, visual)


- Misdirected HDD hitting buried assets or drill break-out draining of a canal
- Accessory failures due to poor jointer training/selection
- Poor programming, lack of time, errors and accidents due to short-cuts
- Removal of special backfill and replacement with sand by third party contractors resulting in cable
    overheating
- Risk of forgetting warning tapes
- Risk of misreporting cable placement
- Cable run-away during pulling on steep inclines
- Gas explosions in joint vaults
- Jointer loss of life due to SF6 suffocation
- Poor cleaning of cable GIS chamber resulting in termination failure
- Poor earthing of GIS terminations resulting in arc flashover of barriers
- Poor labelling hazard resulting in disconnection of live equipment earthing
- Poor cover tile protection allowing penetration to cable between tiles
- Cable flattening due to incorrect roller use / too tight bend or excessive pulling force
- Poor resin selection/mixing for joint protection box filling allowing moisture ingress
- Poor connector or bonding cable contact resistance causing corrosion and joint overheating due to
    contact resistance problems
- Poor link box assembly causing internal flashover


## 4. Chapter 4 - System design principles

## 4.1 Thermal design

The main purpose of the thermal design is to check that with the conductor cross-section and material
selected to carry the specified power the temperature limits of materials used as insulation are not
exceeded as well as to define the impact of cable temperature on the environment in which it is installed.
The observance of these elements is fundamental for a correct design.

It must be observed that also components, like joints and terminations are part of the circuit and need
to be checked for circulating currents (please refer to TB 640 and TB 797 for further information).

The basis of analysis to assess the thermal design is the thermal model of the cable.

During cable operation the heat generated by its components is being transported outwards onto cable
surface, which is then dissipated to the surroundings.

The thermal model describe the cable operating conditions in a specified environment with the use of a
set of formulas and parameters/coefficients.

Capability of the environment surrounding the cable to dissipate the heat generated by the cable plays
a crucial role when estimating long term current-carrying capacity, as specified below, and as such , the
most fundamental factor is to reduce the external thermal resistivity.

In addition to these are the installation requirements, such as the need to use compact cable
configurations to reduce space, ducts and "no-dig" technologies.

### 4.1.1 Current rating and parameters affecting it........................................................................................

The current carrying capacity of a cable is a function of the available thermal rise between the ambient
temperature and the maximum allowable temperature of the insulating material of the cable.

The cable construction, the environmental data and the installation methodology determine the capacity
of the cable and the environment surrounding it to dissipate the heat generated by the power losses.

The main factors affecting the cable thermal rating are:

- Environmental temperature
- Burial depth
- Use of plastic or metallic conduits
- Use of EMF shielding systems
- Spacing between cables/circuits
- Presence of external heating sources
- Soil thermal resistivity
- Solar exposure

All these factors have a direct impact on the performance of the cable system and must therefore be
carefully considered, both in the design phase and in the event of modifications to the circuit.

In particular, for cables laid in the ground thermal resistance depends on the following:

- soil characteristics
- depth of laying
- heat generated by infrastructures in the vicinity of the cable


External thermal resistance of cables laid in free air depends on the impact of solar radiation on the
cable. Two options may be taken into account:

- cable protected from direct solar radiation
- cable subjected to direct impact of solar radiation

The IEC 60287- 3 - 1 standard defines standard environmental conditions for calculating current carrying
capacity for specific countries. It should be noted, however, that also in this regard there are significant
differences in climate of different regions of some countries. If the conditions have not been defined for
a given country, the abovementioned standard contains appropriate procedures and instructions about
what to do in such cases.

Long term current carrying capacity of cables strongly depends on the following parameters:

Thermal properties of soil. The most important soil parameters are its thermal resistivity and ambient
temperature, being the thermal resistivity influenced by soil material, density and moisture content.
When the heat flux generated at the cable surface is high, drying out of soil may occur, which may
reduce the steady state cable rating.

Cable construction / thermal properties. A crucial aspect in this regard the ability to dissipate heat
from the conductor to the outer surface of the cable. This is affected by the materials used and the
number of layers of the cable.

Laying conditions. It can be said that generally cables laid in free air dissipate heat better than those
laid in the ground. Cables that are not protected from direct solar radiation, depending on its intensity,
have lower capacity of heat dissipation to the surroundings compared to the shaded cables. Depth of
laying and phase separation are other important factors affecting cable rating.

Effect of nearby energy infrastructures. Sources of heat generated by heating pipelines, other power
cables and EMF shielding systems must also be taken into account when calculating the current carrying
capacity.

Allowable current carrying capacity may be calculated with the use of the formulas given in IEC 60287
standards.

Further recommendations are included into TB 640.

Calculation of losses
Joule losses depend on current flowing in the conductor. For AC cable systems, losses in the metallic
screen/ metal sheath/ cable armour and in metal elements of the construction depend on earthing
conditions, while for DC cables, they are localised in the conductor only.

For AC systems, voltage dependent losses are also experienced in the insulation material.


## Figure 88 – Losses in power cables - classification

Losses in power cables may be divided into two main categories; current depended losses (Joule
losses), which are related to the current flow and dielectric losses, that are voltage related.

It should be remembered that all the aforementioned losses interact with each other. Additional losses
in metal components of the cable (metallic sheath, metallic screen, armour) may be reduced by doing
the following:

- Using non-magnetic materials for the armouring in order to eliminate magnetic losses
- Using single point bonding for metallic screens and metallic sheaths, so that not to close the circuit
    for induced current flow
- Cross-bonding metallic sheaths of the cable in order to compensate for induced voltages leading to
    minimisation of losses - in spite of earthing both ends of the line
- Using Milliken conductors with shaped segments insulated from each other in order to reduce
    proximity and skin effect
- Using Milliken conductors with wires insulated from each other in order to further reduce the skin
    effect
- Increasing distance between phases in order to reduce the proximity effect. However, increasing
    the spacing increases the value of λ 1 (circulating current losses) for two point bonded systems

Soil thermal resistivity

Soil thermal resistivity is one of the main factors influencing cable current rating.

The main components affecting soil thermal resistivity are:

- Density
- Volume of air relative to other components
- Water content
- Mineral composition of the particles
- Shape of solid elements of soil


The analysis of the soil thermal resistivity is of paramount importance, since the external thermal
resistance can be responsible for up to ¾ of cable thermal resistance, and its change over time might
have a significant impact on the parameters of the cable system.

In any case the following considerations should be made:

- In order to reduce its thermal resistivity, the soil should be heavily compacted in order to minimise
    air-filled spaces, since the air is a poor thermal conductor
- Although moisture introduced into the soil reduces its resistivity, moisture itself is not a good thermal
    conductor either, being the quarz a much better conductor
- Moisture content in ground is not a constant value; not only does it vary depending on weather
    conditions, but it also fluctuates depending on heat dissipated by the loaded power cable. The
    possible soil drying out shall be carefully evaluated

Essential for the thermal performance of the cable in the long period of time is good thermal contact
between the cable surface and the backfill. Increase in thermal resistivity of the medium surrounding
the cable will result in increase of cable temperature, which in turn can cause maximum allowable
temperatures of the materials used in the cable to be exceeded. Such situations, undoubtedly, affect
both the functionality of the cable system as well as its service life.

Selected backfills

Heat generated by the cable is dissipated to the surroundings. In order to ensure controlled heat
dissipation, selected backfill is sometimes used. Its main task is to ensure thermally stable surroundings,
which is independent from changing weather conditions or natural soil properties. In order to achieve
high efficiency of power transmission, selected backfill should have low and stable thermal
conductivity.In most typical installation, the entire trench width is filled with thermal backfill to a minimum
height of 0,3 m above the cables. For poor native soil conditions and heavily loaded cables, the thickness
of the backfill can be increased. A detailed study of the issue can be found in CIGRE TB 714 ‘Long term
performance of soil and backfill system’.

### 4.1.2 Short circuit

The short-circuit current flow results in the following effects that are proportional to the square of its
value, such as:

- Increase of temperature of conductive components of the cable (conductor, metallic screen, metallic
    sheath and armour). As a consequence, temperature of remaining cable components and materials
    also increases
- Generation of electromagnetic forces between conductive components of the cable

### 4.1.3 Cyclic loads

Operating scenarios different from a continuous loading may be considered.

IEC 60853 standard gives the recommendations for calculating cycling ratings by giving the methods
for calculating the appropriate rating factors to be applied to the continuous rating current.

Considering cycling loads (where applicable) brings to a possible reduction of the cable conductor cross
size.


## 4.2 Electrical design

When designing cables/cable systems two major aspects must be taken into account: external
conditions, in which the cable is to operate, as well as electrical stress that may appear during cable
operation.

During the design process, applicable specifications as well as requirements of a given project must be
respected.

In order to appropriately design a cable system, the following basic requirements must be fulfilled:

- Nominal and maximum operating voltage
- The type of cable insulation
- Transmission capacity or nominal current
- Operating mode: permanent operation or partial load operation (load factors)
- Short-circuit capacity or short-circuit current with statement of the effect time
- Economic considerations

### 4.2.1 Voltage stress

Insulating material capability of withstanding a specified voltage applied to it is defined as electric stress.
If the voltage applied causes a higher electric stress than the critical value, breakdown of the insulation
system occurs.

Electric stress of the insulation system depends on:

- Volume of the material subjected to electric stress
- Temperature
- Voltage waveform
- Ageing process

All these factors should be taken into account when the cable system is to be designed.

Insulating system of the cable must be designed in such a way as to ensure that the voltages occurring
in it don’t lead to electrical breakdown (whilst taking into account material ageing process).

Electrical stresses in the insulation system of DC power cables is also influenced by a resistive electrical
distribution, which depends on the operating temperature.

The sizing of the insulation is also influenced by the phenomena of polarity reversal.

### 4.2.2 Earthing of metallic sheaths and coverings and power loss reduction

Magnetic field generated by the current flowing through the cable conductor in AC systems induces
voltage along metallic sheaths of the cable. This voltage is proportional to the current flowing through
the conductor. In order to minimise voltages induced in the metallic sheaths, screens, armours at the
ends of the cable various bonding methods are used, which:

- Reduce or eliminate losses in metallic sheaths
- Reduce voltages in metallic sheaths by using joints sectioning
- Ensure lower impedance for fault currents flowing through the cable
- Maintain continuity of metallic sheaths that ensure proper protection against overvoltage
- Reduce voltages in metallic sheaths that appear during interferences/ failures to lower levels

Here below, a brief summary of the main metallic sheath connection methods are summarised.

For a complete description please refer to CIGRE TB 797 titled "Sheath bonding systems of AC
transmission cables - Design, testing, and maintenance".


### 4.2.2.1 Solid bonding

In this bonding method cable screen / metal sheath of the three cables is normally connected to earth
grids at both ends via link box.

Double point bonding system is the simple and common method.

To eliminate the induced voltages in cable sheath it is necessary to earth the sheath at both ends of the
cable circuit.

This method eliminates the need for the parallel continuity conductor used in single bonding systems. It
also eliminates the need to provide SVL, such as the one used at the free end of single point bonded
cable circuits.

This method has no limitation in length, provided that sheath grounding is provided along the route (e.g.,
every 5 km or depending on national standards and rules).

## Figure 89 – Connectivity of a solid bonding system

Advantages

- No standing voltages occur at the cable ends, which makes it the most secure regarding safety
    aspects
- Minimum material / components required
- Most economical if heating is not a main issue
- Provides path for fault current, minimizing earth return current and Earth Grid Voltage Rise at cable
    destination
- Does not require screen voltage limiter (SVL)
- Less electromagnetic radiation
- Reduced maintenance
- Can lay cables in trefoil formation to reduce screen losses

Disadvantages

- Provides path for circulating current
- Heating effects in cable screen, greater losses
- As a result, cable might need to be de–rated or larger cable required
- Transfers voltages between sites when there is an Earth Grid Voltage Rise at one site


### 4.2.2.2 Single-point bonding

In this bonding method the sheaths of the three cables are connected and grounded at one point only
along their length. The single point bonding method eliminates the current flowing in screen / metal
sheath, but this bonding method does not provide path for the flow of circulating currents or fault currents
coming from external origin.

The voltage is induced linearly along the whole cable length and at the “open end” a standing voltage
occurs. The open end should be protected with a sheath voltage limiter (SVL). This diminishes the
chance of overvoltage occurring inside the cable screen, protects the cable system and ensures that
relevant safety requirements are upheld.

Generally, standing voltage is not of much concern unless the runs are long and conductor currents are
high. Standing voltage figures shall be in accordance with national rules and/or customer’s
requirements.

## Figure 90 – Connectivity of a single-point bonding system

The highest voltages induced in screens are expected to occur at single-phase fault outside of the cable
line. In order to reduce the risk of fault current flowing through layers of soil of underground infrastructure
more distant from the cable, thus increasing the value of the induced voltage across the cable sheath,
an ECC cable (Insulated Earth Continuity Conductor) should be installed either along the route of the
cable line, or in direct contact of the cables.This cable must be on both sides connected with the
elements of the circuits with ground return and grounded. It is recommended to transpose the ECC
cable in the middle of its route.

Advantages

- Losses caused by circulating currents cannot occur - no heating in the cable screen / metallic sheath
- Economical solution
- Lower conductor cross section when compared to solid bonding at the same rating due to the
    elimination of circulating current

Disadvantages

- Standing voltage which occurs at one end of the termination (proportional to the length of the cable
    and the magnitude of current in the conductor)
- Requires SVL
- Requires additional earth continuity conductor for fault current


- This method is typically used for high voltage systems composed of one length only, so with limited
    lengths
- Requires periodical maintenance for checking the SVL’s status and sheath integrity

### 4.2.2.3 Mid-point bonding

Mid point bonding is applied to cable systems composed of two lengths, with one joint in the middle. In
this bonding method the cable sheath is connected in the joint by means of a straight through version
with a grounding connection, which is directly connected to the earth and SVL’s installed at the
terminations, as shown in figure below.

The advantage of mid-point bonding is that losses caused by circulating currents cannot occur. The
disadvantage of mid-point bonding is the voltage that occurs at both end of the termination. The “open
end” shall be protected with SVL. This diminishes the chance of overvoltage occurring inside the cable
sheath, protects the cable system and ensures that relevant safety requirements are upheld.

In order to limit the electrical risk for touch voltages, a solution that foresees a direct earthing at the
termination and SVL’s at the joint bay would be recommended. In this case a sectionalised joint shall
be provided.

## Figure 91 – Connectivity of a mid-point bonding system

Advantages and disadvantages are the same as for the single point bonding system.

### 4.2.2.4 Cross bonding

Cross-bonding is necessary for long cable segments with many joints. This method consists in dividing
the cable length into one or multiple three approximately equal sections with the sheaths connected and
grounded at the ends of the circuit and every three sections. Thus, the vectorial sum of the induced
voltage is practically zero. In an ideal cross-bonding system, the three sections are of equal length and
spacing. The advantage of cross-bonding is the absence of residual voltages at the end of the three
sections. With no driving voltages, the sheath currents and therefore the losses in the system are zero.
In reality, some minor differences between each section and a low current flow in the sheath do actually
cause some losses. However, with a good cross-bonding system, the sheath losses can be kept very
low. Another advantage of regular cross-bonding is that at the grounded termination ends the voltage is
zero.


## transposition Figure 92 – Connectivity of a cross bonding cable system with two major sections and no phase

```
transposition
```
As far as the transposition itself is concerned, it must be considered that while the cables with trefoil
arrangement maintain a geometry such as to guarantee an equal and constant spacing between the
phases, in cables with a flat arrangement, the lateral phases have inductance values higher than that of
the central phase. Therefore, for the practical purposes of the realization, two different transposition
schemes are considered and precisely:

- Cables laid in trefoil formation

With this arrangement, only to the transposition of the metal sheaths will be carried out, keeping the
position of the cables unchanged.

- Cables laid in flat formation

With this provision, both the transposition of the cables and that of the metal screens will be carried out,
thus ensuring a more balanced distribution of the phase inductance.

### 4.2.2.5 Continuous cross bonding

This method of connecting the metal cable screens represents a variant of the cross bonding system,
where the grounding is not carried out every three pieces of cable but in the realization of a continuous
cross-transposition of the metal protections at all the joints and the earthing of metal screens at both
connection ends. This system is used in all those cases where there is a considerable variation in length
between the section lengths or a great variability in the spacing between the phases. In this way, through
continuous transposition, all the aforementioned imbalances are spread over the entire section realized
with this method, thus bringing an average benefit in terms of thermal rating. In order to balance the
losses on the metallic sheaths a cross bonding of the cable sheaths can be provided at each joint.

### 4.2.2.6 Mixed systems

Mixed bonding method is used for long route length when the number of minor sections is not exactly
approximately divisible by three, the system can consist of different combinations, such as cross
bonding with single-point bonding or with double point bonding to limit the sheath voltage level.

This situation is often observed in actual installations, as the number of minor sections is not determined
by the cross-bonding. What is rather more taken into account in this respect is the possibility to reduce
the number of joints to minimum and thus to reduce the costs.

Since the grounding resistance is normally much higher than the resistance at the termination
(substation), this addition of the single-point-bonding section may significantly increase the zero-
sequence impedance of the cable without the ECC.


### 4.2.2.7 Direct cross bonding

Direct cross bonding is a method where the screens are transposed directly (without the need for link
boxes).

In direct cross bonding, shorter single core bonding leads can be used, as the leads do not need go to
a link box, but may go directly from one joint to the next. The highest transient overvoltages occur in
sections closest to the terminations. Therefore, it is conceivable to limit the protection of screen
interruptions to major sections located at both ends of the circuit. At the other cross-bonding points,
cross-bonding of the screens is then performed “directly” by jointing single-core bonding leads without
SVLs.

This solution consists to use single core bonding leads to make joint screen cross-bonding directly from
one joint to the others inside the joint-bay.

## Figure 93 – Direct cross bonding connections

It must be noted that the advantage of reducing the number of link boxes in the system should be
compared with the complexity in locating a fault, as well as the need for testing the cable outer sheaths.

### 4.2.3 Induced voltages

The induced voltage within a cable system depends on the mutual reactance between core and sheath,
the conductor current per phase and the cable length.

The magnitude of EMF induced in the cable sheath is directly proportional to the magnitude of the
current through conductor and the magnitude of mutual inductance between sheaths of the cables,
which depends upon the spacing between cables, cable diameter and length of the circuit. Spacing
between different cores of cable depends upon how the cable has been laid down in trenches i.e. in
trefoil formation or flat formation.

As the inter spacing between single core cables in flat formation is always bigger as compared to trefoil
formation, hence voltage induced is also higher. The voltage induced between the sheaths of each cable
is opposite in magnitude which causes circulating current to flow in the sheath when both ends of
metallic sheaths are bonded to the ground. Sheath circulating current causes power loss and heating
due to which cables are required to be de-rated. In order to utilize full capacity of the cable, special
sheath bonding methods are employed.

### 4.2.3.1 Sheath Voltage Limiter (SVL)

SVL is a protective device to limit induced voltages appearing on the bonded cable system due to
external transient voltages from faults, switching and lightning. It is necessary to install SVL’s between
the metallic screen / metallic sheath and ground inside the link box. The screen/sheath separation of
power cable joint (insulated joint) will be protected against possible damages resulting from induced


voltages caused by short circuit/break down. The necessary SVL minimum AC withstand voltage is
determined by the need to withstand the maximum AC voltage occurring on the cable sheath during the
high current HV cable fault. Additionally, SVL’s must withstand the range of transient surge voltages
and consequent lighting and switching surge current that may occur in the system.

Last but not least, it must be taken into account the voltage increase coming from ground impedance in
series with the SVL’s in case of transients.

### 4.2.3.2 Importance of bonding leads length

Bonding leads are important items in sheath bonding and earthing circuits and whilst for power
frequency applications their influence on induced sheath voltage is negligible, for transient overvoltage
applications bonding leads can have a significant effect on sheath voltages. Bonding lead type and
length have a great influence on sheath interrupt voltage (Barrier Voltage).

While Laboratory measurements have shown that the response time of ZnO surge arresters should not
introduce any significant time delay in the protection of sheath interrupts against fast front transients,
the bonding leads may introduce a delay proportional to their length.

Bonding leads could be of concentric or single-core type.

TB 283 (Special bonding of HV cables) reports a study in which are compared concentric cables single-
core cables in terms of voltage barrier characteristics, where it can be demonstrated that concentric
cables performs better than single-core cables.

However, when using single core leads, the voltage overshoot can be greatly reduced by tie-wrapping
the go/return leads together to minimise inductance or by restricting bonding lead length.

A detailed study on this phenomenon can be found into TB 283.

### 4.2.4 Transient voltages

The study of electromagnetic transients is linked to phenomena that can occur on the network as a
result of:

- Opening or closing (switching) operations of circuit breakers (over-voltages of internal origin)
- Atmospheric lightning strikes (over-voltages of external origin)

The operation of an electrical system involves frequent insertion and exclusion operations of circuits or
portions of the network both in relation to the needs of normal operation and to eliminate any faults that
may arise.

Each topological variation determines a modification of the electrical state of the system accompanied
by voltage and current transients. In particular, the manual or automatic operations that are carried out
give rise to so called over-voltages of internal origin. They must be carefully designed to determine if
auxiliary protection devices (surge arresters) are needed.

The trend of the transients is essentially determined by the parameters L and C of the circuit, with waves
oscillating in a frequency range of 100-1000 Hz which, overlapping the fundamental, can give rise to
over-voltages higher than the insulation level of the network components and/or devices.

The most effective measures for reducing these over-voltages are represented by the installation of
surge arresters sized in order to dissipate excess energy.

Lightning strikes are the main cause of over-voltages of external origin and can be divided into direct
lightning, when the lightning strikes the line directly, and indirect, when the lightning strike falls in the
vicinity of the power line inducing an overvoltage on it due to the electromagnetic coupling.

The situation considered is that of the direct lightning strike of a stretch of overhead line with over-
voltage propagation on a cable circuit.

This is a phenomenon at high frequencies, between 50 kHz and 1 MHz, which is studied through the
