traveling wave theory considering the propagation of overvoltage and current waves along the circuit.
This behavior can be described through the wave impedance of the circuit.

During propagation, the voltage waves undergo deformations and reflections. The worst case in
normally represented by the propagation of the overvoltage wave across the line with high impedance


at one end (e.g., a transformer). In this setting the incident wave meets the transformer which has a
practically infinite wave impedance and the voltage in the cable reaches, due to the reflections, a value
double that of the incident wave at the cable termination.
The impulse withstand voltage levels or Basic Impulse Levels (BIL) depend on the type of phenomenon
and they are referred to in studies for the coordination of insulation

## 4.3 Installation design related to cable system design

The installation design of an HV cable system must consider several parameters, such as:

- phase spacing
- installation depth
- backfill materials
- distance from other services

Cable design can influence installation design, for example:

- weight of cable may dictate support or cleat design
- lead sheaths are susceptible to fatigue
- aluminium sheathed cables are easier to install

Accessory design can influence installation design, for example:

- mechanical performances of the accessories may dictate the type of cable istallation

```
(Rigid/flexible)
```
- weight of outdoor sealing ends may dictate support design
- oil tanks may be required for cables and/or accessories
- allowable length of bonding lead may dictate positions of link boxes or pillars
- Oil tanks required space and position
- Stop joint bay
- GIS terminations

Installation parameters will define the installation design, for example:

- variation of phase spacing with depth
- alternative backfills if chosen material is not available
- existing services or obstructions that may be encountered during installation
- protective measures may affect ventilation in tunnels
- cable depth and spacing
- Design of cable and accessory supports

### 4.3.1 Preliminary study

The rules for making a project become a good project can be summarized into four steps:

- Well designed
- Well manufactured
- Well installed


- Well maintained

A detailed preliminary study is at the base of the above and should at least include the following activities
and evaluations:

### 4.3.1.1 Data collection

Electrical data

Voltage, ampacity, load curve, short circuit/fault level and duration, impulse level, touch voltage, cost of
full load and no-load losses.

Site general environmental data

Type of soil, soil temperature variation, soil thermal resistivity, frost depth, environmental hazards
(seismicity, flood, fire, etc.), stability, hardness, drying out, archaeology and water table.

Identification of obstacles to cross

Roads, railways, rivers, national parks, archaeological sites, thermal and electrical proximities, services,
trees.

Preliminary system design study

Basing on the above information and the length of the link, it is possible to divide (if necessary) the route
into sections and, for each of them, defining the possible civil construction techniques, as well as the
appropriate cable cross section, the earthing method, the architecture and the number of cables per
phase required to satisfy the transmission load requirements.

### 4.3.1.2 Choice of a civil construction technique

The choice of the appropriate civil construction technique must consider the following:

- Local and National regulations
- Allowed civil work techniques
- Allowed time of trench opening
- Right of way
- Site prejudice
- Site duration, site working time
- Construction cost
- Site prejudice cost
- Maintenance and repair methods and costs
- Available civil work techniques in the country
- EMF compatibility
- Safety aspects

### 4.3.1.3 Choice of cable installation technique

The choice of the appropriate cable installation technique must consider the following:


- Access to site: size and weight of the drums according to transportation facilities and regulations

```
(road, railway, sea, river, canal, etc.)
```
- Availability of lifting and handling
- Availability of storage
- Maintenance and repair requirements
- Installation technique: rigid or flexible
- Minimum bending radii
- Pulling technique
- Pulling tension
- Sidewall pressure
- Design loads
- Cable and accessories design
- Cable system performance (ampacity and thermo-mechanical thrusts)

## 4.4 Mechanical design

For most of the Systems the cable main mechanical characteristics are dictated by the electrical and
thermal design. Only in particular cases the cable structure has features that are implemented only to
satisfy other aspects, such as route features or environmental constraints.

The loads to be considered for the mechanical system design can be split into external loads, such as
wind, ice, seismic and intrinsic systems loads, such as cable weight, forces developed by the cables
during normal or exceptional service conditions, forces due to accessories, like joints and terminations.

The following paragraphs summarize the main parameters and consideration affecting the mechanical
design methods.

### 4.4.1 Design Loads

### 4.4.1.1 Typical Design Loads

- Service loads, thrust, short circuits forces
- Weight
- Environmental loads, wind, ice, seismic
- Installation loads

Thrust loads in rigid systems:

- Generated by conductors and metallic sheaths
- Result from service or ambient temperature changes
- Act axially, typically at terminations
- Act radially, at bends

Thrust loads in flexible Systems:

- Cable acts as a spring, the thermal expansion causes increased deflection, this generates a reaction

```
at the supports. Equilibrium at the support if the spans on either side are the same (they usually are)
```
- Generally, the longer the span and the greater the initial offset, the lower the thrust (however, this

```
requires plenty of space)
```

Thrust loads at Rigid-Flexible Interface:

- Some thermal expansion occurs from the rigid section; thrust in rigid section therefore reduces
- Core migration causes increased deflection in flexible span; thrust in flexible span therefore

```
increases
```
### 4.4.1.2 Weight Loads

- Especially important in flexible systems
- Long spans lead to high weight loads at supports
- In shafts weight dictates cleat design

### 4.4.1.3 Short Circuit Loads

- Fault current may be carried in two or more cables
- Conductors experience mutual repulsion
- Force is proportional to current² / distance between phases
- Acts on cables and on support system
- Not a problem for the sheaths of rigidly installed cables

### 4.4.1.4 Environmental loads

- Wind, in any direction
- Ice, extra weight and higher wind loads
- Seismic loads, horizontal or vertical
- Vibrations

### 4.4.2 Rigid or flexible systems, or "how to deal with temperature change and expansion"

- Two causes of change in cable temperature

```
o ambient temperature variations, typically ±10 ºC daily
o service temperature variations, may be much greater
```
- Rigid installation, expansion and shrinkage are restrained, generates thrust (conductor thrust and

```
sheath thrust)
```
- Flexible installation, cable changes shape to accommodate expansion (metallic sheath undergoes

```
strain)
```
### 4.4.2.1 Rigid Installations

- directly buried, filled troughs or ducts, close cleated in air to avoid buckling
- cables cannot expand or move so expansion is restrained
- thrust forces must be withstood by accessories and supports
- cables generate radial forces at bends
- thrust forces reduce as cables relax

### 4.4.2.2 Flexible Installations

- cables in air with long spans between cleats


- cable (or system) expansion causes a change in shape
- waveform is carefully designed so that sheath strain is acceptable
- cables offset vertically (‘sagged’) or horizontally (‘snaked’)
- thrust is developed, but much less than in rigid systems

### 4.4.2.3 Semi-Flexible Installations

The definition of semi flexible installations apply when the full deformation, expansion or contraction, of
the cable is not completely free.

The typical semi flexible installation is a cable in an unfilled duct.

Some thrust is developed, and the ratio between thrust and deformation is dependent on many
parameters. It is very difficult to obtain reliable figures for both thrust and deformation.

### 4.4.2.4 Transitions

- Ducted section in a directly buried route or rigid or semi-flexible sections after installation into duct
- From rigidly installed sections to flexible and vice versa: expansion from rigid section can feed into

```
flexible section
```
- Cable near terminations: flexible sections can be used to reduce the thrust at terminations

Transition between flexible and rigid systems

Transition sections between rigid and flexible runs of cable occur most commonly adjacent to cable
terminations.

Transition sections also occur adjacent to joints in some designs of duct-manhole systems, as well as
between buried cable and bridge crossings at bridge expansion joints. The transition between flexible
and rigid installation give rise to unbalance mechanical thrust of the conductor and axial movement that
could impair cable integrity.

The method of transition from the underground buried cable or tunnel cable has a significant effect on
the magnitude of the axial thrust and retraction forces that the cable termination is required to withstand
in service.

Although it is desirable that any given cable installation should be designed as a wholly rigid or wholly
flexible system, it may sometimes be necessary, however, to mix the two above designs within a single
cable route and special consideration must be given to the interface between the two systems.

Rigidly restrained cable systems are characterized by the presence of a substantial mechanical thrust
in the conductor when the cable is heated, and this is fully transferred to the interfaces (terminations
and joints). The advantage of a rigid system is that it occupies the least space in a substation. The
downside is that the cable termination and support structure must be designed to withstand the force

Flexible systems have a low value of conductor thrust and retraction forces on terminations. The
advantage is that the cable termination and off going equipment such as GIS busbar does not have to
be designed to withstand the full magnitude of thrust. The disadvantages are:

1. Sufficient space is required to accommodate i) the flexible length of cable where it passes from
    the horizontal cable approach, through the quadrant bend, to the vertical termination and ii) its
    lateral deflection, this generally being small. With large conductor areas and large cable diameter
    EHV cables the bending radius is large and so in some congested sub-stations this method may
    be impracticable due to lack of space.
2. The flexible cables are not completely free as they must be constrained to withstand violent
    disturbance by the short circuit expulsive force.


3. The termination of the cable’s metallic sheath at the base of the cable termination must be
    protected against disturbance by thermo-mechanical and short-circuit bending moment.

At the interface between rigid and flexible systems, the conductor will tend to move from the rigidly
restrained section into the flexible section. The amount of the movement depends on the cable
characteristics and particularly on internal friction between the conductor/core and the other cable
components. The movement may extend over a few meters on both sides of the transition section and
may cause damage or disturbance to the insulation and unacceptable sheath strains, if appropriate
precautions are not taken.

In order to reduce the movement and its effect on cable integrity, it is good practice to install the cable
in a series of rigidly fixed curves at the extremity of the buried section, in order to provide a high frictional
resistance to the movement of the conductor within the cable.

If a joint is installed at the transition section, the behaviour of the joint itself must be carefully considered
in relation with the abovementioned phenomena of movement and axial thrust of the conductor.

In the most common joint design, there is no mechanical restriction to the conductor movements,
whereas in other designs a mechanical block of the conductor is provided.

It should be verified that the movements or the mechanical thrust do not exceed acceptable limits for
accessories and for fatigue phenomena.

Transition design plays a very important role also in the transition at joints between cables of
substantially different mechanical rigidity, such as the case of different conductor cross sections/design,
where the thrust forces may cause joint or joint component displacements.

In cable routes with slopes or vertical parts, it may be required to avoid slipping of the cable expansion
length and to avoid its accumulation at the lower position. In horizontal in-air installations it may be
desirable to fully decouple flexible and rigid sections.

Several methods exist to properly design a transition at terminations:

1. Rigid cleating
2. Flexible bends or waves
3. Rigid cleating on a flexible support that allows a limited degree of movement of the termination,
    where this is designed to accept some limited lateral movements

Where cables are partly laid into unfilled ducts and partly directly buried or laid in filled throughs, at the
interface between rigid and flexible systems, the conductor will tend to move from the rigidly restrained
section into the flexible section. Ducts of appropriate size are then required to allow a kind of snaking
inside the duct itself.

One must be aware that the movement of the whole cable inside the duct may extend on both sides of
the duct section. If a joint is installed at the transition section, the behavior of this joint must be carefully
considered in relation with the abovementioned phenomena of possible movement and axial thrust of
the cable. Even when the joint is buried, additional fixing of the joint is required by cleating or locking
bends.

Locking Bend Theory

At the interface between a rigid and a flexible cable installation, the cable will try to expand rapidly from
the restrained section into the flexible section. This movement must be controlled to avoid damage
occurring in the flexible section to the dielectric and to the sheath as it may be subject to unacceptable
strains. For this reason the cable is normally “locked” in a transition section comprising a locking bend
or locking waves (a series of snaking bends). Figure below shows the cumulative bend angle Φ in a
rigidly constrained locking wave:

#### Φ = (Ө1 + Ө2 + Ө3 + Ө4 + Ө5 + Ө6) ( 1 )


## Figure 94 – Accumulation of bend agles in a rigidly constrained cable locking wave

The locking bends are placed in the rigid section of the cable. The locking bends work as by introducing
bends in the installation, the length of cable which will contribute to expansion at the ends is reduced.
This is due to the increasing frictional resistance between the conductor and dielectric.

The actual value depends on different parameters and circumstances so that it is impossible to give a
simple definition. It has been observed that the slipping force depends on conductor temperature, as
well as on cable type (bending stiffness and friction between core and sheath).

The bends will therefore allow thrust to be relieved at the flexible/rigid interface with a much smaller
expansion into the flexible section than if the cable were laid straight. There must still be a holding force
in the flexible section F2 as the thrust cannot be completely eliminated. The locking wave is therefore
designed to limit the expansion of the conductor into the flexible section of the route whilst also reducing
the thrust in the rigid section to a level which can be withstood by a holding force in the flexible section.

Radial and longitudinal forces are transferred from the cable onto the surrounding backfill or supporting
structure.

The locking wave is generally positioned at the end of the main run of rigidly constrained cable (e.g.,
close cleated or buried). There are some situations, for example in the case of inclined ducts, where
locking bends may be installed in an intermediate point of the cable run.

A locking bend can be used to reduce the influence of thermo-mechanical conductor thrust on joints and
terminations. Simple offset locking bends (S-bends) may be located on either side of a buried joint.

The axial force is reduced around the cumulative bend angle. The reduction in force may be
approximated by the formula for the ‘capstan effect’ around a bend. (This equation is also used to
calculate tensile loads on the steel bond during cable installation).

In the simple capstan effect model the degree of force reduction depends on the coefficient of friction μ
between cable core and sheath and on the bend angle.

#### ( 2 )

where:

F 1 : high axial compressive force at the high force entrance to the bend

F 2 : lower axial compressive force at angle Φ in the bend

μ : effective coefficient of friction

Φ : bend angle [radians]

The effective coefficient of friction is high for the thermo-mechanical movement of extruded cores
through a metallic sheath cables or of cable through rigidly fixed cleats. (In comparison the coefficient
of friction is low for pulling cables over rollers or through ducts during installation). Measured values are
not readily available. Investigations made by pulling extruded cores through several type of cable
designs have shown values variable in a quite wide interval, ranging from 0.3 to about 2.


The simple capstan theory assumes that:

- A reaction, (backing force), F 2 , must always be present at the exit of the bend
- The bending stiffness EI of the cable does not contribute to the locking effect
- Friction acts along the complete length of the cumulative bends

As mentioned above, the simple capstan principle assumes that the cable core has no structural bending
stiffness and can move inside the metallic sheath under the constraint of friction.

It must also be noted that, for straight sections, we will have that F 1 is equal to F 2.

This means that the core could be taken out from the cable with no reaction.

This is not true, due both to gravity effect and to the fact that there is always a friction between the
extruded core and the sheath (which value depends on cable design), as well as the core expansion
effect due to the temperature, thus affecting the friction.

Considering the above factors and the bending stiffness of the core, a different holding strength can be
expected in the reality. This consideration should be taken into account for calculations and suggests
the importance of performing measurement of the internal coefficient of friction between the extruded
core and sheath and the withstand force of the locking wave. These are foundamental for both analytical
calculations as well as for FEA analysis.

### 4.4.3 Joint bay and manhole design

Along the route of an underground link, different installation techniques may be used. If all the
techniques are all rigid or all flexible, the behavior of the link seems to be homogeneous, but even in
this case, problems may appear around the jointing areas.

If the installation techniques are different, i.e. unfilled duct and direct burial or filled trough and manhole
or unfilled duct and manhole, damages can happen on the link if transitions between flexible and rigid
installations and of course around the jointing are not treated or badly treated.

### 4.4.3.1 Cable in ducts

The ducts may be unfilled or filled with solid material such as bentonite. The bentonite solution is often
preferred for relatively short ducts (normally in the order of 100 m) used to cross roads, railways and
other obstacles. These ducts are inserted in sections where the cable is directly buried. Any movement
of thermal origin is completely prevented, and the cable behaves as if it were directly buried. This case
can be dealt as rigid installation

In other situations, according to different practices or due to other constraints, the ducts are not filled.

Typically this is the case where the possibility to replace the cable without major civil works is valuable,
for example under roads or in densely populated areas, or where possible cable upgrading is foreseen.

While for the cable design an unfilled ducts represents an issue, since the pattern for heat dissipation is
worsened by the air gap inside the duct, for the system design unfilled ducts can help in the design of
the joint bays / manholes, reducing the forces to be managed.

However this general consideration has to be balanced to the possibility of the cable to move outside
the duct at both ends, and with the difficulties to obtain reliable figures of the thrust and deformation of
cables in ducts, so to properly design the layout and structures at the joints.

Qualitative considerations of cable behavior in ducts

After pulling the cable can be considered in the lower canter part of the duct in the straight sections, and
closer to the inner duct wall in case of bends. Such assumption is already a simplification: actually the
cable has a “memory” of its manufacturing and storage conditions, so small ondulations can be present.
The forces experienced during pulling not only could not be sufficient to “straight” the cable, but also
may induce a certain unbalance: near the pulling side such ondulations could be lower than the reel
side, where much lower pulling forces are experienced.


When loaded the heat generated by Joule effect increase the cable temperature and as a consequence
a thrust is generated.

When the temperature reach a certain value, called first buckling temperature, the thrust is partially
absorbed by the geometrical deformation of the cable.

Tests and calculations has shown that such deformation generate a cylindrical sinusoid with a fixed
pitch. The sag of such sinusoid change in accordance with the cable temperature raise, but is limited by
the clearance between cable and duct inner diameter.

If the temperature further increase exceeding a certain value, called second buckling temperature, the
cable configuration may change to cylindrical helix, with cable touching also the upper part of the duct.

When the maximum possible geometrical deformation in accordance with cable-duct clearance is
reached then the temperature increase generates thrust as in a rigid installation.

Another aspect to be considered is the presence of slopes or significant altitude difference between the
duct section ends.

Such features may give place to additional loads to the joints, lowering the thrust at the upper side and
increasing the thrust at the lower end.

The presence of bends along the route minimize such effects, while vibrations may act as a trigger of
cable permanent displacement along the ducts towards the lower end.

This permanent displacement can be seen as an higher amplitude of the sinusoids of the cable
compared with the amplitude at the upper end.

Quantitative considerations

The formula that provides the relationship among thrust, deformation and temperature is relatively
simple:

#### 𝑇=𝐸𝐴 ×[𝜖− (𝛼 × ∆𝜃)] ( 3 )

where ԑ is the geometrical deformation of the cable and α*Δθ is the deformation due to temperature
change.

The critical point is the calculation of ԑ, which depends on geometry of the cable, geometry of the ducts
and physical parameters, here below listed:

EA, axial stiffness of the cable

EJ, Bending stiffness of the cable

GJ, Torsional stiffness of the cable

α, effective coefficient of expansion of the cable, actually the conductor

μ, friction coefficient between cable outer layer and duct inner layer

W, cable unit weight

ΔD, clearance duct-cable that is duct inner diameter less cable outer diameter

L, R, θ, σ, geometrical duct features, namely length of straight sections, bend radius, bend angle, sloping
angle

The calculation is analytically possible only for simplified cases, such as straight duct sections, while for
real cases finite elements modelling methods shall be applied.

It must also be considered that cable parameters are not fixed, but change, reducing from the initial
values due to relaxation of cable structure.


Other parameters, such as the friction coefficient, may not be the same all along the duct, for example
in case of not homogeneous lubricant application, and may also change during the service life.

The results of such calculation provide the pitch of the cable sinusoid or helix inside the duct, and the
expected deformation in accordance with cable temperature.

Considering the expected daily and seasonal cable temperature changes is it possible to evaluate the
sheath fatigue and verify if the limits applicable in accordance to the sheath material and construction
are respected.

In case of cable without sheath, or installed in moisture free environment, such verification is not
necessary.

In case of duct section with slopes the first check to be carried out is the self stability of the cable.

The general formula for cable stability along a sloping section is:

#### 𝑙 ×𝑤 ×[𝑠𝑖𝑛𝛼− (𝑐𝑜𝑠𝛼 × 𝜇)]≥ 0 ( 4 )

with l= duct length

w= weight per m of the cable

α = the slope angle

μ = the friction coefficient

The stability limit is when sin α = cos α * μ , so when Tan α = μ.

When tan α is greater than μ, the cable is unstable, that is the force due to friction is not any more
sufficient to compensate for the weight component along the duct.

In this case the anchoring force shall be provided at the upper joint bay/manhole, increasing the total
restraining force from the value calculated for the cable deformation inside the duct.

The second check to be performed is about the length of cable affected by the force generated by cable
cooling during the daily cycles.

If such length is greater that the duct length the cable will always be able to recover any possible
displacement towards the lower part of the route.

The formula to calculate such length L, assuming the route straight, is:

#### 𝑙 = 𝛼 × ∆𝜃 ×

```
𝐸𝐴
(𝜇 × 𝑤)^ (^5 )^
```
To be on the safe side the EA value to be used shall be the reduced one.

This general consideration must then be applied taking into the actual route, so the real slope features,
for example short sloping sections with intermediate flat sections, and the position of bends. Bends may
reduce the length where displacement recovery takes place

In any case to be on the safe side the effect of the slope should be considered at joints, taking into
account the altitude difference with the adjacent joints. For the joints at the higher side of the route the
weight of the cable shall be subtracted to the thermo-mechanical forces, the opposite for the joint at the
lower side.

Practical considerations

According to tests, calculations and experiences the following practical considerations should be taken
into account when considering duct systems:


- A clearance increase reduce the thrust and improve the sheath fatigue issue. Clearance of about
    60 mm or at least 1,5 times the ratio between duct diameter and cable diameter should be always
    considered
- Bends along the duct route improves the situation, reducing the thrust at joints and also cable
    stability
- In case of significant slopes the duct route must be analyzed carefully, since load unbalance may
    appear at joints, especially if the joints are in between a continuous sloping section. Such loads
    have to be considered while assessing the design of the joint bay /manhole and the relevant
    structures. Additional locking waves may have to be realized between the joint and the ducted
    cable to increase the anchoring forces. The joint bay structure shall be consequently reinforced.
- In case of long steep sloping sections the possibility to insert an intermediate anchoring point
    shall be considered. This will reduce the forces at joints

The so called “Japanese approach” for such a condition defines three different design concepts that
may be adopted for duct installations:

a. Large diameter duct, cable blocked at the extremities

If the inner diameter of the duct is significantly larger than that of the cable (typically 1.5 to 2 times), the
thermal elongation results in cable snaking. The geometrical configuration is similar to a sinusoid or a
helix with a certain pitch and amplitude, depending on duct and cable diameters, weight, axial rigidity,
flexural rigidity of the cable, friction coefficient between cable and duct, temperature variation. Thanks
to the snaking effect, the axial thrust is drastically reduced with respect to the thrust developed by a
rigidly restrained cable. Because of the thermal cycling, the amplitude of the deformation varies cyclically
and the resistance of the cable to fatigue phenomena must be considered.

b. Small diameter duct, cable blocked at the extremities

If the diameter of the duct is only slightly larger than the cable (minimum clearance to allow the cable
pulling), the very limited snaking is not enough to reduce the axial thrust and the thermal movements
are negligible. In practice this case may be considered to be a rigid installation with full thrust force.

c. Small diameter duct, cable free to move at the extremities

In this type of installation there is a certain movement of the cable from the duct towards the manholes
because of the thermal expansion. In the manholes the cable is installed in a snaking configuration
properly designed in order to maintain the cyclic bending of the cable within acceptable limits.

### 4.4.3.2 Transition between ducts and manholes (unfilled open air manholes)

With the duct installation the full thermal expansion of the cable along the span appears in the manhole.
Therefore, an “Offset” part is provided in the manhole, which must absorb the thermal expansion so that
it does not have a negative effect on the cable and joint in the manhole. The offset design of the XLPE
cable in the manhole uses a geometrical analytical technique so that the cable bend radius does not
become smaller than the minimum allowable bend radius after the cable has thermally expanded out of
the duct.

In the case of a cable having a metal sheath it is necessary to consider the magnitude of the sheath
cyclic bending stress. The permissible amplitude of the sheath strain is read from the S-N curve
(amplitude Strain - Number of times to the destruction) and compared to the daily load cycle data, noting
that the daily thermal expansion number is greater than the yearly number.


**1) Design of the “Straight Offset”**

The first experiments with single core XLPE cables were based on the well-known technique with SCFF
cables and moveable joint offsets, which absorbed the thermal expansion of the cable. However, with
XLPE cables a spiral deformation of the cable occurred in the manhole. During this experiment an
inclination of the joint box and a longitudinal movement of the cable core occurred.

It was concluded that a rigid installation of the joint box is necessary, because the deformation of the
cable is concentrated on one bend only and the permissible minimum bend radius cannot be secured.

At present the following three offset design geometries are applicable:

a. Equal arcs range offset type
b. Long offset type
c. Three equal arcs with pendency type

a. "Equal arcs range offset type”

The geometric shape permits the offset to absorb thermal expansion in two circular arcs without a
change occurring in the position of their central turning point. Because of the possible spiral deformation
of the cable offset during thermal expansion, special attention is needed to ensure that the cable does
not touch other cables and joints in the manhole;

b. “Long offset type”

The geometric shape is provided with a straight section of cable between the joint and the offset bend.

The thermal expansion from the duct is absorbed by an arrangement of three circular arcs, which form
when the straight section bends to absorb the thermal expansion;

c. “Three equal arcs with pendency type”

The geometric shape permits the absorption of thermal expansion for large conductor cable sizes. It
comprises three circular arcs in a three-dimensional arrangement. This geometric form does not have
the straight line present between offset bends as in the “Equal arcs range offset” geometry, because
large cables would sag between the support points, such that the sag at its lowest point would infringe
the permissible bending radius.

Each geometry is shown diagrammatically in Table 5 :

Method of supporting cable in the manhole

Cable guides (cleats) are installed at the duct entry and adjacent to the joint to avoid negative influences
on the cable during thermal expansion. The support methods for the cable, which can be installed
between the cable guides, vary with the type of each offset shape:

- “Equal arcs range offset type”. The cable offset is supported with the cable guide at the duct entry
    and adjacent to the joint. The cable between is not supported
- “Long offset type”. The cable offset is supported at its midpoint to prevent cable sag as, in some
    applications, the length of offset can become unacceptably long during cable expansion due to the
    length of the straight section
- “Three equal arcs with pendency type”. The cable is provided with suitable supports to ensure that
    in the pendent part during thermal expansion the cable’s permissible bending radius is not infringed


- On the other hand, the horizontal part of Triplex XLPE cables is supported with porcelain pillows,
    installed at a spacing of about 1-1.5m

## Table 5 – Offset calculations

m = cable thermal expansion

2) Offset design of the radiused bend part

The objective of this geometric design is to ensure that the bend angle Ө remains constant before and
after thermal expansion, as shown in te following figure.


## Figure 95 – Shape of a bend part

The bend radius is reduced from "R 0 " to "R" by i) the additional thermal expansion length "m" and ii) the
formation of the cable tangential ‘straight parts’.

### 4.4.3.3 Joint bay/manhole positioning

Joint bays/manholes positioning represent an important step in the definition of the cable system design.
Apart from constraints due to maximum cable lengths and/or transportation limits, most of the times
(e.g., in urban areas), the positioning is subjected to the availability of suitable areas, normally free from
subservices, where to locate the joint bay/manhole. The choice of the joint bay position should take into
account some main recommendations such as:

- Location at the midpoint of a straight section equidistant between bends of the same radius and
    same bend angle
- At the top of a hill with equally descending slopes on each side
- At the bottom of a valley with equal ascending slopes on each side
- Avoid entering a joint bay with cables not in axes with joints (e.g., avoiding installing a joint bay close
    to an HDD exit point)

4.4.3.4 Duct system design

Ducts may be of rigid type (normally PVC) or flexible (e.g., double wall corrugated type). HDPE ducts
can be considered as semi-rigid, since it is possible to bend them, but not below a certain bending
radius.

The minimum duct bending radii along the circuit shall be compatible with the value stated by the
manufacturers for the selected cable design for dynamic condition (i.e., when an axial force is applied
to the cable).

Due to their bending stiffness, when HDPE ducts are used, the minimum bending radius is sensibly
bigger than that of the cable itself, being dependent on duct diameter and temperature. Bending radius
values shall also be chosen taking into account possible duct deformation, cable sidewall pressure
exerted both for pulling and thermo-mechanical forces during normal service and overload conditions,
as well as fatigue strain to which the metallic screening/sheathing materials are subjected to.

Duct wall thickness shall also be selected considering the loads due to the environmental conditions,
for example if they are used under roads.

When using flexible ducts, in addition to the above matters, attention shall be paid to the possible
damage that may occur to the ducts caused by the messenger wire during pulling.

Also the duct joints must be carefully selected. In case of HDPE ducts , if a good sealing from external
conditions is required, butt welded joints or electro-fusion joints shall be used. Socket joint with gaskets
can also be used, but then a good soil stability shall be provided to avoid settlement and ducts
misalignment. PVC duct are connected with male/female assembly or special fittings, then glued or
cemented.


In all cases no beads, protrusions or steps shall be present inside the ducts, that may damage the cable
outer sheath and increase the forces during pulling. It must also be verified if duct joints can prevent
pulling in one of the two possible directions.

Where cables are installed into empty ducts, the objective is to design a duct system that reduces the
magnitude of the cable thermo-mechanical axial thrust to an acceptable level by incrementally
increasing the duct diameter clearance ∆D. The level of axial force becomes acceptable when a
manhole constraint system can be designed to withstand it. Different constraint systems depending on
the individual duct situation may lead to different clearances and vice versa.

### 4.4.3.5 Design of the constraint system

A constraint system is designed and located at both ends of the joint to withstand the difference between
the thermo-mechanical cable thrusts at each side. The constraint system is designed to prevent:

- external movement of the complete cable and joint through the manhole
- internal movement of the core through the cable sheath
- internal movement of the joint through its housing

The constraint system and support metalwork are designed to prevent:

1. The cable slipping, permitting its stored thermal strain from moving into the manhole to
    prospectively damage the cable, joint and support metalwork.
2. Longitudinal movement of the cable and joint through the manhole due to asymmetry in the axial
    thrust between the cable at the right-hand and left-hand ends of the manhole. Asymmetric thrust
    arises from the normal imbalance in the route geometries present on each side of the manhole
    and from the use of different cable designs, typically occurring in case of transitions.
3. Lateral movement of the cable, joint and support steel work due to the lateral component of the
    cable thermo-mechanical force and gravitational force.

Three types of constraint system exist for manhole or joint bay installation.

4.4.3.5.1 Train of Fixed Clamp-Cleats for Use with a Non-Anchor Joint

A non-anchor joint is the usual type of straight joint in which the cable is not anchored to the manhole
floor. The joint insulation is usually provided by one or more pre-molded elastomeric sleeves, Figure 96.
This type of joint provides minimal constraint to withstand differential thermo-mechanical conductor
thrust.


## Figure 96 – Non-anchor straight joint components

The rigid constraint system for large conductor EHV cable usually comprises two trains of high strength
clamping cleats in series located at each end of the manhole and a cable snaking, as shown in Figure
97. The cleat train and the snaking are designed to prevent any longitudinal slip of the cable and joints.

## Figure 97 – Clamp cleating and offset bend constraint system in a manhole

If the cable axial force is not balanced, for example the cable axial force FR at the right-hand end is
greater than that at the left-hand end FL such that a differential force (FR-FL) would tend to move the
cable and joints through the manhole from right to left. The objective is to design the manhole constraint
system to have a holding force:

#### 𝐹𝑇 ≥ (𝐹𝑅−𝐹𝐿) ( 6 )

Due to snaking the forces along the cable are reduced passing from the manholes entrances to the joint,
and the cleats keep the cable configuration providing also an additional contribution to such reduction.

A very small longitudinal compressive deflection of the cable occurs inside the train of cleats due to the
combination of i) the elasticity of the cleats’ rubber liner’ and of the cable’s polymeric over sheath and
ii) the axial elastic stiffness of the cable. The deflection is largest at the cleat closest to the entrance of


the manhole, as these experiences the highest cable thrust. The deflection progressively reduces along
the train of cleats towards the joint and in consequence the cleat adjacent to the joint, cleats n.7 in
Figure above have to withstand a significantly lower withstand forces than cleats 0 or 14 at the entrance
to the manhole. The non-linear distribution of cable force, significantly reduces the withstand force
efficiency of the train of cleats. An optimum length of constraint system exists at which adding more
cleats and cable length has no benefit in increasing the withstand force.

The advantages of the fixed clamping cleat constraint method are:

1. Movement of the cable and joint is minimal and so the possibilities are removed of i) disturbance
    of the joint insulation and ii) fatigue of the cable’s metallic sheath.
2. The cable snaking configuration is kept firmly
3. A factor safety exists as it is possible for one or more cleats to exceed their slip withstand force
    without the cable slipping through the complete train of cleats. It is noted that a cleat that exceeds
    its slip limit will still exert it’s withstood constraining force to the cable.

The disadvantage of the clamping cleat constraint method is that special high strength clamping cleats
are required. Conventional cable guide cleats are generally unsuitable for large conductor cables in this
application as they have too low a withstand force.

4.4.3.5.2 Recoil Cleat and Expansion Bend for use with a Non-Anchor Joint

This constraint system permits the cable thermal expansion to move into the manhole by a controlled
distance. The recoil cleat, Figure below, controls the distance. A cable expansion bend, Figure 103 , is
located within the manhole to absorb the movement.

This system is suited to cable designs that have sufficient flexibility to bend to a small radius that can
be accommodated in the manhole i.e. to cables with a smaller conductor size, thinner insulation (lower
voltage) and a less rigid type of metallic sheath and polymeric over sheath.

The recoil-cleat shown diagrammatically in Figure below, is a travelling cleat that is clamped to the
cable. The cleat is fixed to the end wall of the manhole by a number of recoil springs in parallel. When
the cable is heated it is allowed to expand out of the duct into the manhole. The further it enters the
manhole, the more the magnitude of its axial thermo-mechanical thrust is reduced. A distance is reached
at which equilibrium is attained between the cable thermo-mechanical thrust and the retraction force of
the stretched recoil spring and so movement stops. The clamp-cleat is designed to withstand repeated
applications of the equilibrium force during both heating and cooling throughout the life of the cable
system. It is good practice to apply a factor of safety the clamp force.


## Figure 98 – Diagram of recoil clamping cleat - at rest(top) , extended (bottom)

The manhole is designed to accommodate a cable S-bend as shown diagrammatically in Figure below.
The S-bend is designed to absorb the length of cable equal to the cleat’s travel length. In the example
the recoil-cleat permits the cable to move axially 151 mm and to absorb this movement the S-bend
deflects laterally 198 mm. The cable may be supported on a horizontal shelf at the end of which a fixed
stop is located to limit the maximum permissible deflection. An anchor-cleat is positioned on either side
of the joint to protect it from i) the residual axial force in the S-bend and ii) the bending moment exerted
by the S-bend. Upon cooling, the cable contracts, the thermo-mechanical force falls, and the recoil-
springs retract and so encourage the cable to move back, or ‘recoil’, into the duct. On a flat duct route
two identical recoil cleats are located into the manholes at each of its ends and are designed to each
absorb half of the cable expansion length. On a continuous incline the cable at the duct exit in the uphill
manhole is either i) constrained from movement downhill by an anchoring device, such as an anchoring
cleat or stocking, or ii) is constrained by a reversed spring recoil-cleat. The cleat at the downhill end
should be capable of counteracting the thermo-mechanical thrust and expansion from the complete
cable length.


## Figure 99 – Recoiling cleat and offsetbend manhole constraint system (from and FEA model)

The advantage of the recoil cleat system is that the resultant axial force is of reduced magnitude and
thus:

1. One long anchor-cleat only is required to withstand the cable thermo-mechanical thrust.
2. The number of clamp-cleats and quantity of supporting metalwork is reduced.

The prospective disadvantages of the recoil cleat system are:

1. The ability of the recoil-cleat to maintain its performance throughout the life of the cable system is
    critical to the cable system operation. If the grip of the recoil-cleat slips, the maximum length of free
    cable will enter the manhole under the action of thermo-mechanical and gravitational forces.
2. The flexible cable bend and the recoil spring experience cyclic strain and so must have suitable
    fatigue lives.
3. The manhole requires a greater width (or depth) to accommodate the expansion bend. Although the
    manhole may be shorter in length.

4.4.3.5.3 Clamped Constraint System for use with an Anchor Joint

The example “anchor joint” design shown in Figure below is of the composite prefabricated type in which
a metallic HV electrode embedded within a cylindrically shaped cast epoxy resin insulator is bolted to
the conductor connection.

The low voltage part of the insulator is in turn bolted to the floor of the manhole.


## Figure 100 – Prefabricated composite anchor joint components

The advantage of this type of joint is that, in combination with the clamping cleats, Figure below, it can
withstand high unbalanced cable thrusts, for example in excess of 60 kN. The disadvantage is that the
joint is large, has a higher cost and few manufacturers now supply it commercially.

## Figure 101 – Clamping cleat and offset bend constraint system with anchor joint


### 4.4.3.6 Design of a cable manhole/joint bay layout

Two generic types of cable layout are used:

a) Offset S-Bend Cable Configuration

The preferred cable configuration is to install two offset S-bends, one at each end of the manhole, Figure
below. The offsets comprise four bends, which increase the cumulative bend angle and so increase the
frictional constraint on the extruded core from moving inside the metallic sheath and, in particular,
through the pre-molded elastomeric insulation within the joint. The clamping cleats within the external
constraint system are designed to grip the outer surface of the cable and are not relied upon to grip the
internal cable core. Isolated cases of core movement in joints have been reported in straight-through
joint bay configurations.

The offset bends also reduce the risk of slipping of the train of clamping cleats.

## Figure 102 – Manhole offset bends generating internal friction restraint on XLPE core

b) Straight-through Cable Configuration

The cable passes through the duct, manhole and joint location in straight alignment, Figure 103. The
users of this configuration rely on the cable thermo-mechanical forces being balanced in duct route
geometries such that no differential force exists.

The advantages of this method are:

1. The manhole layout is simple.
2. The cost of the train of high strength clamping cleats is reduced.

The disadvantages are:

1. The manhole and its contents are subjected to high thermo-mechanical thrust even if the forces
    are of equal magnitude; thus the constraint system is required to be sufficiently robust to hold the
    cables in straight alignment to prevent a lateral component of force pushing the cable and support
    structure sideways.
2. In a duct route containing bends and changes in elevation it is improbable that the thermo-
    mechanical forces will be balanced at the manhole.
3. Risk exists of the movement of a) the core through the sheath and b) the cable and joints through
    the cleats. Installing an offset in the cable, as shown in Figure 102 , helps to restrain movement
    of the core within the cable sheath and joint housing.


## Figure 103 – Plan view of an in-line manhole layout

### 4.4.3.7 Typical manhole layout

Manholes may be precast concrete or cast-in-place depending upon construction limitations. Precast
manholes should be constructed with as few pieces as possible within weight and transportation
limitations.

Manhole lids and chimneys shall be large enough to allow access of workmen and materials,
approximately 915mm (36 inches). Manhole lid / chimney are typically offset from centerline away from
the wall where cables and splices are supported.

Seismic Design – When Required

Manholes should be designed to restrain and support embedded conduits in the end walls. When
necessary the encasement shall include rebar between the manhole and duct bank to prevent shear
planes at the end wall that could result in damage to the ducts and cables.

External Loading

Manholes, including lids and chimneys, shall be designed to a minimum highway loading per local
requirements. Manholes must also be designed to take in consideration buoyancy forces in high water
table areas.

Internal Loading

Manholes need to be designed to accommodate the forces of the cable restraint system / cable supports,
loading from pulling irons/anchors, and mechanical forces resulting from short circuit conditions.

In the rare event of a manhole explosion the manhole lids can go airborne. Current trends to mitigate to
this include tether systems attached to the lids and vault floor to allow for the release of the pressure
from the explosion but restrain the travel of the manhole lid. Other methods may include venting of the
manhole or specific design venting lids.

Manhole Flooring

Manhole floors should be designed with very gentle slopes toward sump pits located near the manhole
access to allow for ease of complete dewatering prior to entry.

Grounding & Bonding

Bonding leads from the cable splice to the link box shall be as short as possible but no greater than 10m
in total length to the ground point. To this end, link boxes may be mounted on the end walls in lower
voltage (smaller) manholes. EHV manholes may require the link boxes to be positioned behind cable, if
access can be maintained, or on the opposite wall from the cable splices with the bonding leads running
along the ceiling of the manhole.

Manhole internal ground ring should be sized for the rated fault currents. This ring is typically installed
high inside the vault to keep it away from any water ingress. The ring should be installed with no or
minimal sag between supports. Care should be taken when selecting the material of the supports and
associated hardware to prevent corrosion.


Ancillary equipment

Ancillary equipment such as fiber and dataloggers, they are typically installed in separate handholes
adjacent to the manhole. This guarantees ease of access for construction/maintenance/etc. without
having to have a deenergize cable system to enter the manhole.

EMF

If necessary, the manhole can be shielded against EMF by using several techniques, such as:

1. Passive loops;
2. Active systems.

In all the cases, special attention shall be paid to the extra joule losses generated by each single method,
which may impact on the system thermal performances.

## Figure 104 – Manhole EMF shielding by passive loops method

### 4.4.3.8 Typical joint bay layout

Also joint bays may be of precast concrete or cast in place depending upon construction limitations.
Joint bays with reinforced concrete floor and brick walls are also possible.

Depending on the space availability, the joint arrangement for AC circuits may be either:

1. placed one besides the other on the same horizontal axis;
2. on delta configuration, as shown in figure sss;
3. placed one after the other in line.

For DC cables the joint bay may have one or two cables, depending on the system design (Monopolar
or bipolar).

Joints inside joint bays may be backfilled or placed inside protection (normally concrete) encasements.

In the first case, a selected sand backfill is applied all around the joints, while in the second case, it is
recommended to fill in the encasement with cement mortar.


## Figure 105 – Example of joint bay design

Grounding & Bonding

As for manholes, bonding leads from the cable splice to the link box shall be as short as possible but
preferably no greater than 10m in total length to the ground point. Link boxes are normally installed
inside dedicated concrete pits placed besides the joint bay.

Joint bay ground ring should be sized for the rated fault currents. This ring is typically installed below
the joint bay basement and connected to copper spuds. Care should be taken when selecting the
material for the ring and the spuds to prevent corrosion galvanic corrosion.

Ancillary equipment

Ancillary equipment such as fiber and data loggers, they are typically installed in separate handholes
adjacent to the joint bay. This guarantees ease of access for construction/maintenance/etc. without the
need for de-energize the cable system to access the joint bay.

EMF

If necessary, joint bays for AC circuits can be shielded against EMF by using several techniques, such
as:

1. Ferromagnetic raceway;
2. Passive loops;
3. Active systems.

In all the cases, special attention shall be paid to the extra joule losses generated by each single method,
which may impact on the system thermal performances.


## Figure 106 – Passive loops arrangement on a joint bay

Please refer to TB 559 “Impact of EMF on current ratings” for further recommendations.

### 4.4.3.9 Earth resistance

Earth resistance value plays an important role in the definition of the earth potential rise (EPR) in case
of fault. Apart from possible National recommendations, there is a reference in CIGRE Electra n.47
(1976) “The design of specially bonded cable circuits“. At paragraph 11.2.4 there is a recommendation
for joint bays that states that values of earthing resistance of the order of 20 Ω are accepted.

Particular attention must be paid to all links having sections into tunnels. Here, the normally high rock
resistivity could make the above figure very difficult to be obtained or earthing could not be possible due
to external constraints (tunnel services, corrosion issues of the tunnel structure, etc.).

For this reason, it is preferable to avoid grounding at joint bays but adopting a “Delta” configuration for
the surge arresters (where provided).

### 4.4.4 Transitions at terminations

The design of the cable arrangements near terminations is mainly dependant on the terminations, and
the relevant supporting structures, mechanical withstand capabilities. The basic assumption is that any
conductor movement is prevented inside the termination arrangement, both for outdoor and GIS types.

In case of terminations able to withstand the full mechanical forces developed by the cable there are no
particular design issue: the vertical bend usually present below the termination provides a reduction of
the thrust in case of rigid installations such as cable directly buried or close cleated in air. The cable can
be rigidly cleated as well, to supporting frames below the termination.

If the cable is installed with a flexible system, but below the termination, for example due space
constraints, a rigid installation shall be considered as well, care must be taken to avoid that, when


loaded, the cable conductor/core is displaced towards the flexible part of the route. Some locking bends
should be created in between to reduce to safe limits such displacemement.

In case the termination is able to withstand a limited thrust compared to the full cable thrust, flexible
cable arrangement shall be foreseen near the termination, so in practice for the final bend/s below the
termination, to allow the cable expansion and the consequent thrust reduction.

Such flexible arrangement shall be carefully studied, considering the environmental constraints, to
evaluate the thrust foreseen at termination and the maximum acceptabled deformation of the sheath.

In case the cable are rigidly cleated or buried before the termination the contribution of the
conductor/core displacement towards the flexible part shall be considered. As mentioned, some locking
bends may be required at the transition between the rigid and flexible installation.

While the detailed calculation to approach these aspects can be found in TB 669, it is underlined that
also cable straight sections can contribute to a holding force on conductor/core.

The general formula to be used shall be:

#### 𝐹(𝑥+𝑑𝑥) =𝐹𝑥+𝐹𝑥 ×𝑑𝑥 ×

```
𝜇
𝑅𝑥+𝐾^ ×𝑑𝑥^ (^7 )^
```
where:

x= distance along the arc of the wave

dx= elementary distance

Fx=conductor force at x

Rx= radius of curvature at x

μ= coefficient of friction between conductor/core and sheath

K= force per linear m between the cable core/conductor

From a physical view point the value of K represent the force necessary to pull out the cable
conductor/core from the sheath in case of straight cable, so when R is infinite. The K value, as well as
μ, depends on cable structure and may change according to temperature, due to the different behavior
of the interfacing materials.

## 4.5 Cable pulling

It is important to proceed to fundamental checking before pulling a cable on site.

Cable pulling involves a combination of variables which are often difficult to accurately predict.

Factors, such as cable type an size, sheath material, pipe material, lubricants, installation practices,
installation speed and installation direction influence the pulling force and shall be carefully evaluated
in the cable pulling design.

Also the geometrical details of the route, especially in case of ducted sections, may not be so accurate
in terms of bending radiuses and angles.

The calculation methods presented below are particularly applicable for cable pulling in ducts. However,
the approaches can be transposed to other cable layout through engineering considerations, such as
cable laid in air on metallic supports, provided adaptation of the calculation parameters. For example,
to pull a cable length on a chain of rollers, an equivalent friction coefficient is to be assessed over the
corresponding length.

### 4.5.1 Maximum pulling lengths

Factors which generally dictate the amount of cable on one reel includes manufacturing capability, cable
weight and transportation restrictions, etc.

From an installation perspective, maximum allowable pulling tension is dictated by the route length,
friction coefficient, route profile and bends.


Detailed pulling studies are required prior to finalization of the cable route. At all times, care must be
given to reduce the number of bends within a given cable route as too many curves will increase the
risk of unsuccessful cable pulls.

The “average” maximum cable lengths reported in our survey is 850 m in urban areas and 1185 m in
rural areas. The absolute maximum lengths reported are 2200 m for urban areas and 3700 m for rural
areas, such lengths, however, are exceptions and require highly specialized equipment and techniques
for their successful pull.

### 4.5.2 Clearance in ducts

Cables pulling in ducts or pipes requires that the duct/pipe have an internal diameter (D) wider than the
cable outer diameter (d) to allow for a safe operation. The free space between the cable and the duct
inner size is called clearance.

The clearance to be considered is not the result of the implementation of precise formulae, but the result
of practical experiences.

- For single core cable the inner size of the ducts should be at least 1.5 times the cable size,
    particularly for long ducts with some bends along the route. Having clearances between 50 and 80
    mm also assists in the reduction in cable thermo-mechanical axial thrust. Lower ratio of D/d can be
    foreseen for straight cable routes.
- For three cables pulled in the same duct in triangular formation, both a minimum and a maximum
    size of the duct/pipe is to be considered. The ratio D/d between the duct size and the cable size
    should be less than 2.8 in order to avoid jamming. This is because between ratio 2.8 and 3 the
    triangular formation tends to slip towards a cradle formation during pulling, particularly in bends. On
    the other hand, the minimum clearance between the top cable and the duct/pipe shall be 0.5 inches
    (13 mm) with bends and could go down to 0.25 inches (6 mm) in straight line. the clearance should
    also be adequate to accommodate the pulling eye or the cable grip.
- In this last case the presence of additional ducts or cables, for example Fiber Optic cables, to be
    pulled together with the power cables shall be carefully considered, since jamming could be
    originated by them

Note: reference is made to AEIC CG5-15. Reference also to TB669 Clause 6.4 with the Design of a
Duct System.

It is important to mention that pulling cables into ducts requires a careful check of duct cleanliness and
absence of welding burrs in the case of PE pipes.

### 4.5.3 Minimum bending radius

Bending a cable on a radius that is too severe with respect to its structure, could cause damage to the
cable components, thus affecting the cable/system life expectancy.

For this reason, minimum bending radii are recommended by cable manufacturers and standards as a
function of the cable design.

These are normally supplied as multiplicative factor (F) with respect to the cable overall diameter and it
assumes different values for static and dynamic installation modes.

Static installation mode is that occurring at the accessories interface, with no load applied.

The dynamic installation mode is that occurring during pulling, with load applied to the cable. This is
higher than the static one, since it takes into account the sidewall pressure effect.

Note: In case of pulling into ducts, a suitable factor (normally applied to the duct diameter) shall be
adopted, since in this case it is the duct that governs the bending size.

### 4.5.4 Pulling tension

The main parameter to be evaluated when assessing the cable laying aspects is the cable pulling
tension.

The knowledge of the pulling tension is not only essential to plan the actual lay, but also to assess the
suitability of cable design / route design / laying methodologies.


The following equations are applicable to single cables, nose pulled into trenches or into long ducts or
pipes.

The route should be first divided into specific sections of straight, curved, uphill slope and downhill slope.
The pulling tension required for each section is then calculated, starting at the drum and taking the exit
tension for each section as the entry tension for the next.

With:

W cable weight (N/m)

L length of section (m)

K coefficient of friction for that section

Θ angle subtended by the bend (radians)

R bend radius (m)

Ti entry tension (N)

Ti+1 exit tension (N)

Some checks should also be made to assess any potential issue in case of stop of cable pulling. The
pulling restart will involve the static friction coefficient, usually greater than the dynamic one.

### 4.5.4.6 Pulling into long length ducts

Long drill lengths as well as long curved section installations result as gentle curvature bends with very
large radius.

The formulas given above from 9 to 13 , assumes that the cable is being “pulled” to the inside of the
bend. Then the normal component of the pulling force itself becomes the primary contributor to added
frictional resistance and the required pulling force. These bend equations assume a form with the friction
coefficient in an exponent. But, when the weight of the cable in the bend (gravitational force) is greater
or even comparable to the vertical component of the pulling force, the cable will not ride on the inside of
the bend, and the assumptions in the bend equations are not met.

This is because of when a cable’s gravitational weight pulls it away from the inside duct wall, the
equations do not properly apply. The concave (or convex) vertical pulling equations should not used for
large radius bends.

Several alternatives have been proposed to solve this matter, but it can be observed that the above
equations with a large radius bend are very close to an equivalent length straight section.

The proposed method is to consider the section as it was composed of two inclined sections (upward
and downward slopes) with a bent sections in between.

The value of (θ) to be used in the upward and downward segments in the formula is half of the actual
bending radius, while the angle of the compensatory bend section at the bottom is the full value of (θ)
to consider the direction change.

The sidewall pressure produced by this bend can be calculated, but it could be reasonably neglected
since the HDD bending radius is generally very big to cause practical concernes in terms of sidewall
pressure force on cables.

Calculation example:

W cable weight (N/m)


L length of section (m)

K coefficient of friction for that section (m)

Θ angle subtended by the bend (radians)

R bend radius (m)

Ti entry tension (N)

Ti+1 exit tension (N)

The value of the exit tension of each section shall be used as entry tension for the subsequent one.

The pulling force required to pull the cable into a long HDD will be composed of a first inclined pull
down force

#### 𝑇𝑖+ 1 =𝑇𝑖−𝑊×𝐿×[𝑠𝑖𝑛𝜃−𝐾×𝑐𝑜𝑠𝜃] ( 16 )

A conjunction bend that produces a force:

#### 𝑇𝑖+ 1 =𝑇𝑖×𝑒(𝐾×𝜃) ( 17 )

And a final inclined pull up force:

#### 𝑇𝑖+ 1 =𝑇𝑖+𝑊×𝐿×[𝑠𝑖𝑛𝜃+𝐾×𝑐𝑜𝑠𝜃] ( 18 )

### 4.5.5 Pulling limits

Having established the pulling tension, it must be checked that this tension is within the acceptable limits
for the cable.

Pulling limits are given by physical cable limitations (both tensile and crushing limits) and the pulling
method (pulling eye or pulling grip) and ducting structure.

To avoid relative movement between conductor and sheath with possible disturbance of the insulation
it is essential to fit a cable pulling grip which is anchored to the conductor or to the conductor and the
sheath at the leading end of the cable. However, it is assumed that the tension is withstood by the
conductor.

The following values can be used as references.

Single core cables:

- Copper conductors 6 0 N/mm2
- Aluminium conductors 3 0 N/mm2

3 core cables (Triangular formation):

The pulling force is supposed to be mainly taken by the friction of the two lower phases, although some
efforts are spreading through the top phase as well.

- Copper conductors 5 0 N/mm2
- Aluminium conductors 3 0 N/mm2


Alternative values could be considered but, in any case, the maximum permitted levels of conductor
tension have to be checked/agreed with the cable supplier.

### 4.5.6 Coefficient of friction (COF)

There are two types of friction coefficients, which must be considered. The static coefficient of friction
dictates the force required to start a cable in motion. The dynamic coefficient of friction dictates the force
required to keep a cable in motion. The static coefficient of friction is greater than the dynamic coefficient
of friction. Most pulling calculations are made using the dynamic coefficient of friction because most
cable pulls are continuous and it is the force on a cable during a pull that is of concern. If a pull is stopped
before it is complete, a higher tension is required to restart the pull than is required to maintain the cable
in motion. Cable pulls should be started (or restarted) slowly.

The coefficient of friction, K in the formula, is a very important factor in the calculation of the pulling force
and depends on the material of the cable outer sheath, the type of surface with which it is in contact and
also the laying technique (laying in ducts, on rollers, ...).

Lubricant of various types and nature are used to lower the friction coefficient for cable pulled into ducts.
However it is recommended to select materials having neutral chemical effect on cable outer sheaths
such that they do not affect neither the mechanical nor the electrical properties of them. it must be noted
that lubricants are sensitive to temperature and that at low temperatures normally correspond a higher
friction coefficient.

It should also be considered that the value of the coefficient of friction may change with temperature.

For practical use, the friction coefficient figure shall also account for imperfect straightness of the route,
non-homogeneous lubrication and cable stiffness; in other words the real site conditions and may differ
from theoretical values. Therefore, friction coefficient shall be tuned based on experience.

Consideration should also be given to the coefficient of friction for the pulling line. Coefficients of friction
for pulling lines are often high and even if the relevant weight is low, it can affect the maximum tension
in the early part of the pull. This may govern the required capacity of the pulling equipment, sheaves or
eye bolts as well as the duct construction. Pulling line manufacturers should be contacted for this
information.

### 4.5.7 Weight correction factor

Linear weight of the cable (W), is also a predominant parameter in the determination of the pulling force.

The distribution of the weight in the duct (distribution of radial and tangent forces to the inner surface of
the duct) differ for a single cable and three cable pulling. Therefore, a weight correction factor (Wc >=1)
should be considered.

This factor depends on the relative position of the individual cables in the duct. Both the cradled and
triangular configurations produce a greater normal force between cables and conduit than would exist
for a single cable. This can be viewed as an effective increase in the weight of the cables and leads to
the development of the Weight Correction Factor.

The Weight Correction Factor can be calculated using expression of AEIC CG5-15.

### 4.5.8 Side wall pressure

Having established the pulling tension experienced all along the cable route during pulling, the side wall
pressure on the cable at bends must also be verified in order to make sure the cable will not be damaged
by crushing because of important compression on the cable sheath.

For pulling in curved underground ducts, or in air with iron skid plates, the compression force between
cable and the inner surface is given by:

### 4.5.9 Influence of the cable stiffness during pulling

Whilst cable is pulled in bend section, cable bending stiffness induces a resistive contribution to the
overall pulling tension developed to pull the cable in the duct. This effect is probably not predominant
onto the friction but could have a non-negligible effect for large section cables of short to medium length
along a cable route with several curves of large angle and short bending radius. This is not currently
taken into account in most of the referenced pulling calculation methods.

Part of the total pulling force is used to bend up the cable at the curve inlet and then to straighten back
the cable at the curve outlet as illustrated below.


## Figure 112 – Effect of cable bending stiffness during lay

Where Dext is the external cable diameter.

Route profile, including transition bends in vertical plane between two straight lines :


## Figure 113 – Pulling calculation example

Results:

## Figure 114 – Pulling calculation results

Calculations show a difference in tension and side wall pressure of approx. 250 daN and 25daN/m
depending on the pulling direction for the cable route in this example. However, much greater difference
could be found on any other cable route profile. Therefore, it is of utmost importance to check the pulling
force considering the direction intended on site for pulling, direction cannot be neglected.

Furthermore, the transition on site between straight line and a slope (ex: when the cable route needs to
go deeper) would consist practically on site in a simple bend in vertical plane. This is represented by
the sections referenced "AD", "FD", "AM", "FM" and “H” in the example above.

The effect of such bend on the increase in tension is difficult to predict since the cable may lose contact
(not touching) to the the inner diameter of the duct partially, because of the stiffness of the cable, its
own weight, and the tension applied to the cable.

In this regards, calculations could be found conservative. It is recommended to consider transition bends
even if bend parameters (radius angles) are not well known at early stage of the project, otherwise there
are more risks to underestimating the tension and the side wall pressure along the cable route.

The same example as above is calculated without the transition bends.
